_Why:_ The SOP directs staff to use NEWS2 to record monitoring details in the post-injection monitoring section, opening the NEWS2 template from the RT monitoring form, with previous readings shown alongside.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Standard Operating Procedure: Rapid Tranquilisation (Issue 1, no issue or review date completed on the document)

**Q88. [Medium]** How does the trust's High Dose Antipsychotic Treatment (HDAT) policy define a high dose antipsychotic?
- A. Any antipsychotic prescribed alongside a benzodiazepine
- B. Any antipsychotic given by the intramuscular (IM) route
- C. Any dose above 75 per cent of the British National Formulary (BNF) maximum
- **D. A total daily dose of a single antipsychotic above the maximum licensed dose for the patient's age and indication, or a total daily dose of two or more antipsychotics that exceeds the maximum using the percentage method [correct]**
_Why:_ The policy uses the British National Formulary definition: either a single antipsychotic above its maximum licensed dose for the age and indication, or two or more antipsychotics whose combined percentages exceed the maximum, and 'as required' doses must be included at the maximum the prescription permits in 24 hours.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Antipsychotics: High Dose Antipsychotic Treatment (HDAT) Policy (Inpatient and Community Service Users) (November 2024, issue 9, review November 2027)

**Q89. [Medium]** _Scenario: A detained patient was given urgent treatment under Section 62 of the Mental Health Act 1983 during an episode of acute disturbance. The patient has now settled._ What does the trust's Section 62 urgent treatment form say about continuing under Section 62?
- A. Section 62 can continue for up to 72 hours from the first dose
- **B. Urgent treatment cannot be continued beyond the point at which the crisis has been brought to an end, and the usual Part IV safeguards should be observed [correct]**
- C. Section 62 continues until a Second Opinion Appointed Doctor (SOAD) attends the ward
- D. Section 62 covers all further doses of the same medication for the rest of the admission
_Why:_ The trust's Section 62 form states that urgent treatment cannot be continued beyond the point at which the crisis has been brought to an end, and that the usual safeguards provided in Part IV should then be observed.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Mental Health Act 1983 Section 62 Urgent Treatment form (No issue or review date shown on the form)

**Q90. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient who had drunk alcohol before admission has just received intramuscular (IM) Rapid Tranquilisation (RT), so meets the trust's 'higher risk' criteria._ What monitoring frequency does the trust algorithm set for a higher risk patient?
- A. Every 15 minutes for one hour, then hourly for four hours
- B. Every 30 minutes for two hours, then at the nurse's discretion
- **C. Every 15 minutes until a doctor documents that monitoring can be safely reduced or stopped, then every 30 minutes until the patient is ambulatory with no further concerns [correct]**
- D. Continuous one to one observation for 12 hours
_Why:_ For a patient meeting any higher risk criterion the trust algorithm requires monitoring every 15 minutes until a doctor documents that it can be safely reduced or stopped, then every 30 minutes until the patient is ambulatory and there are no further concerns. Where no higher risk factor applies the algorithm sets monitoring every 15 minutes for one hour.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Drug Management of Violence and Aggression: Policy (August 2025, issue 10, review January 2027)

**Q91. [Hard]** _Scenario: Following Rapid Tranquilisation (RT) a patient is deeply asleep on their bed._ What does the trust policy say about monitoring a patient who is asleep or unconscious after RT?
- A. Leave the patient to sleep and check them at the next scheduled observation round
- **B. Continuous pulse oximetry to measure oxygen saturation is desirable, and a nurse should remain with the patient until they are ambulatory [correct]**
- C. Wake the patient every 15 minutes to check their level of consciousness
- D. Move the patient to the seclusion room so they can be observed through the window
_Why:_ The monitoring section of the policy states that if the patient is asleep or unconscious, continuous use of pulse oximetry to measure oxygen saturation is desirable and a nurse should remain with the patient until ambulatory.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Drug Management of Violence and Aggression: Policy (August 2025, issue 10, review January 2027)

**Q92. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient accepts oral 'when required' (PRN) lorazepam for agitation and settles._ What does the trust's oral PRN algorithm require you to document afterwards?
- **A. The patient's activity and alertness one hour after administration [correct]**
- B. A full set of physical observations every 15 minutes for one hour
- C. The patient's activity and alertness four hours after administration
- D. Nothing beyond signing the medication chart, unless the patient deteriorates
_Why:_ The trust's Oral PRN Medication algorithm requires staff to document the patient's activity and alertness one hour after administration of oral PRN, and to follow the rapid tranquilisation monitoring guidance if there are clinical concerns.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Drug Management of Violence and Aggression: Policy (August 2025, issue 10, review January 2027)

**Q93. [Hard]** _Scenario: Rapid Tranquilisation (RT) is given at 2am on a Sunday. The ward's senior medical team are not on site._ How does the trust policy allow the required senior review to happen out of hours?
- A. The review is deferred until the next working day and recorded then
- B. The duty doctor completes the review in their own right and no senior involvement is needed
- C. The nurse in charge documents the review on behalf of the medical team
- **D. The duty doctor can discuss the patient's presentation, care, medication and response with a senior doctor, then document the review in the care record and on the SystmOne RT questionnaire, with the senior doctor remaining responsible for the review [correct]**
_Why:_ The policy states that outside normal working hours a duty doctor may discuss the case with a senior doctor and then document the review in the care record and on the SystmOne RT questionnaire, and that the senior doctor remains responsible for the review. It does not mandate the senior doctor's attendance.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Drug Management of Violence and Aggression: Policy (August 2025, issue 10, review January 2027)

**Q94. [Hard]** Beyond the immediate debrief, what does the trust's Rapid Tranquilisation (RT) monitoring and debrief algorithm say must be offered to the service user, and in what timescale?
- **A. Within 72 hours, the opportunity to discuss the incident in a supportive environment and to write their own perspective of the event for their care record [correct]**
- B. Within 24 hours, a written apology from the ward manager
- C. Within 7 days, a referral to an Independent Mental Health Advocate (IMHA)
- D. Within 28 days, a copy of the DATIX incident report
_Why:_ The algorithm requires that within 72 hours the service user is offered the opportunity to discuss the incident in a supportive environment and to write their perspective of the event for inclusion in their care record, and that their care plan is reviewed and updated.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Drug Management of Violence and Aggression: Policy (August 2025, issue 10, review January 2027)

**Q95. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient prescribed an anticoagulant needs an intramuscular (IM) injection for Rapid Tranquilisation (RT)._ What administration steps does the trust policy set out to reduce the risk of a deep muscle haematoma?
- A. Use a shorter needle and inject into the deltoid rather than the thigh
- B. Massage the site for 30 seconds to disperse the drug, then apply a dressing
- **C. Apply firm pressure to the injection site for at least 2 minutes to aid clotting, and do not rub or massage the site [correct]**
- D. Apply an ice pack to the site for 10 minutes after the injection
_Why:_ The administration algorithm says that for a patient with a bleeding disorder or on an anticoagulant, staff should apply firm pressure to the injection site for at least 2 minutes to aid clotting and must not rub or massage the site. If these steps are impractical this must be discussed with the prescriber and a risk assessment documented first.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Drug Management of Violence and Aggression: Policy (August 2025, issue 10, review January 2027)

**Q96. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient who is not currently violent or aggressive refuses their regular oral antipsychotic. The multidisciplinary team (MDT) has agreed a short term intramuscular (IM) injection as part of their individualised pharmacological strategy._ How does the trust policy say this non-Rapid Tranquilisation (non-RT) IM injection must be handled?
- A. Prescribe it on the Rapid Tranquilisation (RT) formulary and complete an RT form and a DATIX, as for any IM injection
- **B. Prescribe it on the Live Master Formulary rather than the RT formulary, monitor the patient one hour after administration, and do not complete a DATIX or an RT form specifically for this administration [correct]**
- C. Prescribe it on the Live Master Formulary but still complete the SystmOne RT form so that monitoring is triggered
- D. Prescribe it on either formulary, as long as the reason for choosing the IM route is documented
_Why:_ The non-RT IM injection algorithm requires prescribing through the Live Master Formulary on SystmOne and not the RT Formulary, monitoring one hour after administration, and states explicitly that staff should not complete a DATIX specifically for this type of administration and should not record it on a SystmOne RT form.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Drug Management of Violence and Aggression: Policy (August 2025, issue 10, review January 2027)

**Q97. [Hard]** A patient has just been started on High Dose Antipsychotic Treatment (HDAT). Which monitoring schedule does the trust HDAT policy set for the early weeks?
- A. Blood pressure, pulse, QTc and urea and electrolytes (U&Es) daily for the first week only
- B. Blood pressure and pulse alone, monthly for the first three months
- **C. Blood pressure, pulse, QTc and urea and electrolytes (U&Es) weekly for 4 weeks, then one week after each dose increase and at reviews [correct]**
- D. A full physical health screen at 6 weeks and then annually
_Why:_ The HDAT physical monitoring section requires blood pressure, pulse, QTc and U&Es to be recorded weekly for 4 weeks, then one week after each dose increase and at reviews, with a wider repeat panel at least 3-monthly for the first year.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Antipsychotics: High Dose Antipsychotic Treatment (HDAT) Policy (Inpatient and Community Service Users) (November 2024, issue 9, review November 2027)

**Q98. [Hard]** _Scenario: An electrocardiogram (ECG) for a patient on High Dose Antipsychotic Treatment (HDAT) reports a QTc of 510ms._ What does the trust HDAT policy flag this as, and what action does it list?
- **A. A red flag: manually confirm the calculated QTc, review other medication that may increase the QTc, check potassium, magnesium and calcium levels and correct abnormalities, and consider reducing the antipsychotic dose immediately [correct]**
- B. A routine finding: repeat the ECG at the next 3-monthly review
- C. A red flag: stop all medication immediately and transfer the patient to the acute hospital
- D. A borderline result: no action needed unless the patient has symptoms
_Why:_ The HDAT policy sets a red flag at a QTc above 499ms, with four listed actions: manually confirm the calculated QTc, review other QTc-prolonging medication, check potassium, magnesium and calcium and correct abnormalities, and consider reducing the antipsychotic dose immediately. A QTc above 450ms in men or 460ms in women prompts a treatment review.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Antipsychotics: High Dose Antipsychotic Treatment (HDAT) Policy (Inpatient and Community Service Users) (November 2024, issue 9, review November 2027)

**Q99. [Hard]** _Scenario: It is the middle of the night. A Registered Mental Nurse (RMN) takes a telephone call from the Responsible Clinician (RC) authorising urgent treatment under Section 62 of the Mental Health Act 1983._ What does the trust's verbal instruction form require?
- A. Only the RC's verbal authorisation, recorded as a progress note in the care record
- **B. The nurse records the details of the one off treatment authorised and signs the form, and agrees with the RC or Approved Clinician (AC) when they will come in and sign it, that is the next day or the next working day [correct]**
- C. Two nurses to sign the form, with no further medical signature required
- D. The nurse to fax the form to the Mental Health Act office before giving the medication
_Why:_ The trust's verbal instruction form is completed and signed by the nurse who received the telephone instruction, records the details of the one off treatment authorised, and states that the nurse is to agree with the RC or AC when they will come in and sign the form, that is the next day or the next working day.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Verbal Instruction for the administration of Mental Health Act 1983 Section 62 Urgent Treatment (No issue or review date shown on the form)

## Trust Policy - Observations & Risk

**Q100. [Easy]** The trust's Inpatient Therapeutic Observations and Engagement Policy uses four numbered levels of observation. Which level is the highest and most intensive?
- A. Level 4, because the numbers increase with intensity
- **B. Level 1, continuous observation within arm's length [correct]**
- C. Level 2, continuous observation within eyesight
- D. Level 3, intermittent observation within a 15 minute period
_Why:_ The trust numbers its levels downwards: Level 4 is general observation and Level 1, within arm's length, is described in the policy as the highest level of observation.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Inpatient Therapeutic Observations and Engagement Policy and Procedure (February 2025, issue 10, review February 2028)

**Q101. [Easy]** _Scenario: A newly admitted patient is assessed as presenting no serious risk of harm to self or others._ Under trust policy, what is the lowest level of observation this patient can be placed on?
- A. No observation level is needed if risk is low
- B. Level 3, intermittent observation
- **C. Level 4, general observation [correct]**
- D. Observation levels only apply to detained patients
_Why:_ The policy states that Level 4 general observation is the minimally acceptable level of observation for all inpatients, and the clinical skills workbook confirms all inpatients must be under one of the four levels.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Inpatient Therapeutic Observations and Engagement Policy and Procedure (February 2025, issue 10, review February 2028)

**Q102. [Hard]** For a patient on Level 4 general observation, how often does the trust policy say the Nurse in Charge (or delegated person) must visually check their whereabouts?
- **A. Hourly during the day and half hourly at night [correct]**
- B. Half hourly during the day and hourly at night
- C. Hourly during the day and hourly at night
- D. Twice per shift, at the start and the end
_Why:_ The policy sets a minimum of an hourly visual check during the day and half hourly at night for Level 4, unless something different is specified in the patient's care plan.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Inpatient Therapeutic Observations and Engagement Policy and Procedure (February 2025, issue 10, review February 2028)

**Q103. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient is on Level 3 intermittent observation and a colleague sets a phone alarm to check them at exactly 15 minute intervals._ Why does the trust policy say this is the wrong approach?
- A. Checks must be more frequent than every 15 minutes on Level 3
- B. Only the Nurse in Charge may carry out Level 3 checks
- **C. Checks must be done at varying times within the 15 minute period so a patient at risk cannot predict a guaranteed gap [correct]**
- D. Phones are not permitted in clinical areas
_Why:_ The policy requires visual observation within a 15 minute period but at varying times within it, so a patient at risk of suicide does not know they have exactly 15 minutes before the next staff contact.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Inpatient Therapeutic Observations and Engagement Policy and Procedure (February 2025, issue 10, review February 2028)

**Q104. [Easy]** What does Level 2 observation mean in this trust?
- A. The patient is checked randomly within each 15 minute period
- **B. The patient is kept within eyesight at all times, day and night, including toilet and bathing time [correct]**
- C. The patient is kept within arm's length at all times
- D. The patient is checked hourly and must report their whereabouts
_Why:_ Level 2 is 'within eyesight': the patient is kept within sight at all times day and night, and the allocated clinician's total concentration must be on the patient, including time in the toilet, while bathing and when asleep.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Inpatient Therapeutic Observations and Engagement Policy and Procedure (February 2025, issue 10, review February 2028)

**Q105. [Hard]** _Scenario: A support worker has been on continuous observation with the same patient since the start of the shift._ For observation levels 1 to 3, what does trust policy say about how long one staff member should stay on observation duty with a patient?
- A. Up to 4 hours, with a 30 minute break afterwards
- **B. It should not exceed 2 hours at any one time, with 1 hour considered the norm, followed by a break of at least 1 hour [correct]**
- C. There is no time limit as long as the staff member feels able to continue
- D. A maximum of 90 minutes, followed by a 30 minute break
_Why:_ Section 7.5 says time on observation for levels 1 to 3 should not exceed 2 hours at any one time, 1 hour is the norm, and after 2 hours the clinician should hand over and take a break from observation duties for a minimum of 1 hour.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Inpatient Therapeutic Observations and Engagement Policy and Procedure (February 2025, issue 10, review February 2028)

**Q106. [Hard]** _Scenario: Because of short staffing, one member of staff ends up doing 3 hours of continuous Level 2 observation without a break._ What does trust policy require to happen next?
- A. Nothing, provided the Nurse in Charge agreed to it beforehand
- B. It is noted in the patient's care plan only
- C. The staff member submits a formal grievance
- **D. The variance is reported on a DATIX incident form so that it can be reviewed [correct]**
_Why:_ The policy states that any variance in excess of 2 hours of continual observation should be reported on a DATIX incident form so that it can be reviewed.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Inpatient Therapeutic Observations and Engagement Policy and Procedure (February 2025, issue 10, review February 2028)

**Q107. [Hard]** Who does the trust policy say may change observation scheduling on the electronic patient record?
- A. Any member of staff who holds a Brigid handheld device
- B. Only the Responsible Clinician
- **C. Only a registered professional who is aware of the patient's risk management plan and care plan [correct]**
- D. Only the ward manager or matron
_Why:_ The policy states that observation scheduling must only be changed by a registered professional who is aware of the patient's risk management plan and care plan.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Inpatient Therapeutic Observations and Engagement Policy and Procedure (February 2025, issue 10, review February 2028)

**Q108. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient was placed on Level 2 observation at 8pm last night._ Under trust policy, when must the first formal senior review of that decision take place?
- **A. Within 24 hours of commencement, involving senior medical staff and senior nursing staff [correct]**
- B. At the next weekly ward round
- C. Within 72 hours, involving the Head of Nursing
- D. Within 4 hours, involving the on-call doctor
_Why:_ Section 6.1 requires all patients on Level 1 or 2 observations to be reviewed with the involvement of senior medical staff (Consultant Psychiatrist or nominated deputy) and senior nursing staff within 24 hours of commencement, and then formally every 24 hours.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Inpatient Therapeutic Observations and Engagement Policy and Procedure (February 2025, issue 10, review February 2028)

**Q109. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient has now been on Level 1 observation continuously since Monday morning and it is Thursday morning._ At what point does trust policy require an additional review by a more senior manager, on top of the routine 24 hourly reviews?
- A. After 12 hours
- B. After 48 hours
- **C. After 72 hours [correct]**
- D. After 7 days
_Why:_ The policy states that if Level 1 or 2 observations continue for a period of 72 hours, a further review by a senior manager with senior medical or nursing staff from the ward must take place.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Inpatient Therapeutic Observations and Engagement Policy and Procedure (February 2025, issue 10, review February 2028)

**Q110. [Medium]** _Scenario: It is a bank holiday Monday and a patient on Level 1 observation is due their 24 hour review, but no consultant is on site._ What does trust policy allow?
- A. The review is deferred until the next working day
- **B. The review can be undertaken by two senior nursing staff, one of whom should be the bleep-holder, and may be done by phone [correct]**
- C. The Nurse in Charge documents the level and no review is needed
- D. Only the on-call consultant can complete it, and they must attend in person
_Why:_ The policy states that at weekends and on bank holidays the review can be undertaken by two senior nursing staff, one of whom should be the bleep-holder, and that this may be undertaken by phone.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Inpatient Therapeutic Observations and Engagement Policy and Procedure (February 2025, issue 10, review February 2028)

**Q111. [Medium]** How often must patients on Level 4 general observation be formally reviewed at the Multidisciplinary Team (MDT) meeting?
- A. Every 24 hours
- B. Every 72 hours
- **C. Weekly, with a doctor and a nurse present [correct]**
- D. Monthly, or sooner if risk changes
_Why:_ Section 6.3 requires all patients on Level 4 observations to be formally reviewed weekly at the multidisciplinary team meeting with a doctor and a nurse present, with the outcome recorded in the patient's records.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Inpatient Therapeutic Observations and Engagement Policy and Procedure (February 2025, issue 10, review February 2028)

**Q112. [Hard]** _Scenario: The team agrees at review that a patient can step down from Level 2 to Level 3 observation._ Under trust policy, who takes the final decision to reduce a Level 1 or 2 observation?
- A. Any registered nurse on the shift
- **B. The senior medical and/or senior nursing staff (band 6 or above) accountable for the patient's treatment and care [correct]**
- C. The Nurse in Charge of the shift, whatever their band
- D. The Head of Nursing only
_Why:_ The policy states the final decision to reduce Level 1 or 2 observations will be taken by the senior medical and/or senior nursing staff (band 6 or above) accountable for the treatment and care of the patient.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Inpatient Therapeutic Observations and Engagement Policy and Procedure (February 2025, issue 10, review February 2028)

**Q113. [Easy]** _Scenario: The doctor wants to reduce a patient to Level 3 observation, but the nursing team believe Level 2 is still needed. The disagreement is not resolved at the meeting._ What does trust policy say happens while the disagreement is being resolved?
- **A. The patient remains on the higher level of observation [correct]**
- B. The doctor's decision stands because they prescribe the level
- C. The level is reduced but reviewed again in 4 hours
- D. The Nurse in Charge decides on the day
_Why:_ The policy is clear that where there are differing views about the level required, the patient remains on the higher level until the matter is resolved, with immediate discussion with the Consultant Psychiatrist and senior nurse and escalation if needed.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Inpatient Therapeutic Observations and Engagement Policy and Procedure (February 2025, issue 10, review February 2028)

**Q114. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient subject to a Ministry of Justice restriction order is settled and the night team feel their observation level could be reduced._ What does trust policy say about changing observation levels for a restricted patient?
- A. The Nurse in Charge can both increase and decrease the level as clinically indicated
- **B. The Nurse in Charge can increase the level in response to concerns, but any reduction must be made by the Responsible Clinician in consultation with nursing staff [correct]**
- C. Only the Ministry of Justice can approve any change of observation level
- D. Restricted patients must always remain on Level 2 or above
_Why:_ Section 4.3 states that for patients on a Home Office restriction order the level is determined by the Responsible Clinician; the nurse in charge can increase levels in response to concerns but any reduction needs to be undertaken in consultation with the Responsible Clinician.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Inpatient Therapeutic Observations and Engagement Policy and Procedure (February 2025, issue 10, review February 2028)

**Q115. [Medium]** _Scenario: The ward is short staffed and a student nurse offers to take a turn on a patient's arm's length observation._ What does trust policy say about student nurses and Level 1 observation?
- A. Students may undertake Level 1 observation if the Nurse in Charge agrees
- B. Students may undertake Level 1 observation once they have completed the Clinical Observations Booklet
- **C. Students must not undertake Level 1 observation, although they may shadow as a learning experience [correct]**
- D. Students may undertake Level 1 observation only at night
_Why:_ The policy states that student nurses and Allied Health Professional students must not undertake Level 1 observations, although they can observe or shadow as part of their learning experience.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Inpatient Therapeutic Observations and Engagement Policy and Procedure (February 2025, issue 10, review February 2028)

**Q116. [Hard]** _Scenario: A student nurse in the final months of their course has completed the Clinical Observations Booklet and been assessed as competent._ Which statement matches trust policy on what that student may now do?
- **A. They may undertake Level 2 observation, and would not normally exceed 3 hours of observation in any shift [correct]**
- B. They may undertake any level of observation including Level 1
- C. They may only undertake Level 4 observation until they are registered
- D. They may undertake Level 2 observation and may be allocated for a whole shift if staffing requires it
_Why:_ The policy allows students in the last 6 months of their course to undertake Level 2 observations once assessed as competent, and states it would be expected that a student does not exceed 3 hours of any shift on observations, and not at the expense of other learning opportunities.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Inpatient Therapeutic Observations and Engagement Policy and Procedure (February 2025, issue 10, review February 2028)

**Q117. [Medium]** _Scenario: On prescribing an increased observation level, the team decide a search of the patient and their belongings is necessary._ What staffing does trust policy require for that search?
- A. One member of staff, with a second waiting outside the room
- **B. Two members of staff, one of whom should be the same gender as the service user [correct]**
- C. Two members of staff, at least one of whom must be a registered nurse
- D. Three members of staff, including the Nurse in Charge
_Why:_ Section 4.7 requires two members of staff to be present for a personal search, one of whom should be the same gender as the service user, and notes searching is a restrictive practice that should not be routine for all patients.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Inpatient Therapeutic Observations and Engagement Policy and Procedure (February 2025, issue 10, review February 2028)

**Q118. [Easy]** _Scenario: A patient's relative is visiting for the afternoon and offers to sit with them so staff can get on, while the patient is on Level 2 observation._ What does trust policy say?
- A. This is acceptable if the Nurse in Charge stays nearby
- B. This is acceptable for up to one hour
- **C. It is not appropriate for a patient's carer or relative to carry out levels of observation [correct]**
- D. This is acceptable if the relative signs the observation record
_Why:_ Section 7.9 states plainly that it is not appropriate for a patient's carer to carry out levels of observation; observation is a skilled clinical duty allocated by the Nurse in Charge.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Inpatient Therapeutic Observations and Engagement Policy and Procedure (February 2025, issue 10, review February 2028)

**Q119. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient on Level 3 intermittent observation has settled in bed, and the night team suggest dropping the checks until morning._ What does trust policy require?
- A. Level 3 observations may be relaxed overnight if the patient is asleep
- **B. Level 3 observations must not automatically be reduced in the evening or at night, and any reduction must follow a documented multidisciplinary discussion including the patient, with the Safety Plan reviewed [correct]**
- C. The night co-ordinator can authorise a reduction verbally
- D. Level 3 observations always become Level 4 between midnight and 7am
_Why:_ The policy states Level 3 observations must not automatically be reduced in the evening or at night and must only take place following a documented multidisciplinary discussion, including the patient, and a review of the Safety Plan.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Inpatient Therapeutic Observations and Engagement Policy and Procedure (February 2025, issue 10, review February 2028)

**Q120. [Easy]** _Scenario: It is the start of a late shift._ Under the trust's observation policy, what is the Nurse in Charge of the outgoing shift responsible for at the beginning of every shift?
- A. Emailing a written summary to the ward manager
- B. Handing the Brigid device to the incoming Nurse in Charge and nothing further
- **C. Giving a verbal handover to the incoming Nurse in Charge identifying that all patients are present and accounting for those absent and their whereabouts [correct]**
- D. Completing a full risk assessment on every patient
_Why:_ The policy makes this a named responsibility of the outgoing Nurse in Charge, alongside an environmental walk around the ward to check there are no concerns, and it also gives patients the chance to see the incoming Nurse in Charge.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Inpatient Therapeutic Observations and Engagement Policy and Procedure (February 2025, issue 10, review February 2028)

**Q121. [Medium]** Beyond checking whereabouts, what does the trust policy expect on every shift for patients on Level 4 general observation?
- **A. At least one allocated nurse per shift should sit down and talk with their allocated patients and assess their mental state, and record this in the notes [correct]**
- B. A full physical health check using the National Early Warning Score (NEWS2)
- C. A written statement from the patient confirming they feel safe
- D. A face to face review by the Responsible Clinician
_Why:_ The Level 4 section requires at least one allocated nurse per shift to sit down and talk to their allocated patients and assess mental state, including an evaluation of mood and behaviours associated with risk, recorded in the notes.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Inpatient Therapeutic Observations and Engagement Policy and Procedure (February 2025, issue 10, review February 2028)

**Q122. [Hard]** The trust monitors observations from ward to board using SystmOne and the handheld devices. What performance standard does the policy set for the performance and recording of patient observations?
- A. Above 75 per cent
- B. Above 85 per cent
- **C. Above 90 per cent [correct]**
- D. 100 per cent, with no tolerance
_Why:_ The governance section states that performance and recording of patient observations should remain above 90 per cent, monitored through the Observation Trend Report, alongside quality walkabouts by senior clinical staff.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Inpatient Therapeutic Observations and Engagement Policy and Procedure (February 2025, issue 10, review February 2028)

**Q123. [Easy]** _Scenario: An agency healthcare assistant arrives on the ward for their first shift and is offered an observation allocation._ What does trust policy require first?
- A. Nothing extra, as agency staff are checked by the agency before they arrive
- **B. They must be inducted to the observation policy and assessed by the Nurse in Charge as having understanding before undertaking observations [correct]**
- C. They must have worked at least three shifts on the ward
- D. They must be signed off by the Matron in person
_Why:_ Section 12.2 states that no temporary bank or agency staff should be involved in undertaking observation until they have been inducted as to the policy and assessed by the Nurse in Charge to have understanding.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Inpatient Therapeutic Observations and Engagement Policy and Procedure (February 2025, issue 10, review February 2028)

**Q124. [Medium]** _Scenario: A doctor asks to interview a patient in private while that patient is on Level 2 observation._ What does trust policy require of the staff member currently doing the observation?
- A. They should pause the observation record until the interview finishes
- B. They should stand outside the door and resume when the doctor leaves
- **C. They must make clear to the colleague that the colleague takes full responsibility for maintaining the agreed level, and at the end formally hand the observation back to a staff member allocated by the Nurse in Charge [correct]**
- D. They should ask the patient whether they want the observation to continue
_Why:_ Section 9.8 requires the person doing the observation to make it explicit that the requesting colleague takes full responsibility for maintaining the agreed level during the interview, with a formal handover back to a clinician allocated by the Nurse in Charge afterwards.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Inpatient Therapeutic Observations and Engagement Policy and Procedure (February 2025, issue 10, review February 2028)

**Q125. [Easy]** The trust's Clinical Skills Workbook for Therapeutic Observation of Patients is aimed at which staff, and who signs it off as complete?
- A. Registered nurses only, signed off by the Matron
- **B. Clinical and non-clinical staff with patient contact, signed off by the Sister or Lead Nurse [correct]**
- C. Student nurses only, signed off by their university mentor
- D. Bank and agency staff only, signed off by the temporary staffing team
_Why:_ The workbook is titled for clinical and non-clinical staff with patient contact and applies to all direct care staff in inpatient services; the front cover is signed by the staff member and by the Sister or Lead Nurse signing off completion.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Clinical Skills Workbook for Therapeutic Observation of Patients (November 2024, version 2)

**Q126. [Medium]** _Scenario: A ward manager wants to log the risk that the ward's ligature audit has identified._ Which system does the trust's Risk Assessment Policy and Procedure direct staff to for generic and environmental risks like this?
- **A. The Datix Risk Register module, using the Risk Assessment Form [correct]**
- B. The SystmOne Risk Node on the patient record
- C. A locally held ward spreadsheet reviewed at team meeting
- D. The incident reporting module only
_Why:_ The policy requires all generic, environmental, health and safety, corporate and organisational risks to be recorded, reviewed and escalated using the Datix Risk Register module via the Risk Assessment Form.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Risk Assessment Policy and Procedure (June 2024, issue 12, review June 2027)

**Q127. [Medium]** _Scenario: A newly qualified nurse assumes the Risk Assessment Policy and the Datix Risk Register are where an individual patient's clinical risk is recorded._ What does the Risk Assessment Policy actually say?
- A. Individual patient clinical risk should be logged on Datix if it is rated high or extreme
- B. Individual patient clinical risk is covered by this policy for inpatients only
- **C. The Datix risk process must not be used for an individual patient's clinical risk, which is covered by the Safety Needs Assessment and Management of Safety Needs Policy [correct]**
- D. Individual patient clinical risk is covered by the Health and Safety at Work Policy
_Why:_ The Risk Assessment Policy states explicitly that the Datix process must not be used for recording an individual patient's clinical risk, and points staff to the Safety Needs Assessment and Management of Safety Needs Policy instead.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Risk Assessment Policy and Procedure (June 2024, issue 12, review June 2027)

**Q128. [Hard]** _Scenario: A ward risk on the Datix risk register has a residual rating of high._ Under trust policy, what is the maximum interval before it must be reviewed again?
- **A. Within 3 months [correct]**
- B. Within 6 months
- C. Within 12 months
- D. Within 28 days
_Why:_ Section 5.2 sets review intervals by residual rating: very low or low within 12 months, moderate within 6 months, and high or extreme within 3 months, with the next review date no later than the due date of the latest action.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Risk Assessment Policy and Procedure (June 2024, issue 12, review June 2027)

**Q129. [Medium]** Which tool does the trust use for ligature risk assessment?
- A. The Manchester Tool
- **B. The Care Quality Commission (CQC) Ligature Risk Assessment Tool [correct]**
- C. The HCR-20
- D. The National Early Warning Score (NEWS2) environmental annex
_Why:_ The version control notes record that the Manchester Tool is not being used for ligature risk assessment and that the CQC Ligature Risk Assessment Tool is used instead, with requirements set out in the Ligature Risk Reduction Policy and its linked Standard Operating Procedure (SOP).
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Risk Assessment Policy and Procedure (June 2024, issue 12, review June 2027)

**Q130. [Medium]** The trust's Safety Planning Standard Operating Procedure (SOP) sets out a three tiered approach on SystmOne. What are the three tiers, in order?
- A. Safety Box, Safety Plan, Care Plan
- **B. Risk Screen, Risk Assessment, Safety Plan [correct]**
- C. Risk Screen, Safety Plan, Risk Management Plan
- D. Risk Assessment, Formulation, Crisis Plan
_Why:_ The SOP describes the move away from Safety Planning and Safety Boxes to a three tiered approach of Risk Screen, then Risk Assessment, then Safety Planning, with patient involvement at the centre of all three.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Standard Operating Procedure: Safety Planning (13.11.2020, issue 01, review 13.11.2021)
_⚠ review date passed_

**Q131. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient is admitted overnight and the Risk Screen has not yet been done._ Under the trust's Safety Planning Standard Operating Procedure (SOP), who can complete the Risk Screening Tool?
- A. Only a band 6 or above registered nurse
- B. Only the Responsible Clinician or an approved clinician
- **C. Any clinician, and it should be completed for all patients at the point of admission [correct]**
- D. Only staff who have completed the HCR-20 training
_Why:_ The SOP states any clinician can complete the Risk Screening Tool and it should be completed for all patients at the point of admission, new team co-ordination, care change or where risks change or need reviewing.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Standard Operating Procedure: Safety Planning (13.11.2020, issue 01, review 13.11.2021)
_⚠ review date passed_

**Q132. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient strongly disagrees with a risk that the team has identified in their risk screen and asks for it to be taken out._ What does the trust's Safety Planning Standard Operating Procedure (SOP) say should happen?
- A. The risk should be removed, as the safety plan must be collaborative
- **B. The patient's views are recorded in their Care Plan; the risk screen, assessment or safety plan should not be altered because of the disagreement unless there are identified reasons to do so [correct]**
- C. The risk stays and the patient's view is not recorded, to keep the record objective
- D. The disagreement is referred to the Complex Risk Panel before anything is recorded
_Why:_ The SOP states that where a patient refuses to engage or disagrees with the identified risks, their views should be clearly explained within their Care Plan, and the risk screen, assessment or safety plan should not be altered because of the disagreement unless there are identified reasons to do so.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Standard Operating Procedure: Safety Planning (13.11.2020, issue 01, review 13.11.2021)
_⚠ review date passed_

**Q133. [Medium]** _Scenario: A complex case has been discussed at the trust's Complex Risk Panel and written minutes have come back to the ward._ Under the Safety Needs policy, where does responsibility sit after that discussion?
- A. Responsibility transfers to the panel, who then oversee the case
- B. Responsibility is shared equally between the panel and the ward until the actions are complete
- **C. Responsibility for the risks and agreed actions remains with the referring clinical team, who must add the panel minutes to the electronic patient record [correct]**
- D. Responsibility passes to the Head of Nursing as joint chair of the panel
_Why:_ The policy states that responsibility for the identified risks and needs, and any actions agreed, remains with the referring clinical team and does not sit with panel members, and that the referring team must add the panel's written minutes to the electronic patient record.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Safety Needs Assessment and Management of Safety Needs Policy (June 2024, issue 04, review June 2027)

**Q134. [Easy]** _Scenario: A patient is being considered for their first period of Section 17 leave._ Under the trust's Safety Needs policy, when should their safety needs be reviewed?
- A. Only if there has been an incident in the previous week
- **B. Before leave is granted, as this is one of the listed points for review [correct]**
- C. Only at the next Care Programme Approach (CPA) review
- D. Only if the leave is overnight or longer
_Why:_ The policy lists points at which safety needs should be reviewed, including first contact with services, at CPA reviews, on admission and discharge, prior to granting leave, on transfer, after major changes or incidents, and when observation levels need reviewing.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Safety Needs Assessment and Management of Safety Needs Policy (June 2024, issue 04, review June 2027)

**Q135. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient is too unwell to take part in risk discussions and has no family involved._ What does the trust's Safety Needs policy say should happen to the Risk Screen and Safety Plan?
- A. They are deferred until the patient is well enough to take part
- **B. A Risk Screen and Safety Plan should still be completed, then revisited with the patient and their carer or family as soon as they are able or willing to take part [correct]**
- C. Only a Risk Screen is completed; the Safety Plan waits until discharge planning
- D. An advocate must complete them on the patient's behalf
_Why:_ The September 2025 amendment to section 3.5 makes explicit that where the service user or carer cannot or will not participate, a Risk Screen and Safety Plan should still be completed and then revisited with them as soon as they are able or willing.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Safety Needs Assessment and Management of Safety Needs Policy (June 2024, issue 04, review June 2027)

## Trust Policy - Ligature & Missing Patients

**Q136. [Easy]** _Scenario: A bank nurse is working her first shift on a working age adult acute ward and asks where the ligature cutters are._ On the Trust's working age adult acute wards, where are ligature cutters and tough cut scissors kept?
- A. In the locked clinic room cupboard with the controlled drugs
- **B. In a designated area known as 'the green wall' [correct]**
- C. One set carried by each qualified nurse on the shift
- D. In the nursing office desk drawer
_Why:_ The Trust guidance states the knives and tough cut scissors are kept in a designated area of the ward called 'the green wall', so that staff unfamiliar with the ward can find them quickly. All new staff, including bank and agency, must be orientated to this area.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Ligature Cutter Guidance including Storage / Ordering Process (September 2024, issue 01, review September 2027)

**Q137. [Easy]** How often must a working age adult acute ward check that it holds the agreed minimum number of ligature cutters?
- **A. Daily [correct]**
- B. At every shift handover
- C. Weekly
- D. Monthly
_Why:_ The Trust guidance requires a daily check to ensure the ward has the agreed minimum number of ligature cutters available, with the ward manager giving assurance on this as part of senior nurse walkarounds.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Ligature Cutter Guidance including Storage / Ordering Process (September 2024, issue 01, review September 2027)

**Q138. [Hard]** What is the minimum stock of ligature cutters and tough cut scissors the Trust requires an acute inpatient area to hold?
- A. 10 ligature cutters and 2 tough cut scissors
- B. 10 ligature cutters and 5 tough cut scissors
- **C. 20 ligature cutters and 5 tough cut scissors [correct]**
- D. 20 ligature cutters and 10 tough cut scissors
_Why:_ The Trust guidance sets acute inpatient areas at 20 ligature cutters and 5 tough cut scissors, which is a higher local stock level than other inpatient areas because of the higher rate of incidents and acuity.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Ligature Cutter Guidance including Storage / Ordering Process (September 2024, issue 01, review September 2027)

**Q139. [Medium]** _Scenario: A nurse moves from an acute ward to an older adults inpatient ward and expects the same stock levels._ What minimum stock of ligature cutters does the Trust set for older adults, forensic and rehabilitation inpatient areas?
- A. 20 ligature cutters and 5 tough cut scissors, the same as acute
- **B. 10 ligature cutters and 5 tough cut scissors [correct]**
- C. 5 ligature cutters and 5 tough cut scissors
- D. There is no set minimum, it is decided by the ward manager
_Why:_ Because these areas have a lower rate of incidents than working age adult acute wards, the Trust sets a lower local stock level of 10 cutters plus 5 tough cut scissors, and these areas do not use the weekly acute tracking system.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Ligature Cutter Guidance including Storage / Ordering Process (September 2024, issue 01, review September 2027)

**Q140. [Hard]** Under the Trust's stock control process for acute inpatient units, when are the unit stock cupboards audited and by whom?
- A. Every Monday by the ward manager
- **B. Every Tuesday by the clinical leads and Matrons [correct]**
- C. Every Friday by the Estates team
- D. Monthly by the Health and Safety Team
_Why:_ The Trust guidance states unit stock cupboards are audited weekly on a Tuesday by the clinical leads and Matrons, and that this audit is what informs the ordering process for restocking the Trust ligature knife supply.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Ligature Cutter Guidance including Storage / Ordering Process (September 2024, issue 01, review September 2027)

**Q141. [Medium]** _Scenario: A nurse is completing the emergency trolley check at the start of a shift._ According to the Trust's ligature cutter guidance, where must one set of cutters always be kept as part of the emergency equipment?
- A. Taped to the inside of the seclusion room door
- **B. In the airway section of the resuscitation trolley or Red Bag [correct]**
- C. With the ward keys held by the nurse in charge
- D. In the drugs trolley next to the naloxone
_Why:_ The Trust guidance on a page states one set must be in the airway section of the resus trolley or Red Bag, and that compliance with the ligature cutter guidance should be assessed as part of the emergency trolley checks.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Ligature Cutter Guidance including Storage / Ordering Process (September 2024, issue 01, review September 2027)

**Q142. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient has used a leather belt as a ligature._ Which of the two ligature cutters supplied by the Trust is intended for tougher materials such as leather, towelling, some steel cables and electrical flexes?
- A. The disposable cutter
- **B. The tough cut scissors [correct]**
- C. Either, there is no difference in what they will cut
- D. Standard ward scissors from the clinic room
_Why:_ The Trust supplies two products. The disposable cutter is most effective for most items, while the tough cut scissors will cut tougher materials such as leather, towelling, some steel cables and electrical flexes, although more effort and time may be needed.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Ligature Cutter Guidance including Storage / Ordering Process (September 2024, issue 01, review September 2027)

**Q143. [Medium]** Where does the Trust's ligature cutter guidance recommend inserting the cutter blade in relation to the patient's neck?
- A. At the back of the neck
- B. At the front of the neck, over the airway
- **C. To the side of the neck [correct]**
- D. Wherever the knot happens to be
_Why:_ The guidance recommends cutting to the side of the neck where the natural soft tissues and hollows ease insertion of the blade. This reduces pulling on the airway, which happens if the cutter is inserted at the back, and reduces further airway trauma, which can happen from the front.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Ligature Cutter Guidance including Storage / Ordering Process (September 2024, issue 01, review September 2027)

**Q144. [Hard]** _Scenario: A ligature has been tied with a firm knot and a nurse is deciding where to cut._ Why does the Trust's ligature cutter guidance say staff must, where possible, avoid cutting the knot?
- **A. It makes removal more difficult and the knot may be required later for forensic investigation [correct]**
- B. Cutting the knot will blunt the disposable cutter
- C. The knot is the strongest point and the cutter will not go through it
- D. Cutting the knot invalidates the manufacturer's warranty
_Why:_ The guidance says staff must avoid cutting the knot where possible because it makes removal more difficult and the knot may be needed later for forensic investigation.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Ligature Cutter Guidance including Storage / Ordering Process (September 2024, issue 01, review September 2027)

**Q145. [Medium]** _Scenario: A ligature incident on the ward has ended in a fatality._ What does the Trust's ligature guidance say must happen to the room in the event of a fatality?
- A. The room should be cleaned and made safe before relatives arrive
- B. The ligature should be removed and bagged, and the room reopened
- **C. The room and all of its contents must not be touched or moved, the room must be secured, and the ligature left in the room until the police arrive [correct]**
- D. Only the ligature point itself needs to be preserved
_Why:_ The Trust guidance is explicit that in the event of a fatality the room and all of its contents must not be touched or moved, the room must be secured to prevent anyone accessing it until police arrive, and the ligature must be left in the room.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Ligature Cutter Guidance including Storage / Ordering Process (September 2024, issue 01, review September 2027)

**Q146. [Easy]** _Scenario: A ligature cutter has been used and the nurse is completing the paperwork._ What does the Trust's ligature cutter guidance say must be recorded on the incident report after a ligature cutter is used?
- A. Only the patient's observation level at the time
- **B. The type of cutter or scissors that was used, with the incident also recorded in the patient notes [correct]**
- C. The serial number of the cutter
- D. Nothing, unless the patient was injured
_Why:_ The Trust's guidance on a page requires staff to complete a Datix incident report indicating the type of cutter or scissors used, record the incident in the patient notes, and replace the cutter.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Ligature Cutter Guidance including Storage / Ordering Process (September 2024, issue 01, review September 2027)

**Q147. [Medium]** _Scenario: A cutter has been used on an older adults ward and needs replacing._ Outside the working age adult acute units, how does a Trust ward or community base replace a ligature cutter that has been used in an incident?
- A. It is automatically replaced at the next weekly Estates delivery
- B. The ward manager orders directly from the supplier
- **C. A replacement is requested from Kingsway stores [correct]**
- D. The Health and Safety Team issues one on receipt of the Datix
_Why:_ For older adults, forensic, rehabilitation and community bases such as CAMHS, the Trust guidance says that when an incident occurs a replacement cutter should be requested from Kingsway stores, as these areas do not use the weekly acute tracking system.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Ligature Cutter Guidance including Storage / Ordering Process (September 2024, issue 01, review September 2027)

**Q148. [Easy]** Which tool and which system does the Trust's Standard Operating Procedure (SOP) require staff to use to complete and record a ligature risk assessment?
- **A. The Care Quality Commission (CQC) Ligature Risk Assessment Tool, with the risk record held in Datix [correct]**
- B. A locally designed ward checklist stored on the ward shared drive
- C. The SystmOne risk screen template
- D. The Health and Safety Executive workplace risk form
_Why:_ The SOP provides instructions for completing the CQC Ligature Risk Assessment Tool and recording the risk in Datix. The January 2026 update confirms risk records remain in Datix because the intended new risk management system was not delivered.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Ligature Risk Assessment Standard Operating Procedure (January 2026, issue 02, review January 2027)

**Q149. [Easy]** How often does Trust policy require a ligature risk assessment for a ward environment to be reviewed as a minimum?
- A. Every six months
- **B. At least annually, or sooner if the environment or patient population changes significantly [correct]**
- C. Every three years in line with the policy review cycle
- D. Only after a ligature incident or near miss
_Why:_ Both the policy and the SOP require ligature risk assessments to be reviewed at least annually, or sooner if there are significant changes to the environment, the service, the patient population, or following a near miss incident. The frequency may be more often depending on the current risk rating.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Ligature Risk Reduction Policy and Procedure (January 2026, issue 7, review December 2027)

**Q150. [Hard]** _Scenario: A ward assessment has identified one high rated ligature point and several low rated ones._ What does the Trust SOP say the overall Current Risk Rating field in the Datix risk record must be?
- A. An average of all the individual point ratings
- B. The rating agreed by the Estates team
- **C. No less than the highest rated point identified within the CQC tool [correct]**
- D. Always rated as medium until actions are agreed
_Why:_ The SOP states the overall current risk rating recorded in the Datix Current Risk Rating field should be no less than the highest rated point within the CQC tool, so a single high rated point makes the whole assessment high. Ratings are only re-assessed once actions are completed.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Ligature Risk Assessment Standard Operating Procedure (January 2026, issue 02, review January 2027)

**Q151. [Medium]** In the Care Quality Commission (CQC) tier scale used by the Trust, which tier is applied to a ligature point with high likelihood and severity that needs immediate attention?
- A. Tier 1
- B. Tier 2
- **C. Tier 3 [correct]**
- D. Tier 4
_Why:_ The Trust SOP maps the CQC tier scale of 1 to 3 as Tier 3 high risk needing immediate attention, Tier 2 medium risk that can be addressed with mitigations over time, and Tier 1 low risk requiring monitoring but not immediate action.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Ligature Risk Assessment Standard Operating Procedure (January 2026, issue 02, review January 2027)

**Q152. [Medium]** _Scenario: A team is working through the tier guidance linked from the Trust's ligature policy and is deciding which tier applies to each room._ Which ward areas are given as examples of Tier 3 in the Care Quality Commission (CQC) tiers and mitigating controls guidance the Trust uses?
- **A. Bedrooms, bathrooms, en-suites and toilets [correct]**
- B. Lounges, day rooms and dining rooms
- C. Activity rooms, interview rooms and clinic rooms
- D. Corridors and stairwells
_Why:_ The CQC guidance linked from the Trust policy describes Tier 3 as areas where patients may spend a lot of time alone with minimal or no supervision, giving bedrooms, bathrooms, en-suites and toilets as examples. Lounges, day rooms and dining rooms are Tier 2, and activity, interview and clinic rooms are Tier 1.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - CQC Tiers and Mitigating Controls (linked from the Ligature Risk Reduction Policy) (linked document, from Ligature Risk Reduction Policy January 2026, issue 7, review December 2027)

**Q153. [Medium]** _Scenario: During a walkthrough a team spots a low fixture that a patient could not suspend from._ What does the Trust's Ligature Risk Reduction Policy say about low-lying ligature points?
- A. They can be scored as low risk because suspension is not possible
- **B. They should not be judged as low risk and should be removed where possible [correct]**
- C. They do not need recording in the risk assessment
- D. They are only relevant in seclusion and de-escalation areas
_Why:_ The policy states explicitly that low-lying ligature points should not be judged as low risk and should be removed where possible, and that assessments must include both known and new forms of ligature point.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Ligature Risk Reduction Policy and Procedure (January 2026, issue 7, review December 2027)

**Q154. [Hard]** Under the Trust's Ligature Risk Reduction Policy, how often do the Ligature Risk Reduction Group and the Capital Projects team carry out their joint review of matters such as procurement, training and incident data?
- A. Monthly
- **B. Every three months [correct]**
- C. Every six months
- D. Annually
_Why:_ The policy states the Ligature Risk Reduction Group and Capital Projects team will review every three months against a set list including collaboration and best practice, compliance and safety, training effectiveness, patient and carer involvement, and incident review.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Ligature Risk Reduction Policy and Procedure (January 2026, issue 7, review December 2027)

**Q155. [Hard]** _Scenario: A ward is having new fittings installed and a nurse asks what makes something genuinely anti-ligature._ How does the Trust's Ligature Risk Reduction Policy define an anti-ligature fitting?
- A. Any fitting with no exposed edges or gaps
- B. Any fitting that has been Building Research Establishment (BRE) tested
- **C. A fitting designed so a ligature slips off, or the fitting breaks away from its mount at 40kg or less when placed under pressure of weight [correct]**
- D. A fitting that will not support the weight of an average adult, taken as 70kg
_Why:_ The policy defines an anti-ligature fitting as one designed to prevent a ligature being attached, which should either cause the ligature to slip off or break away from its mount at 40kg or less when placed under pressure of weight.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Ligature Risk Reduction Policy and Procedure (January 2026, issue 7, review December 2027)

**Q156. [Easy]** _Scenario: A patient who was on agreed leave has not come back to the ward at the expected time and no one knows where they are._ How does the Derbyshire joint policy distinguish an absent patient from a missing patient?
- A. Absent means gone under two hours, missing means gone over two hours
- **B. Absent means the person is not where they are expected or required to be, missing means their whereabouts cannot be established and there is concern about crime, risk of harm, or out of character circumstances [correct]**
- C. Absent applies to informal patients and missing applies to detained patients
- D. Absent means the ward knows where they are, missing means the police have been called
_Why:_ The joint policy uses two categories. Absent or AWOL means a person is not at a place where they are expected or required to be, having escaped, absconded, gone absent without leave or not returned from authorised leave. Missing means their whereabouts cannot be established with the added concerns listed. The distinction is not based on elapsed time.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Absent and Missing Mental Health Patients Joint System Policy (June 2025, review June 2026 (replaces joint policy version 9))
_⚠ review date passed_

**Q157. [Easy]** _Scenario: A healthcare support worker realises a patient is not on the ward and cannot be found in the communal areas._ Under the Derbyshire joint policy, who must the member of staff who identifies that a patient is absent inform first?
- **A. The Nurse in Charge [correct]**
- B. The police, on 101
- C. The patient's Responsible Clinician
- D. The Mental Health Act office
_Why:_ The on-site search measures begin with the member of staff who identifies that a patient is missing informing the Nurse in Charge. The Nurse in Charge then follows the organisation escalation process and requests a comprehensive search of the ward or department and the immediate environment.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Absent and Missing Mental Health Patients Joint System Policy (June 2025, review June 2026 (replaces joint policy version 9))
_⚠ review date passed_

**Q158. [Hard]** _Scenario: The initial search of the ward has not found the patient and the Nurse in Charge is widening the search._ In the on-site search sequence set out in the Derbyshire joint policy, which area is searched immediately after the ward or unit itself?
- A. Offices
- **B. Toilets [correct]**
- C. Storerooms
- D. The wider hospital and grounds
_Why:_ The policy sets out a widening sequence of ward or unit, toilets, offices, corridors, departments, storerooms, locked rooms, then wider hospital and grounds search. Toilets come second.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Absent and Missing Mental Health Patients Joint System Policy (June 2025, review June 2026 (replaces joint policy version 9))
_⚠ review date passed_

**Q159. [Medium]** In the observation levels described in the Derbyshire joint policy, what does Level 2 mean, and where must any change to a patient's observation level be documented?
- A. Within arm's length, documented in the daily nursing notes
- **B. Within eyesight, documented on the patient's Risk Management Plan [correct]**
- C. Intermittent observation, documented on the handover sheet
- D. General observation, documented on the observation sheet only
_Why:_ The policy sets out four levels: Level 1 within arm's length, Level 2 within eyesight, Level 3 intermittent observation and Level 4 general observation. It states that any change to a patient's observation level must be documented on the patient's Risk Management Plan.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Absent and Missing Mental Health Patients Joint System Policy (June 2025, review June 2026 (replaces joint policy version 9))
_⚠ review date passed_

**Q160. [Easy]** _Scenario: A patient was admitted yesterday afternoon._ Under the Derbyshire joint policy on absent and missing patients, within what time of admission must every patient have a care plan completed?
- A. Within 4 hours
- B. Within 12 hours
- **C. Within 24 hours [correct]**
- D. Within 72 hours
_Why:_ As a preventative measure the policy states that every patient should have a care plan and that this should be completed within 24 hours of admission, with an agreed level of observation forming part of that plan.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Absent and Missing Mental Health Patients Joint System Policy (June 2025, review June 2026 (replaces joint policy version 9))
_⚠ review date passed_

**Q161. [Easy]** Under the Derbyshire joint policy, who agrees a decision on escorted or unescorted leave around the unit?
- A. The Nurse in Charge of the shift alone
- **B. The Responsible Clinician and/or the Named Nurse, in line with the patient's Risk Management Plan and observation level [correct]**
- C. The ward manager at the weekly ward round
- D. The Mental Health Act office
_Why:_ The policy states a decision for escorted and/or unescorted leave around the unit must be in line with the patient's Risk Management Plan and observation level, and must be agreed by the Responsible Clinician and/or Named Nurse.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Absent and Missing Mental Health Patients Joint System Policy (June 2025, review June 2026 (replaces joint policy version 9))
_⚠ review date passed_

**Q162. [Hard]** _Scenario: An informal patient is trying to leave the ward and the nurse in charge is considering the nurse's holding power._ Under the Derbyshire joint policy, what does a Section 5(4) nurse's holding power provide, and what condition must be met?
- **A. Detention for up to 6 hours, and the patient must be on the grounds of the hospital [correct]**
- B. Detention for up to 72 hours, and the patient must be on the grounds of the hospital
- C. Detention for up to 6 hours, and it can be used anywhere in the community
- D. Detention for up to 24 hours, with the Responsible Clinician's agreement
_Why:_ The policy states a Registered Nurse of a prescribed class can issue a 5(4), a legal holding power to detain for up to 6 hours with immediate effect, and that the patient must be on the grounds of the hospital for the holding power to be enacted. A doctor's 5(2) provides up to 72 hours on the same condition.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Absent and Missing Mental Health Patients Joint System Policy (June 2025, review June 2026 (replaces joint policy version 9))
_⚠ review date passed_

**Q163. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient subject to a Deprivation of Liberty Safeguards (DoLS) authorisation has gone from the hospital premises._ What does the Derbyshire joint policy say staff should do for a patient under a Deprivation of Liberty Safeguards (DoLS) authorisation who goes missing?
- A. Follow the absent patient procedure, because DoLS is not a Mental Health Act detention
- B. Wait until the person has been gone for a set period before escalating
- **C. Follow the procedure for missing rather than absent patients, and report the level of risk to police as high [correct]**
- D. Contact the Mental Health Act office to convert the authorisation before acting
_Why:_ The policy directs staff to follow the missing rather than the absent procedure for DoLS patients, and given the level of risk and vulnerability of individuals on DoLS it recommends staff report the level of risk to the individual as high when reporting the incident to police.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Absent and Missing Mental Health Patients Joint System Policy (June 2025, review June 2026 (replaces joint policy version 9))
_⚠ review date passed_

**Q164. [Hard]** The Derbyshire joint policy names the system on which the police record all missing persons. What is it called?
- **A. Compact [correct]**
- B. Datix
- C. THRIVE
- D. Niche
_Why:_ The policy states that the police record all missing persons on a system known as Compact. THRIVE is named separately in the policy as the police framework used to inform their assessment, and Datix is the Trust's own incident reporting system.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Absent and Missing Mental Health Patients Joint System Policy (June 2025, review June 2026 (replaces joint policy version 9))
_⚠ review date passed_

**Q165. [Easy]** _Scenario: A patient reported missing to the police walks back onto the ward at 3am._ What does the Derbyshire joint policy require when a patient who has been reported missing returns to the unit?
- A. Wait until the morning so the day team can update the police in office hours
- **B. Notify the police without delay [correct]**
- C. Notify the police only if the patient is detained under the Mental Health Act
- D. Let the family contact the police, as they made the original report
_Why:_ The policy states that when a missing person returns to the unit the police must be notified without delay, because police resources directed to a missing person enquiry can be extensive and could otherwise continue to be used searching for someone who is already back.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Absent and Missing Mental Health Patients Joint System Policy (June 2025, review June 2026 (replaces joint policy version 9))
_⚠ review date passed_

**Q166. [Medium]** _Scenario: A detained patient has returned to the ward after being absent without leave._ What role does the Trust's Mental Health Act office have in the absent and missing process?
- A. It authorises the search of the hospital grounds
- B. It takes over the police liaison from the Nurse in Charge
- **C. It advises staff on the procedures to follow when an absent detained patient returns, and notifies the Care Quality Commission (CQC) of incidents of absconding detained patients [correct]**
- D. It decides whether the incident is reportable as a serious incident
_Why:_ The policy sets out the Mental Health Act office role as advising all staff on the procedures to be followed when an absent detained patient returns to hospital, and notifying the Care Quality Commission of incidents of absconding detained patients.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Absent and Missing Mental Health Patients Joint System Policy (June 2025, review June 2026 (replaces joint policy version 9))
_⚠ review date passed_

**Q167. [Medium]** _Scenario: An absent detained patient has been located at a friend's address but is refusing to return and will not let staff in._ Under the Derbyshire Right Care Right Person multi-agency agreement, who makes the application for a Section 135(2) warrant when a service user is AWOL?
- A. The Approved Mental Health Professional (AMHP)
- **B. Derbyshire Healthcare NHS Foundation Trust staff [correct]**
- C. The police officer attending
- D. The Responsible Clinician, via the Mental Health Act office
_Why:_ The agreement states that Trust staff make the application for the Section 135(2) warrant if the service user is AWOL, whereas Approved Mental Health Professionals make the application for a Section 135(1) warrant. Police hold the primary responsibility for executing the 135(2) warrant with the clinical team present.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Right Care Right Person Derby and Derbyshire Multi-Agency Agreement (version 16, Final, approved by DHcFT Quality and Safeguarding Committee 10 December 2024, review date TBC)

**Q168. [Easy]** _Scenario: A nurse needs to record on SystmOne that a patient has gone AWOL._ Following the Trust's AWOL Standard Operating Procedure (SOP) on SystmOne, what happens in ward view once a patient has been marked as AWOL?
- A. The patient is removed from the ward list until they return
- **B. The bed turns red [correct]**
- C. An automatic alert is sent to the Mental Health Act office
- D. The bed is released to the bed management team
_Why:_ The SOP describes right clicking the patient in ward view, choosing Clinical then Mark as AWOL, completing the started date and time, indicating whether the bed can be freed, and adding notes. Once saved, the bed turns red in ward view. It reverts to its normal colour when the AWOL leave record is ended.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - AWOL Standard Operating Procedure (SystmOne) (issue 01, no issue or review date recorded on the document)

## Trust Policy - Searching & Ward Security

**Q169. [Medium]** _Scenario: A newly recruited healthcare assistant asks whether they are allowed to help search a patient._ Under the trust's searching policy, which staff may lawfully undertake searches of patients and visitors in inpatient settings?
- A. Any registered nurse, regardless of what training they have completed
- **B. NHS staff who have completed the trust approved Positive and Safe (P&S) training [correct]**
- C. Only staff who hold a Local Security Management Specialist (LSMS) qualification
- D. Any member of staff on shift, provided the nurse in charge agrees
_Why:_ The policy states that within inpatient settings, NHS staff who have completed the trust approved Positive and Safe training can undertake lawful searches of both patients and visitors, provided the action is proportionate and justifiable to the assessed risk.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Searching of Service Users: Room, Possessions, Lockers, Personal Property and Ward Area Policy and Procedure (July 2025, issue 7, review July 2028)

**Q170. [Easy]** What is the minimum staffing requirement for a non-intimate person search under the trust's searching policy?
- A. One member of staff of the same gender as the patient
- B. Two members of staff, both of whom must be registered nurses
- **C. A minimum of two clinical staff, one of whom must be a registered nurse and one of the same gender as the patient [correct]**
- D. Three members of staff, including the nurse in charge of the ward
_Why:_ The policy requires a minimum of two clinical staff for a non-intimate person search, one of whom must be a registered nurse and one of the same gender as the patient.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Searching of Service Users: Room, Possessions, Lockers, Personal Property and Ward Area Policy and Procedure (July 2025, issue 7, review July 2028)

**Q171. [Medium]** _Scenario: Staff strongly suspect a patient has concealed items under their clothing and feel that only a full removal of clothing will find them._ What does the trust's searching policy say about strip searches?
- A. They may be carried out by two trust staff of the same gender as the patient
- **B. Under no circumstances should a strip search be undertaken solely by trust staff - the police MUST be contacted so they may decide how to proceed [correct]**
- C. They may be authorised by the nurse in charge if the risk is judged immediate
- D. They may be carried out by trust staff once the Responsible Clinician has given written consent
_Why:_ The policy is explicit that under no circumstances should a strip search be undertaken solely by trust staff; where one is felt appropriate the police must be contacted, and any dispute is escalated to the clinical matron or on-call manager.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Searching of Service Users: Room, Possessions, Lockers, Personal Property and Ward Area Policy and Procedure (July 2025, issue 7, review July 2028)

**Q172. [Hard]** _Scenario: A detained patient refuses consent to a personal search that staff believe is necessary._ According to the trust's searching policy, what is the first step when a detained patient refuses consent or lacks capacity to consent to a search?
- A. Proceed immediately with a search using physical intervention
- B. Contact the police to conduct the search instead
- **C. Contact their Responsible Clinician (or failing that another senior clinician who knows the case) without delay, so any clinical objection to searching by force may be raised [correct]**
- D. Wait for the next Multidisciplinary Team (MDT) meeting to make the decision
_Why:_ The policy says the Responsible Clinician, or failing that another senior clinician with knowledge of the case, should be contacted without delay so any clinical objection can be raised, while the patient is kept separated and under close observation and told what is happening and why.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Searching of Service Users: Room, Possessions, Lockers, Personal Property and Ward Area Policy and Procedure (July 2025, issue 7, review July 2028)

**Q173. [Hard]** _Scenario: An informal patient who has been assessed as having capacity refuses to be searched or to hand over items, and staff still have concerns._ What does the trust's searching policy say should happen next?
- **A. The consultant in charge of the patient's care is contacted and a Multidisciplinary Team (MDT) meeting is convened to discuss the possibility of discharge and removal from the ward environment [correct]**
- B. The search goes ahead anyway because informal patients have no right to refuse
- C. The patient is automatically placed under Section 5(2) of the Mental Health Act
- D. The nurse in charge alone decides whether to search without consent
_Why:_ For an informal patient with capacity who refuses, the policy directs staff to contact the consultant in charge and convene an MDT meeting to discuss possible discharge and removal from the ward, with appropriate use of the Mental Health Act considered where applicable.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Searching of Service Users: Room, Possessions, Lockers, Personal Property and Ward Area Policy and Procedure (July 2025, issue 7, review July 2028)

**Q174. [Medium]** The trust's searching policy warns staff about a specific limitation of the handheld metal detectors used during searches. What is it?
- A. They cannot be used on patients who have metal implants
- **B. They will not pick up hypodermic needles [correct]**
- C. They must not be used on detained patients
- D. They only work when the patient is standing still
_Why:_ The policy specifically warns that the metal detector will not pick up hypodermic needles, and adds that detectors must only be used in addition to a physical search because they find metallic objects only, not non-metallic weapons or drugs.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Searching of Service Users: Room, Possessions, Lockers, Personal Property and Ward Area Policy and Procedure (July 2025, issue 7, review July 2028)

**Q175. [Hard]** _Scenario: You are the nurse accountable for a search that has just been completed on an early shift._ By when must the documentation actions for that search be completed under the trust's searching policy?
- A. Within 24 hours of the search
