- **C. The Care Co-ordinator, Community Psychiatric Nurse (CPN) or social worker [correct]**
- D. The named nurse
_Why:_ The policy splits the reports as follows: the Mental Health Act team produce Part A, the factual statement; the Responsible Clinician produces the clinical report; the Care Co-ordinator, CPN or social worker produces the social circumstances report; and the named nurse produces the nursing report for inpatients.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Mental Health Tribunal Policy and Procedures (September 2025, issue 04, review September 2028)

**Q260. [Hard]** _Scenario: A face to face tribunal is being held on the ward tomorrow._ As well as submitting them to the tribunal, what does trust policy say should be available on the day?
- A. 2 copies of the reports
- B. 3 copies of the reports
- **C. 4 copies of the reports [correct]**
- D. One copy for the judge only
_Why:_ The policy says that for face to face hearings, 4 copies of the reports should be available on the day in addition to submitting them to the tribunal. For video hearings the tribunal office distributes them to the panel members.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Mental Health Tribunal Policy and Procedures (September 2025, issue 04, review September 2028)

**Q261. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient with a tribunal listed says they would rather attend in person than by video._ Under trust policy, whose job is it to pass that preference on, and by when for a case other than section 2?
- A. The solicitor, at any point before the hearing
- **B. The ward where the patient is residing, telling the Mental Health Act team, with the tribunal wanting to know 14 days before the hearing [correct]**
- C. The Responsible Clinician, on the HQ1 form
- D. The Mental Health Act Administrator, 72 hours before the hearing
_Why:_ The policy makes it the responsibility of the ward where the patient is residing, or the clinical team if the patient is in the community, to tell the Mental Health Act team the patient's preference. For cases other than section 2 the tribunal wants to know 14 days before the hearing; for section 2 it is 72 hours. Where a patient has no preference the default is video.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Mental Health Tribunal Policy and Procedures (September 2025, issue 04, review September 2028)

**Q262. [Easy]** _Scenario: A student on placement asks you who will be sitting on the tribunal panel._ According to trust policy, who makes up the three-person Mental Health Tribunal panel?
- **A. A judge, a tribunal doctor independent of the Trust, and a lay person with specialist knowledge of mental health [correct]**
- B. A judge, the patient's Responsible Clinician, and an Independent Mental Health Advocate
- C. Three Associate Hospital Managers
- D. A judge, a solicitor and a social worker
_Why:_ The policy describes the panel as a judge who chairs the hearing, a tribunal doctor who is independent from the Trust, and a lay person with specialist knowledge of mental health. It also notes that the burden is on those arguing for continued detention to prove their case, not on the patient to disprove it.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Mental Health Tribunal Policy and Procedures (September 2025, issue 04, review September 2028)

**Q263. [Medium]** _Scenario: The tribunal has finished and the patient wants to know the outcome in writing._ What does trust policy say happens after the hearing?
- A. A written decision is handed out on the day and the nurse in charge explains it
- **B. A brief verbal decision is given on the day, with the written decision following about 7 days later [correct]**
- C. The decision is issued in writing within 24 hours by the Mental Health Act Office
- D. The decision is given in writing only, about 28 days later
_Why:_ The policy says a brief verbal decision should be given on the day, followed in writing approximately 7 days after the tribunal. The Mental Health Act Administrator uploads the decision onto the electronic patient record, and the solicitor is responsible for explaining the decision and the right to appeal to the patient.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Mental Health Tribunal Policy and Procedures (September 2025, issue 04, review September 2028)

**Q264. [Easy]** _Scenario: A detained patient has left the ward without leave and is believed to be at a relative's house, where staff have been refused entry._ Under the joint section 135 policy, who should apply for the section 135(2) warrant?
- A. The police, on the ward's behalf
- **B. The patient's Ward Manager or a nominated deputy, the Responsible Clinician, or the Care Co-ordinator [correct]**
- C. The Mental Health Act Office
- D. Any registered nurse on duty
_Why:_ Trust policy requires that wherever practicable the application is made by the patient's Ward Manager (or a nominated deputy), the Responsible Clinician or the Care Co-ordinator, and that the applicant knows the patient's circumstances well enough to answer the magistrate's questions. The policy also notes that Derbyshire Constabulary will not apply for section 135 warrants.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Joint Policy for the Operation of Section 135 Mental Health Act 1983 (September 2023, issue 03, review September 2026)

**Q265. [Hard]** Under the joint section 135 policy, how long is a section 135(2) warrant valid for?
- A. 7 days from issue
- B. 14 days from issue
- **C. 28 days, including the date it was signed and issued by the magistrate [correct]**
- D. Until the patient is returned, with no fixed limit
_Why:_ The policy states the warrant is valid for 28 days including the date on which it was signed and issued by the magistrate, but that it should still be executed as a matter of urgency. It may only be served once, at the address specified on it.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Joint Policy for the Operation of Section 135 Mental Health Act 1983 (September 2023, issue 03, review September 2026)

**Q266. [Medium]** _Scenario: The multidisciplinary team has agreed a section 135(2) warrant is needed and you have been nominated to co-ordinate it._ Under the joint section 135 policy, how is the hearing slot arranged?
- A. By telephoning the local magistrates' court on the day
- **B. By booking an appointment using the web-based Team Up calendar, and a fee is payable [correct]**
- C. By emailing the Mental Health Act Office, who book it for you
- D. By attending the court in person to request a slot
_Why:_ The policy directs applicants to book an appointment using the web-based Team Up calendar and notes that a fee is payable. Before applying, the co-ordinator must liaise with the police to request their assistance in executing the warrant, and paperwork goes to the court's secure email address.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Joint Policy for the Operation of Section 135 Mental Health Act 1983 (September 2023, issue 03, review September 2026)

**Q267. [Medium]** _Scenario: A section 135(2) warrant has just been executed and the patient has been returned to the ward._ Under the joint section 135 policy, where must copies of the warrant end up?
- A. With the police only
- **B. With the occupier of the premises (or left at the address if empty), with the police, and in the electronic patient record [correct]**
- C. In the paper ward file and with the Mental Health Act Office
- D. With the magistrates' court and the Responsible Clinician
_Why:_ The policy requires staff accompanying the police to ensure a copy is given to the occupier of the premises, which may be the patient, or left at the address if empty; a copy is retained by the police; and a copy is retained in the electronic patient record.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Joint Policy for the Operation of Section 135 Mental Health Act 1983 (September 2023, issue 03, review September 2026)

**Q268. [Medium]** _Scenario: The patient returns to the ward voluntarily before the section 135(2) warrant is used, so it is never executed._ What does the joint section 135 policy require you to do with the unused warrant?
- A. Shred it once the patient is back on the ward
- B. File it in the ward's Mental Health Act folder in case it is needed again
- **C. Return it to the issuing Court by secure email with an explanatory covering letter, and scan a copy of both onto the electronic patient record [correct]**
- D. Send it to the Mental Health Act Office, who will return it to the court
_Why:_ The policy says that if the decision is taken not to execute the warrant, or it cannot be executed while valid, the applicant must return it to the issuing Court with an explanatory covering letter using their secure email address, and a copy of the un-executed warrant and covering letter must be scanned onto the patient's electronic patient record.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Joint Policy for the Operation of Section 135 Mental Health Act 1983 (September 2023, issue 03, review September 2026)

**Q269. [Hard]** _Scenario: Out of hours, the consultant on call decides a detained patient needs urgent treatment under section 62 and gives the instruction over the phone._ Under the trust urgent treatment policy, what must nursing staff do?
- A. Wait until the section 62 form is signed in person before giving the treatment
- **B. Record the instruction clearly on the MHA Section 62 Verbal Instruction form and in the electronic patient record, then administer the treatment [correct]**
- C. Record it in the nursing notes only and ask the Responsible Clinician to write it up at the next ward round
- D. Ask the on-call manager to authorise it in writing first
_Why:_ The policy says that where the decision is made out of hours by the consultant on call by verbal instruction, nursing staff should clearly document this on the verbal instruction form and in the electronic patient record, and treatment should then be administered. Once signed by the Responsible Clinician or Approved Clinician the form goes to the Mental Health Act team, and the section 62 form can be completed the next working day.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Mental Health Act 1983 Urgent Treatment Policy and Procedures (December 2025, issue 04, review December 2028)

**Q270. [Medium]** Under the trust urgent treatment policy, who must be told about every single use of the urgent treatment provisions, and how?
- A. The ward manager, by handover
- B. The Care Quality Commission, by incident report
- **C. The Mental Health Act Administration team, by completion of the urgent treatment form [correct]**
- D. The Medical Director, by email
_Why:_ Hospital Managers must monitor use of urgent treatment so it is not used inappropriately or excessively, and the policy delegates this to the Mental Health Act Administration team, who must be informed of every use by completion of the urgent treatment form. The team then uploads the completed form to the electronic patient record.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Mental Health Act 1983 Urgent Treatment Policy and Procedures (December 2025, issue 04, review December 2028)

**Q271. [Easy]** _Scenario: You are asked to give a treatment under section 62 but the paperwork is not in place._ What does the trust urgent treatment policy expect of clinical staff?
- A. Give the treatment and sort out the paperwork afterwards
- **B. Be satisfied the relevant documentation is in place before administering, seek advice from your line manager if in doubt, and Datix report any breaches [correct]**
- C. Refuse and wait for the Second Opinion Appointed Doctor (SOAD)
- D. Ask the patient to consent in writing instead
_Why:_ The policy says that before administering any treatment prescribed under section 62 or section 64, clinical staff must be satisfied the relevant documentation is in place and should seek advice from their line manager if there is any doubt. Any breaches should be Datix reported in line with Trust policy.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Mental Health Act 1983 Urgent Treatment Policy and Procedures (December 2025, issue 04, review December 2028)

**Q272. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient already detained on your ward needs to move to another hospital._ Under the trust conveyance protocol, who is responsible for arranging the transport?
- A. The Approved Mental Health Professional (AMHP)
- **B. The nurse in charge of the ward [correct]**
- C. The Responsible Clinician
- D. The bed flow co-ordinator
_Why:_ The protocol splits this clearly: where the transfer involves a service receiver who is already detained, the nurse in charge of the ward arranges transport. Where the person is being newly detained, for example moving from a medical ward to a psychiatric ward, the AMHP is responsible for arranging conveyance.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Protocol and Policy for Conveyance of Service Receivers subject to the Mental Health Act (1983) (interim review, all-agency review date December 2026)

**Q273. [Easy]** _Scenario: A patient who has been sedated needs to be conveyed to hospital._ What does the trust conveyance protocol require?
- **A. Transport in an ambulance, not a police vehicle, accompanied by a nurse or doctor experienced in managing such patients [correct]**
- B. Transport in a police vehicle with an ambulance following behind
- C. Transport by taxi with two staff escorts
- D. Transport in whichever vehicle arrives first, with a police escort
_Why:_ The protocol is explicit that a sedated person must be transported in an ambulance and not a police vehicle, and must be accompanied by a nurse or doctor experienced in the management of such patients. Responsibility for arranging this rests with the assessing doctor, and police may support the conveyance if needed.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Protocol and Policy for Conveyance of Service Receivers subject to the Mental Health Act (1983) (interim review, all-agency review date December 2026)

**Q274. [Hard]** _Scenario: A detained patient has not returned from section 17 leave and an ambulance is needed to bring them back._ Under the trust conveyance protocol, what must the Responsible Clinician (RC) or delegated staff arrange?
- A. A verbal handover to the ambulance crew on arrival
- **B. Written authority to convey, scanned and emailed to ambulance control, along with the name of the authorising RC [correct]**
- C. A copy of the section 17 leave form faxed to ambulance control
- D. Nothing extra, as the detention papers are sufficient authority
_Why:_ The protocol requires the RC or delegated staff to arrange for ambulance control to be sent written authority to convey, scanned and emailed, and to be given the name of the authorising RC. An emergency ambulance should only be used where there is a clear clinical need, otherwise it falls under patient transport service protocols.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Protocol and Policy for Conveyance of Service Receivers subject to the Mental Health Act (1983) (interim review, all-agency review date December 2026)

## Trust Policy - Capacity, DoLS & Consent

**Q275. [Easy]** _Scenario: You need to record a full capacity assessment for a newly admitted patient._ In SystmOne, which route does the trust Standard Operating Procedure (SOP) tell you to take to start a full Mental Capacity Act (MCA) capacity assessment?
- **A. Select the patient, click Launch Pad, then click the MCA Capacity/Competency tab [correct]**
- B. Select the patient, click Alerts at the bottom of the page, then Patient Alert Indicators
- C. Open the care plan and add a Capacity section as free text
- D. Open the patient's Risk screen and select Capacity from the domain list
_Why:_ The Capacity and Competency SOP sets out the process as: select patient, click Launch Pad, click the MCA Capacity/Competency tab, then Capacity to consent (MCA). The Alerts route is the one used for advance statements and Power of Attorney, not capacity assessments.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Standard Operating Procedure: Capacity and Competency (Issue 1, trust wide, no issue or review date printed on the document)

**Q276. [Hard]** A patient's presentation and care plan are both unchanged, but time has passed since their last recorded formal capacity assessment. Under the trust Standard Operating Procedure (SOP), after how long should a further full capacity assessment always be completed?
- A. 3 months
- B. 6 months
- **C. 12 months [correct]**
- D. 2 years
_Why:_ The Capacity and Competency SOP lists three triggers for a further full assessment: a significant change in presentation, a change in the care plan with a new and significant intervention, or 12 months since a formal capacity assessment was recorded.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Standard Operating Procedure: Capacity and Competency (Issue 1, trust wide, no issue or review date printed on the document)

**Q277. [Medium]** _Scenario: After seeing a patient you make a case entry and notice the Capacity/Competency drop-down box._ Under the trust Standard Operating Procedure (SOP), when may you record capacity using that drop-down box rather than completing a full assessment?
- A. Any time the decision is a day to day one
- **B. Only if a full capacity assessment has already been completed and it is allied to the current care plan [correct]**
- C. Only if a doctor has countersigned the entry
- D. Only during the first 72 hours of admission
_Why:_ The SOP is explicit that the drop-down facility can only be used if a full capacity assessment has already been completed which is allied to the current care plan.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Standard Operating Procedure: Capacity and Competency (Issue 1, trust wide, no issue or review date printed on the document)

**Q278. [Easy]** _Scenario: A capacity assessment on SystmOne concludes that the patient lacks capacity for the decision in question._ Which SystmOne tab does the trust Standard Operating Procedure (SOP) say you complete next?
- A. The Consent to Share tab
- B. The Mental Health Act (MHA) Legal Status tab
- **C. The DHCFT Best Interest Decision tab [correct]**
- D. The Safeguarding Referral tab
_Why:_ The SOP states that if the patient lacks capacity you click the DHCFT Best Interest Decision tab and complete the Best Interest questionnaire, saving either for future editing or as a final version.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Standard Operating Procedure: Capacity and Competency (Issue 1, trust wide, no issue or review date printed on the document)

**Q279. [Easy]** _Scenario: A patient has told you they have made an advance statement and named a relative with Power of Attorney._ Under the trust Standard Operating Procedure (SOP) for Advanced Statements, Power of Attorney (POA) and Do Not Attempt Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation (DNACPR), how is this flagged on SystmOne?
- A. It is typed into the daily progress note only
- B. It is added to the handover sheet by the nurse in charge
- C. It is emailed to the Mental Health Act (MHA) office to add centrally
- **D. It is entered via Alerts from the ward view, and a red exclamation mark then appears on the patient details box [correct]**
_Why:_ The SOP process is: from ward view select the patient, click Alerts, complete the Patient Alert Indicators box and save. The alert then shows on the bed view and a red exclamation mark appears on the patient details box, which you hover over to see the recorded alerts.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Standard Operating Procedure: Advanced Statements, POA/DNACPR (Issue 1, trust wide, no issue or review date printed on the document)

**Q280. [Easy]** Under the trust Deprivation of Liberty Safeguards (DoLS) policy, an urgent authorisation that the Trust grants itself makes the deprivation of liberty lawful for a period not exceeding how long?
- A. 24 hours
- B. 72 hours
- **C. 7 days [correct]**
- D. 14 days
_Why:_ The DoLS policy states the Managing Authority may grant itself an urgent authorisation lasting no more than 7 days, and that a request for standard authorisation must be made at the same time.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Deprivation of Liberty (DoLS) Policy and Procedures (April 2023, issue 3, review Sept 2026 (six month extension approved))

**Q281. [Hard]** _Scenario: An urgent Deprivation of Liberty Safeguards (DoLS) authorisation is running out and the standard authorisation has not yet been dealt with._ What does the trust DoLS policy say about extending it?
- **A. The Managing Authority may ask the Supervisory Body to extend it by a maximum of a further 7 days, and the request must be made before the current authorisation expires [correct]**
- B. It can be extended once by 28 days by the ward manager
- C. It rolls over automatically until the Supervisory Body responds
- D. It can be extended for a further 14 days by the responsible clinician
_Why:_ The policy allows a request to the Supervisory Body to extend an urgent authorisation for a maximum of a further 7 days, in exceptional circumstances, and states the request must come before the expiry of the present urgent authorisation.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Deprivation of Liberty (DoLS) Policy and Procedures (April 2023, issue 3, review Sept 2026 (six month extension approved))

**Q282. [Hard]** In the standard Deprivation of Liberty Safeguards (DoLS) form set listed in the trust policy, which form is used by the Managing Authority to request a standard or an urgent authorisation, and to extend an urgent authorisation?
- A. Form 2
- **B. Form 1 [correct]**
- C. Form 5
- D. Form 10
_Why:_ The policy lists Form 1 for requesting a standard or urgent authorisation, including where the Managing Authority gives itself an urgent authorisation, and notes it can also be used to extend the urgent authorisation. Form 2 is a further standard authorisation, Form 5 is the Supervisory Body granting authorisation, and Form 10 covers suspension or review.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Deprivation of Liberty (DoLS) Policy and Procedures (April 2023, issue 3, review Sept 2026 (six month extension approved))

**Q283. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient on a Deprivation of Liberty Safeguards (DoLS) authorisation is being transferred to another trust unit._ What does the trust DoLS policy say happens to the existing authorisation?
- A. It transfers with the patient for the remainder of its period
- B. It stays valid for 7 days after the move to allow a new application
- C. It stays valid provided the Supervisory Body is the same local authority
- **D. It ceases to be valid, and the accepting hospital or care home must apply for its own authorisation [correct]**
_Why:_ The policy states that on transfer to another hospital, unit or care home the authorisation ceases to be valid, and those coordinating the transfer must advise the accepting service that it needs to apply to continue depriving the person of their liberty.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Deprivation of Liberty (DoLS) Policy and Procedures (April 2023, issue 3, review Sept 2026 (six month extension approved))

**Q284. [Medium]** Under the trust Deprivation of Liberty Safeguards (DoLS) policy, whose listed responsibility is it to notify the Care Quality Commission (CQC) of new DoLS authorisations?
- A. The Supervisory Body (the local authority)
- B. The Mental Health Act (MHA) office
- C. The responsible clinician
- **D. The ward staff [correct]**
_Why:_ Notifying the CQC of new authorisations is listed in the policy under Ward Staff responsibilities, alongside uploading completed forms to the electronic patient record, notifying the Mental Health Act office, and keeping a record of DoLS patients and expiry dates.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Deprivation of Liberty (DoLS) Policy and Procedures (April 2023, issue 3, review Sept 2026 (six month extension approved))

**Q285. [Hard]** _Scenario: The ward has contacted the local authority for a Deprivation of Liberty Safeguards (DoLS) assessment and days are passing with no assessor allocated._ Which role does the trust DoLS policy say records the request and chases the local authority?
- **A. The Mental Capacity Act (MCA) / Mental Health Act (MHA) Team Leader [correct]**
- B. The ward manager
- C. The on call manager
- D. The Assistant Director of Safeguarding Adults
_Why:_ The policy says the MCA/MHA team leader is alerted when ward staff contact the local authority, records the request, and if there are delays in a DoLS assessor coming to the ward will contact the local authority on a regular basis to ensure the assessment is completed.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Deprivation of Liberty (DoLS) Policy and Procedures (April 2023, issue 3, review Sept 2026 (six month extension approved))

**Q286. [Easy]** In the trust Deprivation of Liberty Safeguards (DoLS) policy, who is the Managing Authority and who is the Supervisory Body?
- A. The Managing Authority is the Care Quality Commission (CQC) and the Supervisory Body is the Trust
- B. The Managing Authority is the responsible clinician and the Supervisory Body is the Mental Health Act (MHA) office
- C. The Managing Authority is the local authority and the Supervisory Body is the Court of Protection
- **D. The Managing Authority is the Trust, via the Ward Manager, and the Supervisory Body is the relevant local authority [correct]**
_Why:_ The definitions section of the policy states that for the purposes of the policy the Managing Authority means the Trust, via the Ward Manager, and the Supervisory Body is the relevant local authority for the area (Derby City Council or Derbyshire County Council).
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Deprivation of Liberty (DoLS) Policy and Procedures (April 2023, issue 3, review Sept 2026 (six month extension approved))

**Q287. [Easy]** According to the trust Consent to Examination and Treatment policy, which is the only treatment given in this Trust where the consent forms referred to in that policy must be used?
- A. Rapid Tranquillisation (RT)
- B. Depot antipsychotic medication
- C. Clozapine initiation
- **D. Electroconvulsive Therapy (ECT) [correct]**
_Why:_ The policy states that ECT is the only treatment within the Trust where its consent forms must be used, while reminding staff that separate statutory Mental Health Act forms such as T2, T3 and CTO11 apply where relevant.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Consent to Examination and Treatment Policy and Procedure (June 2025, issue 5, review April 2028)

**Q288. [Hard]** Appendix A of the trust Consent to Examination and Treatment policy lists the consent forms in use. Which numbered form is recorded as no longer in use in this organisation?
- A. Form 1
- B. Form 2
- **C. Form 3 [correct]**
- D. Form 4
_Why:_ The policy lists three versions in use, Electroconvulsive Therapy (ECT) consent forms 1, 2 and 4, and states Form 3 is no longer in use in this organisation. All are held with the Approved Forms on the trust intranet.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Consent to Examination and Treatment Policy and Procedure (June 2025, issue 5, review April 2028)

**Q289. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient whose first language is not English needs a detailed treatment discussion and you want a face to face interpreter._ What does the trust Consent to Examination and Treatment policy say about arranging this?
- **A. Onsite interpreters are available where 24 hours notice of need is given, and telephone translation is available in the meantime [correct]**
- B. Onsite interpreters must be booked at least one week ahead
- C. A family member should interpret wherever one is available
- D. Interpreters can only be booked by the Mental Health Act (MHA) office
_Why:_ The policy states the Trust has full access to telephone translation services and onsite interpreters where 24 hours notice of need is required. It also states that using a relative or friend as interpreter is not good practice and should only happen in exceptional circumstances.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Consent to Examination and Treatment Policy and Procedure (June 2025, issue 5, review April 2028)

**Q290. [Hard]** _Scenario: It is a Sunday night and a complex consent and capacity dispute on the ward needs legal advice before morning._ Under the arrangements set out in the trust Consent to Examination and Treatment policy, how is out of hours legal advice obtained?
- A. Any registered nurse may instruct the Trust's solicitors directly
- **B. Through the second on call Executive Director, who can access external legal providers [correct]**
- C. By emailing the Legal Services Manager and waiting for the next working day
- D. Through the Care Quality Commission (CQC) out of hours line
_Why:_ Appendix C of the policy, taken from the Accessing Legal Advice Policy, states the Trust has access to out of hours legal advice via external legal providers and that advice from those firms can be sought via the second on call Executive Director. In hours, staff go via their line manager to the Legal Services Manager and should not instruct solicitors directly.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Consent to Examination and Treatment Policy and Procedure (June 2025, issue 5, review April 2028)

**Q291. [Medium]** _Scenario: An adult lacks capacity to consent to admission and treatment, and is clearly objecting to both._ According to the trust Consent to Examination and Treatment policy, which legal framework is available?
- A. A Deprivation of Liberty Safeguards (DoLS) authorisation
- B. A Court of Protection order
- **C. Formal admission under the Mental Health Act 1983 only [correct]**
- D. A best interests decision under the Mental Capacity Act (MCA) alone
_Why:_ The policy states that where an adult lacks capacity and objects to admission and treatment, the only legal framework available is formal admission under the Mental Health Act 1983, and that for the avoidance of doubt neither a Court of Protection order nor DoLS are available. Where the patient lacks capacity and does not object, all three may be available.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Consent to Examination and Treatment Policy and Procedure (June 2025, issue 5, review April 2028)

**Q292. [Hard]** Under the monitoring section of the trust Consent to Examination and Treatment policy, who coordinates the audit of the consent process and where do the results go?
- A. The ward manager, reporting to the Mental Health Act (MHA) Committee
- B. The Care Quality Commission (CQC), with results presented to the Trust
- C. The Legal Services Manager, with results presented to the Board
- **D. The Audit Manager, with results presented to the Quality Governance Committee [correct]**
_Why:_ The policy states the consent audit is coordinated by the Audit Manager and the results are presented to the Quality Governance Committee, with incidents, complaints and claims reported through the Risk Management Committee and learning shared in the Trust Lessons Learned newsletter.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Consent to Examination and Treatment Policy and Procedure (June 2025, issue 5, review April 2028)

**Q293. [Easy]** _Scenario: A colleague asks you to obtain consent for a procedure they are about to carry out._ Under the trust Consent to Examination and Treatment policy, who is ultimately responsible for ensuring the patient is genuinely consenting?
- **A. The health professional carrying out the procedure [correct]**
- B. The nurse in charge of the shift
- C. The person who signs the consent form as witness
- D. The responsible clinician for the ward
_Why:_ The policy states the health professional carrying out the procedure is ultimately responsible for ensuring the patient is genuinely consenting, and that it is they who will be held responsible in law if this is challenged later. It also says professionals must be confident that any colleague they ask to seek consent is competent to do so.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Consent to Examination and Treatment Policy and Procedure (June 2025, issue 5, review April 2028)

**Q294. [Easy]** _Scenario: A relative arrives on the ward saying they hold Lasting Power of Attorney (LPA) for the patient and want to make decisions about care._ What does the trust Advance Decisions, Advance Statements and Lasting Power of Attorney policy require staff to do?
- A. Accept the relative's word and record it in the notes
- B. Refer them straight to the Court of Protection
- **C. View a copy of the document and establish whether it is registered with the Office of the Public Guardian, because an unregistered LPA is ineffective [correct]**
- D. Ask the responsible clinician to decide whether to involve them
_Why:_ The policy says patients should routinely be asked if they have an LPA, that it must be recorded on the health record and trust electronic record with details of its nature, and that staff should view a copy and know whether it is registered with the Office of the Public Guardian. An unregistered LPA is ineffective, and only a personal welfare attorney can be a decision maker on welfare matters.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Advance Decisions to Refuse Medical Treatment, Advance Statements and Lasting Power of Attorney Policy (December 2025, issue 5, review December 2028)

**Q295. [Hard]** _Scenario: There is genuine doubt about whether a claimed Lasting Power of Attorney (LPA) actually exists._ Which form does the trust policy say is used to request that information from the Office of the Public Guardian (OPG)?
- **A. Form OPG 100 [correct]**
- B. Form LPA 114
- C. Form COP 1
- D. Form T3
_Why:_ The policy states that if there are doubts regarding the existence of an LPA, form OPG 100 is used to request this information from the Office of the Public Guardian.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Advance Decisions to Refuse Medical Treatment, Advance Statements and Lasting Power of Attorney Policy (December 2025, issue 5, review December 2028)

**Q296. [Medium]** _Scenario: A member of staff says they cannot in conscience follow a patient's valid advance decision refusing life sustaining treatment._ What does the trust policy say should happen?
- A. They must follow it regardless of their beliefs
- **B. They should raise it with their manager, and the patient's care must be transferred quickly to a professional who has no objection [correct]**
- C. The advance decision should be referred to the Court of Protection to be overturned
- D. The responsible clinician should decide whether the advance decision still stands
_Why:_ The policy says a member of staff who disagrees in principle must bring this to their manager's attention. They do not have to act against their beliefs, but the patient's care must not be affected and so must be quickly transferred to another professional who has no objection to following the advance decision.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Advance Decisions to Refuse Medical Treatment, Advance Statements and Lasting Power of Attorney Policy (December 2025, issue 5, review December 2028)

**Q297. [Hard]** The trust policy on advance decisions points to the trust systems that alert staff that an advance decision or advance statement exists. Which standard of the Minimum Standards for Health Records deals with alerts?
- A. Standard 2
- B. Standard 5
- **C. Standard 8 [correct]**
- D. Standard 11
_Why:_ The policy states that Standard 8 of the Minimum Standards for Health Records deals with alerts, requiring an Administrative Alert in the relevant record, and that for electronic records a Clinical Alert can be created showing an advance decision or advance statement has been made.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Advance Decisions to Refuse Medical Treatment, Advance Statements and Lasting Power of Attorney Policy (December 2025, issue 5, review December 2028)

**Q298. [Hard]** The trust policy describes two categories of person who can give a Lasting Power of Attorney (LPA) certificate. What defines a Category A certificate provider?
- A. A relative of the donor who lives at the same address
- **B. A person chosen by the donor who has known them for at least 2 years [correct]**
- C. Any registered health professional involved in the donor's care
- D. A person appointed by the Court of Protection
_Why:_ The policy sets out that Category A is a person chosen by the donor who has known the person for at least 2 years, while Category B is someone with relevant professional skills. Family members of the donor, the attorney, and care home staff are disqualified from giving a certificate.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Advance Decisions to Refuse Medical Treatment, Advance Statements and Lasting Power of Attorney Policy (December 2025, issue 5, review December 2028)

**Q299. [Easy]** _Scenario: A patient is admitted with a ReSPECT form in their notes._ What does the trust policy say about how ReSPECT relates to an Advance Decision to Refuse Treatment (ADRT)?
- A. ReSPECT replaces any ADRT the patient has made
- B. ReSPECT only applies to patients on end of life care pathways, so an ADRT overrides it
- C. ReSPECT and an ADRT cannot both be held for the same patient
- **D. ReSPECT replaced the Do Not Attempt Cardiopulmonary Resuscitation (DNACPR) form, and does not replace an ADRT but should signpost staff to it and summarise its content [correct]**
_Why:_ The policy states that ReSPECT replaced the DNACPR documentation, and that the ReSPECT form does not replace an ADRT but should signpost clinical staff to the existence of an ADRT and provide a summary of its content, referring staff to the ReSPECT policy.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Advance Decisions to Refuse Medical Treatment, Advance Statements and Lasting Power of Attorney Policy (December 2025, issue 5, review December 2028)

**Q300. [Hard]** The trust Mental Capacity Policy states that its approach to assessing capacity differs from the Mental Capacity Act (MCA) Code of Practice. What is the trust's stated approach?
- A. Establish the impairment or disturbance of mind or brain first, and only then apply the four part functional test
- **B. Apply the four part functional test first, and only if the person cannot do one of those four things consider whether that is because of an impairment or disturbance of mind or brain [correct]**
- C. Assess capacity only where a doctor has requested it
- D. Assess capacity globally rather than decision by decision
_Why:_ The policy sets out the functional test (understand, retain, use or weigh, communicate) first, and only where the person cannot do one of those does the clinician go on to consider impairment or disturbance and whether the inability is because of it. The policy states this is consistent with case law, which overrides the Code.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Mental Capacity Policy (Overarching) (April 2023, issue 2, review Sept 2026 (extension approved))

**Q301. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient's behaviour has changed markedly with no obvious explanation, and they are making a decision that carries real risk. A colleague says capacity is presumed so nothing needs recording._ What does the trust Mental Capacity Policy say?
- A. The presumption of capacity means no record is needed unless an assessment is done
- B. Capacity should only be assessed if the patient asks for one
- **C. Clinicians must be prepared to explain why they decided not to carry out a capacity assessment, and should not record that capacity is presumed where there is legitimate reason to doubt it [correct]**
- D. A full assessment is required for every patient on the ward every week
_Why:_ The policy states that clinicians must be prepared to explain why they decided not to carry out a capacity assessment, and should not record that capacity is presumed where there is legitimate reason to doubt it, such as an unexplained change in presentation or behaviour, especially where the decision may cause harm.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Mental Capacity Policy (Overarching) (April 2023, issue 2, review Sept 2026 (extension approved))

**Q302. [Hard]** _Scenario: On admission you assess a patient's capacity to consent to sharing their clinical information with family and carers, and they have capacity._ What does Appendix 2 of the trust Mental Capacity Policy say you do next?
- **A. Assist the patient to complete the Advance Statement form in the trust Sharing Information with Family and Carers booklet, then scan the form onto SystmOne and give a copy of the form and booklet to the patient and carers [correct]**
- B. Record their verbal agreement in the daily notes and take no further action
- C. Complete a Best Interest form and inform the carers of the outcome
- D. Ask the carers to sign an information sharing agreement
_Why:_ Appendix 2 sets out that where the patient has capacity, staff assist them to complete the Advance Statement form in the trust Sharing Information with Family and Carers booklet, documenting their wishes on sharing if capacity is later lost, then scan the form onto SystmOne and give a copy of the form and booklet to the patient and carers.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Mental Capacity Policy (Overarching) (April 2023, issue 2, review Sept 2026 (extension approved))

**Q303. [Medium]** _Scenario: A best interests decision about serious medical treatment is contentious and cannot be resolved by the team._ What does the trust Mental Capacity Policy say about going to court?
- A. The patient's family must apply to the Court of Protection themselves
- **B. For serious or major medical treatment decisions the Trust will usually make the application, and this should be discussed with the Legal Department [correct]**
- C. The ward manager applies to the Court of Protection using the DoLS forms
- D. Applications are always made by the local authority as Supervisory Body
_Why:_ The policy states that for cases about serious or major decisions concerning medical treatment the Trust will usually make an application to the Court, that controversial and complex best interests decisions should be discussed with the Legal Department, and that where social care staff are concerned about a welfare decision the relevant local authority should make the application.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Mental Capacity Policy (Overarching) (April 2023, issue 2, review Sept 2026 (extension approved))

**Q304. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient lacking capacity needs help with washing, dressing, meals and getting to a hospital appointment._ According to the trust Capacity Manual on best interests, how is this normally handled?
- A. A formal Best Interest Meeting must be held before any personal care is given
- **B. It is recorded by ticking the routine care best interests box on the capacity to consent record, which does not require a formal Best Interest Meeting [correct]**
- C. It requires an Independent Mental Capacity Advocate (IMCA) referral in every case
- D. It requires written agreement from the next of kin
_Why:_ The manual states that for routine care, an acknowledgement that it is being provided in the patient's best interests can be recorded by ticking the relevant box in the capacity to consent record, and that this does not require a formal Best Interest Meeting. It lists washing, dressing, food, fluids, essential medication, mobility, ward activities and hospital appointments as examples of routine care.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - DHCFT Capacity Manual: Best Interests (November 2017)
_⚠ predates the move from PARIS to SystmOne - check current form names before use_

**Q305. [Medium]** _Scenario: The team is considering covert medication for a patient who lacks capacity._ What does the trust Capacity Manual on best interests indicate about this type of decision?
- A. It can be treated as routine care and simply recorded on the capacity to consent form
- B. It is a medical decision for the doctor alone and needs no wider discussion
- C. It always requires an application to the Court of Protection
- **D. It is the kind of decision, involving a range of professionals in a specific intervention, for which a formal Best Interest Meeting should be held [correct]**
_Why:_ The manual gives covert medication as an example of a decision involving a range of professionals in a specific intervention, which sits in its list of circumstances where a formal Best Interest Meeting should be held, along with complex, contentious or risk laden decisions, major life decisions and decisions with legal consequences.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - DHCFT Capacity Manual: Best Interests (November 2017)
_⚠ predates the move from PARIS to SystmOne - check current form names before use_

**Q306. [Medium]** _Scenario: A formal Best Interest Meeting is arranged but the patient's social worker and one carer cannot attend._ What does the trust Capacity Manual on best interests say should happen?
- A. The meeting should be postponed until everyone invited can attend
- B. Only professionals in attendance may contribute to the decision
- C. Non attendance is taken as agreement with the decision reached
- **D. Their opinions should be sought and recorded beforehand, raised at the meeting on their behalf, and they should be informed of the outcome afterwards [correct]**
_Why:_ The manual says all relevant parties should be invited, including relevant professionals, carers and where appropriate the patient's Independent Mental Capacity Advocate (IMCA), and that where some cannot attend you seek and record their opinions beforehand, raise these during the meeting on their behalf, and inform them of the outcome afterwards.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - DHCFT Capacity Manual: Best Interests (November 2017)
_⚠ predates the move from PARIS to SystmOne - check current form names before use_

## Trust Policy - Admission, Discharge & Transfer

**Q307. [Easy]** _Scenario: A patient was admitted to your ward at 2pm yesterday._ Under the trust's Acute Inpatient policy, by when must a named nurse be allocated to a newly admitted patient?
- **A. Within 24 hours of admission [correct]**
- B. Within 72 hours of admission
- C. At the first ward round
- D. By the admission meeting in the first week
_Why:_ The trust admission checklist lists 'Allocate named nurse (within 24 hours of admission)', to be done by the Lead or Senior Nurse and recorded in the central index under involved staff.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Acute Inpatient Mental Health Services for Adults of Working Age Policy and Procedures (May 2023, issue 2, review May 2026 (version control update Aug 2024))
_⚠ review date passed_

**Q308. [Easy]** A patient on your ward is assessed as needing Psychiatric Intensive Care Unit (PICU) level intervention. What does the trust's Acute Inpatient policy say happens next?
- A. They are transferred to the trust's own PICU at the Radbourne Unit
- **B. The trust does not currently have a PICU, so they must be referred to an out-of-area PICU provider, coordinated by the PICU Case Manager [correct]**
- C. They are nursed on enhanced observations on the acute ward until settled, as PICU is not commissioned in Derbyshire
- D. The ward manager applies to the Managing Director for a temporary PICU bay to be opened
_Why:_ The policy states that Derbyshire Healthcare does not currently have a PICU, so all patients needing that level of intervention are referred to out-of-area PICU providers, with referral, monitoring and repatriation coordinated by the PICU Case Manager.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Acute Inpatient Mental Health Services for Adults of Working Age Policy and Procedures (May 2023, issue 2, review May 2026 (version control update Aug 2024))
_⚠ review date passed_

**Q309. [Easy]** Under the trust's gatekeeping protocol for informal admissions, who holds the ultimate decision on whether an informal admission goes ahead or an alternative pathway is offered?
- A. The ward consultant on the receiving ward
- B. The bed management team
- **C. The Crisis Resolution and Home Treatment Team (CRHT) [correct]**
- D. The Approved Mental Health Professional (AMHP) on duty
_Why:_ The protocol is explicit that CRHT is the gatekeeper for all informal hospital admissions, so the ultimate decision to admit or offer an alternative pathway is held by CRHT.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Protocol for Gatekeeping Informal Admissions: Working Age Adults CRHT (No issue date, issue number or review date shown on document)

**Q310. [Easy]** _Scenario: You are attending a gatekeeping Multi-Disciplinary Meeting (MDM) as the ward representative._ Under the trust's gatekeeping protocol, who chairs the gatekeeping MDM and completes the gatekeeping questionnaire?
- **A. A Crisis Resolution and Home Treatment Team (CRHT) representative [correct]**
- B. The practitioner who requested the admission
- C. The inpatient clinical lead
- D. Ward admin, who minutes the meeting for the chair
_Why:_ The protocol states the CRHT representative will chair the MDM and complete the gatekeeping questionnaire in full during the meeting, using the version accessed via the CRHT launchpad on SystmOne.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Protocol for Gatekeeping Informal Admissions: Working Age Adults CRHT (No issue date, issue number or review date shown on document)

**Q311. [Easy]** According to the trust's Acute Inpatient policy, when does discharge planning begin?
- A. At the first weekly ward round after admission
- **B. At the point of admission [correct]**
- C. Once the patient is assessed as medically ready for discharge
- D. At the pre-discharge multi-disciplinary meeting
_Why:_ The policy states that discharge planning begins at the point of admission, and that all involved professionals and the patient should be clear on the treatment plan and what needs to happen to effect discharge.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Acute Inpatient Mental Health Services for Adults of Working Age Policy and Procedures (May 2023, issue 2, review May 2026 (version control update Aug 2024))
_⚠ review date passed_

**Q312. [Easy]** _Scenario: A patient is discharged home from your ward on a Tuesday morning._ Under the trust's Acute Inpatient policy, within what timeframe must the General Practitioner (GP) receive a copy of the discharge summary?
- **A. Within 24 hours of the discharge [correct]**
- B. Within 48 hours of the discharge
- C. Within 7 days of the discharge
- D. Within 14 days of the discharge
_Why:_ The policy states plainly that the GP must receive a copy of the discharge summary within 24 hours of the discharge. This was tightened from 48 hours to 24 hours in an earlier revision of the trust's discharge policy.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Acute Inpatient Mental Health Services for Adults of Working Age Policy and Procedures (May 2023, issue 2, review May 2026 (version control update Aug 2024))
_⚠ review date passed_

**Q313. [Easy]** _Scenario: A 17-year-old has been admitted to your adult acute ward in an emergency._ What does the trust's policy say about the room this young person should be given?
- A. Any available bed, provided a member of staff is allocated to them
- B. A shared bay so they are not isolated from other patients
- **C. An en-suite single room in close proximity to the ward office or nurses' station [correct]**
- D. A room at the far end of the ward, away from the busiest area
_Why:_ The policy states the young person must have an en-suite single room which is in close proximity to the ward office or nurses' station.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Admission of Young People to an Adult Mental Health Inpatient Unit Policy and Procedure (April 2024, issue 04, review March 2027)

**Q314. [Easy]** _Scenario: The bleep holder calls at 3am asking whether your adult acute ward can take a 15-year-old from the Emergency Department._ What does the trust's policy on admitting young people to adult wards say?
- **A. Under no circumstances will a young person under the age of 16 be admitted to an adult ward [correct]**
- B. A 15-year-old may be admitted overnight only, and must be moved by 9am
- C. A 15-year-old may be admitted if the on-call manager and the Emergency Department consultant both agree
- D. A 15-year-old may be admitted if placed on 1 to 1 observations and the Care Quality Commission is notified
_Why:_ The policy is absolute on this point: under no circumstances will a young person under the age of 16 be admitted to an adult ward. Only 16 to 18 year olds may be admitted, and only in exceptional circumstances with senior agreement.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Admission of Young People to an Adult Mental Health Inpatient Unit Policy and Procedure (April 2024, issue 04, review March 2027)

**Q315. [Easy]** Under the trust's Acute Inpatient policy, how often as a minimum should the medical team review a patient's medication during an acute admission?
- A. Daily
- **B. Weekly [correct]**
- C. Fortnightly
- D. Only at the point of any change in presentation
_Why:_ The policy states medications will be reviewed as a minimum on a weekly basis by the medical teams, with a documented record of adherence, response to treatment and any side effects.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Acute Inpatient Mental Health Services for Adults of Working Age Policy and Procedures (May 2023, issue 2, review May 2026 (version control update Aug 2024))
_⚠ review date passed_

**Q316. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient admitted this morning tells you she is not sure why she is in hospital._ Under the trust's admission checklist, by when must the purpose of admission be explained or reiterated to the patient, and when is it revisited?
- A. Within the first 4 hours, revisited at the admission meeting
- **B. Within the first 12 hours, then reviewed again in the first Multidisciplinary Team (MDT) meeting [correct]**
- C. Within the first 24 hours, then reviewed at discharge planning
- D. Within the first 7 days, at the admission meeting
_Why:_ The trust admission checklist requires the nurse to explain or reiterate the purpose of admission to the patient within the first 12 hours of admission and to review it again in the first MDT, documenting this in the patient's notes.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Acute Inpatient Mental Health Services for Adults of Working Age Policy and Procedures (May 2023, issue 2, review May 2026 (version control update Aug 2024))
_⚠ review date passed_

**Q317. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient was admitted on Monday and ward admin is arranging the first big meeting._ Under the trust's Acute Inpatient policy, when should the admission meeting take place, and who starts arranging it?
- **A. Within 7 working days of admission, with ward admin starting arrangements on the day of admission or the next working day [correct]**
- B. Within 14 days of admission, arranged by the named nurse once assessments are complete
- C. Within 72 hours of admission, arranged by the responsible clinician
- D. At the first available ward round slot, arranged by the bed management team
_Why:_ The trust's admission meeting pro-forma states the meeting should occur within the first 7 days of admission, and that ward admin, directed by the Senior Nurse or Nurse in Charge, will begin arrangements on the day of admission or the next working day to allow community representatives time to attend.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Acute Inpatient Mental Health Services for Adults of Working Age Policy and Procedures (May 2023, issue 2, review May 2026 (version control update Aug 2024))
_⚠ review date passed_

**Q318. [Medium]** _Scenario: A newly admitted patient hands you a watch and a wallet containing cash, and you are working through the property check on the admission checklist._ What does the trust's admission checklist say about valuables?
- A. All valuables must be locked in the ward's controlled drugs cupboard
- **B. Valuable items above 50 pounds should go home with relatives or be placed in the patient's bank for safekeeping [correct]**
- C. Valuables must be photographed and returned to the patient's bedroom locker
- D. Valuables above 20 pounds must be handed to the on-call manager
_Why:_ The trust admission checklist states that property and valuables are checked, paperwork completed and scanned onto the Electronic Patient Record, and that valuable items above 50 pounds should go home with relatives or be placed in the patient's bank for safekeeping, with the record kept in the cash and valuables book.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Acute Inpatient Mental Health Services for Adults of Working Age Policy and Procedures (May 2023, issue 2, review May 2026 (version control update Aug 2024))
_⚠ review date passed_

**Q319. [Medium]** _Scenario: It is 10pm and a referrer is asking who is coordinating an urgent admission request._ Under the trust's Acute Inpatient policy, what are the bed management team's core hours, and who coordinates admissions outside them?
- A. 9am to 5pm, with the on-call manager coordinating outside those hours
- **B. 8am to 8pm, with each unit's bleep holder coordinating outside those hours [correct]**
- C. 24 hours a day, so the bed management team always coordinates
- D. 7am to 7pm, with the Crisis Resolution and Home Treatment Team coordinating outside those hours
_Why:_ The policy states the dedicated bed management team operates core hours of 8am to 8pm, when most admission requests occur, and that outside these hours each unit bleep holder will coordinate admissions.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Acute Inpatient Mental Health Services for Adults of Working Age Policy and Procedures (May 2023, issue 2, review May 2026 (version control update Aug 2024))
_⚠ review date passed_

**Q320. [Medium]** The trust's Clinical Priorities for Admission policy lists primary diagnoses for which the acute inpatient wards are not commissioned to accept admissions, unless there is a co-occurring mental health condition. Which set is correct?
- **A. Organic mental illness, alcohol induced mental illness, primary eating disorder, and primary diagnosis of learning disability or Autism Spectrum Disorder (ASD) [correct]**
- B. Personality disorder, substance misuse, self-harm, and dementia
- C. Dementia, epilepsy, acquired brain injury, and post-traumatic stress disorder
- D. Primary eating disorder, bipolar affective disorder, psychosis in older adults, and learning disability
_Why:_ The policy lists exactly these four: organic mental illness, alcohol induced mental illness, primary eating disorder, and primary diagnosis of learning disability or ASD. Each is excluded only where it is the primary diagnosis with no co-occurring mental health condition.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Clinical Priorities for Admission Policy and Procedure (June 2025, issue 1, review Nov 2026)

**Q321. [Medium]** _Scenario: A community practitioner has assessed a patient at home, and both the patient and her carer are asking whether she can come into hospital._ What does the trust's gatekeeping protocol say about offering admission at this stage?
- A. Admission can be offered if the assessing practitioner is a registered professional, with the Crisis Resolution and Home Treatment Team (CRHT) informed afterwards
- **B. Admission should be discussed as one option, but should not be offered to the person before discussion with CRHT [correct]**
- C. Admission can be offered if the patient's consultant agrees by telephone
- D. Admission should never be discussed with a patient before a gatekeeping Multi-Disciplinary Meeting has taken place
_Why:_ The protocol says admission should be discussed with the person and their carers as one option so their views inform the process, but it is important admission is not offered before discussion with CRHT, because less restrictive alternatives are much harder to promote once the person has been told they are being admitted.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Protocol for Gatekeeping Informal Admissions: Working Age Adults CRHT (No issue date, issue number or review date shown on document)

**Q322. [Medium]** _Scenario: A gatekeeping Multi-Disciplinary Meeting (MDM) is being convened at short notice out of hours and several invitees cannot make it._ Under the trust's gatekeeping protocol, which attendance is mandatory rather than 'routinely invited'?
- A. A clinical lead from inpatient services and a representative from early discharge services
- B. The local crisis house and the patient's Community Mental Health Team (CMHT)
- **C. The practitioner requesting admission (or who conducted the assessment) and a Crisis Resolution and Home Treatment Team (CRHT) representative [correct]**
- D. The patient and their carer
_Why:_ The protocol states the practitioner requesting admission (or the person who conducted the assessment) and a CRHT representative must attend. An inpatient clinical lead, early discharge services and the crisis house are routinely invited, and the patient and carers may attend, but if they cannot their views must still be sought and represented.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Protocol for Gatekeeping Informal Admissions: Working Age Adults CRHT (No issue date, issue number or review date shown on document)

**Q323. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient on your ward meets the criteria for the Care Programme Approach (CPA) but has no community care coordinator allocated yet._ Under the trust's Care Principles and CPA policy, who acts as the interim Care Co-ordinator for a patient in mental health inpatient services?
- A. The ward manager
- **B. The named nurse, if appropriate [correct]**
- C. The responsible clinician
- D. The Community Mental Health Team duty worker
_Why:_ The policy states that for people in mental health inpatient services, the named nurse will be the interim Care Co-ordinator if appropriate.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Care Principles and Care Programme Approach (CPA) Policy and Procedure (May 2023, issue 06, review October 2026)

**Q324. [Medium]** _Scenario: The ward team believes a patient is ready for discharge, but the community team strongly disagrees about the level of current risk._ Under the trust's Discharge, Transfers/Transitions and Leave policy, what must happen before any act of discharge or leave?
- A. The ward consultant makes the final decision as the responsible clinician
- **B. The disagreement is escalated to the Clinical Directors for both areas [correct]**
- C. A second opinion is requested from the on-call consultant
- D. A Datix is completed and the discharge proceeds as planned
_Why:_ The policy states that where there is disagreement around risk or clinical presentation between inpatient and community teams, this must be escalated to the Clinical Directors for both areas prior to any act of discharge or leave.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Discharge, Transfers/Transitions and Leave for People with Mental Health Difficulties Policy and Procedure (November 2023, issue 5, review November 2026)

**Q325. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient needs to move from your acute ward to another acute ward within the trust._ Under the trust's Acute Inpatient policy, what must an inter-ward transfer between adult acute wards involve?
- **A. A consultant to consultant discussion, with the transfer planned during working hours [correct]**
- B. Agreement between the two ward managers only
- C. Authorisation by the bed management flow coordinator
- D. A Multidisciplinary Team (MDT) vote recorded in the Electronic Patient Record
_Why:_ The policy states inter ward transfers within acute inpatient services should be planned during working hours and must involve a consultant to consultant discussion, with nursing staff coordinating the transfer on the direction of the Responsible Clinician. Out-of-hours emergency transfers are discussed with the on-call consultant and on-call manager.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Acute Inpatient Mental Health Services for Adults of Working Age Policy and Procedures (May 2023, issue 2, review May 2026 (version control update Aug 2024))
_⚠ review date passed_

**Q326. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient on your acute ward has been accepted for a rehabilitation ward bed and you are recording the move on SystmOne._ Under the trust's Standard Operating Procedure (SOP), how is a move from an acute ward to a rehabilitation ward recorded?
- A. As an internal transfer, using the transfer option on the ward view
- B. As a planned transfer, ticking the Move Bed box
- **C. It is not classed as a transfer: the patient is discharged from the acute ward and then admitted to the rehabilitation ward [correct]**
- D. As a medically fit for transfer entry, with the discharge recorded afterwards
_Why:_ Both the Admission and the Discharge SOPs state that moving from an acute ward to a rehabilitation ward will not be classed as a transfer. The patient is discharged from the acute ward and then admitted to the rehabilitation ward as a planned admission.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Standard Operating Procedure: Admission to Inpatient Unit (Issue 01, trust wide; no issue date or review date shown on document)

**Q327. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient is admitted at 9am. You are working out what physical health tasks are due and when._ Under the trust's Acute Inpatient policy, which set of physical health tasks falls in the 'within 4 hours' group rather than the 'within 24 hours' group?
- A. Physical examination, height and weight, blood tests and Electrocardiogram (ECG)
- **B. Medicines reconciliation history, current medication and adherence, consideration of alcohol or drug withdrawal risk, and baseline observations of blood pressure, pulse and respirations [correct]**
- C. Waterlow assessment, personal handling risk assessment and Health of the Nation Outcome Scales (HoNOS)
- D. Blood tests and ECG only, with all other physical health tasks due within one week
_Why:_ The policy splits the physical health assessment: within four hours it covers past medicines reconciliation history, current medication including side effects and adherence, consideration of alcohol or drug withdrawal risk, and baseline observations of blood pressure, pulse and respirations. Physical examination, height and weight, blood tests and ECG sit in the within 24 hours group.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Acute Inpatient Mental Health Services for Adults of Working Age Policy and Procedures (May 2023, issue 2, review May 2026 (version control update Aug 2024))
_⚠ review date passed_

**Q328. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient has been on your ward for five weeks with no incidents and a stable presentation._ Under the trust's Acute Inpatient policy, what is the minimum frequency for updating a patient's safety and risk assessment?
- A. Weekly, and after any incident
- B. Fortnightly, and before any period of leave
- **C. According to clinical need, or a minimum of once per month, or following any incident [correct]**
- D. Only at the admission meeting and again before discharge
_Why:_ The policy states safety and risk assessments will be updated according to clinical need, or a minimum of once per month, or following any incident.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Acute Inpatient Mental Health Services for Adults of Working Age Policy and Procedures (May 2023, issue 2, review May 2026 (version control update Aug 2024))
_⚠ review date passed_

**Q329. [Hard]** _Scenario: The bed management team ask whether your ward can take an admission at 2pm._ The trust's Acute Inpatient policy names three staff shift handover periods when admissions should be avoided where possible. Which set is correct?
- **A. 7am to 7.30am, 1.45pm to 2.30pm, and 9pm to 9.30pm [correct]**
- B. 8am to 8.30am, 12noon to 1pm, and 8pm to 8.30pm
- C. 7.30am to 8am, 2pm to 3pm, and 10pm to 10.30pm
- D. 6.45am to 7.15am, 1pm to 2pm, and 9.30pm to 10pm
_Why:_ The policy names 7am to 7.30am, 1.45pm to 2.30pm and 9pm to 9.30pm as the handover periods to avoid, unless there is an urgent requirement related to immediate patient safety. Learning from incidents showed admissions in these windows increase the risk of information not transferring and admission actions not being allocated.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Acute Inpatient Mental Health Services for Adults of Working Age Policy and Procedures (May 2023, issue 2, review May 2026 (version control update Aug 2024))
_⚠ review date passed_

**Q330. [Hard]** Under the trust's Clinical Priorities for Admission policy, when does the bed management meeting take place, and who chairs it at weekends and on bank holidays?
- A. Twice daily at 9am and 4pm, chaired by the Area Service Manager at weekends
- **B. Once a day at 10.30am, 7 days a week, chaired at weekends and bank holidays by the Crisis or Liaison teams and bleep holders with support from the Bed Management FLOW team [correct]**
- C. Once a day at 10.30am, Monday to Friday only, with no weekend meeting
- D. Once a day at 8am, 7 days a week, always chaired by the Bed Manager
_Why:_ The policy states a bed management meeting is held once a day at 10.30am, 7 days a week. Monday to Friday it is chaired by the Bed Manager, and at weekends and on bank holidays it is chaired by the Crisis or Liaison teams and bleep holders, with support from the Bed Management FLOW team, who ensure everyone on the list is discussed.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Clinical Priorities for Admission Policy and Procedure (June 2025, issue 1, review Nov 2026)

**Q331. [Hard]** _Scenario: The bed management meeting has agreed the order of priority for the day's admissions. Later, an on-call manager contacts the ward asking for a different patient to be admitted first because of a 12-hour breach in the Emergency Department._ What does the trust's Clinical Priorities for Admission policy say about this?
- **A. Prioritisation agreed at the bed management meeting should not be overridden by on-call managers, bleep holders or directors, and an Emergency Department 12-hour breach request is given as an example [correct]**
- B. The on-call manager can override the clinical priority where a 12-hour breach applies, as this is a national performance standard
- C. The ward manager decides, as they hold operational responsibility for the bed
- D. The Emergency Department consultant and the on-call manager jointly agree a new order of priority
_Why:_ The policy states that once prioritisation is agreed by the bed management meeting it should not be overridden by other areas such as an on-call manager, bleep holder or director, and it gives a 12-hour breach request from the Emergency Department as the example. Unresolved disagreements go to the on-call consultant.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Clinical Priorities for Admission Policy and Procedure (June 2025, issue 1, review Nov 2026)

**Q332. [Hard]** _Scenario: An Approved Mental Health Professional (AMHP) has completed a Mental Health Act (MHA) assessment meeting the 'special urgency' threshold under Section 140, and is waiting on a bed._ Under the trust's Section 140 arrangements, how is 'a short period of time' for a bed becoming available interpreted locally?
- A. Thirty minutes
- B. One hour
- **C. Two hours [correct]**
- D. Four hours
_Why:_ The policy states the local interpretation of 'short period of time' is two hours. The AMHP may make the application for detention once informed a bed is available or will become available within that period; if no bed is expected within two hours, the application cannot be made.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Clinical Priorities for Admission Policy and Procedure (June 2025, issue 1, review Nov 2026)

**Q333. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient on the Care Programme Approach (CPA) has had a significant deterioration in their illness during this admission._ Under the trust's Care Principles and CPA policy, what is the review pattern for their care plan?
- **A. A minimum of annually, with a review at the point of deterioration and again six months later, reverting to annual once stable [correct]**
- B. A minimum of six-monthly at all times, regardless of stability
- C. Quarterly for the first year after any deterioration
- D. A minimum of annually, with no additional review required unless the person requests one
_Why:_ The policy sets a minimum standard of at least one review a year (with every 9 months recommended), and states that if a person experiences a deterioration in their illness a review should be held at that time and again six months later, reverting to annual reviews once stable. A review is also held before any discharge or transfer.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Care Principles and Care Programme Approach (CPA) Policy and Procedure (May 2023, issue 06, review October 2026)

**Q334. [Hard]** _Scenario: A 17-year-old was admitted to your adult ward at 6pm on Friday and is still with you on Sunday evening._ Under the trust's policy, what notification duty has now been triggered?
- A. No external notification is needed unless the young person is detained under the Mental Health Act
- **B. The Care Quality Commission (CQC) must be notified, as the young person has remained on an adult ward continuously for over 48 hours, and a Datix must be completed in all cases [correct]**
- C. Only NHS England must be notified, via the Tier 4 referral form
- D. The CQC must be notified only if the admission exceeds 7 days
_Why:_ The policy requires the CQC to be notified without delay if an under 18-year-old is placed on an adult psychiatric ward for longer than a continuous period of 48 hours, using the Appendix 3 template, and states a Datix must be completed in all cases.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Admission of Young People to an Adult Mental Health Inpatient Unit Policy and Procedure (April 2024, issue 04, review March 2027)

**Q335. [Hard]** _Scenario: A 16-year-old has been admitted to your adult acute ward in core hours._ Under the trust's policy, what must the Child and Adolescent Mental Health Services (CAMHS) Area Service Manager do, and what happens if the timeframe is missed?
- **A. Visit the adult ward within 24 hours to delegate a competent person to conduct a safeguarding assessment; if this timeframe is not achieved a Datix must be completed and the records updated [correct]**
- B. Visit within 72 hours; if missed, the ward manager escalates to the Head of Nursing
- C. Telephone the ward within 4 hours; no Datix is required as the visit is advisory
- D. Visit within 7 days, or before the first Multidisciplinary Team (MDT) meeting, whichever is sooner
_Why:_ The policy states that in core hours the CAMHS Area Service Manager liaises directly with the admitting ward and arranges to visit within 24 hours to delegate an appropriately competent person to conduct a safeguarding assessment using the Royal College of Psychiatrists guidelines. Where that timeframe is not achieved, a Datix form must be completed and the young person's records updated.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Admission of Young People to an Adult Mental Health Inpatient Unit Policy and Procedure (April 2024, issue 04, review March 2027)

**Q336. [Hard]** _Scenario: A 17-year-old admitted to your adult ward is finding constant close staff presence distressing, and the team is discussing reducing her observation level._ Under the trust's policy, what is the starting observation level for a young person on an adult ward, and how far can it be reduced?
- A. General observations, increased only if risk indicates
- B. Within eyesight observations at all times, reducible to hourly checks by Multidisciplinary Team (MDT) agreement
- **C. 1 to 1 within arm's length observations at all times, reducible to no less than within eyesight observations if the clinical team agrees it is in her best interests and safety can be maintained [correct]**
- D. 1 to 1 within arm's length observations for the first 24 hours only, then general observations
_Why:_ The policy states a young person under 18 on an adult ward must be placed on 1 to 1 within arm's length observations at all times, and that this can only be reduced to no less than within eyesight observations if agreed by the clinical team, it is in the young person's best interests and their safety can be maintained. Any decision not to nurse within eyesight must be fully risk assessed and reviewed at least every 24 hours.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Admission of Young People to an Adult Mental Health Inpatient Unit Policy and Procedure (April 2024, issue 04, review March 2027)

## Trust Policy - Physical Health & Emergencies

**Q337. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient is transferred to your ward straight from an acute medical ward, where they were seen by a doctor two days ago._ Under the Trust's Physical Care of Adult Inpatients policy, by when must a detailed physical examination, including weight and height, be completed for this patient?
- A. Within 4 hours of admission
- **B. Within 48 hours of admission, even though they came from an acute medical ward [correct]**
- C. It is not needed because they were examined on the acute ward
- D. Within 7 days of admission
_Why:_ The policy requires a comprehensive physical examination including observations, weight and height within 48 hours of admission, and states explicitly that this must be completed even if the patient has been transferred from another acute medical ward or inpatient setting.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Physical Care of Adult Inpatients Policy and Procedure (August 2025, issue 05, review April 2028)

**Q338. [Medium]** The Trust's Physical Care of Adult Inpatients policy sets a standard for completing a full physical health review after admission, quoting the Accreditation for Inpatient Mental Health Services (AIMS) standard. What is the timescale?
- **A. Within 2 days of admission [correct]**
- B. Within 5 working days of admission
- C. By the first ward round
- D. Within 14 days of admission
_Why:_ The policy states a full physical health review should be completed within 2 days of admission (AIMS standard), ordinarily requiring liaison with the GP, the summary care record and carers.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Physical Care of Adult Inpatients Policy and Procedure (August 2025, issue 05, review April 2028)

**Q339. [Easy]** Where nothing else has been specified for an individual patient, what does the Trust's Physical Care of Adult Inpatients policy set as the MINIMUM frequency for recording physical clinical observations?
- A. Daily
- B. Twice weekly
- **C. Weekly, including weight, pulse and blood pressure [correct]**
- D. Monthly
_Why:_ The policy states that where it is not specified otherwise, physical clinical observations should be recorded as a minimum weekly, including weight, pulse and blood pressure. Frequency above that minimum is guided by clinical need and the NEWS2 escalation plan.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Physical Care of Adult Inpatients Policy and Procedure (August 2025, issue 05, review April 2028)

**Q340. [Easy]** _Scenario: A patient on your ward has now been an inpatient for seven months._ How often does the Trust's Physical Care of Adult Inpatients policy say a comprehensive physical health review must be repeated for long-stay patients?
- A. Every three months
- **B. Every six months [correct]**
- C. Every twelve months
- D. Only if a new physical problem is identified
_Why:_ The policy states that Physical Health Assessment documentation in the Electronic Patient Record must be completed on admission and at agreed timescales during the stay, with a minimum of every six months, and that for patients in hospital for extended periods a comprehensive physical health review should be repeated every six months.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Physical Care of Adult Inpatients Policy and Procedure (August 2025, issue 05, review April 2028)
