**Q341. [Hard]** _Scenario: You have just recorded a set of observations on a patient who feels hot and unwell._ According to the Trust's Physical Care of Adult Inpatients policy, which National Early Warning Score 2 (NEWS2) findings should prompt you to complete the Trust sepsis screening tool?
- A. Any score of 1 or more
- **B. A score of 3 in a single parameter, or an aggregate score above 5 [correct]**
- C. An aggregate score of 3 or more only
- D. Only an aggregate score of 7 or more
_Why:_ The policy states that where patients trigger 3 in a single parameter or have an aggregate score greater than 5, a Trust sepsis screening tool should be completed.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Physical Care of Adult Inpatients Policy and Procedure (August 2025, issue 05, review April 2028)

**Q342. [Hard]** _Scenario: A physically unwell patient has been placed on Enhanced Physical Care Monitoring (E-PCM)._ What monitoring and recording interval does the Trust's Physical Care of Adult Inpatients policy set out for Enhanced Physical Care Monitoring (E-PCM)?
- A. Every 5 minutes
- **B. Every 15 minutes, or more often as the condition dictates [correct]**
- C. Every 30 minutes
- D. Hourly
_Why:_ The E-PCM procedure in Appendix 1 of the policy says to monitor and record every 15 minutes or more as the condition dictates, using the Early Warning System, and requires enough handover overlap for both practitioners to visually agree the patient's condition and the frequency of observation.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Physical Care of Adult Inpatients Policy and Procedure (August 2025, issue 05, review April 2028)

**Q343. [Hard]** _Scenario: A pregnant woman is admitted to the ward overnight._ The Trust's Physical Care of Adult Inpatients policy requires a full physical history and examination for all women admitted, including blood pressure, pulse, temperature, respiration rate and urinalysis. By when must these be recorded in the patient notes?
- **A. Within 4 hours of admission [correct]**
- B. Within 12 hours of admission
- C. Within 24 hours of admission
- D. By the next working day
_Why:_ The policy states that all women admitted must have a full physical history and examination including blood pressure, pulse, temperature, respiration rate and urinalysis (dipsticks) recorded in the patient notes within 4 hours of admission.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Physical Care of Adult Inpatients Policy and Procedure (August 2025, issue 05, review April 2028)

**Q344. [Hard]** _Scenario: A woman is admitted three days after giving birth._ What does the Trust's Physical Care of Adult Inpatients policy require for a woman admitted within 5 days post-partum?
- A. Daily National Early Warning Score 2 (NEWS2) and a midwife visit within five days
- B. Weekly National Early Warning Score 2 (NEWS2) and a GP review
- **C. An agreed physical health National Early Warning Score 2 (NEWS2) twice daily until no longer clinically necessary, and to be seen by a midwife within two days [correct]**
- D. No routine observations unless she reports feeling unwell
_Why:_ The policy states that women admitted within 5 days post-partum should have an agreed physical health NEWS2 completed twice daily until clinically no longer necessary, and be seen by a midwife within two days, with the visit recorded in the electronic patient record.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Physical Care of Adult Inpatients Policy and Procedure (August 2025, issue 05, review April 2028)

**Q345. [Medium]** Under the Trust's Physical Care of Adult Inpatients policy, how quickly must the discharge summary and discharge clinical narrative be sent to the GP after discharge?
- **A. Within 24 hours of discharge, by NHS.net email [correct]**
- B. Within 72 hours of discharge, by internal post
- C. Within one week of discharge
- D. Within two weeks of discharge
_Why:_ The policy requires the discharge summary and discharge clinical narrative to be completed on SystmOne and sent to GPs within 24 hours of discharge by NHS.net email. Follow-up summaries should be sent within 2 weeks of discharge.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Physical Care of Adult Inpatients Policy and Procedure (August 2025, issue 05, review April 2028)

**Q346. [Easy]** _Scenario: A patient on your ward has just been started on an antipsychotic._ Under the responsibilities of the registered nurse in the Trust's Physical Care of Adult Inpatients policy, how often should weight be checked for patients initiated on antipsychotics and mood stabilisers?
- A. Daily
- **B. Weekly [correct]**
- C. Fortnightly
- D. Monthly
_Why:_ The policy lists 'ensuring weekly weight checks for patients initiated on antipsychotics and mood stabilisers' as a specific duty of the registered nurse, alongside implementing the physical care plan including Lester Tool monitoring.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Physical Care of Adult Inpatients Policy and Procedure (August 2025, issue 05, review April 2028)

**Q347. [Medium]** _Scenario: A newly admitted patient gives an account of recent substance use, and a urine drug screen is indicated._ Within what period of admission does the Trust's Physical Care of Adult Inpatients policy say an indicated urine drug screen should be taken?
- A. Within 4 hours of admission
- **B. Within 24 hours of admission [correct]**
- C. Within 48 hours of admission
- D. At the first ward round
_Why:_ In the list of investigations required on admission, the policy specifies a urine drug screen if indicated within 24 hours of admission.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Physical Care of Adult Inpatients Policy and Procedure (August 2025, issue 05, review April 2028)

**Q348. [Easy]** The Trust's Resuscitation Policy sets the frequency of emergency equipment checks. How often must grab bags and the contents on the TOP of the emergency trolley be checked in an inpatient setting?
- **A. Every 24 hours [correct]**
- B. Every 48 hours
- C. Weekly
- D. Monthly
_Why:_ The policy's frequency of checks table gives 'DHcFT Emergency Trolley top contents - Daily (24hrs)', with grab bags and trolley top checked every 24 hours in inpatient settings and weekly in community settings.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Resuscitation Policy and Procedures (Sept 2025, issue 13, review Sept 2028)

**Q349. [Easy]** How often does the Trust's Resuscitation Policy require the DRAWER contents of the inpatient resuscitation trolley to be checked?
- A. Every 24 hours
- **B. Weekly, ensuring a tamper proof tag is used [correct]**
- C. Monthly
- D. Only after the trolley has been used
_Why:_ The policy states the checks are weekly for the resuscitation trolley drawer contents, ensuring a tamper proof tag is used. The Trust's Resuscitation Trolley Check List is headed 'CHECKED WEEKLY (unless security tab has been broken)'.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Resuscitation Policy and Procedures (Sept 2025, issue 13, review Sept 2028)

**Q350. [Hard]** _Scenario: Your ward manager asks how long the completed resuscitation equipment check records need to be kept._ According to the Trust's Resuscitation Policy, how long must the signed and dated record of emergency equipment checks be maintained?
- A. 1 year
- B. 2 years
- **C. 3 years [correct]**
- D. 7 years
_Why:_ The policy states that a signed and dated record of these checks should be maintained and kept for 3 years, and should be available for inspection, with assurance routinely confirmed to line managers.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Resuscitation Policy and Procedures (Sept 2025, issue 13, review Sept 2028)

**Q351. [Hard]** _Scenario: You are doing the daily emergency equipment check and reach the oxygen cylinder._ What does the Trust's Resuscitation Policy say about checking oxygen cylinders?
- A. Cylinders must be at least 50% full and all cylinders should be turned on to test flow
- **B. Cylinders must be at least 75% full, and cylinders with integrated BOC valve heads should NOT be turned on during the checking procedure [correct]**
- C. Cylinders must be completely full and replaced weekly regardless of contents
- D. Cylinder contents only need checking after the cylinder has been used
_Why:_ The policy states oxygen cylinders must be at least 75% full, that cylinders with integrated BOC valve heads should NOT be turned on during the checking procedure, and that those without the live gauge should be turned on to confirm 75% capacity. The Daily Clinic Checklist likewise records the large oxygen cylinder as 'min 75% full'.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Resuscitation Policy and Procedures (Sept 2025, issue 13, review Sept 2028)

**Q352. [Hard]** The Trust's Resuscitation Policy states that the Automated External Defibrillator (AED) runs its own self-tests. What is the ONLY check staff need to carry out on the AED on a daily basis?
- A. Run a manual shock test and record the result
- **B. Confirm the status indicator shows a flashing hour glass symbol, and that 2 sets of defibrillator pads are in date and available for immediate use [correct]**
- C. Remove and re-seat the battery, then check the pads
- D. Download the device memory and file it in the patient record
_Why:_ The policy states AEDs have a comprehensive self-test system running daily, weekly and monthly diagnostic checks, and that the only daily check needed by staff is to confirm the status indicator shows a flashing hour glass symbol and that 2 sets of defibrillator pads are in date and available for immediate use.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Resuscitation Policy and Procedures (Sept 2025, issue 13, review Sept 2028)

**Q353. [Medium]** _Scenario: A cardiopulmonary resuscitation (CPR) attempt is underway on the ward and you are the nurse leading it._ What does the Trust's Resuscitation Policy require in relation to the Trust's CPR Report Form?
- **A. The lead must allocate a member of staff to complete it DURING the event so correct timings are captured, and the form is then scanned into the DATIX incident form [correct]**
- B. It should be completed from memory by the lead at the end of the shift
- C. It is only needed if the patient does not survive
- D. It replaces the need for a DATIX incident report
_Why:_ The policy requires the Trust's CPR Report Form for every resuscitation event, with the member of staff taking the lead role allocating someone to complete the documentation during the event to capture correct timings. The form is kept and scanned into the DATIX incident form, and an electronic DATIX incident report must be completed for all CPR attempts.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Resuscitation Policy and Procedures (Sept 2025, issue 13, review Sept 2028)

**Q354. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient has regained a pulse after a resuscitation attempt and you are waiting for the paramedics._ What does the Trust's Resuscitation Policy say about immediate post-resuscitation care?
- A. Recommence ABCDE assessment and document findings every 15 minutes, and switch the Automated External Defibrillator (AED) off to save the battery
- **B. Recommence ABCDE assessment and document findings every two minutes using National Early Warning Score 2 (NEWS2), leave the AED switched on and leave the pads adhered and plugged in [correct]**
- C. Record a full set of observations once, then wait for the ambulance crew
- D. Remove the AED pads and clean the equipment before the crew arrives
_Why:_ The policy's post-resuscitation care section requires ABCDE assessment recommenced and findings documented every two minutes using NEWS2, with the AED left switched on and the chest pads left adhered and plugged in, so the AED can be used without delay if the patient re-arrests.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Resuscitation Policy and Procedures (Sept 2025, issue 13, review Sept 2028)

**Q355. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient's National Early Warning Score 2 (NEWS2) total comes out at 7. You have contacted the doctor, who says they cannot get to the ward for about half an hour._ Under the escalation table in the Trust's Resuscitation Policy, what should you do?
- A. Wait for the doctor and repeat the observations in 30 minutes
- **B. Call an ambulance, because at a total of 7 or more a delay of more than 15 minutes means an ambulance should be called unless the doctor advises otherwise [correct]**
- C. Increase observations to 2 hourly and review at the next handover
- D. Call an ambulance only if the score rises above 9
_Why:_ The policy's NEWS2 escalation table sets a total of 7 or more as the emergency response threshold, requiring the registered nurse to inform the medical team immediately at at least registrar level, and to call the doctor for an immediate visit or, if a delay of more than 15 minutes, to call an ambulance unless advised otherwise by the doctor. At a total of 5 or more the equivalent delay trigger is 30 minutes.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Resuscitation Policy and Procedures (Sept 2025, issue 13, review Sept 2028)

**Q356. [Medium]** The Trust's Resuscitation Policy sets a minimum standard for clinical areas that practise Rapid Tranquillisation (RT). What must those areas have access to?
- A. A full resuscitation trolley and a resident anaesthetist
- **B. An Automated External Defibrillator (AED), a grab bag and appropriately trained staff [correct]**
- C. An AED only
- D. A pulse oximeter and a first aid kit
_Why:_ The policy states that those areas which practice rapid tranquillisation should have access to an AED, grab bag and appropriately trained staff as a minimum. It also requires staff involved in RT to have ongoing competency training to a minimum of Resuscitation Council (UK) Immediate Life Support (ILS) and to be trained in the use of pulse oximeters.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Resuscitation Policy and Procedures (Sept 2025, issue 13, review Sept 2028)

**Q357. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient collapses in cardiac arrest on a ward where emergency bleep holders are expected to attend._ In what order does the Trust's Resuscitation Policy say the emergency calls should be made?
- A. The internal medical emergency bleep request first, then the ambulance call
- **B. The 999 ambulance call first, then the internal medical emergency bleep request, stating a Medical Emergency, the location and whether the victim is an adult or child [correct]**
- C. Only the internal bleep request; the bleep holder will call the ambulance
- D. Only 999; the bleep is not used for cardiac arrest
_Why:_ The policy states that in all cases of respiratory and cardiopulmonary arrest 999 must be dialled to summon the ambulance service, and that in areas where bleep holders are expected to attend, the ambulance call should be made FIRST, followed by the internal bleep request, stating a Medical Emergency, the location of the event and whether the victim is an adult or child. The Hartington Unit has a separate arrangement with the Chesterfield Royal Hospital switchboard and cardiac arrest team.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Resuscitation Policy and Procedures (Sept 2025, issue 13, review Sept 2028)

**Q358. [Easy]** How often does the Trust's Resuscitation Policy say ALL emergency equipment will be audited, and who coordinates that audit?
- A. Every six months, coordinated by the ward manager
- **B. A minimum of once a year, coordinated by the Physical Health Care and Infection Control Committee (PHCIC) [correct]**
- C. Every two years, coordinated by People Development Services
- D. Only when an incident occurs, coordinated by the Lead Resuscitation Officer
_Why:_ The policy states that all emergency equipment will be audited a minimum of once a year, that the audit is coordinated by the PHCIC, and that any remedial action required is reported to the appropriate unit manager immediately.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Resuscitation Policy and Procedures (Sept 2025, issue 13, review Sept 2028)

**Q359. [Easy]** The Trust's Resuscitation Trolley Check List is headed with the frequency it must be completed. What does it say?
- A. Checked daily
- **B. Checked weekly, unless the security tab has been broken [correct]**
- C. Checked monthly
- D. Checked at every shift handover
_Why:_ The Trust's Resuscitation Trolley Check List is headed 'CHECKED WEEKLY (unless security tab has been broken)', and requires the ward, date, quantity, expiry date of the first item to expire, staff signature and the security tag numbers removed and added.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Resuscitation Trolley Check List (General) (April 2026, version 1.4)

**Q360. [Easy]** _Scenario: You need a ligature cutter from the resuscitation trolley in a hurry._ On the Trust's Resuscitation Trolley Check List, which drawer holds the ligature cutter and the tough cut scissors?
- **A. The 1st drawer, Airway [correct]**
- B. The 2nd drawer, Breathing
- C. The 3rd drawer, Circulation
- D. The bottom drawer
_Why:_ The checklist lists the ligature cutter, tough cut scissor and pen torch in the 1st drawer (Airway), alongside airway adjuncts, pocket mask, Magills forceps and spare suction tubing. The bottom drawer holds the infusion fluids, the Adrenaline Shock Pack and the Naloxone 400 microgram Emergency Pack.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Resuscitation Trolley Check List (General) (April 2026, version 1.4)

**Q361. [Hard]** _Scenario: You are working through the Daily Clinic Checklist and reach the glucometer section._ What does the Trust's Daily Clinic Checklist say about the glucometer control solutions?
- **A. Check the date of the glucose control solutions, which must be less than 3 months since opened, and recalibrate if replaced with new solutions [correct]**
- B. Control solutions last 12 months once opened and no recalibration is needed
- C. Control solutions only need checking when a reading looks wrong
- D. Control solutions must be replaced weekly
_Why:_ The Daily Clinic Checklist includes 'Check date of glucose control solutions (less than 3 months since opened)' and carries the note that if they are replaced with new solutions you must remember to recalibrate.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Daily Clinic Checklist (April 2026)

**Q362. [Medium]** The Trust's Daily Clinic Checklist includes a stock check of disposable ligature knives. How many must be available in EVERY green zone?
- A. One
- B. Two
- **C. Three [correct]**
- D. Five
_Why:_ The Daily Clinic Checklist requires disposable ligature knives x3 and disposable tough cut scissors x3 in every green zone, plus a storage box holding 20 ligature knives and 5 tough cut scissors, with the stock check form updated to reflect current stock.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Daily Clinic Checklist (April 2026)

**Q363. [Medium]** Besides the defibrillator and airway kit, which of the following does the Trust's Daily Clinic Checklist require to be present on TOP of the resuscitation trolley?
- **A. ReSPECT forms and an SBARD notepad and pen [correct]**
- B. The patient's paper drug chart
- C. A copy of the ward's off-duty rota
- D. A spare set of patient observation charts only
_Why:_ The Daily Clinic Checklist lists ReSPECT forms and an SBARD notepad and pen among the items checked daily on top of the resuscitation trolley, along with the AED and pads, the printed BLS algorithm hung off the drip stand, an empty resus-only sharps bin, a small full oxygen cylinder, the suction machine and the bag valve mask with HEPA filter.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Daily Clinic Checklist (April 2026)

**Q364. [Easy]** _Scenario: A patient is being managed for suspected sepsis while transfer is arranged._ What oxygen saturation target does the Trust's Sepsis Policy set within the Sepsis Six?
- A. Saturations of 88% or higher for everyone
- **B. Saturations of 94% or higher, or above 88% for patients known to have moderate to severe pulmonary disease [correct]**
- C. Saturations of 90% or higher for everyone
- D. Oxygen should be withheld until a prescription is written
_Why:_ The policy states oxygen should be given to maintain target saturations of 94% or higher, and that where patients are known to have moderate to severe pulmonary disease the recommendation to give oxygen remains but with a lower target above 88%. It notes patients with sepsis are exempt from British Thoracic Society oxygen guidance because hypoxia will kill before hypercapnia.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Sepsis Policy and Procedure (August 2025, issue 003, review August 2028)

**Q365. [Hard]** _Scenario: A deteriorating patient with suspected Red Flag Sepsis needs high flow oxygen immediately and no prescription has been written._ What does the Trust's Sepsis Policy say about the need for an oxygen prescription in this situation?
- A. All oxygen administration requires a prescription first, without exception
- **B. High flow continuous oxygen via a non-rebreathing mask for the express purpose of life saving does not require a prescription, although titrating oxygen to maintain a specified saturation is oxygen therapy and does require one [correct]**
- C. Oxygen never requires a prescription in any circumstances
- D. Only a doctor may apply the mask, and only after prescribing
_Why:_ The policy states that titrating oxygen delivery to maintain a specified saturation is provision of oxygen therapy and requires a prescription, but that high flow continuous oxygen delivery via non-rebreathing mask for the express purpose of life saving does not require a prescription.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Sepsis Policy and Procedure (August 2025, issue 003, review August 2028)

**Q366. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient on your ward has one Red Flag Sepsis criterion and you are arranging transfer._ What does the Trust's Sepsis Policy say about how the ambulance call and the referral to the Emergency Department (ED) should be made?
- A. Request a routine ambulance and let the crew brief the ED on arrival
- **B. Transfer by blue light ambulance with a paramedic crew if immediately available, with the call using the term 'Red Flag Sepsis', and where possible a telephone referral to the receiving ED also using the term 'Red Flag Sepsis' [correct]**
- C. Ask the on-call doctor to arrange transfer the following morning
- D. Transfer by ward staff car with an escort, since this is usually quicker
_Why:_ The policy states that transfer should be by blue light ambulance with a paramedic crew if immediately available, that the call should include direct reference to the acuity using the term 'Red Flag Sepsis', and that where possible a telephone referral to the receiving Emergency Department should be made using the same term. The equivalent wording for Amber Flag is 'Amber Flag Sepsis'.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Sepsis Policy and Procedure (August 2025, issue 003, review August 2028)

**Q367. [Hard]** Under the Trust's Sepsis Policy, who is responsible for making sure sepsis screening in a clinical area is audited, and how often?
- A. The Lead Resuscitation Officer, every three years
- **B. The senior clinician in overall charge of each clinical area, at least annually [correct]**
- C. The Chief Executive, every two years
- D. The ward manager, only after a serious incident
_Why:_ The policy states the senior clinician in overall charge of each clinical area is responsible for ensuring the standard of sepsis screening in their area is audited for compliance at least annually. Clinical audit outcomes are reported to the Safeguarding and Patient Safety Group and the Mortality Review Panel, and all sepsis incidents are reviewed through DATIX.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Sepsis Policy and Procedure (August 2025, issue 003, review August 2028)

**Q368. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient has a tonic clonic seizure that stops after about three minutes. Two hours later they have another one._ How does the Trust's tonic clonic seizure template classify this situation?
- A. Routine observation only, because each seizure was under 5 minutes
- **B. A medical emergency, because a seizure under 5 minutes followed by a further seizure within 24 hours (or a sustained injury) meets the emergency criteria, as does any seizure lasting more than 5 minutes [correct]**
- C. Urgent action but not an emergency, because only seizures over 10 minutes are emergencies
- D. A medical emergency only if the patient has no diagnosis of epilepsy
_Why:_ The Trust's template sets the medical emergency threshold as a seizure lasting more than 5 minutes (consider status epilepticus), OR a seizure under 5 minutes where the person suffers a further seizure within 24 hours or sustains an injury. Where a seizure resolves in under 5 minutes with no repeat, the template directs urgent action, contacting the doctor or Advanced Clinical Practitioner (ACP) to review immediately and agree a management plan, which can be done by phone.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Tonic Clonic Seizure Template (September 2025)

**Q369. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient is having a tonic clonic seizure on the ward._ Alongside performing ABCDE and National Early Warning Score 2 (NEWS2), which specific bedside test does the Trust's tonic clonic seizure template tell you to do, and what must be recorded?
- A. A urine dipstick, recording only the time the seizure started
- **B. A blood glucose test, keeping an accurate record of the time, duration and description of the seizure [correct]**
- C. A 12 lead ECG, recording only the patient's response afterwards
- D. A peak flow, recording the recovery position used
_Why:_ The Trust's template directs staff to perform ABCDE and NEWS2, do a blood glucose test, secure the airway and consider oxygen, and to keep an accurate record of time, duration and description. It also says do not restrain the person, do not put anything in their mouth, do not move them unless they are in danger, and stay with them until they have recovered.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Tonic Clonic Seizure Template (September 2025)

**Q370. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient on the ward has sudden facial droop and cannot raise one arm. You have dialled 999._ According to the Trust's stroke protocol for a patient presenting as unwell in an inpatient setting, what should you do while you await assistance?
- A. Sit the patient up and give sips of water to check their swallow
- **B. Perform a blood glucose measurement to exclude hypoglycaemia and keep the patient warm, and do NOT provide anything orally [correct]**
- C. Give aspirin immediately and record a 12 lead ECG
- D. Walk the patient to the clinic room for a full examination
_Why:_ The Trust's stroke protocol, based on the FAST campaign, treats suspected stroke as a medical emergency: perform ABCDE and an early warning score, dial 999, make sure resuscitation equipment is available, and while awaiting assistance perform a BM to exclude hypoglycaemia and keep the patient warm. It states explicitly: do not provide anything orally.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Stroke Medical Protocol, Patient Presents as Unwell in Inpatient Setting (issue date, issue number and review date left blank on the document)

**Q371. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient who has taken an overdose on the ward refuses to go to the Emergency Department (ED)._ What does the Trust's Immediate Management of Overdose / Self-Poisoning (Inpatient) procedure say should happen next?
- A. Accept the refusal and monitor observations on the ward overnight
- **B. A capacity assessment is required; if the patient is capacitous and still declines, a Mental Health Act assessment should be considered, and charcoal given while this is arranged if the ED advises it [correct]**
- C. Restrain and convey the patient to the ED immediately
- D. Discharge responsibility to the ambulance crew and take no further action
_Why:_ The procedure states that if a patient refuses transfer to the ED a capacity assessment is required, that if a capacitous patient declines a Mental Health Act assessment should be considered, and that whilst this is being arranged charcoal should be given if the ED advises it.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Immediate Management of Overdose / Self-Poisoning: Inpatient (no issue or review date shown on the document)

**Q372. [Hard]** _Scenario: A 17 year old on the ward has taken an overdose and is refusing treatment. They appear to have capacity._ What does the Trust's Immediate Management of Overdose / Self-Poisoning (Inpatient) procedure say about this?
- A. Capacity settles it, so the refusal should simply be documented
- **B. It is a safeguarding issue even when the young person has capacity, and advice or support should be sought from the safeguarding lead or social care as appropriate [correct]**
- C. Parental consent alone resolves the situation with no further action
- D. A safeguarding referral is only needed if the young person is under 16
_Why:_ The procedure's safeguarding considerations box states that a young person under 18 years refusing treatment is a safeguarding issue even when there is capacity, and that advice or support should be sought from the safeguarding lead or social care as appropriate. The procedure also directs staff to alert the ED of the pending arrival with the risk and relevant history, and to inform family or carers with the patient's consent.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Immediate Management of Overdose / Self-Poisoning: Inpatient (no issue or review date shown on the document)

**Q373. [Hard]** _Scenario: You are recording oxygen saturations on a patient using the ward's finger pulse oximeter._ What does the Trust's Use of Pulse Oximetry Standard Operating Procedure (SOP) say about when to take the reading?
- A. Take the reading as soon as any number appears on the display
- **B. Take the reading only once the oximeter has been displaying a green light for at least 10 seconds [correct]**
- C. Take the reading after a full 60 seconds regardless of the display
- D. Take three readings and average them
_Why:_ The SOP states that readings should only be taken once the oximeter has been displaying a green light for at least 10 seconds, and warns that cold extremities, nail polish, nicotine stained fingers and dark skin pigmentation may interfere with the reading. It also requires staff to document the readings in the patient's electronic record.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - (Use of) Pulse Oximetry Standard Operating Procedure (SOP) (August 2025, issue 6, review August 2028)

**Q374. [Hard]** Under the Trust's Use of Pulse Oximetry Standard Operating Procedure (SOP), how often must planned preventative maintenance of pulse oximeters be undertaken, and by whom?
- A. Every three months, by the ward manager
- **B. Every six months, through Clinical Engineering [correct]**
- C. Annually, by the supplier
- D. Only when a fault is reported
_Why:_ The SOP states that regular checks on the equipment's functionality must be made and that planned preventative maintenance must be undertaken every six months through Clinical Engineering.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - (Use of) Pulse Oximetry Standard Operating Procedure (SOP) (August 2025, issue 6, review August 2028)

**Q375. [Easy]** _Scenario: A patient's condition changes acutely during your shift and you need to escalate to the on-call doctor._ Which structured communication tool does the Trust's Physical Care of Adult Inpatients policy tell ward teams to use for escalation, referrals and emergency situations?
- **A. SBARD (Situation, Background, Assessment, Recommendation, Decision) [correct]**
- B. SOAP (Subjective, Objective, Assessment, Plan)
- C. PINCH ME
- D. AVPU (Alert, Voice, Pain, Unresponsive)
_Why:_ The policy states that each ward should use the SBARD communication system for continuity of care and completion of outstanding tasks, and that communication of referrals and emergency situations is delivered using SBARD to ensure effective, structured communication of key information. AVPU is used within Enhanced Physical Care Monitoring to assess conscious level, and PINCH ME is used to identify likely causes of delirium.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Physical Care of Adult Inpatients Policy and Procedure (August 2025, issue 05, review April 2028)

**Q376. [Medium]** Under the Trust's Physical Care of Adult Inpatients policy, how often must ward nursing staff check the ward's physical health assessment equipment, such as the sphygmomanometer, oximeter and auroscope?
- **A. At least weekly, with a clear procedure for reporting equipment failure [correct]**
- B. At least monthly
- C. Only at the annual equipment audit
- D. Only when a member of staff reports a fault
_Why:_ The equipment section of the policy states there should be a clear procedure for nursing staff to check the equipment on at least a weekly basis, reporting equipment failure, with a budget for maintenance and replacement. It adds that resuscitation equipment is checked in accordance with the Resuscitation Policy, which sets separate daily and weekly frequencies.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Physical Care of Adult Inpatients Policy and Procedure (August 2025, issue 05, review April 2028)

## Trust Policy - Medicines Management

**Q377. [Easy]** Which trust group ratifies the Medicines Code Policy and holds responsibility for reviewing it?
- A. The Trust Board Quality Committee
- **B. The Medicines Management Committee [correct]**
- C. The Clinical Governance Department
- D. The Drugs and Therapeutics Panel
_Why:_ The front sheet of the Medicines Code Policy names the Medicines Management Committee as both the ratifying body and the group responsible for review. The policy is sponsored by the Medical Director and authored by the Deputy Chief Pharmacist for Medicine Safety and Governance.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - The Medicines Code Policy (March 2025, issue 01, review October 2026)

**Q378. [Medium]** The Medicines Code Policy is broken into named chapters. Which of the following has its own chapter in the trust's Medicines Code?
- A. Rapid Tranquillisation (RT)
- B. Seclusion review timings
- **C. Medicine Management in the 136 Suite [correct]**
- D. Ligature risk reduction
_Why:_ The Medicines Code contents list includes a dedicated chapter on Medicine Management in the 136 Suite, alongside chapters such as Security of medicine keys, Patients' Own Drugs (PODs), Self-administration of medicines and Controlled Drugs. Rapid tranquillisation and seclusion sit in separate trust policies.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - The Medicines Code Policy (March 2025, issue 01, review October 2026)

**Q379. [Easy]** _Scenario: A patient asks if they can start looking after their own tablets while on the ward._ Under the trust's Self-Administration of Medicines (SAM) policy, whose agreement is needed before any patient may self-administer their prescribed medicines?
- **A. The multidisciplinary team (MDT) [correct]**
- B. The nurse in charge of the shift
- C. The ward manager
- D. The ward pharmacist alone
_Why:_ The policy states plainly that patients may only self-administer their prescribed medicines following the agreement of the multidisciplinary team, and that the MDT decides the self-administration training level. The MDT discussion must include a registered pharmacy professional.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Self-Administration of Medicines Policy and Support Pack (August 2023, issue 06, review August 2026)

**Q380. [Easy]** Which items are excluded from being kept in a patient's own medicines locker on Self-Administration of Medicines (SAM) Level 3?
- A. Any medicine started within the last seven days
- B. All medicines prescribed more than twice a day
- **C. Controlled drugs, fridge items and injectables [correct]**
- D. All 'as required' (PRN) medicines without exception
_Why:_ The policy's exclusion criteria are fridge items, injectables, controlled drugs subject to legal storage requirements, and medication regimes which are not stable. Other medicines, including some PRNs, may also be excluded at the discretion of the nurse in charge, but that is a judgement rather than a blanket exclusion.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Self-Administration of Medicines Policy and Support Pack (August 2023, issue 06, review August 2026)

**Q381. [Medium]** _Scenario: You supervise a patient on Level 2 taking their morning doses._ How should that supervised dose be signed on the medicine card under the trust's Self-Administration of Medicines (SAM) policy?
- A. The nurse's initials only, as for any other dose
- **B. 'S2' alongside the supervising nurse's initials [correct]**
- C. 'S3' plus the patient's own signature
- D. The patient's initials only
_Why:_ The policy summary table sets out the signing convention by level: Level 1 the nurse signs their initials, Level 2 is signed 'S2' alongside the supervising nurse's initials, and Level 3 is signed 'S3'. On the electronic chart the self-administered tick box is also selected.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Self-Administration of Medicines Policy and Support Pack (August 2023, issue 06, review August 2026)

**Q382. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient using original packs of their medicines has been doing well on Level 2 and the team want to move them to Level 3._ What is the minimum period of Level 2 training the trust policy requires before a patient using original packs can progress to Level 3?
- A. 3 days
- **B. 7 days [correct]**
- C. 14 days
- D. 28 days
_Why:_ The Level 3 criteria state the patient must have undergone Level 2 training for at least 7 days where original packs are being used, or for at least 3 days if a compliance device is being used.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Self-Administration of Medicines Policy and Support Pack (August 2023, issue 06, review August 2026)

**Q383. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient has just started on Self-Administration of Medicines (SAM) Level 3 and holds their own locker key._ How often should the medication count sheet be completed when a patient first starts on Level 3?
- A. Once at the start and once at the end of the period
- B. Weekly for the first month
- C. Twice weekly indefinitely
- **D. Daily for at least the first week, then at a frequency set by the MDT [correct]**
_Why:_ The policy requires that when patients first start on Level 3, medication count checks are performed daily for at least the first week and thereafter at a frequency determined by the multidisciplinary team. The count is recorded on Assessment Sheet 3, the Medication Count Sheet.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Self-Administration of Medicines Policy and Support Pack (August 2023, issue 06, review August 2026)

**Q384. [Medium]** Which document must a Level 3 patient sign before they are issued with the key to their own medicines locker?
- **A. Form 5, the Key Holding Record [correct]**
- B. Form 2, the Multidisciplinary Screening Form
- C. Form 3, the Agreement Plan Level 2
- D. Assessment Sheet 3, the Medication Count Sheet
_Why:_ The Level 3 criteria state the patient will be issued with a key to their locker after signing Form 5, the Key Holding Record. Form 4, the Assessment Plan Level 3, is then used for the ongoing plan.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Self-Administration of Medicines Policy and Support Pack (August 2023, issue 06, review August 2026)

**Q385. [Hard]** _Scenario: The MDT has agreed the maximum quantity of medicines a Level 3 patient may hold at any one time._ Where does the trust policy say that maximum quantity must be documented?
- A. On a label attached to the front of the medicine trolley
- B. In the ward diary and handover sheet
- **C. In the 'Risk' node, in the summary section, on SystmOne [correct]**
- D. On the paper agreement plan only
_Why:_ The policy requires the MDT-assigned maximum supply quantity for Level 2 and 3 patients to be documented in the 'Risk' node summary section. Staff also check against this before sending any supply out with a patient on leave.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Self-Administration of Medicines Policy and Support Pack (August 2023, issue 06, review August 2026)

**Q386. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient on Level 3 is going home for two nights and taking their own supply with them._ Which record must be completed for medication taken out on leave and returned afterwards?
- A. Form 1, the Unit Environmental Policy Requirement
- **B. Assessment Sheet 4, the Leave and Discharge Medication Checklist [correct]**
- C. A fresh Form 2, the Multidisciplinary Screening Form
- D. Assessment Sheet 1, the Assessment Sheet Boxes and Bottles
_Why:_ The policy requires Assessment Sheet 4, the Leave and Discharge Medication Checklist, to be completed for medication taken out on leave and returned from leave. Completed sheets are then scanned and uploaded to the patient record under 'communications and letters' on SystmOne on return.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Self-Administration of Medicines Policy and Support Pack (August 2023, issue 06, review August 2026)

**Q387. [Medium]** _Scenario: A nurse reduced a patient from Level 3 to Level 2 last week after concerns about their ability to manage safely. The patient is now settled and asks to go back up._ What does the trust policy say about reinstating the previous self-administration level?
- A. The nurse who reduced the level may reinstate it once concerns have resolved
- B. Any nurse in charge may reinstate it at their discretion
- **C. The MDT must re-appraise the patient before agreeing to reinstate the previous level [correct]**
- D. Only the responsible clinician acting alone can reinstate it
_Why:_ The policy is explicit that where a nurse has reduced the level or stopped self-administration, the MDT is responsible for re-appraisal before agreeing to any request to reinstate at the previous level, and the nurse cannot act independently without first conferring with the MDT.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Self-Administration of Medicines Policy and Support Pack (August 2023, issue 06, review August 2026)

**Q388. [Easy]** _Scenario: A patient is admitted already taking sodium valproate for epilepsy and the ward cannot immediately find their Risk Acknowledgement Form._ What does the trust valproate policy say about stopping valproate in this situation?
- **A. Valproate should never be abruptly stopped, and if no longer appropriate it should be titrated down [correct]**
- B. Withhold all doses until the form is located
- C. Halve the dose until a specialist review takes place
- D. Stop it immediately and inform the GP
_Why:_ The policy states valproate should never be abruptly stopped in any patient, and that if it is no longer appropriate it should be titrated down to stop. Where the form cannot be obtained, all attempts must be made to get it, and if it cannot be obtained one must be completed by two specialists as a priority.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Valproate prescribing and monitoring policy (January 2024, issue 04, review January 2027)

**Q389. [Medium]** Where does the trust require a completed valproate Risk Acknowledgement Form to be filed so that it can be found and reported on?
- A. Scanned into the paper drug chart folder in the clinic room
- B. Emailed to the ward manager and stored in the ward's shared drive
- **C. Uploaded under sodium valproate in communications and letters on SystmOne [correct]**
- D. Attached to the physical health assessment section on SystmOne
_Why:_ The policy repeatedly states that the Risk Acknowledgement Form must be uploaded to the patient's electronic care record and recorded under sodium valproate in communications and letters on SystmOne, specifically to enable accurate trust reporting and easy access for all healthcare professionals.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Valproate prescribing and monitoring policy (January 2024, issue 04, review January 2027)

**Q390. [Medium]** _Scenario: A non-medical prescriber (NMP) is asked to write up the continuation of a patient's valproate._ Under the trust policy, when may a non-specialist prescriber such as an NMP or Advanced Clinical Practitioner (ACP) prescribe a continuation of valproate?
- **A. Only where a completed and in-date Risk Acknowledgement Form exists and can be located [correct]**
- B. At any time, as continuation prescriptions are exempt
- C. Only if the patient is male and over 55
- D. Only with verbal agreement from the ward pharmacist
_Why:_ The policy allows non-specialist prescribers, including NMPs and ACPs, to prescribe continuation prescriptions only where a completed and in-date Risk Acknowledgement Form exists and can be located. Where the form is not in place or not annually updated, prescribing must be referred back to the specialist.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Valproate prescribing and monitoring policy (January 2024, issue 04, review January 2027)

**Q391. [Hard]** _Scenario: Valproate is being initiated for a patient under 55 and two specialist signatories are required._ How does the trust policy define the required independence of the second signatory?
- A. They must work in a different trust
- **B. They must not be in direct line management of the primary signatory [correct]**
- C. They must be from a different professional discipline
- D. They must not have met the patient before
_Why:_ The policy adopts the Commission on Human Medicines definition, stating the second signatory must be independent of the first and that this means not being in direct line management of the primary signatory. Both signatories' details are recorded on the Risk Acknowledgement Form.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Valproate prescribing and monitoring policy (January 2024, issue 04, review January 2027)

**Q392. [Hard]** _Scenario: A woman under 55 taking valproate has repeatedly missed her appointments for the annual review and Risk Acknowledgement Form update._ After how many Did Not Attend (DNA) episodes does the trust policy say a multidisciplinary team (MDT) discussion should take place?
- A. After 1 DNA
- B. After 2 DNAs
- **C. After 3 DNAs [correct]**
- D. After 6 DNAs
_Why:_ The policy states an MDT discussion should occur after 3 DNAs, but may be sooner where there is clinical risk. The MDT must consider risk to any unborn child, the patient's capacity to understand the risks and possible safeguarding involvement, document the decision, and send the patient a copy of the MHRA valproate booklet.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Valproate prescribing and monitoring policy (January 2024, issue 04, review January 2027)

**Q393. [Hard]** _Scenario: A consultant wants to start valproate for a patient whose diagnosis is neither bipolar disorder nor schizoaffective disorder._ What does the trust policy require before that prescription can start?
- A. Nothing extra, provided a Risk Acknowledgement Form is completed
- B. A second opinion from the ward pharmacist only
- **C. Comprehensive peer review and submission to the Medicine Management Committee, using the specific valproate unlicensed form, before initiation [correct]**
- D. Approval by the Responsible Clinician and the patient's GP
_Why:_ The policy states that all new valproate prescriptions not for a bipolar or schizoaffective diagnosis need comprehensive peer review and submission to the Medicine Management Committee for consideration prior to initiation, using a specific valproate unlicensed form.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Valproate prescribing and monitoring policy (January 2024, issue 04, review January 2027)

**Q394. [Easy]** Which brand of clozapine, and which associated blood monitoring service, does the trust's point of care testing policy identify as the product of choice in Derbyshire?
- A. Clozaril, monitored through the Clozaril Patient Monitoring Service
- B. Denzapine, monitored through the Denzapine Monitoring Service
- **C. Zaponex, monitored through the Zaponex Treatment Access System (ZTAS) [correct]**
- D. Generic clozapine, monitored through the local hospital laboratory
_Why:_ The policy states that in Derbyshire the clozapine product of choice is the Zaponex brand, and that ZTAS is the haematological monitoring service associated with that brand. The point of care analyser used is the Sysmex pocH-100i.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Policy for use of Point of Care Haematological Analysis (PocHI) in DHCFT approved Clozapine Clinics (January 2024, issue 03, review January 2027)

**Q395. [Easy]** _Scenario: A patient's point of care blood result at a clozapine clinic comes back RED._ What does the trust's Standard Operating Procedure (SOP) require?
- **A. No further medication is issued, and ZTAS is contacted directly before the patient leaves the clinic [correct]**
- B. Issue one week of clozapine and arrange a repeat test in seven days
- C. Issue the medication and ask the patient's GP to review
- D. Halve the dose and repeat the test the following week
_Why:_ On a red result the patient must not be issued with any more medication, and ZTAS must be contacted directly before the patient is allowed to leave the clinic. The patient is told to take no further clozapine doses, a repeat Full Blood Count is arranged, remaining supplies are collected back, and the sample is sent to hospital haematology for independent confirmation.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Policy for use of Point of Care Haematological Analysis (PocHI) in DHCFT approved Clozapine Clinics (January 2024, issue 03, review January 2027)

**Q396. [Easy]** _Scenario: A patient's point of care result at a clozapine clinic comes back AMBER._ Under the trust SOP, can clozapine be supplied on an amber result?
- A. No, amber is treated the same as red
- **B. Yes, medication is supplied and a repeat test date is arranged with ZTAS [correct]**
- C. Only if a doctor attends the clinic in person to authorise it
- D. Only a single dose may be given under direct supervision
_Why:_ The SOP states that if a patient is green or amber a supply of clozapine can be made. On amber the result is recorded on the clinic patient list, ZTAS is contacted immediately to check when the next sample is required, and a repeat appointment is arranged and recorded.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Policy for use of Point of Care Haematological Analysis (PocHI) in DHCFT approved Clozapine Clinics (January 2024, issue 03, review January 2027)

**Q397. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient is waiting for their point of care blood result at the clozapine clinic._ Which observations does the trust SOP say should be taken while awaiting the result, and where are they recorded?
- A. Weight and blood glucose, recorded in the tabbed journal
- **B. Blood pressure, pulse, temperature and respiratory rate, recorded in the DHCFT Physical Health Assessment on SystmOne [correct]**
- C. Blood pressure and pulse only, recorded on the clinic patient list
- D. Oxygen saturations and a full NEWS2 score, recorded on the ZTAS website
_Why:_ The SOP lists blood pressure, pulse, temperature and respiratory rate, recorded in SystmOne in the DHCFT Physical Health Assessment. Separately, an assessment of side effects, constipation and the patient's mental health is documented in the SystmOne tabbed journal.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Policy for use of Point of Care Haematological Analysis (PocHI) in DHCFT approved Clozapine Clinics (January 2024, issue 03, review January 2027)

**Q398. [Hard]** _Scenario: At a clozapine clinic appointment, a patient tells you they have not taken any clozapine for the last three days._ What does the trust SOP say the threshold for withholding clozapine is?
- A. Clozapine is withheld if more than 24 hours have been missed consecutively
- **B. Clozapine is withheld if more than 48 hours have been missed consecutively, and a prescriber or pharmacy contacted to discuss [correct]**
- C. Clozapine is withheld only if more than one week has been missed
- D. Clozapine can be issued as normal provided the blood result is green
_Why:_ The SOP directs staff to ask about compliance before supplying clozapine, and states that if more than 48 hours have been missed consecutively then clozapine should be withheld and a prescriber or pharmacy contacted to discuss. Restarting after a break needs re-titration.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Policy for use of Point of Care Haematological Analysis (PocHI) in DHCFT approved Clozapine Clinics (January 2024, issue 03, review January 2027)

**Q399. [Hard]** _Scenario: The clozapine clinic has finished for the day and there are analysed blood samples in the fridge._ How long does the trust SOP say analysed blood samples should be kept in the point of care blood sample fridge before disposal?
- A. Until the end of the clinic session on the same day
- **B. 24 hours after the clinic [correct]**
- C. 72 hours after the clinic
- D. Seven days after the clinic
_Why:_ The SOP states analysed blood samples should be stored in the point of care blood sample fridge for 24 hours after the clinic, then disposed of in line with the Trust Waste Management and Infection Control policies. That fridge is used solely for those samples and has daily temperature checks.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Policy for use of Point of Care Haematological Analysis (PocHI) in DHCFT approved Clozapine Clinics (January 2024, issue 03, review January 2027)

**Q400. [Easy]** _Scenario: A service user is due their olanzapine long acting injection._ For how long must they be observed by a qualified clinical member of staff after the injection under trust policy?
- A. One hour
- B. Two hours
- **C. Three hours [correct]**
- D. Six hours
_Why:_ The policy requires observation in a healthcare facility by a qualified clinical member of staff for three hours after the injection, watching for signs of olanzapine overdose. After three hours a final assessment confirms the patient is alert, orientated and free of signs of overdose before they leave.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Olanzapine Long-Acting Injection Prescribing and Administration Policy (July 2025, issue 05, review June 2028)

**Q401. [Hard]** _Scenario: You are the nurse monitoring a service user after their olanzapine long acting injection._ What monitoring times does the trust policy suggest during the post-injection period?
- A. Every 15 minutes throughout the three hours
- **B. 30 minutes, 60 minutes, 90 minutes and 3 hours post-dose [correct]**
- C. Hourly at 1, 2 and 3 hours only
- D. Once at the start and once at the end of the three hours
_Why:_ The policy summary gives suggested monitoring times of 30 minutes, 60 minutes, 90 minutes and 3 hours post-dose. Any symptoms suggesting Post-Injection Syndrome (PIS) must be recorded at each interval, and the record must also state where no symptoms were seen.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Olanzapine Long-Acting Injection Prescribing and Administration Policy (July 2025, issue 05, review June 2028)

**Q402. [Medium]** _Scenario: You are preparing an olanzapine long acting injection and have worked out the diluent volume and the volume to inject._ What does the trust policy require at this point?
- **A. The calculation must be checked by a second qualified person [correct]**
- B. The calculation must be checked by the ward pharmacist by telephone
- C. No check is needed if you have completed the Zypadhera training
- D. The prescriber must witness the whole preparation
_Why:_ The policy states that calculations of the volume of diluent and the volume to administer are to be checked by a second qualified person. It also requires the vial to be fully reconstituted before drawing up, and the plunger to be drawn back for five seconds to check for blood before injecting.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Olanzapine Long-Acting Injection Prescribing and Administration Policy (July 2025, issue 05, review June 2028)

**Q403. [Hard]** _Scenario: A community patient is being considered for olanzapine long acting injection._ According to the trust policy, where is the only suitable location for community patients to receive olanzapine long acting injection?
- A. Any Derbyshire depot clinic with resuscitation facilities
- **B. The Olanzapine clinic based at the Radbourne Unit in Derby [correct]**
- C. The Hartington Unit in Chesterfield
- D. Their local GP practice with a nurse in attendance
_Why:_ The policy states that at present, within the trust, the only location suitable for community patients to receive olanzapine long acting injection is the Olanzapine clinic based at the Radbourne Unit in Derby. Referral must be made to that clinic, who review and approve the patient's suitability.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Olanzapine Long-Acting Injection Prescribing and Administration Policy (July 2025, issue 05, review June 2028)

**Q404. [Medium]** _Scenario: Ninety minutes after an olanzapine long acting injection, a service user is markedly sedated, ataxic and slurring their speech._ What does the trust policy direct you to do?
- A. Extend the observation period by a further three hours and reassess
- B. Give oral fluids, lie the patient flat and continue observing to three hours
- **C. Contact the duty doctor urgently, arrange transfer to the Emergency Department as an accidental olanzapine overdose, record physical observations, and send the olanzapine long acting patient information leaflet with the service user [correct]**
- D. Contact ZTAS and arrange an urgent Full Blood Count
_Why:_ The policy separates a suspected but uncertain post-injection syndrome, where symptoms are very mild and the ward doctor is called urgently, from a definite post-injection syndrome with more marked symptoms. For the latter, contact the duty doctor urgently, transfer to the Emergency Department as an accidental olanzapine overdose, record physical observations, and make sure the product literature or patient information leaflet goes with the service user.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Olanzapine Long-Acting Injection Prescribing and Administration Policy (July 2025, issue 05, review June 2028)

**Q405. [Easy]** _Scenario: A 17 year old inpatient with a history of opioid use is being discharged._ Under the trust's inpatient take-home Prenoxad (naloxone injection) guidance, can they be supplied with take-home Prenoxad?
- **A. No, service users under 18 years of age are excluded from the Patient Group Direction (PGD) [correct]**
- B. Yes, provided a parent also completes the training
- C. Yes, with the agreement of the responsible clinician
- D. Yes, but only a half-strength supply
_Why:_ The eligibility criteria are inpatients aged 18 years and over in adult services, and being under 18 is listed as an exclusion criterion in both the guidance and the Patient Group Direction. Other exclusions are no valid consent, refusal to complete the training, not meeting the PGD inclusion criteria, and known hypersensitivity to naloxone.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Guidance for Psychiatric Inpatient settings on the provision of Take-Home Prenoxad (naloxone injection) (February 2024, issue 1, review October 2026)

**Q406. [Hard]** _Scenario: Your ward has just started stocking take-home Prenoxad packs._ Where does the trust protocol say Prenoxad should be stored on the ward?
- A. In the controlled drugs cupboard
- B. In the medicines fridge
- **C. In the patients' Own Drugs (POD) cupboard [correct]**
- D. In the emergency resuscitation grab bag
_Why:_ The protocol states Prenoxad will be stored in the patients' Own Drugs (POD) cupboard. The Ward Manager and leadership team are responsible for making sure all clinical staff know the agreed location, and new staff must be told during induction. Pharmacy technicians check expiry dates and re-order stock.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Guidance for Psychiatric Inpatient settings on the provision of Take-Home Prenoxad (naloxone injection) (February 2024, issue 1, review October 2026)

**Q407. [Hard]** _Scenario: You are teaching a patient how to use their take-home Prenoxad pre-filled syringe._ How many doses is the Prenoxad pre-filled syringe marked out into, according to the trust protocol?
- A. Two doses of 1mg
- **B. Five doses of 0.4mg [correct]**
- C. Four doses of 0.5mg
- D. One single 2mg dose
_Why:_ The protocol states each pack contains one naloxone injection 1mg/ml as a 2ml pre-filled syringe, and that each 2ml syringe is marked out with 5 doses of 0.4mg, 0.4mg being the minimum effective dose to reverse the effects of opioid overdose. The pack also holds two 23G needles and a product instruction sheet.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Guidance for Psychiatric Inpatient settings on the provision of Take-Home Prenoxad (naloxone injection) (February 2024, issue 1, review October 2026)

**Q408. [Medium]** _Scenario: A registered nurse has read the Patient Group Direction (PGD) and watched the Prenoxad administration video._ What happens next before they can supply take-home Prenoxad under the trust protocol?
- A. Nothing further, watching the video is sufficient authorisation
- B. They email pharmacy to be added to a trust-wide database
- **C. The leadership team adds their name to the Register, both they and a leadership team member sign it, and the list of authorised staff is kept in the PGD folder in the clinic room [correct]**
- D. They complete a Datix to record their new competency
_Why:_ The protocol requires the leadership team to add the staff member's name to the Register authorising them to supply take-home Prenoxad under the PGD, with both the staff member and a leadership team member signing, and the list of all authorised staff kept in the PGD folder in the clinic room. Annual updates in resuscitation and overdose management are also required.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Guidance for Psychiatric Inpatient settings on the provision of Take-Home Prenoxad (naloxone injection) (February 2024, issue 1, review October 2026)

**Q409. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient returns from leave and hands back their unused Prenoxad pack._ What does the trust protocol say should happen to it?
- **A. It should be stored with the patient's own medication in the trolley [correct]**
- B. It should be returned to pharmacy for destruction
- C. It should be placed back in the ward stock for another patient
- D. It should be disposed of in the sharps bin regardless of whether it was used
_Why:_ The protocol states that when Prenoxad is returned by the patient on their return from leave, if it has not been used it should be stored in the patient's own medication in the trolley, and if it has been used it should be safely disposed of in the sharps bin. The supply, including batch number and expiry date, is recorded in session notes on SystmOne.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Guidance for Psychiatric Inpatient settings on the provision of Take-Home Prenoxad (naloxone injection) (February 2024, issue 1, review October 2026)

**Q410. [Easy]** _Scenario: You are administering a controlled drug on the electronic Prescribing and Medicines Administration (ePMA) system on SystmOne._ What does the trust's inpatient controlled drugs workflow require after you select 'Fully Administer Now'?
- A. A paper controlled drugs register entry only, with two wet signatures
- **B. A Medication Countersigning box opens, you select the countersigner's name, and the countersigner inputs their own PIN [correct]**
- C. The system automatically countersigns using the ward's shared login
- D. A task is sent to pharmacy who countersign remotely
_Why:_ The trust's inpatient controlled drugs process map shows that after administering, the Medication Countersigning box opens, the countersigner's name is selected, and the countersigner enters their own PIN before the record is saved. Administration notes are used to record circumstances such as a student nurse administering under supervision.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Inpatient Controlled Drugs Workflow (ePMA process maps) (May 2023, version 3, draft 'To Be' process for DHCFT inpatient units)

**Q411. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient on a controlled drug is going out on planned leave and needs a supply to take with them._ According to the trust's inpatient controlled drugs workflow, how is the controlled drug re-prescribed and ordered?
- A. Use the 'leave' re-prescribe option, and pharmacy dispense straight from the electronic record with no paper form
- **B. Use 're-prescribe as TTO' rather than the leave option, then generate the Inpatient Controlled Drug Request, which produces a Controlled Drugs Prescription Form for the prescriber to sign by hand and hand to a pharmacy colleague [correct]**
- C. Hand-write a paper FP10 and post it to pharmacy
- D. Ask pharmacy to supply against the existing inpatient prescription without any new form
_Why:_ The trust process is to right click the controlled drug and select re-prescribe as TTO rather than the leave option, entering the continuation reason of Leave or Discharge, then order via the Inpatient Controlled Drug Request under Letters and Resources, which generates a Controlled Drugs Prescription Form. That form must be signed by the prescriber and handed to a pharmacy colleague, because a wet signature is legally required for a controlled drug prescription.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Inpatient Controlled Drugs Workflow (ePMA process maps) (May 2023, version 3, draft 'To Be' process for DHCFT inpatient units)

## Trust Policy - Infection Prevention & Control

**Q412. [Easy]** _Scenario: Two patients in the same bay develop diarrhoea within a day of each other._ Under the trust's Managing an Outbreak of Infection policy, what is the definition of an outbreak?
- **A. Two or more cases linked by time, place and person [correct]**
- B. Any single case of diarrhoea on an inpatient ward
- C. Five or more laboratory confirmed cases in one unit
- D. Three or more cases in a single bay within one week
_Why:_ The trust policy defines an outbreak as two or more cases linked by time, place and person, indicating a transmissible agent is affecting service users and/or staff.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Managing an Outbreak of Infection (including Diarrhoea and Vomiting) Policy and Procedure (July 2025, issue 08, review July 2028)

**Q413. [Hard]** The trust uses the term 'period of increased incidence' (PII) when a clinical team reports one or more cases of a possible infection. At what point does that become a declared outbreak?
- A. As soon as any single case of possible infection is reported
- **B. When two or more cases are linked, the situation has been assessed and verified by the Infection Control Team and confirmed by the Director of Infection Prevention and Control (DIPC) or their nominated deputy [correct]**
- C. When the ward has been closed to admissions for 48 hours
- D. When the Consultant Microbiologist confirms the organism on culture
_Why:_ The policy states a PII is not considered an outbreak until there is a significant link between cases (time, place, person), there have been two or more linked cases, and the situation has been assessed and verified by the Infection Control Team and confirmed by the DIPC or nominated deputy.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Managing an Outbreak of Infection (including Diarrhoea and Vomiting) Policy and Procedure (July 2025, issue 08, review July 2028)

**Q414. [Medium]** _Scenario: You are the nurse in charge on a night shift during a declared outbreak of diarrhoea and vomiting._ How often must the Outbreak Log Sheet and the Outbreak Checklist be completed, and whose responsibility is this?
- A. Log sheet weekly and checklist monthly, completed by the ward administrator
- B. Both completed once at the start of the outbreak by the Infection Control Team
- **C. The log sheet is updated every shift and the checklist completed daily, and this is the responsibility of the nurse in charge, although the tasks can be appropriately delegated [correct]**
- D. Log sheet daily and checklist only at the end of the outbreak, completed by the Matron
_Why:_ The policy makes the nurse in charge responsible for accurate completion of the Outbreak Log Sheet on a shift by shift basis and the Outbreak Checklist daily, with the forms supplied by the Infection Control Team. These tasks can be appropriately delegated.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Managing an Outbreak of Infection (including Diarrhoea and Vomiting) Policy and Procedure (July 2025, issue 08, review July 2028)

**Q415. [Hard]** _Scenario: It is 11pm on a Saturday and the Infection Control Team advises that the ward may need to close to admissions._ Under the trust's outbreak policy, who has the authority to close a ward to admissions out of hours?
- A. The nurse in charge of the affected ward
- B. The bleep holder, who acts as the Divisional Nurse
- **C. The 2nd on call manager, who acts as the Director of Infection Prevention and Control (DIPC) until the return of normal business hours [correct]**
- D. The Infection Control Team on call
_Why:_ Out of hours the 2nd on call manager acts as DIPC and has the authority to close a ward if required. The decision must be kept under daily review and notified to the Chief Executive Officer and the DIPC when normal business hours resume.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Managing an Outbreak of Infection (including Diarrhoea and Vomiting) Policy and Procedure (July 2025, issue 08, review July 2028)

**Q416. [Hard]** In an outbreak of diarrhoea and vomiting, when does the trust's policy say the terminal clean should be booked and approved?
- **A. Pre-booked once the ward has been 24 hours free of new cases, with final approval given at 48 hours free of new cases [correct]**
- B. Booked as soon as the first case is reported
- C. Booked only after seven days free of new cases
- D. Booked when the last symptomatic patient is discharged
_Why:_ The policy says the terminal clean will be pre-booked at the 24 hours free stage, with final approval at 48 hours free of new cases. The whole ward is terminally cleaned, including shampoo or steam cleaning of soft furnishings and laundering of curtains, before admissions recommence.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Managing an Outbreak of Infection (including Diarrhoea and Vomiting) Policy and Procedure (July 2025, issue 08, review July 2028)

**Q417. [Medium]** How must an outbreak of infection be recorded on the trust's incident reporting system?
- A. No incident report is needed because the Infection Control Team keeps its own chronology
- **B. On a Trust Incident Report via the DATIX system, graded as a 'red' incident [correct]**
- C. By email to the Director of Infection Prevention and Control (DIPC) only
- D. On a DATIX report graded 'amber', with a separate report naming each affected patient
_Why:_ The policy requires any outbreak to be detailed on a Trust Incident Reporting form via DATIX and graded as a 'red' incident. It may be completed as an overall situation report rather than naming individual patients, or using initials.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Managing an Outbreak of Infection (including Diarrhoea and Vomiting) Policy and Procedure (July 2025, issue 08, review July 2028)

**Q418. [Medium]** _Scenario: The ward is in a declared outbreak and the linen skip needs emptying._ How must used linen from the affected ward be sent to the laundry during the outbreak period?
- A. In a standard white linen bag, as normal
- B. In an orange clinical waste bag
- C. In a clear plastic bag placed inside a black bag
- **D. As infected or contaminated linen, in a tied dissolvo (water soluble) sack which is then tied inside a red bag [correct]**
_Why:_ During an outbreak the policy requires all used linen to be sent as infected or contaminated, in a tied dissolvo sack and then tied in a red bag, because of the potential for spread during onward transit.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Managing an Outbreak of Infection (including Diarrhoea and Vomiting) Policy and Procedure (July 2025, issue 08, review July 2028)

**Q419. [Easy]** _Scenario: A patient has passed two type 7 stools in the last 12 hours._ The trust's Clostridioides difficile (C. diff) policy asks ward staff to use the SIGHT mnemonic. What does SIGHT stand for?
- **A. Suspect, Isolate, Gloves and aprons, Handwashing, Test samples [correct]**
- B. Screen, Isolate, Gel, Hydrate, Treat
- C. Suspect, Inform, Gown, Hydrate, Transfer
- D. Sample, Isolate, GP inform, Hand gel, Treat
_Why:_ The policy lists SIGHT (Suspect, Isolate, Gloves and aprons, Handwashing and Test samples) as a responsibility of clinical ward staff, and specifically says to avoid the use of alcohol hand gel.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Clostridiodes Difficile (C. Diff) Policy and Procedure (July 2023, issue 07, review May 2026)
_⚠ review date passed_

**Q420. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient's Clostridioides difficile (C. diff) symptoms have settled and the team is discussing stopping the stool chart._ How long does the trust's C. diff policy say stool charts must continue for a patient with confirmed infection?
- A. Until the first negative repeat sample is received
- B. For 72 hours after the diarrhoea stops
- **C. Every day until the patient has had 14 days of normal (for them) bowel activity [correct]**
- D. Only for the duration of the antibiotic course
_Why:_ The policy states patients with C. diff infection must have stool charts completed every day until they have had 14 days of normal (for them) bowel activity, and staff must remain vigilant for relapse.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Clostridiodes Difficile (C. Diff) Policy and Procedure (July 2023, issue 07, review May 2026)
_⚠ review date passed_

**Q421. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient with diarrhoea has been using a dedicated commode in their side room._ Under the trust's C. diff policy, how long are separate toilet or commode facilities required?
- A. Until the stool sample result comes back
- B. Until the patient has been symptom free for 24 hours
- **C. Until the patient has been symptom free for 72 hours and has passed a formed stool (not type 6 or 7) [correct]**
- D. For the whole of the admission once C. diff has ever been diagnosed
_Why:_ The policy requires separate toilet or commode facilities until the patient is symptom free for 72 hours and a formed stool has been passed. Commodes must be cleaned after each use with a sporicidal agent.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Clostridiodes Difficile (C. Diff) Policy and Procedure (July 2023, issue 07, review May 2026)
_⚠ review date passed_

**Q422. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient with a positive C. diff toxin result is still symptomatic five days into treatment and a colleague asks whether to send another sample._ What does the trust's C. diff policy say about repeat stool samples?
- A. Send a repeat sample every 48 hours until it is negative
- B. Send a repeat sample weekly until the patient is discharged
- C. Send a repeat sample only if the patient deteriorates clinically
- **D. Do not send repeat samples unless specifically requested by the Infection Control Team (ICT), and do not retest toxin positive cases within 28 days while symptoms persist [correct]**
_Why:_ The policy states repeat samples should not be sent unless specifically requested by the ICT, and toxin positive cases should not be retested within 28 days unless symptoms resolve and then recur and recurrent infection needs confirming.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Clostridiodes Difficile (C. Diff) Policy and Procedure (July 2023, issue 07, review May 2026)
_⚠ review date passed_

**Q423. [Medium]** How does the trust's C. diff policy define 'resolved' and 'recurrence'?
- A. Resolved means one formed stool has been passed; recurrence means any diarrhoea within 7 days
- **B. Resolved means the patient has normal formed stools for 72 hours; recurrence means becoming symptomatic again within 28 days of the diarrhoea having resolved [correct]**
- C. Resolved means a negative toxin test; recurrence means any second positive test at any point
- D. Resolved means 48 hours symptom free; recurrence means symptoms returning within 14 days
_Why:_ The definitions section of the policy sets 'resolved' as normal formed stools for 72 hours and 'reoccurrence' as becoming symptomatic again within 28 days of the diarrhoea having resolved.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Clostridiodes Difficile (C. Diff) Policy and Procedure (July 2023, issue 07, review May 2026)
_⚠ review date passed_

**Q424. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient is admitted to the ward directly from a nursing home._ Under the trust's MRSA policy, which patients are screened for Methicillin Resistant Staphylococcus aureus (MRSA)?
- A. Every inpatient, on admission and then weekly
- B. Only patients who have an open wound
- C. No one, because mental health services are classed as low risk
- **D. Defined groups only, screened on admission: those admitted directly from another healthcare provider, those with a known MRSA history, those with a proven history of intravenous drug use, those with an open wound or indwelling device, and any patient with MRSA found on another clinical specimen [correct]**
_Why:_ Routine admission screening is not recommended in mental health settings, but the trust has defined a limited list of risk groups who are screened, and screening takes place on admission only.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - MRSA Policy and Procedure (August 2025, issue 08, review August 2028)

**Q425. [Medium]** When should an MRSA screen ideally be taken after admission, and how long do results usually take?
- **A. Ideally within 48 hours of admission, with results generally available at around 48 hours [correct]**
- B. Within 7 days of admission, with results in 24 hours
- C. At the pre-discharge review, with results in one week
- D. Within 2 hours of admission, with results the same day
_Why:_ The policy says the screen should ideally be taken within 48 hours of admission (clinical judgement prevails, and any delay must be documented and reviewed by the multidisciplinary team), and patients should be told results are available to clinical staff in approximately 48 hours.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - MRSA Policy and Procedure (August 2025, issue 08, review August 2028)

**Q426. [Hard]** _Scenario: An inpatient's admission screen comes back MRSA positive._ What decolonisation regime does the trust's MRSA policy describe?
- A. A single dose of oral antibiotic prescribed by the Consultant Microbiologist
- **B. A 5 day course of Octenisan body wash plus Mupirocin 2% applied to each anterior nare three times a day, with the hair washed with the Octenisan twice within the 5 days [correct]**
- C. A 10 day course of chlorhexidine body wash alone
- D. A 5 day course of nasal Mupirocin only, with no body wash
_Why:_ The policy specifies a 5 day course of Octenisan body wash with Mupirocin 2% to each anterior nare three times daily, and the hair washed with Octenisan twice within the 5 days because the scalp can be heavily colonised.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - MRSA Policy and Procedure (August 2025, issue 08, review August 2028)

**Q427. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient has just finished the 5 day decolonisation course._ What does the trust's MRSA policy say happens next?
- A. Re-screen immediately at the end of treatment, and one negative screen is enough
- B. No re-screening is needed unless the patient becomes unwell
- **C. Stop treatment for two days, then re-screen, with a second screen a further 24 hours later; two negative screens are needed to class the patient as negative [correct]**
- D. Re-screen one week later and automatically repeat the treatment if positive
