_Why:_ The trust's minimum standards table rates overnight mixing of sexes in a bay as Red, stating it should be a 'never event' and must not occur. Amber is reserved for things like a temporary single en-suite room in the opposite-gender area during a clinical emergency.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Privacy and Dignity (Including Delivering Same Sex Accommodation Guidance) Policy and Procedures (May 2026, issue 7, review May 2029)

**Q512. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient has been placed in a bed bay of the opposite sex overnight and the decision was not based on clinical need._ What does the trust's Privacy and Dignity policy require after a Red-rated same sex accommodation breach?
- A. An incident (breach) report only, closed at ward level
- **B. An incident (breach) report plus a Root Cause Analysis and an action plan [correct]**
- C. A verbal handover to the matron and an entry in the ward diary
- D. No report unless the patient or a relative makes a complaint
_Why:_ For Red-rated breaches the policy requires an incident report indicating a breach, a Root Cause Analysis and a development of an action plan, plus a plan to move the patient out of the mixed sex environment at the soonest opportunity.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Privacy and Dignity (Including Delivering Same Sex Accommodation Guidance) Policy and Procedures (May 2026, issue 7, review May 2029)

**Q513. [Hard]** _Scenario: An incident report has been submitted flagging a possible Amber same sex accommodation (SSA) breach, and the ward team are unsure whether it will count as a breach._ Under the trust's SSA Breach Reporting Guidance, who confirms whether an Amber event is a breach?
- A. The ward manager, in the next ward governance meeting
- **B. The Head of Patient Experience (the Delivering Same Sex Accommodation Lead), who then informs the Contracting Department [correct]**
- C. The Care Quality Commission (CQC), at the next inspection
- D. The Mental Health Act (MHA) office
_Why:_ The Amber key in the policy states that a decision about whether a possible breach is a breach will be confirmed by the Head of Patient Experience (the Delivering Same Sex Accommodation Lead), who will inform the Contracting Department.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Privacy and Dignity (Including Delivering Same Sex Accommodation Guidance) Policy and Procedures (May 2026, issue 7, review May 2029)

**Q514. [Hard]** Which of the following does the trust's policy list as an acceptable justification, meaning it is NOT counted as a same sex accommodation breach?
- A. The ward is short staffed, so patients have been cohorted differently for the night
- B. The skill mix on the late shift did not allow the usual bed layout to be maintained
- **C. An acutely ill or disturbed patient requires constant one-to-one nursing care, for example in seclusion [correct]**
- D. The patient said they did not mind being in the opposite sex bay
_Why:_ Acceptable justifications listed are a life-threatening emergency, an acutely ill or disturbed patient needing constant one-to-one nursing care such as seclusion, and situations where a nurse must be physically present at all times. The policy states explicitly that staff shortages or skill mix are not acceptable as the rationale.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Privacy and Dignity (Including Delivering Same Sex Accommodation Guidance) Policy and Procedures (May 2026, issue 7, review May 2029)

**Q515. [Medium]** _Scenario: On a mixed ward, a patient has to walk through the opposite sex bed area to reach the bathroom._ How does the trust's policy define this situation?
- A. Acceptable, as long as the patient is escorted by a staff member of their own sex
- **B. A same sex accommodation breach, because accessing bathing or toileting facilities requires the patient to pass through (not by) a bed area of the opposite sex [correct]**
- C. Acceptable, because only sleeping accommodation counts
- D. A breach only if it happens more than once in a 24 hour period
_Why:_ The definition of a breach in Appendix 1 includes where accessing bathing or toileting facilities requires a patient to pass through (not by) a bed area of the opposite sex. The same sex accommodation definition also states no one should have to pass through rooms occupied by the opposite sex to reach their toilet and washing facilities.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Privacy and Dignity (Including Delivering Same Sex Accommodation Guidance) Policy and Procedures (May 2026, issue 7, review May 2029)

**Q516. [Hard]** _Scenario: The Multidisciplinary Team (MDT) decides that one particular visitor's contact is anti-therapeutic and agrees to restrict their visits._ Under the Adult Inpatient Visiting Policy, how much notice should be given of a restriction made on clinical grounds when possible?
- **A. 24 hours [correct]**
- B. 48 hours
- C. 7 days
- D. No notice is expected, the restriction takes effect immediately
_Why:_ The policy says the reason for a restriction on clinical grounds should be recorded and explained to both the patient and the visitor, orally and in writing, and that when possible 24 hours' notice should be given of the decision.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Adult Inpatient Visiting Policy and Procedure (May 2026, issue 3, review November 2028)

**Q517. [Medium]** _Scenario: A visitor has been excluded from the ward after repeatedly attempting to bring illicit substances in._ Besides documenting the reasons and explaining them to the patient orally and in writing, what does the trust's visiting policy require?
- A. A safeguarding referral in every case
- **B. A Datix incident report is completed [correct]**
- C. A letter from the Chief Executive to the visitor
- D. Nothing further, provided the ward round notes are updated
_Why:_ The policy states that any decision to exclude a visitor must be fully documented with reasons, made available for independent scrutiny by regulators such as the Care Quality Commission (CQC), and that a Datix should be completed.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Adult Inpatient Visiting Policy and Procedure (May 2026, issue 3, review November 2028)

**Q518. [Hard]** _Scenario: A norovirus outbreak on the unit means the ward is considering closing to visitors for several days._ Under the Adult Inpatient Visiting Policy, who must agree infection prevention restrictions on visiting?
- A. The nurse in charge of the affected ward
- B. The Multidisciplinary Team (MDT) at the next ward round
- **C. The Director of Infection Control or their nominated deputy, with the restrictions reviewed by the Infection Control Team [correct]**
- D. The Mental Health Act (MHA) Committee
_Why:_ The policy states restrictions of this kind will be reviewed by the Infection Control Team and agreed by the Director of Infection Control or their nominated deputy, so there is clear support, governance and accountability for the decision. They must be time limited and reviewed in line with infection prevention control guidelines.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Adult Inpatient Visiting Policy and Procedure (May 2026, issue 3, review November 2028)

**Q519. [Easy]** _Scenario: A patient asks whether they can see their visitor somewhere other than the busy communal visiting room._ What does the trust's visiting policy say about where visits can take place?
- A. Visits must always take place in the designated visitors' room
- **B. Where possible patients should be able to see visitors in private, including in their own bedroom if the patient wishes [correct]**
- C. Private visits are only permitted for informal patients
- D. Private visits require written authorisation from the Responsible Clinician
_Why:_ The policy states that patients, when possible, should be able to see all their visitors in private, including in their own bedroom if the patient wishes. Where dormitories are in use, the privacy and dignity of others sharing must be considered and alternative private areas made available.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Adult Inpatient Visiting Policy and Procedure (May 2026, issue 3, review November 2028)

**Q520. [Easy]** _Scenario: A relative of an older adult patient asks to come in at lunchtime to help their family member eat, but the ward operates protected mealtimes._ According to the trust's visiting policy, how should this be handled?
- A. Refuse, because protected mealtimes cannot be varied for any reason
- B. Allow it only if the patient is an informal patient
- **C. It can be arranged following discussion with the ward manager or nurse in charge [correct]**
- D. Refer the request to the Mental Health Act office
_Why:_ The policy acknowledges that some older adult wards adhere to protected mealtimes but that some patients may prefer support and encouragement to eat from their relatives, and this can be arranged following discussions with the ward manager or nurse in charge.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Adult Inpatient Visiting Policy and Procedure (May 2026, issue 3, review November 2028)

**Q521. [Easy]** _Scenario: A newly admitted patient's daughter has been supporting them daily at home and you have taken her contact details._ Where does the trust's carers policy say carer data should be recorded?
- **A. The Carer Dashboard on SystmOne [correct]**
- B. A paper carers file kept in the ward office
- C. The ward handover sheet only
- D. The Datix system
_Why:_ The policy states the Carer Dashboard is available on SystmOne and is where staff should record all carer data from first contact wherever possible. The dashboard also holds an introduction letter template and signposting information.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Carers: Working with and Supporting Carers Policy (June 2025, issue 6, review May 2028)

**Q522. [Hard]** How often does the trust expect teams and service lines to complete their Triangle of Care self-assessment and update their action plan?
- A. Every 6 months
- B. Every 12 months
- **C. Every 18 months [correct]**
- D. Every 3 years
_Why:_ The policy states there is an expectation that trust teams and service lines will complete their Triangle of Care self-assessments and update their action plan every 18 months. The trust holds two-star Triangle of Care status with the Carers Trust.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Carers: Working with and Supporting Carers Policy (June 2025, issue 6, review May 2028)

**Q523. [Medium]** _Scenario: A newly detained patient refuses permission for anything to be shared with their mother, who has been caring for them for years and is now on the phone, distressed._ What does the trust's carers policy say staff must still do?
- A. Decline to speak to her at all until consent is given
- **B. Give general information about mental illness and treatment options, discuss her concerns and signpost her to carer support services [correct]**
- C. Share the diagnosis only, since that is not personal information
- D. Ask the Responsible Clinician to override the patient's decision
_Why:_ The policy is explicit that even if permission to share is refused, staff must still give general information about mental illness and treatment options, discuss the carer's concerns or fears and signpost them to carer support services. It also notes the refusal should be revisited regularly as things settle.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Carers: Working with and Supporting Carers Policy (June 2025, issue 6, review May 2028)

**Q524. [Hard]** _Scenario: A carer asks about a carer's assessment, but the person they care for is currently refusing all services._ What does the trust's carers policy say about offering the carer an assessment in this situation?
- A. The carer cannot be assessed while the cared-for person is refusing services
- B. The carer can only be assessed once the cared-for person is accepted onto a caseload
- **C. The cared-for person refusing services does not prevent the carer from being offered an assessment [correct]**
- D. The assessment must be deferred until the next care plan review
_Why:_ The Procedure for working with carers states that if the service user they care for is currently refusing services, this does not prevent the carer from being offered an assessment. Good practice is to explain that the assessment identifies help and support rather than being a test of how well someone is caring.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Carers: Working with and Supporting Carers Policy (June 2025, issue 6, review May 2028)

**Q525. [Hard]** _Scenario: An out of area bed is declined for a trans patient, and the reason given relates to their gender identity._ Under the trust's transgender and gender nonconforming policy, what must happen?
- **A. It is recorded as a Datix by the bleepholder or flow coordinator [correct]**
- B. It is raised only at the next divisional governance meeting
- C. The ward manager writes to the receiving provider
- D. No action is needed if an alternative bed is found the same day
_Why:_ The policy states twice that if a trans person is refused an out of area admission based on their gender identity or trans status, this should be recorded as a Datix by the bleepholder or flow coordinator. It also says a trust bed should be prioritised where possible given the complexity of out of area placement.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Policy for Community and Inpatient services on the Care and Support of Transgender and Gender Nonconforming Service Users and Staff (November 2023, issue 3, review November 2026)

**Q526. [Hard]** _Scenario: An intimate external search is needed for a trans patient who identifies as non-binary and, when asked, expresses no preference about the gender of the searching staff member._ What does the trust's policy say should happen?
- A. The search should be postponed indefinitely until a preference is given
- **B. The search should be matched to the biological sex of the person being searched [correct]**
- C. The nurse in charge chooses the searcher regardless of gender
- D. Two staff of different genders should carry out the search together
_Why:_ The policy says intimate external searches should generally be performed by a gender chosen by the trans person; if they cannot confirm, the searcher should be aligned with their affirmed gender; and if the person is non-binary and gives no preference, searches should be matched with the biological sex of the person being searched.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Policy for Community and Inpatient services on the Care and Support of Transgender and Gender Nonconforming Service Users and Staff (November 2023, issue 3, review November 2026)

**Q527. [Easy]** _Scenario: A patient tells you they want to be known by a different name and different pronouns from those on their records._ What does the trust's transgender and gender nonconforming policy say is needed before staff address them that way?
- A. A Gender Recognition Certificate
- B. A deed poll or statutory declaration
- **C. Nothing formal - the way a service user wishes to be addressed does not require any paperwork or official notification of name change [correct]**
- D. Written agreement from the Responsible Clinician
_Why:_ The policy states that the way a service user wishes to be addressed does not require any formal paper or other official notification of a name change, and that names and titles should be changed as a matter of courtesy, not dependent on holding a Gender Recognition Certificate. Where families disagree, the trans person's preference must be followed.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Policy for Community and Inpatient services on the Care and Support of Transgender and Gender Nonconforming Service Users and Staff (November 2023, issue 3, review November 2026)

**Q528. [Medium]** _Scenario: Mental Health Act (MHA) paperwork is being completed for a trans patient whose legal name differs from the name they use._ What does the trust's policy say about how the name should be recorded?
- A. Use only the name the person currently uses
- **B. Give the legal name, and it is permissible to add the other name in brackets, for example '(who identifies as NAME)' [correct]**
- C. Leave the name blank and attach a separate note
- D. Use the legal name and do not record the other name anywhere
_Why:_ The policy states that MHA paperwork must pertain to the person in front of the person completing it, that it should give their legal name, and that if they identify as another name it is permissible to put the name and then brackets '(who identifies as NAME)'. Use of he or she is not detrimental so long as it is clear who it relates to.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Policy for Community and Inpatient services on the Care and Support of Transgender and Gender Nonconforming Service Users and Staff (November 2023, issue 3, review November 2026)

**Q529. [Hard]** _Scenario: A healthcare assistant asks whether two staff should always be present for personal care, to protect staff from allegations._ What does the trust's Intimate Care Policy say?
- A. Two staff must always be present for any intimate care
- B. Two staff are required only for patients detained under the Mental Health Act
- **C. It is not always necessary, for example unless needed for health and safety reasons such as moving and handling, and if more than one person is required the rationale must be documented in the care record [correct]**
- D. One staff member is always sufficient and a second is never justified
_Why:_ The policy states it is not always necessary for two staff to be involved unless for health and safety reasons, and notes that while a second person may protect against allegations it further erodes the person's privacy. Where more than one person is required, the rationale must be documented in the care record.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Intimate Care Policy and Procedure (March 2024, issue 5, review March 2027)

**Q530. [Easy]** _Scenario: While assisting a patient with washing, you notice unexplained bruising on their upper arm._ What does the trust's Intimate Care Policy require?
- A. Record it in the notes at the end of the shift and mention it on handover
- **B. Record it, complete a body map as soon as possible and report it to the nurse in charge or designated manager [correct]**
- C. Photograph the bruising on a ward mobile phone
- D. Wait to see whether it worsens before recording anything
_Why:_ The policy states all unusual markings, discolouration or swelling must be recorded and a body map completed as soon as possible and reported to the nurse in charge or designated manager, with the safeguarding team contacted where there are concerns.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Intimate Care Policy and Procedure (March 2024, issue 5, review March 2027)

**Q531. [Easy]** _Scenario: A patient's partner is on the ward and offers to stay in the room during an intimate examination so that a staff member is not needed._ Under the current Chaperone Policy, can the partner act as the chaperone?
- A. Yes, a family member or carer counts as a chaperone if the patient consents
- B. Yes, provided the examining clinician agrees
- **C. No - family members or carers may provide emotional support but do not replace a formal chaperone, who must be a trained member of staff [correct]**
- D. Only if no trained staff member is available within the hour
_Why:_ The 2026 version of the policy changed this. A formal chaperone must be a trained member of staff; family or carers may give emotional support but cannot replace a formal chaperone. All intimate examinations of under-18s require a formal chaperone even when a parent or carer is present.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Chaperone Policy and Procedure (April 2026, issue 7, review April 2029)

**Q532. [Medium]** Under the trust's Chaperone Policy, which staff may act as a formal chaperone?
- A. Any registered nurse on duty
- **B. Only staff who have been Disclosure and Barring Service (DBS) checked and have completed trust safeguarding training [correct]**
- C. Any substantive member of staff, but not bank or agency staff
- D. Any member of staff who has completed an annual appraisal
_Why:_ The training and competency section states that only staff who have been DBS checked and who have completed trust safeguarding training may act as chaperones. 'Trained' for the purposes of this policy means having undergone safeguarding adults or children's training.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Chaperone Policy and Procedure (April 2026, issue 7, review April 2029)

**Q533. [Hard]** _Scenario: Mid-way through an intimate procedure the chaperone is called away to an emergency elsewhere on the ward._ What does the trust's Chaperone Policy say should happen?
- A. The examiner may continue if the patient says they are happy to
- **B. The procedure should be halted until the chaperone returns, with the patient's privacy and dignity maintained, for example with a cover blanket [correct]**
- C. Another patient's visitor may step in temporarily
- D. The examiner should finish as quickly as possible and document the gap afterwards
_Why:_ The policy states that if the chaperone has to leave the room the procedure should be halted until they return, and during this time the patient's privacy and dignity must be maintained, for example by using a cover blanket. The chaperone must also stay in a position to witness the examination.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Chaperone Policy and Procedure (April 2026, issue 7, review April 2029)

**Q534. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient on the ward needs daily help with washing, dressing and using the toilet._ What does the trust's Chaperone Policy require in relation to who provides that personal care?
- A. Care must always be given by a staff member of the same gender as the patient
- **B. All patients who need assistance with personal care must be asked what gender of staff they would prefer to be assisted by [correct]**
- C. The named nurse decides based on the ward's staffing rota
- D. Gender preference only needs to be considered for intimate examinations, not personal care
_Why:_ The policy states that all patients who require assistance with meeting their personal care needs must be asked what gender staff they would prefer. It also recognises that some people have personal reasons for not wanting to be assisted by someone of the same gender, and preferences must be clearly documented in the patient's record.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Chaperone Policy and Procedure (April 2026, issue 7, review April 2029)

**Q535. [Easy]** _Scenario: A patient is admitted to the ward at 8pm on a Monday._ Under the trust's Nutrition and Hydration Policy, what nutritional risk screening is required and by when?
- **A. The Malnutrition Universal Screening Tool (MUST), completed within 48 hours of admission [correct]**
- B. The Malnutrition Universal Screening Tool (MUST), completed within 7 days of admission
- C. The Lester tool, completed within 24 hours of admission
- D. No screening tool is required unless the patient looks underweight
_Why:_ The policy states every adult patient admitted to an inpatient ward will have a nutritional risk screen completed within 48 hours of admission, that the approved tool is the Malnutrition Universal Screening Tool (MUST), and that any exception must be clearly documented. MUST is built into the SystmOne electronic patient record.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Nutrition and Hydration Policy and Procedure (August 2024, issue 5, review July 2027)

**Q536. [Hard]** _Scenario: A Malnutrition Universal Screening Tool (MUST) screen has just identified that a patient is at nutritional risk._ Within what timeframe does the trust's policy say a care plan must be developed?
- **A. Within 6 hours of the risk being identified [correct]**
- B. Within 24 hours of the risk being identified
- C. By the next multidisciplinary team (MDT) review
- D. Within 72 hours of the risk being identified
_Why:_ The policy states that those identified to be at risk must have a care plan developed within 6 hours of the risk being identified, with referral to Dietetics if required, and the care plan must reflect the first line advice provided by the dietetics department.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Nutrition and Hydration Policy and Procedure (August 2024, issue 5, review July 2027)

**Q537. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient scores 2 on the Malnutrition Universal Screening Tool (MUST)._ What does the trust's Nutrition and Hydration Policy say this means?
- A. Low risk - repeat the screen at the next routine review
- B. Medium risk - consider first line advice at ward level only
- **C. High risk - a care plan using the tool's management guidelines is required and a referral to a dietitian must be made [correct]**
- D. The score is inconclusive and the screen should be repeated the following day
_Why:_ A MUST score of 2 or above is high risk. The policy requires a care plan developed using the tool's management guidelines, discussion of the tool, care plan and evaluation as part of multidisciplinary review, and states that in undernutrition identified at MUST 2 or above a referral to a dietitian must be made.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Nutrition and Hydration Policy and Procedure (August 2024, issue 5, review July 2027)

**Q538. [Hard]** _Scenario: A colleague asks how often patients should be weighed on an adult mental health ward compared with an older people's inpatient ward._ What does the trust's Nutrition and Hydration Policy give as the indicative frequencies?
- A. Weekly on adult mental health wards and monthly on older people's wards
- **B. Monthly on adult mental health wards and weekly on older people's wards, with weekly monitoring where medium or high nutritional risk is identified [correct]**
- C. Fortnightly on both, regardless of risk
- D. On admission and discharge only, unless the dietitian asks otherwise
_Why:_ The policy gives an indicative frequency of monthly for adult mental health wards and weekly for older people's inpatient wards, with clinical judgement prevailing, and states that where medium or high nutritional risk is identified weight monitoring and nutritional screening should be repeated weekly as per the MUST tool guidelines.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Nutrition and Hydration Policy and Procedure (August 2024, issue 5, review July 2027)

**Q539. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient has begun refusing meals and most drinks over the last two days._ What does the trust's Nutrition and Hydration Policy require?
- **A. Document all diet and fluid offered and refused, and have the patient reviewed by members of the multidisciplinary team (MDT) daily [correct]**
- B. Wait 72 hours before escalating, to allow the pattern to settle
- C. Start a fluid balance chart only once the patient becomes physically unwell
- D. Refer straight to the enteral feeding pathway
_Why:_ The policy states all diet and fluid offered and refused must be documented and the patient will be required to be reviewed by members of the MDT daily. Patients at risk of malnutrition or reduced intake must also have an electronic Food and Fluid Record chart, with a fluid balance chart where dehydration is a risk, and concerns escalated to the medical team.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Nutrition and Hydration Policy and Procedure (August 2024, issue 5, review July 2027)

**Q540. [Medium]** _Scenario: A healthcare assistant with a food hygiene qualification offers to run a baking session with a patient in the ward's therapeutic kitchen._ What does the Use of Therapeutic Kitchens Policy require?
- A. Only Occupational Therapists (OTs) may ever use a therapeutic kitchen with a patient
- B. Any member of staff may supervise as long as the ward manager agrees
- **C. Non-OT staff such as healthcare assistants or peer support workers may facilitate the activity provided they hold the required Level 2 Food Safety certification and an Occupational Therapist provides oversight [correct]**
- D. A Level 3 food safety qualification is required for all staff leading kitchen sessions
_Why:_ The policy requires staff to complete the Level 2 Award in Food Safety in Catering before leading or supervising food-related therapeutic activity, plus Food Hygiene Awareness e-learning on starting with the trust. Non-OT staff with the Level 2 certification may facilitate sessions where an Occupational Therapist provides oversight.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Use of Therapeutic Kitchens Policy (February 2026, issue 1, review February 2029)

**Q541. [Hard]** _Scenario: At the end of a cooking group, one of the knives cannot be found._ What does the trust's Use of Therapeutic Kitchens Policy require?
- A. Note it in the session record and check again the next time the kitchen is used
- **B. Inform the nurse in charge or team leader immediately, treat it as a security incident, search the area and escalate through the incident reporting procedure if it is not found quickly [correct]**
- C. Report it only if a patient is thought to have taken it
- D. Replace the knife from stock and continue as normal
_Why:_ The policy requires all cutlery and utensils to be counted before and after each session against a checklist. If any item is missing or unaccounted for, staff must immediately inform the nurse in charge or team leader and treat it as a security incident, search the area, and escalate per the trust's incident reporting procedures. Sharps must be stored in locked cupboards with staff holding the keys.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Use of Therapeutic Kitchens Policy (February 2026, issue 1, review February 2029)

**Q542. [Easy]** _Scenario: You are setting up a therapeutic kitchen session and find a wooden chopping board in the cupboard._ What does the trust's Use of Therapeutic Kitchens Policy say about wooden chopping boards?
- A. They may be used for dry ingredients only
- B. They may be used if scrubbed with hot water immediately after use
- **C. They must not be used at all, because of the difficulty in sanitising them - use colour-coded plastic boards instead [correct]**
- D. They may be used for vegetables but never for meat
_Why:_ The policy states wooden chopping boards must not be used at all due to the difficulty in sanitising them, and that plastic cutting boards should be used following the standard colour-coding system (red raw meat, blue raw fish, green salad and fruit, brown root vegetables, white dairy and bakery, yellow cooked meats).
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Use of Therapeutic Kitchens Policy (February 2026, issue 1, review February 2029)

## Trust Policy - Records, Incidents & Duty of Candour

**Q543. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient on the ward has come to moderate harm following an incident, and the incident has been assessed as meeting the Duty of Candour._ Under the Trust's Duty of Candour policy, by when must the patient or their relevant person be given the notification?
- A. Within 24 hours of the incident happening
- B. Within 28 days of the incident being reported on the incident system
- C. Once the learning response has finished and all the facts are confirmed
- **D. As soon as possible and within 10 working days of the incident being assessed as Duty of Candour [correct]**
_Why:_ The Trust policy says notification should be given in person, sharing all factual information known to date, as soon as possible and within 10 working days of the incident being assessed as Duty of Candour.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Policy and Procedure for Duty of Candour (March 2025, issue 9, review February 2028)

**Q544. [Medium]** Which levels of harm bring an incident within the statutory Duty of Candour under the Trust's policy?
- A. Any incident at all, including near misses
- B. Low harm and above
- C. Severe harm or death only
- **D. Moderate harm, prolonged pain, prolonged psychological harm, severe harm or death [correct]**
_Why:_ The policy defines the trigger as moderate harm, prolonged pain, prolonged psychological harm, severe harm or death, and states that near misses, no harm and low harm incidents are not required to follow the Duty of Candour process.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Policy and Procedure for Duty of Candour (March 2025, issue 9, review February 2028)

**Q545. [Hard]** The Trust's Duty of Candour policy defines 'prolonged psychological harm'. Over what continuous period must the harm have been experienced, or be likely to be experienced?
- A. 7 days or more
- B. 14 days or more
- C. 21 days or more
- **D. 28 days or more [correct]**
_Why:_ The policy defines both prolonged pain and prolonged psychological harm as lasting, or being likely to last, for a continuous period of at least 28 days.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Policy and Procedure for Duty of Candour (March 2025, issue 9, review February 2028)

**Q546. [Medium]** _Scenario: You have just had the Duty of Candour conversation with a patient's relative and apologised._ Where does the Trust policy say this must be recorded?
- **A. Clearly in the patient's electronic patient record, with the incident record also updated to log the discussion and any letters uploaded [correct]**
- B. In the ward handover book, so the next shift knows
- C. In your own supervision notes only
- D. On the incident record only, because the electronic patient record is not used for incidents
_Why:_ The policy requires the apology and what further enquiries will be made to be clearly documented in the patient's electronic patient record, and says all contacts should be documented there with the incident record also updated and copies of letters uploaded.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Policy and Procedure for Duty of Candour (March 2025, issue 9, review February 2028)

**Q547. [Easy]** Which team at this Trust offers training, support and advice to staff on applying the Duty of Candour, and supports patients and families after an incident?
- A. The Mental Health Act office
- **B. The Family Liaison Team [correct]**
- C. The Freedom to Speak Up Guardians
- D. The Practice Development Team
_Why:_ The policy names Family Liaison as the team that offers training, support and advice on Duty of Candour and supports patients and families following an incident of moderate harm or above.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Policy and Procedure for Duty of Candour (March 2025, issue 9, review February 2028)

**Q548. [Medium]** In the event of a homicide, who does the Trust's Duty of Candour policy apply to?
- A. The victim's families and carers only
- B. The perpetrator's families and carers only
- **C. The families and carers of both the victim and the perpetrator [correct]**
- D. Neither, because homicides are handled entirely by an independent investigation
_Why:_ The policy states explicitly that in the event of a homicide it applies to the families and carers of both the victim and the perpetrator.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Policy and Procedure for Duty of Candour (March 2025, issue 9, review February 2028)

**Q549. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient known to the service has died and the death meets the Trust's red flag criteria for reporting as an incident._ How quickly must the incident form be completed?
- **A. Within 24 hours of the death, or of staff becoming aware of the death [correct]**
- B. Within 3 working days
- C. Within 5 working days
- D. Before the end of the shift on which the death occurred
_Why:_ The Learning from Deaths policy says that for deaths which need to be reported as an incident, an incident form must be completed within 24 hours of the death or of staff becoming aware of the death.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Learning from Deaths Policy and Procedure (June 2024, issue 03, review April 2027)

**Q550. [Medium]** Which deaths are screened against the Trust's mortality and incident red flags?
- A. Only deaths that happen on an inpatient ward
- B. Only deaths of patients detained under the Mental Health Act
- **C. All deaths of patients known to services within the last 6 months [correct]**
- D. All deaths of patients known to services within the last 12 months
_Why:_ The policy states that all deaths of patients known to services within 6 months are considered against the mortality and incident Trust red flags for reporting onto the incident system.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Learning from Deaths Policy and Procedure (June 2024, issue 03, review April 2027)

**Q551. [Hard]** Which of these appears on the Trust's list of 'red flags' for a death that must be reported as an untoward incident?
- A. The death of any former patient discharged in the last 12 months
- **B. The death of an inpatient who died within 30 days of discharge from a Trust hospital [correct]**
- C. The death of any patient over the age of 75
- D. The death of any patient who missed an outpatient appointment
_Why:_ The red flag list includes death whilst an inpatient and death of an inpatient who died within 30 days of discharge from a Trust hospital, alongside suicide, death following restraint or absconsion, detention under the Mental Health Act or Deprivation of Liberty Safeguards, learning disability and autism.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Learning from Deaths Policy and Procedure (June 2024, issue 03, review April 2027)

**Q552. [Hard]** One of the Trust's levels of scrutiny after a flagged death is the Incident Review Tool. Within what timescale must the service complete it?
- A. Within 24 hours
- B. Within 3 working days
- C. Within 10 working days
- **D. Within 5 working days, or sooner [correct]**
_Why:_ The policy says the Incident Review Tool is completed by the service within 5 working days (or sooner) and includes immediate themes, learning and best practice.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Learning from Deaths Policy and Procedure (June 2024, issue 03, review April 2027)

**Q553. [Medium]** _Scenario: The ward is notified that a patient recently open to the service has died._ What does the Trust's Learning from Deaths process ask the clinical team to complete?
- A. A paper mortality form sent directly to the coroner's office
- B. A Health of the Nation Outcome Scales (HoNOS) assessment
- C. A Coroner's Regulation 28 report
- **D. A SystmOne proforma, which the mortality technician then quality assures [correct]**
_Why:_ The policy says the red flag screening is supported by a SystmOne proforma completed by operational clinical teams when they are notified of a death, and that the mortality technician is responsible for quality assuring that process.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Learning from Deaths Policy and Procedure (June 2024, issue 03, review April 2027)

**Q554. [Medium]** Which group receives an overview of the deaths of patients in the Trust's care and decides which need further scrutiny, and how often does it do this?
- A. The Quality and Safeguarding Committee, weekly
- B. The ward Multidisciplinary Team (MDT), at each ward round
- **C. The Operational Incident Review Group, monthly [correct]**
- D. The Trust Board, at every public meeting
_Why:_ The policy sets out that the Operational Incident Review Group receives an overview of deaths recorded of patients in the Trust's care on a monthly basis and determines which need further scrutiny. The Trust Board receives the mortality report quarterly.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Learning from Deaths Policy and Procedure (June 2024, issue 03, review April 2027)

**Q555. [Easy]** _Scenario: A patient has died on the ward and the record needs updating on SystmOne._ Under the Trust's Deceased Patient (Inpatient) Standard Operating Procedure (SOP), who is responsible for ending the care of the patient on the system?
- **A. The doctor [correct]**
- B. The nurse in charge of the shift
- C. The ward administrator
- D. The bed management team
_Why:_ The SOP states under responsibilities that the doctor will be responsible for ending the care of the patient on the system.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Standard Operating Procedure for Deceased Patient - Inpatient (Issue 01, first version (no issue date or review date recorded on the document))

**Q556. [Medium]** Recording an inpatient death on SystmOne, which of the following steps does the Trust Standard Operating Procedure (SOP) include?
- A. Leaving the bed open on the ward view until the coroner has reported
- **B. Completing Bed Clean Up, selecting the type of cleaning, and closing the bed [correct]**
- C. Marking the whole patient record in error
- D. Transferring the open referral to the community team
_Why:_ The SOP takes you through discharge with method 'Patient died', then Bed Clean Up where you select the type of cleaning and close the bed, before entering date and time of death, the informant, cause of death and place of death.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Standard Operating Procedure for Deceased Patient - Inpatient (Issue 01, first version (no issue date or review date recorded on the document))

**Q557. [Easy]** The Trust's Case Notes Standard Operating Procedure (SOP) splits clinical note taking on SystmOne into three note types. Which three?
- **A. Session notes, Duty notes and Triage notes [correct]**
- B. Admission notes, Review notes and Discharge notes
- C. Nursing notes, Medical notes and Therapy notes
- D. Progress notes, Handover notes and Incident notes
_Why:_ The SOP distinguishes clinical note taking into Session notes, Duty notes and Triage notes, with Admin / Communication used by administrative staff for non clinical entries only.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Standard Operating Procedure Case Notes (Issue 01, version dated 30/10/2020 (no issue date or review date recorded on the document))

**Q558. [Medium]** _Scenario: A colleague writes the patient's care plan into the 'Action Plan / Next Steps' box of a session note._ What does the Trust's Case Notes Standard Operating Procedure (SOP) say about this?
- A. That is correct, the action plan box is where care plans belong
- B. Care plans can go in either place as long as the entry is countersigned
- **C. Action Plan / Next Steps is for general next steps only and is not to be used for care planning, which follows the Care Planning SOP [correct]**
- D. Care plans should be typed into Admin / Communication instead
_Why:_ The SOP repeats for session, triage and duty notes that the Action Plan / Next Steps field covers general next steps such as calling the GP or making a referral, and is not to be used for care planning.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Standard Operating Procedure Case Notes (Issue 01, version dated 30/10/2020 (no issue date or review date recorded on the document))

**Q559. [Medium]** How does the Trust's Care Planning Standard Operating Procedure (SOP) say the care plan review date should be set?
- A. Automatically 12 weeks from the start date for every patient
- B. By the responsible clinician at the next ward round, and nowhere else
- **C. In line with your local care plan review policy [correct]**
- D. There is no review date field on the care plan
_Why:_ The SOP says all care plans require a review date and that it should be added in line with local departmental care plan review policy. It does not set a single trust-wide frequency.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Standard Operating Procedure Care Planning (Issue 01, version dated 30/10/2020 (no issue date or review date recorded on the document))

**Q560. [Hard]** _Scenario: You are starting a new care plan on SystmOne and the system asks whether you want to link a referral._ What does the Trust's Care Planning Standard Operating Procedure (SOP) tell you to do?
- A. Link the referral that led to the admission
- B. Link a referral only for detained patients
- C. Link a referral only when the plan relates to Section 117 aftercare
- **D. Do not link a referral, so that every pathway can add to the plan as they join or leave the patient's journey [correct]**
_Why:_ The SOP is explicit: do not link a referral, leave it as no linked referral, so that all pathways can add to the document. When the system asks you to confirm you did not want to link a referral, click yes.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Standard Operating Procedure Care Planning (Issue 01, version dated 30/10/2020 (no issue date or review date recorded on the document))

**Q561. [Easy]** Under the Trust's Care Planning Standard Operating Procedure (SOP), when is a care plan closed?
- A. At the end of every internal pathway the patient moves through
- **B. On discharge from the service, not for internal pathways [correct]**
- C. Every 6 months, whether or not the patient is still open
- D. Whenever the named nurse changes
_Why:_ The SOP describes the care plan as a live document that remains open and active throughout the patient journey, and says the plan is only closed on discharge from the service, not for internal pathways.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Standard Operating Procedure Care Planning (Issue 01, version dated 30/10/2020 (no issue date or review date recorded on the document))

**Q562. [Easy]** At first contact with a patient, which three identifiers must always be checked under the Trust's Patient Identification Policy?
- A. Name, NHS number and next of kin
- B. Name, date of birth and GP practice
- C. Date of birth, ward and responsible clinician
- **D. Name, address and date of birth [correct]**
_Why:_ The policy states that at the first contact with a patient the three identifiers of name, address and date of birth must always be checked.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Patient Identification Policy and Procedure (April 2024, issue 05, review April 2027)

**Q563. [Hard]** How often, as an absolute minimum, does the Trust's Patient Identification Policy require a patient's demographic details to be checked for accuracy?
- A. Monthly
- B. Every three months
- **C. Every six months [correct]**
- D. Every twelve months
_Why:_ The policy says demographic details should be checked regularly, for example at reviews and on admission, and that as a minimum this must be done six monthly.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Patient Identification Policy and Procedure (April 2024, issue 05, review April 2027)

**Q564. [Easy]** Wristbands are not routinely worn by patients at this Trust. In which situation does the policy say a wristband will always be worn?
- A. By every patient detained under the Mental Health Act
- B. By every patient on enhanced observations
- C. By every patient on their first admission
- **D. By all service users prior to receiving Electro Convulsive Therapy (ECT) [correct]**
_Why:_ The policy states that all patients receiving Electro Convulsive Therapy will wear an identification wristband prior to arrival in the department. Older adult organic wards use them when care is being transferred to another hospital, but not routinely.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Patient Identification Policy and Procedure (April 2024, issue 05, review April 2027)

**Q565. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient with a known allergy is being transferred to the acute hospital and needs a wristband._ What does the Trust's Patient Identification Policy require?
- A. A red wristband with the allergen and severity written on it
- B. A standard white wristband, with a red sticker added to the drug chart
- **C. A red wristband carrying only patient identification, with allergy details kept in the patient's notes [correct]**
- D. A yellow wristband, checked by two registered nurses
_Why:_ The policy requires all patients with known allergies to have a red wristband on a limb that can easily be seen, but says only patient identification should appear on the wristband and staff must refer to the patient's notes for details of the allergy or alert.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Patient Identification Policy and Procedure (April 2024, issue 05, review April 2027)

**Q566. [Hard]** _Scenario: You have completed the risk screening tool on SystmOne for a newly admitted patient._ What is the practical difference between 'save for future editing' and 'save final version'?
- A. There is no difference, both publish the risk alerts
- **B. 'Save for future editing' will not pull the risk alerts through - only 'save final version' does [correct]**
- C. 'Save final version' deletes the draft and starts again
- D. 'Save for future editing' notifies the whole team by task
_Why:_ The Alerts on System SOP flags this directly: save for future editing will not pull risk alerts through, whereas save final version does, generating the derived content box and the risk summary.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Standard Operating Procedure Alerts on System (Issue 01, first version (no issue date or review date recorded on the document))

**Q567. [Medium]** Alerts show in the demographics box when a patient record is retrieved. What does the Trust's Alerts on System Standard Operating Procedure (SOP) say specifically about safeguarding alerts?
- A. They are never displayed on the record
- **B. They are shown, but only users with the relevant access rights can view the alert itself [correct]**
- C. They can only be viewed by the Caldicott Guardian
- D. They are emailed to the ward manager each morning
_Why:_ The SOP says that although safeguarding alerts are shown, only users with the relevant access rights will be able to view the alert. Alerts can be reviewed, amended and ended when they are no longer relevant.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Standard Operating Procedure Alerts on System (Issue 01, first version (no issue date or review date recorded on the document))

**Q568. [Easy]** Under the Trust's Allergy Recording Standard Operating Procedure (SOP), who holds the principal responsibility for establishing a patient's allergy status?
- **A. The prescriber [correct]**
- B. The admitting nurse
- C. The ward pharmacist
- D. The patient's GP practice
_Why:_ The SOP states the prescriber retains the principal responsibility for ascertaining allergy status for any patient they prescribe for, while everyone involved in prescribing, dispensing or administering must take every practical step to establish it.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Standard Operating Procedure Allergy Recording (Issue 01, first version dated 12/11/2020 (no issue date or review date recorded on the document))

**Q569. [Medium]** How often does the Trust's Allergy Recording Standard Operating Procedure (SOP) say allergy and sensitivity status should be reconfirmed?
- **A. On a regular basis and upon every change in prescription [correct]**
- B. Once, on admission
- C. Annually
- D. Only if the patient reports a new reaction
_Why:_ The SOP says allergy and sensitivity status can change over time, so it must be reconfirmed on a regular basis and upon every change in prescription. It also advises using more than one information source where possible.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Standard Operating Procedure Allergy Recording (Issue 01, first version dated 12/11/2020 (no issue date or review date recorded on the document))

**Q570. [Medium]** Which statement matches the Trust's Standard Operating Procedure (SOP) on confidential notes in SystmOne?
- A. A confidential note can be locked so that only its author can ever see it
- B. Confidential notes are hidden from everyone outside the psychology team
- **C. A confidential note is never locked to an individual member of staff, and roles such as the Caldicott Guardian and Clinical Safety Officer can always access the record [correct]**
- D. Once saved, a confidential note cannot be viewed by anyone else at all
_Why:_ The SOP states the confidential note will never be locked to an individual member of staff, and that the Clinical Safety Officer, Caldicott Guardian, Chief Clinical Informatics Officer, Clinical Records Manager and Patient Safety Lead will always be able to access the records.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Standard Operating Procedure Confidential Notes: Marking as Private and Recording Restrictions (Issue 01, first version (no issue date or review date recorded on the document))

**Q571. [Easy]** What does marking an entry as 'private' on SystmOne do, under the Trust's confidential notes Standard Operating Procedure (SOP)?
- A. It hides the entry from everyone in the Trust except the author
- B. It deletes the entry after 30 days
- C. It flags the entry for the ward manager to approve
- **D. It stops the entry being seen on the shared record outside the Trust [correct]**
_Why:_ The SOP explains that marking an entry as private means the note will not be seen on the shared record outside of the Trust. Limiting access within or across organisations is done separately, through record restrictions and exclusions.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Standard Operating Procedure Confidential Notes: Marking as Private and Recording Restrictions (Issue 01, first version (no issue date or review date recorded on the document))

**Q572. [Easy]** Completing a Health of the Nation Outcome Scales (HoNOS) assessment on SystmOne, what does the Trust Standard Operating Procedure (SOP) require?
- **A. An answer must be selected for every question [correct]**
- B. Answer only the questions that are relevant to the patient
- C. Only the responsible clinician may complete it
- D. A written note must be added for every behaviour scored
_Why:_ The SOP says you must select an answer for all the questions. Notes against each behaviour, added using the green bubble, are described as not a mandatory entry.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Standard Operating Procedure HoNOS (Issue 01, first version (no issue date or review date recorded on the document))

**Q573. [Hard]** _Scenario: A student nurse on the ward has written a session note that needs countersigning._ Under the Trust's Countersigning Standard Operating Procedure (SOP), how is the need identified and how is the countersignature requested on SystmOne?
- A. The need is set automatically by staff band, and the system routes the entry to a supervisor
- B. All entries by unregistered staff are countersigned by the ward manager at the end of each shift
- **C. The need is identified through supervision or with a mentor based on clinical judgement of competency, and the request is sent as a 'New Task' to the named individual expected to countersign [correct]**
- D. The entry is printed and signed by hand, then scanned back into the record
_Why:_ The SOP says the requirement is identified following discussion during supervision or with a mentor, based on clinical judgement of competency level, and the request is sent using the 'New Task' button to the specific person expected to countersign, with clear instructions.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Standard Operating Procedure Countersigning (March 2024, issue 03, review March 2025)
_⚠ review date passed_

**Q574. [Hard]** Since electronic prescribing was introduced, when does the Medication Countersigning box appear on SystmOne and what is needed to complete it?
- A. For every medication administered - the second checker signs the paper chart
- **B. For controlled drugs or injectable medication - a qualified member of staff enters their name and SystmOne PIN number [correct]**
- C. For as required (PRN) medication only - a second nurse adds their initials
- D. For rapid tranquillisation only - the doctor enters a password
_Why:_ The Countersigning SOP states that with the introduction of electronic prescribing, countersigning of the administration of controlled drugs and injectable medication is now required on SystmOne, and a qualified member of staff must enter their name and SystmOne PIN number in the Medication Countersigning box.
_Source:_ Derbyshire Healthcare - Standard Operating Procedure Countersigning (March 2024, issue 03, review March 2025)
_⚠ review date passed_

# PART 2 - National guidance and law

> NICE, BNF, MHRA, NMC, RCN, Royal Colleges, Resuscitation Council UK, SCIE, legislation.

## Mental Health Act & Legal

**Q575. [Easy]** How long can a patient be detained for assessment under Section 2 of the Mental Health Act 1983?
- A. Up to 72 hours
- **B. Up to 28 days [correct]**
- C. Up to 6 months
- D. Up to 12 months
_Why:_ Section 2 permits detention for assessment for a maximum of 28 days, and cannot be extended or renewed.
_Source:_ Mental Health Act 1983 - Section 2 - https://www.mentalhealthlaw.co.uk/Section_2:_admission_for_assessment

**Q576. [Easy]** What is the maximum duration of the nurse's holding power under Section 5(4) of the Mental Health Act 1983?
- A. 2 hours
- **B. 6 hours [correct]**
- C. 24 hours
- D. 72 hours
_Why:_ Section 5(4) allows a suitably qualified nurse to detain an informal inpatient already receiving treatment for mental disorder for up to 6 hours, and it cannot be renewed.
_Source:_ Mental Health Act 1983 - Section 5 holding powers - https://www.mentalhealthlaw.co.uk/Section_5:_holding_powers

**Q577. [Easy]** What is the maximum duration of the doctor's holding power under Section 5(2) of the Mental Health Act 1983?
- A. 6 hours
- B. 24 hours
- **C. 72 hours [correct]**
- D. 28 days
_Why:_ Section 5(2) allows the doctor or approved clinician in charge (or their nominee) to detain a voluntary inpatient for up to 72 hours to allow a Mental Health Act assessment to be arranged.
_Source:_ Mental Health Act 1983 - Section 5 holding powers - https://www.mentalhealthlaw.co.uk/Section_5:_holding_powers

**Q578. [Easy]** How long does an initial detention under Section 3 (admission for treatment) of the Mental Health Act 1983 last before it must be renewed?
- A. 28 days
- B. 3 months
- **C. 6 months [correct]**
- D. 12 months
_Why:_ Section 3 lasts up to 6 months initially, may be renewed for a further 6 months, and thereafter renewed for periods of 12 months.
_Source:_ Mental Health Act 1983 - Section 3 - https://www.mentalhealthlaw.co.uk/Section_3:_admission_for_treatment

**Q579. [Easy]** Which professional makes the formal application to detain a patient under Section 2 or 3 of the Mental Health Act 1983 once the medical recommendations are in place?
- A. The Responsible Clinician
- **B. The Approved Mental Health Professional (AMHP) [correct]**
- C. The ward manager
- D. The nearest relative's solicitor
_Why:_ Doctors provide the medical recommendations, but it is the Approved Mental Health Professional (AMHP) who decides whether to make, and then makes, the application for detention (the nearest relative may alternatively apply).
_Source:_ Rethink Mental Illness - What is the Mental Health Act? - https://www.rethink.org/advice-and-information/rights-laws-and-criminal-justice/mental-health-laws/mental-health-act/

**Q580. [Easy]** Under Section 136 of the Mental Health Act 1983, the police may remove a person from a public place to a place of safety. For how long can that person normally be detained?
- A. Up to 6 hours
- **B. Up to 24 hours [correct]**
- C. Up to 72 hours
- D. Up to 28 days
_Why:_ Since the Policing and Crime Act 2017 changes, Section 136 detention lasts up to 24 hours, extendable by a further 12 hours only if assessment could not be completed for clinical reasons.
_Source:_ Mental Health Act 1983 - Section 136 (as amended 2017) - https://www.rethink.org/advice-and-information/rights-laws-and-criminal-justice/mental-health-laws/section-136-of-the-mental-health-act-emergency-police-powers-to-take-you-from-a-public-place-to-a-place-of-safety/

**Q581. [Medium]** _Scenario: A voluntary (informal) patient on the ward tells staff at 2am they are leaving immediately, and the nurse believes they are at significant risk. No doctor is immediately available._ Which power allows the registered mental health nurse to prevent the patient from leaving until a doctor can attend?
- A. Section 2
- B. Section 5(2)
- **C. Section 5(4) [correct]**
- D. Section 136
_Why:_ Section 5(4) is the nurse's holding power, used to detain an informal inpatient already receiving treatment for mental disorder for up to 6 hours when no doctor is immediately available to use Section 5(2).
_Source:_ Mental Health Act 1983 - Section 5 holding powers - https://www.mentalhealthlaw.co.uk/Section_5:_holding_powers

**Q582. [Medium]** Which power does the nearest relative have in relation to a Section 3 admission that no one else has?
- A. They can discharge the patient without notice at any time
- **B. They can object to the Section 3, which prevents the admission from proceeding [correct]**
- C. They can appoint the Responsible Clinician
- D. They can grant Section 17 leave
_Why:_ The AMHP must consult the nearest relative before a Section 3 application, and if the nearest relative objects the application cannot proceed (unless the nearest relative is displaced by the court).
_Source:_ Mental Health Act 1983 - nearest relative powers - https://www.gnlaw.co.uk/news/powers-of-the-nearest-relative/

**Q583. [Medium]** Who has the sole authority to grant Section 17 leave of absence to a detained patient?
- A. The named nurse
- B. The Approved Mental Health Professional (AMHP)
- **C. The patient's Responsible Clinician [correct]**
- D. The ward manager
_Why:_ Only the patient's Responsible Clinician can grant or revoke Section 17 leave, and this power cannot be delegated.
_Source:_ Mental Health Act 1983 - Section 17 leave of absence - https://www.mentalhealthlaw.co.uk/Leave_of_absence

**Q584. [Medium]** Which of the following is one of the five guiding principles of the Mental Health Act 1983 Code of Practice (2015)?
- A. Presumption of capacity
- **B. Least restrictive option and maximising independence [correct]**
- C. Duty to refer
- D. Duty of candour
_Why:_ The Code of Practice (2015) sets out five guiding principles: least restrictive option and maximising independence; empowerment and involvement; respect and dignity; purpose and effectiveness; and efficiency and equity.
_Source:_ MHA Code of Practice 2015 - guiding principles (2015) - https://www.mentalhealthlaw.co.uk/New_principles_to_inform_decisions

**Q585. [Medium]** Under Section 117 of the Mental Health Act 1983, aftercare services for eligible patients (for example those discharged from Section 3) must be provided in what way?
- A. Charged according to the patient's means
- **B. Free of charge to the patient [correct]**
- C. Only for the first 28 days after discharge
- D. Only if the patient is on a Community Treatment Order
_Why:_ Integrated care boards and local authorities have a joint legal duty to provide Section 117 aftercare free of charge, regardless of means, until both agree the person no longer needs it.
_Source:_ Mental Health Act 1983 - Section 117 aftercare - https://www.rethink.org/advice-and-information/rights-laws-and-criminal-justice/mental-health-laws/section-117-aftercare-under-the-mental-health-act-1983/

**Q586. [Medium]** Which of these professionals is NOT eligible, once approved, to act as an Approved Mental Health Professional (AMHP)?
- A. A registered social worker
- B. A registered mental health nurse
- C. A registered occupational therapist
- **D. A registered medical practitioner (doctor) [correct]**
_Why:_ AMHPs are drawn from social workers, mental health or learning disability nurses, occupational therapists and psychologists. Doctors provide medical recommendations and cannot be AMHPs, preserving separation of the two roles.
_Source:_ Mental Health Act 2007 - AMHP eligibility - https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Approved_mental_health_professional

**Q587. [Medium]** What is the primary role of a Second Opinion Appointed Doctor (SOAD) under the Mental Health Act 1983?
- A. To chair the patient's care programme approach review
- **B. To independently review certain treatment decisions and certify whether the treatment is appropriate [correct]**
- C. To carry out the AMHP assessment
- D. To act as the patient's Responsible Clinician
_Why:_ A SOAD is an independent psychiatrist appointed by the Care Quality Commission to review certain treatments (such as medication beyond 3 months for a non-consenting patient) and certify whether they are appropriate.
_Source:_ Mental Health Act 1983 - Second Opinion Appointed Doctor - https://www.mentalhealthlaw.co.uk/Second_Opinion_Appointed_Doctor

**Q588. [Hard]** Under Section 4 (emergency admission) of the Mental Health Act 1983, how many medical recommendations are required and for how long can the patient be detained?
- A. Two doctors, up to 28 days
- **B. One doctor, up to 72 hours [correct]**
- C. Two doctors, up to 72 hours
- D. One doctor, up to 24 hours
_Why:_ Section 4 is used in genuine emergencies where waiting for a second doctor would cause undesirable delay. It requires only one medical recommendation and lasts up to 72 hours, within which a second recommendation may convert it to a Section 2.
_Source:_ Mental Health Act 1983 - Section 4 - https://www.mentalhealthlaw.co.uk/Mental_Health_Act_1983_Overview

**Q589. [Hard]** Under Section 58 of the Mental Health Act 1983, medication for mental disorder can be given to a detained patient without their consent and without a Second Opinion Appointed Doctor (SOAD) certificate for how long before the safeguard is triggered?
- A. Up to 72 hours
- B. Up to 14 days
- **C. Up to 3 months [correct]**
- D. Up to 6 months
_Why:_ Under the Section 58 'three-month rule', medication for mental disorder may be given without consent for up to 3 months from when it was first administered; after that a SOAD certificate (or the patient's certified consent) is required to continue.
_Source:_ Mental Health Act 1983 - Section 58 consent to treatment - https://www.mentalhealthlaw.co.uk/Consent_to_treatment_provisions

**Q590. [Hard]** _Scenario: A Section 3 patient who has capacity clearly and consistently refuses electroconvulsive therapy (ECT). It is not an emergency._ Under Section 58A of the Mental Health Act 1983, can the ECT lawfully be given against the wishes of this capacitous, refusing patient (outside an emergency)?
- A. Yes, if the Responsible Clinician authorises it
- B. Yes, if a SOAD certifies it is appropriate
- **C. No, ECT cannot be given to a capacitous patient who refuses it (except in an emergency under Section 62) [correct]**
- D. Yes, if the nearest relative consents
_Why:_ Section 58A gives ECT extra protection: a detained patient with capacity who refuses ECT cannot be given it against their will except in an emergency under Section 62. A SOAD can only authorise ECT for a patient who lacks capacity.
_Source:_ Mental Health Act 1983 - Section 58A (ECT) - https://www.mentalhealthlaw.co.uk/Additional_safeguards_for_ECT_introduced_in_new_s58A

**Q591. [Hard]** Section 62 of the Mental Health Act 1983 allows treatment to be given urgently without the usual Part 4 consent/SOAD safeguards. Which of the following is NOT one of the permitted urgent-treatment grounds?
- A. Treatment immediately necessary to save the patient's life
- B. Treatment immediately necessary to prevent a serious deterioration of the patient's condition (and not irreversible)
- **C. Treatment given because it is more convenient for staffing on the ward [correct]**
- D. Treatment immediately necessary to alleviate serious suffering by the patient (and not irreversible or hazardous)
_Why:_ Section 62 urgent treatment must be immediately necessary to save life, prevent serious deterioration, alleviate serious suffering, or prevent the patient behaving violently/being a danger. Convenience is never a lawful ground.
_Source:_ Mental Health Act 1983 - Section 62 urgent treatment - https://www.mentalhealthlaw.co.uk/Consent_to_treatment_provisions

**Q592. [Hard]** For a Community Treatment Order (CTO) to be made under Section 17A of the Mental Health Act 1983, whose agreement is required in addition to the Responsible Clinician?
- A. A magistrate
- **B. An Approved Mental Health Professional (AMHP) [correct]**
- C. The nearest relative
- D. A Second Opinion Appointed Doctor (SOAD)
_Why:_ A CTO can only be made if an AMHP agrees in writing that the criteria are met and that making the order is appropriate; the Responsible Clinician cannot make it alone.
_Source:_ Mental Health Act 1983 - Section 17A Community Treatment Order - https://www.mentalhealthlaw.co.uk/Community_Treatment_Order

**Q593. [Hard]** A warrant is needed to enter a private dwelling to remove a person believed to have a mental disorder to a place of safety. Under which section is this warrant obtained, and who must accompany the constable?
- A. Section 136, accompanied by two doctors
- **B. Section 135(1), accompanied by an AMHP and a doctor [correct]**
- C. Section 5(2), accompanied by the nearest relative
- D. Section 2, accompanied by an AMHP only
_Why:_ Section 135(1) allows a magistrate to issue a warrant for a constable to enter and remove the person to a place of safety; the constable must be accompanied by an AMHP and a registered medical practitioner. Detention lasts up to 24 hours (extendable by 12).
_Source:_ Mental Health Act 1983 - Section 135(1) warrant - https://www.rethink.org/advice-and-information/rights-laws-and-criminal-justice/mental-health-laws/section-135-of-the-mental-health-act-police-taking-you-to-a-place-of-safety-from-a-private-place/

**Q594. [Hard]** How long does a Community Treatment Order (CTO) under Section 17A of the Mental Health Act 1983 last before its first renewal?
- A. 28 days
- B. 3 months
- **C. 6 months [correct]**
- D. 12 months
_Why:_ A CTO lasts for up to 6 months initially, can be renewed once for a further 6 months, and thereafter renewed for 12-month periods.
_Source:_ Mental Health Act 1983 - Section 17A Community Treatment Order - https://www.mentalhealthlaw.co.uk/Community_Treatment_Order

## Mental Capacity & DoLS

**Q595. [Easy]** How many statutory principles underpin the Mental Capacity Act 2005?
- A. Three
- B. Four
- **C. Five [correct]**
- D. Seven
_Why:_ The Mental Capacity Act 2005 is built on five statutory principles, including the presumption of capacity and that any act for a person lacking capacity must be in their best interests and least restrictive.
_Source:_ SCIE - Mental Capacity Act 2005 at a glance - https://www.scie.org.uk/mca/introduction/mental-capacity-act-2005-at-a-glance/

**Q596. [Easy]** Under the Mental Capacity Act 2005, a person must be assumed to have capacity unless it is established that they lack it. What is this principle called?
- A. The best interests principle
- **B. The presumption of capacity [correct]**
- C. The least restrictive principle
- D. The functional test
_Why:_ The first statutory principle of the Mental Capacity Act 2005 is the presumption of capacity: every adult has the right to make their own decisions and is assumed able to do so unless proved otherwise.
_Source:_ SCIE - Mental Capacity Act 2005 at a glance - https://www.scie.org.uk/mca/introduction/mental-capacity-act-2005-at-a-glance/

**Q597. [Medium]** As of 2026, which legal framework is in force in England for authorising a deprivation of liberty in a care home or hospital for someone who lacks capacity?
- A. The Liberty Protection Safeguards (LPS)
- **B. The Deprivation of Liberty Safeguards (DoLS) [correct]**
- C. Section 5(2) of the Mental Health Act
- D. There is no legal framework
_Why:_ The Deprivation of Liberty Safeguards (DoLS) remain the operative framework. The Liberty Protection Safeguards (LPS), though passed in 2019, have not been implemented and remain delayed.
_Source:_ GOV.UK / CQC - Deprivation of Liberty Safeguards (2025) - https://www.gov.uk/government/statistics/deprivation-of-liberty-safeguards-england-2024-to-2025

**Q598. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient who lacks capacity faces a decision about serious medical treatment. They have no family or friends who can be consulted about their best interests._ Which safeguard under the Mental Capacity Act 2005 must be instructed in this situation?
- A. An Approved Mental Health Professional (AMHP)
- B. A Second Opinion Appointed Doctor (SOAD)
- **C. An Independent Mental Capacity Advocate (IMCA) [correct]**
- D. A nearest relative
_Why:_ An Independent Mental Capacity Advocate (IMCA) must be instructed for decisions about serious medical treatment or a change in accommodation when the person lacks capacity and has no appropriate family or friends to consult.
_Source:_ SCIE - Mental Capacity Act 2005 at a glance - https://www.scie.org.uk/mca/introduction/mental-capacity-act-2005-at-a-glance/

**Q599. [Hard]** Under the Mental Capacity Act 2005, the functional part of the capacity test asks whether the person can do four things. Which of the following is one of them?
- A. Name their next of kin
- **B. Retain the relevant information long enough to make the decision [correct]**
- C. Agree with the clinician's recommendation
- D. Demonstrate they have made a wise decision
_Why:_ The functional test requires the person to be able to understand, retain, and use or weigh the relevant information, and to communicate their decision. A person is not incapable merely for making an unwise decision.
_Source:_ SCIE - Mental Capacity Act 2005 at a glance - https://www.scie.org.uk/mca/introduction/mental-capacity-act-2005-at-a-glance/

## Patient Rights, Advocacy & Tribunals

**Q600. [Easy]** Under Section 132 of the Mental Health Act 1983, hospital managers have a duty to inform a newly detained patient of their legal rights. How must this information be given?
- A. Orally only
- B. In writing only
- **C. Both orally and in writing [correct]**
- D. By showing the patient a poster on the ward
_Why:_ Section 132 requires managers to take practicable steps to ensure the patient understands their detention and rights, and these steps must include giving the information both orally and in writing.
_Source:_ Mental Health Act 1983 - Section 132 - https://www.mentalhealthlaw.co.uk/MHA_1983_s132

**Q601. [Easy]** When must the Section 132 duty to inform a detained patient of their rights be carried out?
- A. Within 14 days of detention
- **B. As soon as practicable after detention begins [correct]**
- C. Only if the patient asks
- D. At the first ward round
_Why:_ Section 132 requires the steps to inform the patient to be taken as soon as practicable after the commencement of the patient's detention, not delayed.
_Source:_ Mental Health Act 1983 - Section 132 - https://www.mentalhealthlaw.co.uk/MHA_1983_s132

**Q602. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient detained under Section 3 is not fluent in English and has a hearing impairment._ What does Section 132 of the Mental Health Act 1983 require the trust to do in this situation?
- A. Wait until the patient learns English
- **B. Take reasonable steps to give the rights explanation in a way appropriate to the patient's needs (e.g. interpreter) [correct]**
- C. Rely on a family member to translate informally
- D. Only provide the written leaflet, as that satisfies the duty
_Why:_ Section 132 places a duty to take all reasonable steps to facilitate the patient's understanding; where a patient has a language, learning or sensory need, arrangements must be made so the explanation is delivered appropriately.
_Source:_ MHA Code of Practice - Section 132 rights (2015) - https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/code-of-practice-mental-health-act-1983

**Q603. [Easy]** What is the main role of an Independent Mental Health Advocate (IMHA)?
- A. To provide the patient's medical treatment
- **B. To help the patient understand and exercise their rights under the Mental Health Act [correct]**
- C. To act as the patient's Responsible Clinician
- D. To decide whether the patient is discharged
_Why:_ An IMHA is a specialist advocate whose role is to help qualifying patients understand and exercise their rights under the Mental Health Act 1983 and participate in decisions about their care and treatment.
_Source:_ Care Quality Commission - Independent mental health advocates - https://www.cqc.org.uk/about-us/mental-health/your-rights-under-mental-health-act/imha

**Q604. [Medium]** Which of the following patients is a 'qualifying patient' entitled to an Independent Mental Health Advocate (IMHA)?
- A. A patient held under Section 5(2) doctor's holding power
- B. A patient detained under Section 4 in an emergency
- **C. A patient detained under Section 3 for treatment [correct]**
- D. A patient held under Section 136 at a place of safety
_Why:_ Patients detained under longer-term sections such as Section 3 (and those on a CTO or under guardianship) are qualifying patients. Those under short-term holding powers (s5), emergency s4, or s135/136 place of safety are not entitled to an IMHA.
_Source:_ Mind - Independent mental health advocates (IMHAs) - https://www.mind.org.uk/information-support/guides-to-support-and-services/advocacy/imhas/

**Q605. [Hard]** _Scenario: An informal (voluntary) adult patient on the ward is being considered for a specific treatment._ In which circumstance does an informal, voluntary adult patient become entitled to an Independent Mental Health Advocate (IMHA)?
- A. Whenever they are admitted to any mental health ward
- **B. When they are being considered for a Section 57 treatment such as neurosurgery for mental disorder [correct]**
- C. When they request extra visiting time
- D. When their leave is refused
_Why:_ Informal patients generally do not qualify for IMHA support, but there is a specific exception: an informal patient being considered for a Section 57 treatment (e.g. neurosurgery for mental disorder) qualifies, as do under-18s being considered for Section 58A/ECT.
_Source:_ MHA Code of Practice - IMHA eligibility (2015) - https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/code-of-practice-mental-health-act-1983

**Q606. [Medium]** Under the Independent Mental Health Advocates (England) Regulations 2008, which is an essential requirement for a person to act as an IMHA?
- A. They must be a registered nurse
- B. They must be employed by the detaining hospital
- **C. They must act independently of anyone professionally concerned with the patient's medical treatment [correct]**
- D. They must be approved by the patient's nearest relative
_Why:_ The 2008 Regulations require an IMHA to have appropriate experience/training AND to act independently of any person professionally concerned with the qualifying patient's medical treatment and of any person requesting the visit.
_Source:_ legislation.gov.uk - MHA 1983 (IMHA) (England) Regulations 2008 (2008) - https://www.legislation.gov.uk/uksi/2008/3166/made

**Q607. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient is detained under Section 2 for assessment._ Within what period from the start of detention can a Section 2 patient apply to the First-tier Tribunal (Mental Health)?
- **A. Within the first 14 days [correct]**
- B. Within the first 28 days
- C. At any time during the six months
- D. Only after the section is renewed
_Why:_ A Section 2 patient may apply to the Tribunal within the first 14 days of detention (Section 2 lasts up to 28 days).
_Source:_ GOV.UK - Apply to the Mental Health Tribunal - https://www.gov.uk/mental-health-tribunal

**Q608. [Hard]** How often can a patient detained under Section 3 apply to the First-tier Tribunal (Mental Health)?
- A. Only once, ever
- **B. Once in the first six months, once in the second six months, then once each year [correct]**
- C. Every 14 days
- D. Only after the nearest relative applies first
_Why:_ A Section 3 patient may apply once during the first six-month period of detention, once in the second six months, and then once in each subsequent 12-month period.
_Source:_ Royal College of Psychiatrists - Guide to Mental Health Tribunals - https://www.rcpsych.ac.uk/mental-health/treatments-and-wellbeing/guide-to-mental-health-tribunals

**Q609. [Easy]** How much does a detained patient have to pay for a solicitor to represent them at a First-tier Tribunal (Mental Health) hearing?
