- **A. Nothing - legal aid is free and non-means-tested for tribunal representation [correct]**
- B. A fixed fee of 100 pounds
- C. It depends on their savings and income
- D. The ward pays and recovers it later
_Why:_ Legal aid for Mental Health Tribunal representation is free and non-means-tested, so the patient pays nothing regardless of savings or income.
_Source:_ Mind - What is the mental health tribunal? - https://www.mind.org.uk/information-support/legal-rights/leaving-hospital/mental-health-tribunal/

**Q610. [Medium]** _Scenario: A detained patient tells staff they want to appeal but has not contacted a solicitor and may lack capacity to appoint one._ What can the First-tier Tribunal (Mental Health) do?
- A. Refuse to hear the case until the patient finds their own solicitor
- **B. Appoint a solicitor for the patient, including where they lack capacity and it is in their best interests [correct]**
- C. Ask the Responsible Clinician to choose a solicitor
- D. Proceed only if a family member pays for representation
_Why:_ The tribunal can appoint a legal representative if the patient wishes to be represented, and may appoint one where the patient lacks capacity to appoint their own and it is in their best interests.
_Source:_ Mind - What is the mental health tribunal? - https://www.mind.org.uk/information-support/legal-rights/leaving-hospital/mental-health-tribunal/

**Q611. [Hard]** When a Section 2 patient applies to the First-tier Tribunal (Mental Health), how quickly must the hearing normally take place?
- A. Within 7 days of the application
- **B. Within about 10 days of the application (since the 2022 rule change) [correct]**
- C. Within 8 weeks of the application
- D. Within 6 months of the application
_Why:_ The Tribunal Procedure (Amendment No. 2) Rules 2022 changed the Section 2 hearing deadline from 7 days to 10 days (in force from 1 November 2022); Section 3 hearings are typically heard within around 8 weeks.
_Source:_ Mental Health Law Online - Section 2 (2022) - https://www.mentalhealthlaw.co.uk/Section_2:_admission_for_assessment

**Q612. [Easy]** Which body hears Hospital Managers' hearings and can order a detained patient's discharge?
- **A. A panel of associate hospital managers appointed by the trust [correct]**
- B. The First-tier Tribunal
- C. The Care Quality Commission
- D. The Court of Protection
_Why:_ Hospital Managers' hearings are conducted by a panel of associate (or 'Mental Health Act') managers - volunteers appointed by the trust board - who have power under Section 23 to discharge detained patients and CTO patients.
_Source:_ East London NHS FT - Hospital Managers Power of Discharge - https://www.elft.nhs.uk/health-professionals/mental-health-law/hospital-managers-power-discharge

**Q613. [Hard]** _Scenario: A Hospital Managers' panel is reviewing a detained patient's case._ How many panel members must agree for a patient to be discharged at a Hospital Managers' hearing?
- A. A simple majority of the panel
- **B. At least three panel members, all of whom must agree [correct]**
- C. Any one member
- D. Only the chair
_Why:_ A Hospital Managers' panel is made up of three or more associate managers, and all of them (at least three) must agree in order to discharge the patient.
_Source:_ Rethink Mental Illness - Discharge from the Mental Health Act - https://www.rethink.org/advice-and-information/rights-laws-and-criminal-justice/mental-health-laws/discharge-from-the-mental-health-act/

**Q614. [Medium]** _Scenario: The nearest relative of a Section 3 patient wants to secure the patient's discharge._ What must the nearest relative do to order the discharge of a detained patient?
- **A. Give at least 72 hours' written notice to the hospital managers [correct]**
- B. Attend the next ward round in person
- C. Apply directly to the tribunal
- D. Get a court order
_Why:_ The nearest relative may order discharge by giving the hospital managers not less than 72 hours' notice in writing.
_Source:_ Weightmans - How can the nearest relative request discharge? - https://www.weightmans.com/insights/how-can-the-nearest-relative-request-discharge/

**Q615. [Hard]** _Scenario: A nearest relative has given 72 hours' notice to discharge a Section 3 patient._ On what ground can the Responsible Clinician issue a barring report (report barring discharge)?
- A. That the patient still has symptoms of a mental disorder
- **B. That the patient would be likely to act in a manner dangerous to themselves or others if discharged [correct]**
- C. That the nearest relative is not suitable
- D. That the ward is short-staffed
_Why:_ The Responsible Clinician can bar the nearest relative's discharge only by certifying that the patient would, if discharged, be likely to act in a manner dangerous to themselves or to other persons - dangerousness is the sole ground.
_Source:_ Mental Health Law Online - Right to request discharge - https://www.mentalhealthlaw.co.uk/Right_to_request_discharge_of_s2_or_s3_or_CTO

**Q616. [Hard]** _Scenario: A Responsible Clinician has issued a barring report preventing a nearest relative from discharging a Section 3 patient._ For how long is the nearest relative then barred from ordering discharge again?
- A. 24 hours
- B. 28 days
- **C. 6 months from the date of the barring report [correct]**
- D. Permanently
_Why:_ Once a barring report is issued, the nearest relative cannot order the patient's discharge again for a period of six months from the date of the barring report.
_Source:_ Weightmans - How can the nearest relative request discharge? - https://www.weightmans.com/insights/how-can-the-nearest-relative-request-discharge/

**Q617. [Hard]** _Scenario: A nearest relative's discharge order for a Section 3 patient has been barred by the Responsible Clinician._ What right does the nearest relative then acquire?
- A. To discharge the patient immediately anyway
- **B. To apply to the First-tier Tribunal within 28 days of being informed of the barring report [correct]**
- C. To dismiss the Responsible Clinician
- D. No further rights
_Why:_ When a nearest relative's discharge of a Section 3 patient is barred, they gain the right to apply to the First-tier Tribunal, and must do so within 28 days of being informed of the barring report.
_Source:_ Mental Health Law Online - Right to request discharge - https://www.mentalhealthlaw.co.uk/Right_to_request_discharge_of_s2_or_s3_or_CTO

**Q618. [Medium]** _Scenario: A Section 3 patient has never applied to the tribunal and their case has not otherwise been reviewed._ Under Section 68 of the Mental Health Act 1983, what triggers an automatic referral of the patient's case to the tribunal by hospital managers?
- **A. The patient reaching six months of detention without any tribunal review having taken place [correct]**
- B. The patient asking for a second opinion
- C. The patient going on Section 17 leave
- D. The patient's nearest relative visiting
_Why:_ Section 68 requires hospital managers to refer a patient to the tribunal if, on expiry of six months from the applicable day, no tribunal review has taken place.
_Source:_ legislation.gov.uk - Mental Health Act 1983 Section 68 (1983) - https://www.legislation.gov.uk/ukpga/1983/20/section/68

**Q619. [Medium]** What is the purpose of the automatic (mandatory) referral to tribunal under Section 68?
- A. To speed up medication changes
- **B. To ensure patients who do not exercise their own right to apply still receive independent judicial oversight of their detention [correct]**
- C. To replace Hospital Managers' hearings
- D. To transfer the patient to another hospital
_Why:_ The mandatory reference scheme ensures that patients who do not, or cannot, apply themselves still get their detention independently scrutinised by the tribunal.
_Source:_ Mental Health Law Online - Mandatory and discretionary references - https://www.mentalhealthlaw.co.uk/Mandatory_and_discretionary_references

**Q620. [Easy]** Which organisation has the statutory duty to monitor how the Mental Health Act is used and to visit and interview detained patients in England?
- **A. The Care Quality Commission (CQC) [correct]**
- B. NHS England
- C. The General Medical Council
- D. The Parliamentary Ombudsman
_Why:_ The CQC has a statutory duty to monitor how providers use their powers and discharge their duties under the Mental Health Act, including visiting and interviewing detained patients.
_Source:_ Care Quality Commission - Mental Health Act - https://www.cqc.org.uk/guidance-providers/mental-health-services/mental-health-act

**Q621. [Medium]** _Scenario: A detained patient and their family are unhappy about how the Mental Health Act powers have been used on the ward._ Which body specifically investigates complaints about the use of Mental Health Act powers?
- **A. The Care Quality Commission (CQC) [correct]**
- B. The local council
- C. The police
- D. The coroner
_Why:_ The CQC investigates complaints about how powers have been used or duties carried out under the Mental Health Act; such complaints can be made by patients, staff or any member of the public.
_Source:_ Care Quality Commission - Complain about the use of the Mental Health Act - https://www.cqc.org.uk/contact-us/how-complain/complain-about-use-mental-health-act

**Q622. [Easy]** What is the key difference between an advance decision and an advance statement?
- **A. An advance decision is legally binding to refuse specific treatment; an advance statement records preferences but is not legally binding [correct]**
- B. Both are legally binding in all circumstances
- C. An advance statement is legally binding; an advance decision is only advisory
- D. Neither has any legal status
_Why:_ An advance decision (to refuse treatment) made under the Mental Capacity Act 2005 is legally binding if valid and applicable, whereas an advance statement records general wishes and preferences that should be taken into account but is not legally binding.
_Source:_ Rethink Mental Illness - Advance statements and advance decisions - https://www.rethink.org/advice-and-information/rights-laws-and-criminal-justice/your-rights/advance-statements-and-advance-decisions-planning-your-future-care/

**Q623. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient detained under the Mental Health Act made a valid advance decision refusing a particular treatment before being sectioned._ How does detention under the Mental Health Act affect that advance decision?
- A. It remains fully binding for all treatment
- **B. Compulsory treatment for mental disorder can generally override it, but an advance decision refusing ECT must still be respected (with limited exceptions) [correct]**
- C. It is automatically cancelled the moment the patient is sectioned
- D. Only the nearest relative can enforce it
_Why:_ Under Part 4 of the Mental Health Act, treatment for mental disorder can be given to a detained patient despite an advance decision; however, Section 58A gives special protection so a valid advance decision refusing ECT must generally be respected.
_Source:_ Mind - Advance decisions (legal information) - https://www.mind.org.uk/information-support/legal-rights/mental-capacity-act-2005/advance-decisions/

**Q624. [Medium]** _Scenario: A solicitor is deciding whether they can act for a detained patient at a tribunal._ What is the correct starting point when assessing whether the patient can instruct a solicitor for their tribunal?
- A. Assume the patient lacks capacity because they are detained
- **B. Presume the patient has capacity to give instructions, as with anyone else, and assess capacity to instruct on the conduct of proceedings [correct]**
- C. Ask the Responsible Clinician to decide for the patient
- D. Only proceed if the nearest relative agrees
_Why:_ The starting point is the statutory presumption of capacity: the patient is presumed to have capacity to instruct a solicitor on the conduct of the proceedings, and detention alone does not remove it.
_Source:_ Mental Health Law Online - Case preparation - https://www.mentalhealthlaw.co.uk/Case_preparation

## Restrictive Practice & Human Rights

**Q625. [Easy]** How does the Mental Health Act (MHA) 1983 Code of Practice (2015) define seclusion?
- **A. The supervised confinement and isolation of a patient, away from other patients, in an area from which the patient is prevented from leaving [correct]**
- B. Nursing a patient one-to-one in an open ward area
- C. Any use of as-required (PRN) sedating medication
- D. Confining a patient to their own bedroom overnight for sleep
_Why:_ The Code (para 26.103) defines seclusion as the supervised confinement and isolation of a patient, away from other patients, in an area from which they are prevented from leaving, to contain severe behavioural disturbance likely to cause harm to others.
_Source:_ MHA 1983 Code of Practice, para 26.103 (2015) - https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/code-of-practice-mental-health-act-1983

**Q626. [Easy]** According to the Mental Health Act (MHA) Code of Practice, seclusion should only be used for which group of patients?
- A. Any patient on the ward showing agitation
- **B. Only patients detained under the Mental Health Act [correct]**
- C. Only informal (voluntary) patients
- D. Only patients under the age of 18
_Why:_ The Code states seclusion should only be used in relation to patients detained under the Act, and it should never be used as punishment, a threat, or because of a shortage of staff.
_Source:_ MHA 1983 Code of Practice, ch 26 (2015) - https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/code-of-practice-mental-health-act-1983

**Q627. [Medium]** _Scenario: A nurse is asked why seclusion cannot be used tonight simply because the ward is short-staffed and a patient is loud but not dangerous._ Which statement best reflects the Mental Health Act (MHA) Code of Practice position on when seclusion must NOT be used?
- A. Seclusion may be used for staff convenience if documented
- **B. Seclusion must never be used as a punishment, a threat, or because of a shortage of staff [correct]**
- C. Seclusion is acceptable to manage any noisy patient
- D. Seclusion can substitute for one-to-one observation to save staff
_Why:_ The Code is explicit that seclusion is only for containing severe behavioural disturbance likely to harm others, and must never be used as punishment, threat, for convenience, or because of staff shortages.
_Source:_ MHA 1983 Code of Practice, ch 26 (2015) - https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/code-of-practice-mental-health-act-1983

**Q628. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient has just been placed in seclusion on an inpatient ward._ Under the Mental Health Act (MHA) Code of Practice, how frequently should the nursing review of a secluded patient take place?
- A. Every 30 minutes
- **B. At least every 2 hours [correct]**
- C. Once per shift
- D. Only when the patient requests it
_Why:_ The Code requires a documented nursing review at least every 2 hours, alongside continuous observation of the secluded patient.
_Source:_ MHA 1983 Code of Practice, ch 26 (2015) - https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/code-of-practice-mental-health-act-1983

**Q629. [Hard]** _Scenario: Seclusion was started at 22:00 and was not personally authorised by a doctor at the outset._ Under the Mental Health Act (MHA) Code of Practice, when must the first medical review of the secluded patient occur, and how often thereafter until the first internal multidisciplinary team (MDT) review?
- **A. Within 1 hour, then every 4 hours [correct]**
- B. Within 4 hours, then every 12 hours
- C. Within 24 hours only
- D. Within 15 minutes, then hourly
_Why:_ If seclusion is not authorised by a doctor at the start, a medical review must take place within 1 hour of seclusion commencing, and medical reviews continue at least every 4 hours until the first internal MDT review.
_Source:_ MHA 1983 Code of Practice, ch 26 (2015) - https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/code-of-practice-mental-health-act-1983

**Q630. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient remains in seclusion and the team is planning the independent review._ Under the Mental Health Act (MHA) Code of Practice, an independent multidisciplinary team (MDT) review of seclusion should be triggered once the patient has been secluded for how long?
- A. 1 hour continuously
- **B. 8 hours consecutively, or 12 hours intermittently over a 48-hour period [correct]**
- C. 72 hours continuously
- D. As soon as seclusion begins
_Why:_ The Code requires an independent MDT review if seclusion continues for 8 hours consecutively, or for 12 hours intermittently over a period of 48 hours.
_Source:_ MHA 1983 Code of Practice, ch 26 (2015) - https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/code-of-practice-mental-health-act-1983

**Q631. [Hard]** After the first internal multidisciplinary team (MDT) review of a secluded patient, how often must medical seclusion reviews then take place under the Mental Health Act (MHA) Code of Practice?
- A. Weekly
- **B. At least twice daily, with at least one carried out by the responsible clinician (RC) [correct]**
- C. Only once every 24 hours
- D. Every 4 hours indefinitely
_Why:_ Following the first internal MDT review, the Code requires medical reviews at least twice daily, of which at least one each day should be conducted by the patient's responsible clinician (RC) or their deputy.
_Source:_ MHA 1983 Code of Practice, ch 26 (2015) - https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/code-of-practice-mental-health-act-1983

**Q632. [Medium]** Which description best matches long-term segregation as understood in Mental Health Act (MHA) and Care Quality Commission (CQC) guidance?
- A. A brief episode of seclusion lasting under one hour
- **B. Nursing or caring for a person in enforced isolation, kept apart from other patients on an ongoing basis to reduce a sustained risk of harm to others [correct]**
- C. Placing a patient in a low-stimulus room for 10 minutes
- D. Locking the main ward door at night for all patients
_Why:_ Long-term segregation refers to keeping a patient in enforced, ongoing isolation from other patients because they present a sustained risk of harm judged to be a lasting feature, distinct from the immediate, short-term containment of seclusion.
_Source:_ MHA 1983 Code of Practice, ch 26 (2015) - https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/code-of-practice-mental-health-act-1983

**Q633. [Easy]** How does the Mental Health Act (MHA) Code of Practice define blanket restrictions?
- A. Restrictions agreed individually in a patient's care plan
- **B. Rules or policies restricting liberty and rights that are routinely applied to all patients, or classes of patients, without individual risk assessment to justify them [correct]**
- C. Restrictions that only apply to patients on Section 3
- D. Temporary restrictions lasting less than 24 hours
_Why:_ The Code (para 8.5) defines blanket restrictions as rules or policies that restrict liberty and other rights, routinely applied to all patients or classes of patients, without individual risk assessment to justify their application.
_Source:_ MHA 1983 Code of Practice, para 8.5 (2015) - https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/code-of-practice-mental-health-act-1983

**Q634. [Medium]** _Scenario: A ward routinely locks all patients' bedrooms during the day and bans everyone from having phone chargers, without any individual assessment._ Why does the Mental Health Act (MHA) Code of Practice say such blanket restrictions should be avoided?
- A. They are cheaper to run and so should be discouraged
- **B. They restrict patients' liberty and rights without individual justification and should never be for the convenience of the provider [correct]**
- C. They are only a problem if a relative complains
- D. They are acceptable provided they apply to every ward in the trust
_Why:_ The Code states blanket restrictions should be avoided unless justified by a specific risk for the particular ward or group; any restriction must not be for the convenience of the service and should be individually justified and regularly reviewed.
_Source:_ MHA 1983 Code of Practice, ch 8 (2015) - https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/code-of-practice-mental-health-act-1983

**Q635. [Easy]** Which is the first of the five guiding principles in the Mental Health Act (MHA) Code of Practice (2015)?
- A. Efficiency and equity
- **B. Least restrictive option and maximising independence [correct]**
- C. Purpose and effectiveness
- D. Respect and dignity
_Why:_ The Code lists five guiding principles; the first is 'least restrictive option and maximising independence', meaning restrictions on liberty should be kept to the minimum necessary.
_Source:_ MHA 1983 Code of Practice, ch 1 (2015) - https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/code-of-practice-mental-health-act-1983

**Q636. [Medium]** _Scenario: Two options could manage a patient's risk: increased observation, or seclusion._ Applying the least restrictive principle, which approach should the team take?
- A. Always choose seclusion because it is safest for staff
- **B. Choose the option that achieves the purpose while imposing the fewest restrictions on the patient's liberty [correct]**
- C. Choose whichever option is quickest regardless of restriction
- D. Choose the more restrictive option to be cautious
_Why:_ The least restrictive principle requires that where an aim can be achieved safely, the option imposing the least restriction on liberty should be used; seclusion is a last resort for the shortest time.
_Source:_ MHA 1983 Code of Practice, ch 1 and ch 26 (2015) - https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/code-of-practice-mental-health-act-1983

**Q637. [Easy]** The Mental Health Units (Use of Force) Act 2018 is commonly known by which name, and in memory of whom?
- **A. Seni's Law, after Olaseni Lewis [correct]**
- B. Herbert Protocol, after a missing persons scheme
- C. Bournewood safeguards, after a legal case
- D. Winterbourne Law, after a hospital
_Why:_ The Act is known as Seni's Law, named after Olaseni (Seni) Lewis, who died in 2010 after being restrained as a voluntary patient in a mental health unit.
_Source:_ UK Government - Mental Health Units (Use of Force) Act 2018 statutory guidance (2021) - https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/mental-health-units-use-of-force-act-2018-statutory-guidance-for-nhs-organisations-in-england-and-police-forces-in-england-and-wales

**Q638. [Medium]** Under the Mental Health Units (Use of Force) Act 2018, every mental health unit must appoint a 'responsible person'. Who is this expected to be?
- A. Any available nurse on shift
- **B. A senior board-level individual, such as the chief nurse or medical director, of appropriate seniority [correct]**
- C. The patient's nearest relative
- D. A member of the local police force
_Why:_ The statutory guidance requires the responsible person to be a board-level or equivalent senior figure (for example the chief nurse or medical director) who ensures the organisation complies with the Act.
_Source:_ UK Government - Mental Health Units (Use of Force) Act 2018 statutory guidance (2021) - https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/mental-health-units-use-of-force-act-2018-statutory-guidance-for-nhs-organisations-in-england-and-police-forces-in-england-and-wales

**Q639. [Hard]** The Mental Health Units (Use of Force) Act 2018 defines 'use of force'. Which of the following does its definition include alongside physical restraint?
- A. Only physical holds by staff
- **B. Mechanical restraint, chemical restraint, and isolation (seclusion or segregation) [correct]**
- C. Only verbal de-escalation techniques
- D. Only the use of locked ward doors
_Why:_ The Act's definition of use of force covers physical, mechanical and chemical restraint, and isolation (which includes any seclusion or segregation imposed on the patient).
_Source:_ UK Government - Mental Health Units (Use of Force) Act 2018 statutory guidance (2021) - https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/mental-health-units-use-of-force-act-2018-statutory-guidance-for-nhs-organisations-in-england-and-police-forces-in-england-and-wales

**Q640. [Hard]** _Scenario: A trust wants to analyse whether force is used disproportionately against particular groups._ Under the Mental Health Units (Use of Force) Act 2018, what must records of every use of force include to enable this analysis?
- A. Only the date and duration of the incident
- **B. Demographic data across the protected characteristics defined in the Equality Act 2010, such as race, sex, age and disability [correct]**
- C. Only the name of the staff member involved
- D. Nothing beyond a tick to say force was used
_Why:_ The Act requires the responsible person to record each use of force including the patient's relevant protected characteristics under the Equality Act 2010, so disproportionate use against groups such as by race, sex, age or disability can be identified.
_Source:_ UK Government - Mental Health Units (Use of Force) Act 2018 statutory guidance (2021) - https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/mental-health-units-use-of-force-act-2018-statutory-guidance-for-nhs-organisations-in-england-and-police-forces-in-england-and-wales

**Q641. [Medium]** _Scenario: Police are called into the ward to assist staff with an incident._ Under the Mental Health Units (Use of Force) Act 2018, what is required of the attending police officers?
- A. They must remain outside the building at all times
- **B. They must wear and operate a body camera at all times when reasonably practicable [correct]**
- C. They must not carry any recording equipment
- D. They must take over full charge of the unit
_Why:_ The Act requires police officers going into a mental health unit on duty to assist staff to wear and operate a body camera at all times when it is reasonably practicable.
_Source:_ UK Government - Mental Health Units (Use of Force) Act 2018 statutory guidance (2021) - https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/mental-health-units-use-of-force-act-2018-statutory-guidance-for-nhs-organisations-in-england-and-police-forces-in-england-and-wales

**Q642. [Medium]** Under the Mental Health Units (Use of Force) Act 2018, what must a newly admitted patient be given about the use of force?
- A. Nothing, unless they ask
- **B. Published information about their rights, stating force is only ever used proportionately, as a last resort, and never to cause pain, humiliation or as punishment [correct]**
- C. A bill for any restraint used
- D. A consent form waiving their rights
_Why:_ The Act requires patients to be given published information about their rights in relation to use of force, including that force is only used proportionately and as a last resort and never to cause pain, suffering, humiliation or as punishment.
_Source:_ UK Government - Mental Health Units (Use of Force) Act 2018 statutory guidance (2021) - https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/mental-health-units-use-of-force-act-2018-statutory-guidance-for-nhs-organisations-in-england-and-police-forces-in-england-and-wales

**Q643. [Easy]** Which article of the Human Rights Act 1998 protects against torture and inhuman or degrading treatment, and is most often engaged by seclusion and restraint?
- A. Article 2 (right to life)
- **B. Article 3 (freedom from torture and inhuman or degrading treatment) [correct]**
- C. Article 8 (respect for private and family life)
- D. Article 12 (right to marry)
_Why:_ The Care Quality Commission identifies Article 3 as most relevant to restrictive measures such as restraint and seclusion; improper use can amount to inhuman or degrading treatment.
_Source:_ Care Quality Commission - Human rights articles relevant to health and social care - https://www.cqc.org.uk/about-us/our-updated-human-rights-approach/human-rights-articles-relevant-health-and-social-care

**Q644. [Easy]** Which article of the Human Rights Act 1998 protects the right to liberty and security, and is engaged when someone is detained or unlawfully secluded?
- A. Article 3
- **B. Article 5 (right to liberty and security) [correct]**
- C. Article 8
- D. Article 10
_Why:_ Article 5 protects the right to liberty and security; the Care Quality Commission links it to restrictive practices such as being kept in seclusion for extensive periods without proper review.
_Source:_ Care Quality Commission - Human rights articles relevant to health and social care - https://www.cqc.org.uk/about-us/our-updated-human-rights-approach/human-rights-articles-relevant-health-and-social-care

**Q645. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient complains that keeping them isolated in seclusion, cut off from family contact, breaches their rights._ Which Human Rights Act 1998 article, concerning respect for private and family life, is most directly relevant to this complaint?
- A. Article 2
- **B. Article 8 (respect for private and family life) [correct]**
- C. Article 5
- D. Article 6
_Why:_ Article 8 protects respect for private and family life; seclusion, if improperly used, can breach Article 8 unless it can be justified as necessary and proportionate under Article 8(2).
_Source:_ Care Quality Commission - Human rights articles relevant to health and social care - https://www.cqc.org.uk/about-us/our-updated-human-rights-approach/human-rights-articles-relevant-health-and-social-care

**Q646. [Hard]** _Scenario: A detained patient dies suddenly. The trust must ensure the state meets its human rights obligations._ Which Human Rights Act 1998 article imposes a positive duty on the state to protect life and can require an independent investigation into the death of a detained patient?
- **A. Article 2 (right to life) [correct]**
- B. Article 8 (private and family life)
- C. Article 14 (non-discrimination)
- D. Article 10 (freedom of expression)
_Why:_ Article 2 protects the right to life and carries a positive obligation to protect life and to investigate deaths of those in state detention, including detained mental health patients.
_Source:_ Care Quality Commission - Human rights articles relevant to health and social care - https://www.cqc.org.uk/about-us/our-updated-human-rights-approach/human-rights-articles-relevant-health-and-social-care

**Q647. [Hard]** _Scenario: An audit finds one ethnic group is restrained far more often than others without clinical justification._ Which Human Rights Act 1998 article, prohibiting discrimination in the enjoyment of other rights, is most directly engaged?
- A. Article 5
- **B. Article 14 (prohibition of discrimination) [correct]**
- C. Article 2
- D. Article 9
_Why:_ Article 14 prohibits discrimination in securing the other Convention rights; disproportionate use of force against a protected group engages Article 14 (and links to the Equality Act 2010 and the Use of Force Act's recording duties).
_Source:_ Care Quality Commission - Human rights articles relevant to health and social care - https://www.cqc.org.uk/about-us/our-updated-human-rights-approach/human-rights-articles-relevant-health-and-social-care

**Q648. [Easy]** Chemical restraint, as a restrictive intervention, refers to which of the following?
- A. Locking a patient in a room
- **B. Using medication intended to prevent, restrict or subdue a patient's movement, primarily for behavioural control [correct]**
- C. Applying a physical hold to a patient's arm
- D. Using a mechanical belt or strap
_Why:_ Chemical restraint means the use of medication intended to prevent, restrict or subdue movement, distinct from physical restraint (bodily holds) and mechanical restraint (devices).
_Source:_ UK Government - Mental Health Units (Use of Force) Act 2018 statutory guidance (2021) - https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/mental-health-units-use-of-force-act-2018-statutory-guidance-for-nhs-organisations-in-england-and-police-forces-in-england-and-wales

**Q649. [Hard]** _Scenario: A trust is choosing a training provider for staff who use physical interventions._ Since April 2020, what has NHS England and the Care Quality Commission (CQC) expected of restrictive intervention training in relevant services?
- A. No formal standard is required
- **B. Training must be certified as compliant with the Restraint Reduction Network (RRN) Training Standards, which focus on human rights and reducing reliance on restrictive practice [correct]**
- C. Only online e-learning is permitted
- D. Training must be delivered solely by police officers
_Why:_ From April 2020, NHS-commissioned services using restrictive interventions are expected to use training certified against the Restraint Reduction Network Training Standards (2019), which emphasise human rights and minimising restrictive practice.
_Source:_ Restraint Reduction Network - RRN Training Standards (2019) - https://restraintreductionnetwork.org/know-the-standard/

## Consent, Capacity & Chaperones

**Q650. [Easy]** _Scenario: An adult patient is about to have an intimate examination._ What is the standard regarding a chaperone?
- A. A chaperone is optional
- **B. A formal chaperone should be offered and is expected for intimate examinations [correct]**
- C. Only needed if the patient asks
- D. A family member must act as chaperone
_Why:_ A trained, formal chaperone should be offered for intimate examinations to protect both patient and clinician. It should be documented.
_Source:_ Trust Chaperone Policy; NMC / GMC guidance

**Q651. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient needs an interpreter for an intimate procedure and their friend offers to interpret._ Is it acceptable to use the friend as interpreter?
- A. Yes, if the patient agrees
- **B. No, a professional interpreter should be used, not family or friends [correct]**
- C. Yes, family always know best
- D. Only if no professional interpreter exists anywhere
_Why:_ Family or friends should not be used as interpreters for clinical matters - use a professional interpreter to protect accuracy, confidentiality and consent.
_Source:_ Trust Chaperone / Interpreting guidance; NHS England

**Q652. [Medium]** _Scenario: A person who has taken an overdose has capacity and is refusing transfer to the Emergency Department._ What is the first clinical/legal step?
- A. Physically restrain them for transfer
- **B. Carry out a mental capacity assessment for this decision [correct]**
- C. Let them leave with no further action
- D. Wait until they become unconscious
_Why:_ A decision-specific mental capacity assessment is required. If the person lacks capacity, act in their best interests; if they have capacity, consider a Mental Health Act assessment and safeguarding.
_Source:_ Mental Capacity Act 2005; Trust Overdose Policy

**Q653. [Hard]** _Scenario: A young person under 18 has taken an overdose, appears to have capacity, and is refusing treatment._ How should this be framed?
- A. Simply respect the refusal and take no further action
- **B. As a safeguarding issue, even where the young person has capacity [correct]**
- C. Ignore it as they are nearly an adult
- D. Only involve parents and nobody else
_Why:_ Refusal of potentially life-saving treatment by a young person is a safeguarding concern. Treatment refusal by under-18s can be overridden through the appropriate legal routes, and safeguarding must be considered.
_Source:_ Trust Overdose Policy; safeguarding children guidance

## Self-harm & Suicide Prevention

**Q654. [Easy]** According to National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) guidance on self-harm (NG225), should risk assessment tools and scales be used to predict future suicide or repetition of self-harm?
- A. Yes, they should be used to triage every patient
- **B. No, they should not be used to predict future suicide or repetition of self-harm [correct]**
- C. Only if completed by a consultant psychiatrist
- D. Yes, but only for adults over 65
_Why:_ NICE recommendation 1.6.1 explicitly states: do not use risk assessment tools and scales to predict future suicide or repetition of self-harm. Assessment should instead focus on the person's needs and their psychological and physical safety.
_Source:_ NICE - Self-harm (NG225) (2022, reviewed 2024) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng225/chapter/Recommendations

**Q655. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient is admitted following an overdose. Staff want to defer the mental health assessment until physical treatment is finished._ What does National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) guidance NG225 advise about the timing of psychosocial assessment relative to medical treatment after self-harm?
- A. Complete all medical treatment before any psychosocial assessment
- **B. Do not delay the psychosocial assessment until after medical treatment is completed [correct]**
- C. Only assess once the patient has been discharged from the medical ward
- D. Psychosocial assessment is optional if physical treatment is needed
_Why:_ NICE (1.5.2) advises not to delay the psychosocial assessment until after medical treatment is completed; physical healthcare and the psychosocial assessment should run concurrently.
_Source:_ NICE - Self-harm (NG225) (2022, reviewed 2024) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng225/chapter/Recommendations

**Q656. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient has had a psychosocial assessment and ongoing safety concerns are identified._ Within what timeframe does National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) NG225 advise that initial aftercare should be provided after the psychosocial assessment when there are ongoing safety concerns?
- A. Within 7 days
- B. Within 72 hours
- **C. Within 48 hours of the psychosocial assessment [correct]**
- D. Within 2 weeks
_Why:_ NICE recommendation 1.10.2 states initial aftercare should be provided within 48 hours of the psychosocial assessment where there are ongoing safety concerns.
_Source:_ NICE - Self-harm (NG225) (2022, reviewed 2024) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng225/chapter/Recommendations

**Q657. [Medium]** National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) NG225 advises against global risk stratification into which categories?
- A. Physical, psychological and social
- **B. Low, medium or high risk [correct]**
- C. Acute, subacute and chronic
- D. Voluntary, informal and detained
_Why:_ NICE recommendation 1.6.3 states not to use global risk stratification into low, medium or high risk to predict future suicide or repetition of self-harm.
_Source:_ NICE - Self-harm (NG225) (2022, reviewed 2024) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng225/chapter/Recommendations

**Q658. [Hard]** _Scenario: Ward staff are deciding whether to place a newly admitted patient on enhanced observation._ According to National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) NG225, decisions about observation of a person who has self-harmed should be made how?
- A. Solely at the discretion of the nurse in charge without documentation
- **B. With the informed consent of the person or within an appropriate legal framework, and reviewed regularly [correct]**
- C. Only after 24 hours have elapsed since admission
- D. Fixed for a minimum of 7 days once started
_Why:_ NICE (1.7.23) advises that observation should be with the informed consent of the person or within an appropriate legal framework, reviewed regularly, using the least restrictive approach.
_Source:_ NICE - Self-harm (NG225) (2022, reviewed 2024) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng225/chapter/Recommendations

## Suicide Prevention & Safety Planning

**Q659. [Easy]** A safety plan (also called a Safety Planning Intervention, SPI) is most effective when it is:
- A. Written by the nurse and handed to the patient to sign
- **B. Co-produced collaboratively with the patient, in the patient's own words [correct]**
- C. A standard printed leaflet given to every admission
- D. Completed only after the patient is discharged
_Why:_ The Stanley-Brown Safety Planning Intervention is explicitly a collaborative process where clinician and patient work as a team, and the best plans are individualised and use the person's own words.
_Source:_ Stanley & Brown - Safety Planning Intervention; NHS England - Staying safe from suicide (2025) - https://www.england.nhs.uk/long-read/staying-safe-from-suicide/

**Q660. [Easy]** Which of these is a widely repeated myth about suicide?
- **A. Asking someone directly about suicide can plant the idea and increase their risk [correct]**
- B. Most people who feel suicidal are ambivalent about dying
- C. Talking openly can help a person feel listened to
- D. Suicidal crises are often time-limited
_Why:_ Evidence consistently shows that asking about suicide does NOT increase risk and may slightly reduce suicidal ideation. Samaritans states asking can protect a person by helping them feel listened to.
_Source:_ Samaritans - Myths about suicide - https://www.samaritans.org/how-we-can-help/if-youre-worried-about-someone-else/myths-about-suicide/

**Q661. [Easy]** On an inpatient mental health ward, which is the most common method of suicide?
- A. Overdose of medication
- **B. Hanging or strangulation using a ligature [correct]**
- C. Jumping from height
- D. Cutting
_Why:_ NCISH data show approximately three-quarters of patients who die by suicide on psychiatric wards do so by hanging/strangulation, which is why removing ligature points is central to ward safety.
_Source:_ NCISH - National Confidential Inquiry annual report (2025) - https://sites.manchester.ac.uk/ncish/

**Q662. [Easy]** 'Means restriction' in suicide prevention refers to:
- A. Restricting a patient's leave from the ward
- **B. Reducing a person's access to methods and lethal means they might use to harm themselves [correct]**
- C. Limiting how often risk assessments are updated
- D. Restricting who can visit the patient
_Why:_ Means restriction (reducing access to lethal means, e.g. ligatures/ligature points, medication, high-risk locations) is a core, evidence-based suicide prevention strategy and is the final step of the Stanley-Brown safety plan.
_Source:_ NHS England - Staying safe from suicide (2025) - https://www.england.nhs.uk/long-read/staying-safe-from-suicide/

**Q663. [Easy]** A 'no-suicide contract' (also called a no-harm contract), where a patient promises in writing not to attempt suicide, is:
- A. An evidence-based intervention that reliably prevents suicide
- **B. Not supported by evidence as a way to prevent suicide [correct]**
- C. A legal safeguard that protects the clinician from liability
- D. Required before any patient can be placed on observation
_Why:_ No research demonstrates that no-suicide contracts reduce suicide attempts or deaths; they can create false reassurance for staff. Safety planning is the recommended evidence-based alternative.
_Source:_ PMC - Safety planning as an evidence-based alternative to no-suicide contracts (2025) - https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC11859503/

**Q664. [Easy]** Which of the following is a recognised protective factor against suicide?
- A. Social isolation
- **B. A strong sense of connectedness and belonging [correct]**
- C. Recent bereavement
- D. Access to lethal means
_Why:_ Connectedness and belonging (supportive relationships, feeling valued) are established protective factors, whereas isolation, loss and access to lethal means increase risk.
_Source:_ Zero Suicide Alliance - Individual protective factors - https://www.zerosuicidealliance.com/individual-protective-factors

**Q665. [Easy]** Clinical observation of a patient at risk of suicide should be understood primarily as:
- A. A passive watching task to record whereabouts
- **B. An opportunity for active therapeutic engagement and rapport building, not just supervision [correct]**
- C. A punishment for patients who have self-harmed
- D. A substitute for building a therapeutic relationship
_Why:_ NICE NG225 states observation should be seen as an opportunity for active engagement as well as sensitive supervision, and staff should be trained in engagement and rapport building.
_Source:_ NICE - NG225 Self-harm (2022) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng225/chapter/Recommendations

**Q666. [Easy]** How many core steps make up the Stanley-Brown Safety Planning Intervention?
- A. Three
- B. Four
- **C. Six [correct]**
- D. Ten
_Why:_ The Safety Planning Intervention has six steps: warning signs, internal coping strategies, social contacts/settings for distraction, people to ask for help, professionals/agencies to contact, and making the environment safe (means restriction).
_Source:_ SPRC - Stanley-Brown Safety Plan - https://sprc.org/resources/stanley-brown-safety-plan/

**Q667. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient is admitted to your acute ward today after a suicide attempt._ Based on National Confidential Inquiry into Suicide and Safety in Mental Health (NCISH) findings, when is the in-patient suicide risk particularly heightened?
- A. Only after the first month of admission
- **B. Within the first week of admission [correct]**
- C. Risk is evenly spread throughout the admission
- D. Only in the final week before discharge
_Why:_ NCISH reports that observation policies should recognise suicide risk is increased within the first week of admission, making early engagement and environmental safety especially important.
_Source:_ NCISH - University of Manchester - https://sites.manchester.ac.uk/ncish/

**Q668. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient is being discharged home from your ward next week._ According to NCISH, the period of highest suicide risk following discharge from psychiatric inpatient care is:
- **A. The first two weeks after discharge [correct]**
- B. Between three and six months after discharge
- C. After one year post-discharge
- D. There is no elevated post-discharge risk
_Why:_ NCISH identifies the first two weeks after leaving hospital as the time of highest post-discharge suicide risk, with the peak in the first few days. This underpins prompt follow-up and safety planning at discharge.
_Source:_ NCISH - Suicide within 2 weeks of discharge (2023) - https://sites.manchester.ac.uk/ncish/2023/02/02/suicide-within-2-weeks-of-discharge-from-psychiatric-in-patient-care-a-case-control-study/

**Q669. [Medium]** _Scenario: You are completing a safety plan with a distressed patient._ What is the correct first step of the Stanley-Brown Safety Planning Intervention?
- A. Contacting emergency services
- **B. Identifying the patient's personal warning signs of an approaching crisis [correct]**
- C. Removing all belongings from the patient
- D. Signing a no-suicide contract
_Why:_ Step 1 is recognising the personal warning signs (thoughts, feelings, behaviours) that precede a suicidal crisis, so the person can begin using the plan early.
_Source:_ SPRC - Stanley-Brown Safety Plan - https://sprc.org/resources/stanley-brown-safety-plan/

**Q670. [Medium]** _Scenario: A colleague argues that removing ligature points is unnecessary if observation levels are high._ What does the evidence base indicate about ward environmental safety?
- A. Observation alone removes the need for any environmental changes
- **B. Removing ligature points and reducing access to lethal means is a key measure that has reduced inpatient suicide [correct]**
- C. Ligature reduction has no measurable effect
- D. Only the newest wards need ligature reduction
_Why:_ NCISH-driven removal of ligature points (a non-collapsible ligature point suicide became an NHS 'never event' in 2009) is associated with substantial falls in inpatient suicide; environmental safety and observation are complementary.
_Source:_ NCISH - University of Manchester - https://sites.manchester.ac.uk/ncish/

**Q671. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient says 'You can just ask my wife, she knows the signs.'_ When completing the 'social contacts and support' steps of a safety plan, best practice is to:
- A. Include only professional crisis lines, never family or friends
- **B. Identify the patient's own trusted people and social settings that can provide distraction and support [correct]**
- C. Refuse to name anyone to protect confidentiality
- D. List only the ward staff
_Why:_ Steps 3 and 4 of the Stanley-Brown plan draw on the person's own social contacts and settings for distraction and support, reflecting the collaborative, individualised nature of the intervention.
_Source:_ SPRC - Stanley-Brown Safety Plan - https://sprc.org/resources/stanley-brown-safety-plan/

**Q672. [Medium]** _Scenario: A nurse worries that raising suicide directly with a quiet, low-mood patient could 'make things worse.'_ What is the most appropriate response grounded in the evidence?
- A. Avoid the topic until the patient raises it themselves
- **B. Ask directly and openly, as this does not increase risk and can help the person feel supported [correct]**
- C. Only ask using an anonymous written questionnaire
- D. Refer the question entirely to the doctor
_Why:_ Meta-analytic evidence shows asking about suicide does not induce or increase suicidal ideation and may reduce it; direct, compassionate enquiry supports engagement.
_Source:_ Samaritans / Psychological Medicine review - https://www.samaritans.org/how-we-can-help/if-youre-worried-about-someone-else/myths-about-suicide/

**Q673. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient's safety plan has been completed on the ward._ To be useful during a future crisis, the safety plan should be:
- A. Filed only in the electronic record where the patient cannot see it
- **B. Accessible to the patient, who should agree where they will keep it and how they will find it [correct]**
- C. Kept solely by the named nurse
- D. Destroyed once the patient's mood improves
_Why:_ Guidance stresses discussing with the patient where they will keep the plan and how it will be located in a crisis; the plan is a tool for the person to use, so accessibility is essential.
_Source:_ Stanley & Brown - Safety Planning intervention - https://suicidesafetyplan.com/

**Q674. [Medium]** _Scenario: Your ward is reviewing its induction training on suicide prevention._ Which statement best reflects the NICE NG225 principle for staff carrying out observations?
- A. Only qualified doctors need engagement training
- **B. All staff observing people who have self-harmed should be trained in therapeutic observation, engagement and rapport building [correct]**
- C. Observation should be silent and detached to avoid influencing the patient
- D. Engagement training is optional if observation levels are documented
_Why:_ NG225 recommends that all staff observing people who have self-harmed are trained in therapeutic observation methods, including engagement and rapport building.
_Source:_ NICE - NG225 Recommendations (2022) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng225/chapter/Recommendations

**Q675. [Medium]** _Scenario: A team debates replacing safety planning with a signed promise not to self-harm._ Compared with a no-suicide contract, why is a safety plan preferred?
- A. It shifts all responsibility onto the patient to keep a promise
- **B. It gives the patient an active, step-by-step set of coping actions and supports for a crisis [correct]**
- C. It provides stronger legal protection for the clinician
- D. It removes the need for follow-up
_Why:_ A safety plan offers an active roadmap of coping strategies and supports, whereas a no-suicide contract passively relies on a promise made when judgement is most impaired and has no evidence of effectiveness.
_Source:_ PMC - Safety planning as evidence-based alternative to no-suicide contracts (2025) - https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC11859503/

**Q676. [Hard]** In 2009, following NCISH recommendations, which specific event was designated an NHS 'never event' (a serious, preventable incident)?
- A. Any inpatient self-harm episode
- **B. Suicide using non-collapsible ligature points in inpatient settings [correct]**
- C. Absconding from a ward
- D. Medication overdose on a ward
_Why:_ Suicide using non-collapsible ligature points became an NHS 'never event' in 2009, requiring services to monitor incidence and remove such points. This was associated with major reductions in inpatient hanging deaths.
_Source:_ NCISH - University of Manchester; UK Parliament written evidence - https://committees.parliament.uk/writtenevidence/70932/html/

**Q677. [Hard]** _Scenario: A colleague assumes that if a ward has no obvious high ligature points, hanging cannot occur._ What does NCISH evidence indicate about ligature points and inpatient hanging?
- A. Only high anchor points pose any risk
- **B. Many deaths involve low-lying ligature points, and self-strangulation can occur even without an identifiable ligature point [correct]**
- C. Ligature points are irrelevant once observation is in place
- D. Removing all doors eliminates the risk entirely
_Why:_ NCISH highlights that many inpatient deaths involve low-lying ligature points and that strangulation can occur without an identifiable ligature point, so vigilance and relational engagement remain essential.
_Source:_ NCISH - University of Manchester; PMC - Inpatient suicide prevention measures (2022) - https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC9650354/

**Q678. [Hard]** Which sequence correctly orders three consecutive steps of the Stanley-Brown Safety Planning Intervention?
- A. Professional/agency contacts, then warning signs, then internal coping
- **B. Internal coping strategies, then social contacts for distraction, then people to ask for help [correct]**
- C. Making the environment safe, then warning signs, then internal coping
- D. Social supports first, then warning signs, then means restriction
_Why:_ The ordered steps are: (1) warning signs, (2) internal coping strategies, (3) social contacts/settings for distraction, (4) people to ask for help, (5) professionals/agencies, (6) making the environment safe. Option B gives the correct consecutive run of steps 2 to 4.
_Source:_ SPRC - Stanley-Brown Safety Plan - https://sprc.org/resources/stanley-brown-safety-plan/

**Q679. [Hard]** _Scenario: You are auditing your service against NCISH ward safety recommendations._ Which of the following is a specific NCISH ward safety recommendation, beyond removing ligature points?
- A. Abolishing all patient leave
- **B. Better monitoring of ward entry and exit points so patients cannot leave without staff agreement [correct]**
- C. Removing all personal possessions permanently
- D. Ending therapeutic engagement to focus on observation
_Why:_ NCISH recommends measures to prevent patients leaving without staff agreement through better monitoring of ward entry/exit points, alongside improving the inpatient experience.
_Source:_ NCISH - University of Manchester; UK Parliament written evidence - https://committees.parliament.uk/writtenevidence/70932/html/

**Q680. [Hard]** _Scenario: A manager cites falling inpatient suicide figures as proof the problem is solved._ What does the most recent NCISH trend evidence actually show about inpatient suicide in England?
- A. It has fallen to zero
- **B. It fell substantially after ligature-point action but has not continued to decrease in recent years (around 2020-2023) [correct]**
- C. It has risen sharply above pre-2009 levels
- D. There is no reliable national data
_Why:_ Inpatient suicide fell markedly after NCISH ligature recommendations, but NCISH notes figures have not decreased further in more recent years, indicating continued vigilance and relational safety are needed.
_Source:_ NCISH - University of Manchester - https://sites.manchester.ac.uk/ncish/

**Q681. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient who attempted suicide in hospital had previously signed a no-suicide contract._ Which finding best explains why services have moved away from no-suicide contracts?
- A. They take too long to complete
- **B. A signed contract can falsely reassure staff and reduce vigilant risk monitoring, with no evidence it prevents suicide [correct]**
- C. They are only valid for 24 hours
- D. They are legally binding on the patient
_Why:_ No-suicide contracts have no empirical support and can create false reassurance, leading to less vigilant monitoring; a substantial proportion of people who attempted suicide in hospital had signed one.
_Source:_ PMC - alternatives to no-suicide contracts (2025) - https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC11859503/

**Q682. [Hard]** _Scenario: Your ward is reviewing which patients need the most proactive engagement in the first days after admission._ Which combination of factors did NCISH associate with increased suicide risk around inpatient admission and discharge?
- A. Long admissions and absence of any life events
- **B. Short admission, recent adverse life events, and psychiatric comorbidity [correct]**
- C. Older age with no comorbidity and strong aftercare
- D. Enhanced aftercare and stable housing
_Why:_ NCISH identified short admission, recent adverse life events and psychiatric comorbidity as risk factors, while enhanced aftercare was protective, informing early engagement and assertive follow-up.
_Source:_ NCISH - Suicide within 2 weeks of discharge case-control study (2023) - https://sites.manchester.ac.uk/ncish/2023/02/02/suicide-within-2-weeks-of-discharge-from-psychiatric-in-patient-care-a-case-control-study/

**Q683. [Hard]** _Scenario: A nurse frames safety on the ward as purely a matter of stricter observation levels._ How does NICE NG225 position observation within suicide/self-harm safety?
- A. As a stand-alone activity separate from the therapeutic relationship
- **B. Within a framework of relational, therapeutic engagement alongside assessment, formulation and management [correct]**
- C. As a replacement for formulation and care planning
- D. As applicable only to detained patients
_Why:_ NG225 sets safety assessment, formulation, management and planning in the context of relational, therapeutic engagement, which is known to improve outcomes; observation is one part of that relational approach, not a substitute.
_Source:_ NICE - NG225 Self-harm (2022) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng225/chapter/Recommendations

## Violence, Restraint & Rapid Tranquillisation

**Q684. [Easy]** According to National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) NG10, which of the following are recommended first-line options for rapid tranquillisation given intramuscularly?
- A. Intramuscular diazepam on its own
- **B. Intramuscular lorazepam on its own, or intramuscular haloperidol combined with intramuscular promethazine [correct]**
- C. Intramuscular chlorpromazine on its own
- D. Intramuscular haloperidol on its own
_Why:_ NICE NG10 recommends intramuscular lorazepam on its own, or intramuscular haloperidol combined with intramuscular promethazine, as the options for rapid tranquillisation.
_Source:_ NICE - Violence and aggression (NG10) (2015, reviewed 2024) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng10/chapter/Recommendations

**Q685. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient has a known prolonged QT interval and cardiovascular disease and requires rapid tranquillisation._ Per National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) NG10, which rapid tranquillisation option should be avoided in a patient with cardiovascular disease or a prolonged QT interval?
- A. Intramuscular lorazepam
- **B. Intramuscular haloperidol combined with promethazine [correct]**
- C. Oral promethazine
- D. Any benzodiazepine
_Why:_ NICE NG10 advises avoiding intramuscular haloperidol combined with promethazine if there is cardiovascular disease or a prolonged QT interval; lorazepam alone is preferred in that situation and if the person is antipsychotic-naive.
_Source:_ NICE - Violence and aggression (NG10) (2015, reviewed 2024) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng10/chapter/Recommendations

**Q686. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient has received intramuscular rapid tranquillisation. No BNF maximum has been exceeded, they are not asleep, and there are no other risk factors._ According to National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) NG10, how often should vital signs be monitored after rapid tranquillisation in this standard situation?
- A. Every 5 minutes
- **B. At least every hour until there are no further concerns [correct]**
- C. Once at 4 hours
- D. Every 15 minutes for 24 hours
_Why:_ NICE NG10 specifies monitoring at least every hour until there are no further concerns. Monitoring every 15 minutes is reserved for higher-risk situations.
_Source:_ NICE - Violence and aggression (NG10) (2015, reviewed 2024) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng10/chapter/Recommendations

**Q687. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient requires enhanced 15-minute monitoring after rapid tranquillisation._ Under National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) NG10, which of the following is a trigger for increasing monitoring to every 15 minutes after rapid tranquillisation?
- A. The patient is fully alert and calm
- **B. The maximum British National Formulary (BNF) dose has been exceeded, or the person appears asleep or sedated [correct]**
- C. The rapid tranquillisation was given orally
- D. The patient has no physical health problems
_Why:_ NICE NG10 requires monitoring every 15 minutes if the maximum BNF dose has been exceeded, the person appears asleep or sedated, has taken illicit drugs or alcohol, has pre-existing physical health problems, or has been harmed by other restrictive interventions.
_Source:_ NICE - Violence and aggression (NG10) (2015, reviewed 2024) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng10/chapter/Recommendations

**Q688. [Medium]** _Scenario: During manual restraint a patient is taken to the floor._ What does National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) NG10 advise about positioning during manual restraint if a person must be taken to the floor?
- A. Always use the prone (face down) position for control
- **B. Use the supine (face up) position if possible; if the prone (face down) position is necessary, use it for as short a time as possible [correct]**
- C. Keep the person prone for at least 10 minutes to ensure safety
- D. Position does not matter provided enough staff are present
_Why:_ NICE NG10 advises avoiding taking the person to the floor, but if necessary use the supine position if possible; if prone is needed, use it for as short a time as possible, never interfering with airway, breathing or circulation.
_Source:_ NICE - Violence and aggression (NG10) (2015, reviewed 2024) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng10/chapter/Recommendations

**Q689. [Medium]** According to National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) NG10, manual restraint should not routinely be used for longer than how long?
- A. 5 minutes
- **B. 10 minutes [correct]**
- C. 30 minutes
- D. 1 hour
_Why:_ NICE NG10 states: do not routinely use manual restraint for more than 10 minutes.
_Source:_ NICE - Violence and aggression (NG10) (2015, reviewed 2024) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng10/chapter/Recommendations

**Q690. [Easy]** _Scenario: A patient is becoming increasingly agitated on the ward._ According to National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) NG10, what should be attempted before using restrictive interventions such as restraint or rapid tranquillisation?
- A. Immediate seclusion
- **B. De-escalation and other preventive strategies [correct]**
- C. Physical restraint by four staff
- D. Transfer to a psychiatric intensive care unit
_Why:_ NICE NG10 states restrictive interventions should only be used once de-escalation and other preventive strategies have failed and there is potential for harm, choosing the least restrictive option.
_Source:_ NICE - Violence and aggression (NG10) (2015, reviewed 2024) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng10/chapter/Recommendations

## Mental Health Conditions & NICE

**Q691. [Easy]** According to National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) CG178, before a person starts antipsychotic medication, which baseline measures should be recorded?
- A. Only weight and blood pressure
- **B. Weight, waist circumference, pulse and blood pressure, fasting glucose or HbA1c, blood lipids and prolactin [correct]**
- C. Only an electrocardiogram (ECG)
- D. Only fasting glucose
_Why:_ CG178 requires baseline weight, waist circumference, pulse and blood pressure, fasting blood glucose or HbA1c, blood lipids and prolactin, plus assessment of movement disorders and nutritional status before starting an antipsychotic.
_Source:_ NICE - Psychosis and schizophrenia in adults (CG178) (2014) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg178/chapter/Recommendations

**Q692. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient started on an antipsychotic 3 weeks ago._ Per National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) CG178, how frequently should weight be monitored in the first phase after starting an antipsychotic?
- A. Once at 1 year only
- **B. Weekly for the first 6 weeks, then at 12 weeks, at 1 year and then annually [correct]**
- C. Monthly for the first year
- D. Only if the patient reports weight gain
_Why:_ CG178 recommends plotting weight weekly for the first 6 weeks, then at 12 weeks, at 1 year and then annually.
_Source:_ NICE - Psychosis and schizophrenia in adults (CG178) (2014) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg178/chapter/Recommendations

**Q693. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient with schizophrenia has not improved despite adequate trials of two antipsychotics._ According to National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) CG178, at what point should clozapine be offered?
- A. After failure of one antipsychotic
- **B. After the sequential use of adequate doses of at least 2 different antipsychotic drugs [correct]**
- C. Only after electroconvulsive therapy has failed
- D. As a first-line antipsychotic for all new patients
_Why:_ CG178 recommends offering clozapine when the illness has not responded adequately despite the sequential use of adequate doses of at least 2 different antipsychotic drugs, at least one of which should be a non-clozapine second-generation antipsychotic.
_Source:_ NICE - Psychosis and schizophrenia in adults (CG178) (2014) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg178/chapter/Recommendations

**Q694. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient is being started on lithium for bipolar disorder for the first time._ According to National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) CG185, what is the target plasma lithium level for a person prescribed lithium for the first time?
- A. 0.2 to 0.4 mmol per litre
- **B. 0.6 to 0.8 mmol per litre [correct]**
- C. 1.0 to 1.5 mmol per litre
- D. 2.0 to 2.5 mmol per litre
_Why:_ CG185 advises aiming for a plasma lithium level between 0.6 and 0.8 mmol per litre for people prescribed lithium for the first time.
_Source:_ NICE - Bipolar disorder (CG185) (2014, updated 2025) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg185/chapter/Recommendations

**Q695. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient has been established on lithium and is stable within the first year of treatment._ Per National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) CG185, how often should plasma lithium levels be measured during the first year of treatment once stable?
- A. Weekly
- **B. Every 3 months [correct]**
- C. Every 6 months
- D. Once only at 12 months
_Why:_ CG185 recommends measuring plasma lithium every 3 months for the first year (after initial weekly checks until stable), then every 6 months, or 3-monthly for higher-risk groups.
_Source:_ NICE - Bipolar disorder (CG185) (2014, updated 2025) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg185/chapter/Recommendations

**Q696. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient with bipolar disorder on lithium is due a routine 6-monthly blood screen._ Besides plasma lithium level, which tests does National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) CG185 recommend every 6 months for a person on lithium?
- A. Full blood count only
- **B. Urea and electrolytes including calcium, estimated glomerular filtration rate (eGFR) and thyroid function [correct]**
- C. Liver function tests only
- D. No further blood tests are needed
_Why:_ CG185 recommends measuring weight or BMI and testing urea and electrolytes including calcium, eGFR and thyroid function every 6 months, more often if renal or thyroid function is impaired.
_Source:_ NICE - Bipolar disorder (CG185) (2014, updated 2025) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg185/chapter/Recommendations

**Q697. [Easy]** _Scenario: An older adult with dementia is showing agitation and distress on the ward._ According to National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) NG97, what should be offered as the initial and ongoing management of distress in a person living with dementia?
- A. Antipsychotic medication for everyone
- **B. Psychosocial and environmental interventions [correct]**
- C. Immediate physical restraint
- D. Routine sedation at night
_Why:_ NG97 recommends offering psychosocial and environmental interventions as initial and ongoing management, after a structured assessment for causes of distress such as pain, delirium or inappropriate care.
_Source:_ NICE - Dementia (NG97) (2018) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng97/chapter/Recommendations

**Q698. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient with dementia with Lewy bodies is being considered for an antipsychotic to manage severe distress._ What specific caution does National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) NG97 give about antipsychotics in dementia with Lewy bodies or Parkinson's disease dementia?
- A. They are the safest first-line option in these conditions
- **B. They can worsen the motor features and in some cases cause severe antipsychotic sensitivity reactions [correct]**
- C. They have no particular risks in these conditions
- D. They should be given at higher doses to be effective
_Why:_ NG97 warns that in people with dementia with Lewy bodies or Parkinson's disease dementia, antipsychotics can worsen motor features and in some cases cause severe antipsychotic sensitivity reactions.
_Source:_ NICE - Dementia (NG97) (2018) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng97/chapter/Recommendations

**Q699. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient admitted with psychosis is found to be using illicit substances on leave. Some staff suggest discharging them._ According to National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) CG120, can a person be discharged from an inpatient mental health service solely because of their substance misuse?
- A. Yes, substance misuse is always grounds for discharge
- **B. No, do not discharge solely because of substance misuse [correct]**
- C. Only if they refuse a drug test
- D. Only if they have used substances more than twice
_Why:_ CG120 states: do not discharge adults and young people with psychosis and coexisting substance misuse from an inpatient mental health service solely because of their substance misuse.
_Source:_ NICE - Coexisting severe mental illness and substance misuse (CG120) (2011) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg120/chapter/Recommendations

**Q700. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient with severe depression is not eating or drinking and their life is at risk; other treatments have not helped._ According to National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) NG222, in which situation may electroconvulsive therapy (ECT) be considered for depression?
- A. As routine first-line treatment for all depression
- **B. When a rapid response is needed, for example depression is life-threatening because the person is not eating or drinking, or other treatments have been unsuccessful [correct]**
- C. Only for patients under 30
- D. Never in an inpatient setting
_Why:_ NG222 supports considering ECT when a rapid response is needed (e.g. life-threatening depression because the person is not eating or drinking), when other treatments have been unsuccessful, or when the person chooses it based on prior experience.
_Source:_ NICE - Depression in adults (NG222) (2022) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng222/chapter/Recommendations

## Substance Misuse & Withdrawal

**Q701. [Easy]** Wernicke's encephalopathy is caused by a deficiency of which vitamin?
- **A. Vitamin B1 (thiamine) [correct]**
- B. Vitamin B12 (cobalamin)
- C. Vitamin C (ascorbic acid)
- D. Vitamin D (cholecalciferol)
_Why:_ Wernicke's encephalopathy results from thiamine (vitamin B1) deficiency, common in alcohol dependence due to poor diet and impaired absorption.
_Source:_ NICE - CG100 Alcohol-use disorders: physical complications (2010, updated 2017) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg100/chapter/recommendations

**Q702. [Easy]** The classic clinical triad associated with Wernicke's encephalopathy consists of confusion, ataxia and which third feature?
- **A. Ophthalmoplegia (eye movement abnormalities) [correct]**
- B. Hypertension
- C. Jaundice
- D. Bradycardia
_Why:_ The classic triad is confusion, ataxia and ophthalmoplegia/nystagmus. All three are present in only a minority of cases, so treatment should not wait for the full triad.
_Source:_ NCBI - Wernicke's Encephalopathy review (2018) - https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC6199146/

**Q703. [Medium]** _Scenario: An intoxicated, malnourished patient with alcohol dependence is found drowsy with a low blood glucose in the emergency department._ To reduce the risk of precipitating Wernicke's encephalopathy, thiamine should ideally be given in what relation to intravenous glucose?
- **A. Thiamine before or with the glucose [correct]**
- B. Glucose alone first, then thiamine days later
- C. Thiamine only after 48 hours of glucose
- D. Neither is needed if the patient is drinking
_Why:_ Administering glucose to a thiamine-deficient patient can consume remaining thiamine and precipitate or worsen Wernicke's encephalopathy. Thiamine should be given before or alongside glucose in at-risk patients.
_Source:_ Mayo Clinic Proceedings - Wernicke Encephalopathy Clinical Pearls (2019) - https://www.mayoclinicproceedings.org/article/S0025-6196(19)30255-1/fulltext

**Q704. [Hard]** According to NICE CG100, parenteral thiamine for a person with suspected Wernicke's encephalopathy should be continued for a minimum of how long unless the diagnosis is excluded?
- **A. A minimum of 5 days [correct]**
- B. A single dose only
- C. A minimum of 24 hours
- D. A minimum of 28 days
_Why:_ NICE CG100 recommends parenteral thiamine for suspected Wernicke's encephalopathy be given for a minimum of 5 days, unless it is excluded, followed by oral thiamine.
_Source:_ NICE - CG100 Alcohol-use disorders: physical complications (2010, updated 2017) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg100/chapter/recommendations

**Q705. [Medium]** The CIWA-Ar (Clinical Institute Withdrawal Assessment for Alcohol, revised) is a tool used primarily to assess what?
- **A. The severity of alcohol withdrawal and guide medication [correct]**
- B. A patient's long-term risk of liver cirrhosis
- C. Nicotine dependence severity
- D. Opioid overdose reversal response
_Why:_ CIWA-Ar is a 10-item scale scoring symptoms such as tremor, sweating, agitation and hallucinations to grade alcohol withdrawal severity and guide benzodiazepine dosing in symptom-triggered regimens.
_Source:_ MDCalc / VA - CIWA-Ar Alcohol Withdrawal Assessment - https://www.mdcalc.com/calc/1736/ciwa-ar-alcohol-withdrawal

**Q706. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient undergoing alcohol detox has a CIWA-Ar (Clinical Institute Withdrawal Assessment for Alcohol, revised) score of 18._ A CIWA-Ar score in this range (15 or above) most indicates what?
- **A. Severe withdrawal with risk of impending delirium tremens [correct]**
- B. No withdrawal, safe to discharge
- C. Mild withdrawal needing no treatment
- D. Opioid rather than alcohol withdrawal
_Why:_ CIWA-Ar scores of 8-15 indicate moderate withdrawal, while scores of 15 or more indicate severe withdrawal with risk of impending delirium tremens, warranting close monitoring and higher benzodiazepine doses.
_Source:_ NCBI PMC - Alcohol Withdrawal Severity Measures (2024) - https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC10950191/

**Q707. [Medium]** Delirium tremens (DTs), the most severe form of alcohol withdrawal, typically has its peak onset how long after the last drink?
- **A. 48 to 72 hours [correct]**
- B. Within the first 2 hours
- C. Around 2 weeks
- D. Around 30 days
_Why:_ Withdrawal seizures peak at around 24-48 hours, whereas delirium tremens typically peaks at 48-72 hours after the last drink. DTs is a medical emergency with significant untreated mortality.
_Source:_ NCBI StatPearls - Alcohol Withdrawal Syndrome (2023) - https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK441882/

**Q708. [Hard]** According to NICE CG100, what is the recommended first-line treatment for delirium tremens?
- **A. Oral lorazepam [correct]**
- B. Intramuscular adrenaline
- C. Oral naltrexone
- D. Intravenous flumazenil
_Why:_ NICE CG100 recommends oral lorazepam as first-line for delirium tremens. If symptoms persist or oral medication is declined, parenteral lorazepam or haloperidol may be used.
_Source:_ NICE - CG100 Alcohol-use disorders: physical complications (2010, updated 2017) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg100/chapter/recommendations

**Q709. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient in alcohol withdrawal has a generalised tonic-clonic seizure on the ward._ According to NICE CG100, what is recommended to reduce the likelihood of a further alcohol withdrawal seizure?
- **A. A quick-acting benzodiazepine such as lorazepam [correct]**
- B. Long-term phenytoin monotherapy
- C. Oral thiamine alone
- D. Intramuscular naloxone
_Why:_ NICE CG100 advises considering a quick-acting benzodiazepine such as lorazepam to reduce the likelihood of further alcohol withdrawal seizures. Phenytoin is not recommended for alcohol withdrawal seizures.
_Source:_ NICE - CG100 Alcohol-use disorders: physical complications (2010, updated 2017) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg100/chapter/recommendations

**Q710. [Easy]** Which medication is most commonly used in the UK for a fixed-dose reducing regimen to manage alcohol withdrawal?
- **A. Chlordiazepoxide [correct]**
- B. Paracetamol
- C. Metformin
- D. Amoxicillin
_Why:_ NICE CG115 recommends a benzodiazepine (chlordiazepoxide or diazepam) for medically assisted alcohol withdrawal. Chlordiazepoxide is the most widely used, given as a fixed-dose regimen reduced to zero over about 7-10 days.
_Source:_ NICE - CG115 Alcohol-use disorders: harmful drinking and dependence (2011) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg115

**Q711. [Hard]** In a NICE CG115 fixed-dose community regimen, over roughly what period is the benzodiazepine dose typically reduced to zero?
- **A. 7 to 10 days [correct]**
- B. 2 to 3 hours
- C. 6 to 12 months
- D. 3 to 5 years
_Why:_ NICE CG115 defines a fixed-dose regimen as starting at a dose titrated to dependence severity and reducing to zero over 7-10 days according to a standard protocol.
_Source:_ NICE - CG115 Alcohol-use disorders: harmful drinking and dependence (2011) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg115

**Q712. [Medium]** _Scenario: A person is managed for alcohol withdrawal in the community with chlordiazepoxide._ To reduce the risk of overdose or diversion, how should the benzodiazepine typically be supplied?
- **A. In frequent small amounts (for example instalment dispensing, no more than 2 days' supply at a time) [correct]**
- B. One large 4-week supply handed to the patient
- C. No supply limits are needed for benzodiazepines
- D. Only by intravenous infusion at home
_Why:_ NICE advises against giving people who misuse alcohol large quantities of medication to take home; prescribing for instalment dispensing with no more than 2 days' medication at a time reduces overdose and diversion risk.
_Source:_ NICE - CG115 Alcohol-use disorders: harmful drinking and dependence (2011) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg115

**Q713. [Easy]** Naloxone is used in the emergency treatment of overdose from which class of drugs?
- **A. Opioids (for example heroin, methadone) [correct]**
- B. Benzodiazepines
