- C. Stimulants such as cocaine
- D. Alcohol
_Why:_ Naloxone is an opioid receptor antagonist that reverses the respiratory depression and sedation of opioid overdose. It has no useful effect in benzodiazepine, stimulant or alcohol overdose.
_Source:_ emc - Prenoxad Solution for Injection SmPC - https://www.medicines.org.uk/emc/product/3054/smpc

**Q714. [Medium]** _Scenario: You find a person unresponsive with pinpoint pupils and slow, shallow breathing after suspected heroin use._ Which set of features most strongly suggests opioid overdose?
- **A. Pinpoint pupils and respiratory depression [correct]**
- B. Widely dilated pupils and rapid breathing
- C. Fever and a widespread rash
- D. High blood pressure and tremor
_Why:_ The classic triad of opioid overdose is reduced consciousness, respiratory depression and pinpoint (miotic) pupils. Recognising this prompts calling 999 and giving naloxone.
_Source:_ emc - Prenoxad Solution for Injection SmPC - https://www.medicines.org.uk/emc/product/3054/smpc

**Q715. [Medium]** _Scenario: You are administering a Prenoxad (naloxone) take-home kit to a person in opioid overdose._ By which route is Prenoxad naloxone administered by a bystander or non-clinical responder?
- **A. Intramuscular injection (into the outer thigh or upper arm) [correct]**
- B. Intravenous infusion only
- C. Oral tablet
- D. Rectal suppository
_Why:_ Prenoxad is a pre-filled syringe given by intramuscular injection into the outer thigh or upper arm, making it suitable for take-home use by peers, family and non-clinical responders.
_Source:_ emc - Prenoxad Solution for Injection SmPC - https://www.medicines.org.uk/emc/product/3054/smpc

**Q716. [Hard]** _Scenario: A Prenoxad kit is being used and the first 0.4ml dose has produced no response._ According to the Prenoxad SmPC, how often can a 0.4ml (0.4mg) dose be repeated while awaiting the ambulance?
- **A. Every 2 to 3 minutes until the syringe is used up or the patient responds [correct]**
- B. Only once, then wait one hour
- C. Every 30 seconds continuously
- D. Not at all; a single dose is the maximum
_Why:_ The Prenoxad SmPC states 0.4ml can be administered every 2-3 minutes in subsequent cycles until the ambulance arrives, the patient regains consciousness, or the syringe (5 doses) is used up.
_Source:_ emc - Prenoxad Solution for Injection SmPC - https://www.medicines.org.uk/emc/product/3054/smpc

**Q717. [Hard]** _Scenario: Naloxone has successfully revived someone who took methadone._ Why must the person still be kept under observation and 999 be called even after they wake up?
- **A. Naloxone's action (about 1-2 hours) can wear off before the opioid does, so overdose can recur [correct]**
- B. Naloxone permanently cures the addiction
- C. Once awake there is no further risk at all
- D. Naloxone converts the opioid into a harmless substance
_Why:_ Naloxone has a short duration of action (roughly 1-2 hours) that can be outlasted by long-acting opioids such as methadone. The person can re-sedate, so emergency services must be called and the person observed.
_Source:_ emc - Prenoxad Solution for Injection SmPC - https://www.medicines.org.uk/emc/product/3054/smpc

**Q718. [Easy]** Methadone and buprenorphine are both used in the UK as opioid substitution treatment. Which best describes buprenorphine?
- **A. A partial agonist at the mu-opioid receptor [correct]**
- B. A full opioid agonist like heroin
- C. An opioid receptor antagonist with no agonist activity
- D. A benzodiazepine
_Why:_ Methadone is a full mu-opioid agonist, whereas buprenorphine is a partial agonist at the mu-opioid receptor with high receptor affinity, giving it a ceiling effect on respiratory depression.
_Source:_ NICE - TA114 Methadone and buprenorphine for opioid dependence (2007) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ta114

**Q719. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient still has significant heroin on board when their first dose of buprenorphine is given too early._ What can happen because buprenorphine displaces full agonists from the mu-opioid receptor?
- **A. Precipitated (sudden) opioid withdrawal [correct]**
- B. Immediate deep sedation and coma
- C. A serotonin syndrome
- D. No effect at all
_Why:_ As a high-affinity partial agonist, buprenorphine can displace full agonists such as heroin from receptors without fully activating them, precipitating acute withdrawal. This is why patients must be in mild-to-moderate withdrawal before induction.
_Source:_ NCBI PMC - Managing opioid withdrawal precipitated by buprenorphine (2021) - https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC8248003/

**Q720. [Hard]** In the combination product buprenorphine/naloxone (for example Suboxone), why is naloxone included?
- **A. To deter misuse by injection, since injected naloxone precipitates withdrawal [correct]**
- B. To reverse the buprenorphine when taken correctly under the tongue
- C. To increase the pain-relieving effect
- D. To treat co-existing alcohol dependence
_Why:_ Naloxone is poorly absorbed sublingually, so when the tablet/film is taken correctly it has little effect. If crushed and injected, naloxone becomes bioavailable and precipitates withdrawal, deterring misuse by injection.
_Source:_ MetaPhi / BNF - Buprenorphine with naloxone - https://bnf.nice.org.uk/drugs/buprenorphine-with-naloxone/

**Q721. [Medium]** The COWS (Clinical Opiate Withdrawal Scale) is used to assess what?
- **A. The severity of opioid withdrawal [correct]**
- B. The severity of alcohol withdrawal
- C. A patient's nicotine dependence
- D. Blood alcohol concentration
_Why:_ COWS is an 11-item validated scale scoring signs such as pulse, sweating, restlessness, pupil size, aches, GI upset, tremor, yawning and anxiety, used to grade opioid withdrawal severity and guide treatment.
_Source:_ NIDA - Clinical Opiate Withdrawal Scale (2003) - https://nida.nih.gov/sites/default/files/ClinicalOpiateWithdrawalScale.pdf

**Q722. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient's abrupt benzodiazepine cessation is being considered after several months of daily use._ Why is abrupt withdrawal of benzodiazepines after prolonged use dangerous?
- **A. It can precipitate life-threatening withdrawal seizures [correct]**
- B. It causes immediate liver failure
- C. It has no risks and is preferred
- D. It reverses opioid overdose
_Why:_ Abrupt cessation of benzodiazepines after weeks to months of use can precipitate acute withdrawal including life-threatening seizures. The dose should be reduced gradually, often by switching to a long-acting agent such as diazepam and tapering slowly.
_Source:_ NCBI PMC - Management of benzodiazepine misuse and dependence (2015) - https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4657308/

**Q723. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient dependent on multiple short-acting benzodiazepines is starting a withdrawal plan._ What is the usual UK approach to withdrawing them safely?
- **A. Switch to an equivalent dose of a long-acting benzodiazepine (for example diazepam) and taper gradually [correct]**
- B. Stop all benzodiazepines the same day
- C. Add a stimulant to counteract sedation
- D. Give a single dose of naloxone
_Why:_ UK practice, per the BNF, is usually to transfer to an equivalent daily dose of diazepam (long half-life) then reduce gradually over weeks to months; it is better to withdraw too slowly than too quickly.
_Source:_ BNF - Benzodiazepines, withdrawal - https://bnf.nice.org.uk/treatment-summaries/hypnotics-and-anxiolytics/

**Q724. [Easy]** _Scenario: A newly admitted patient who smokes is on a smoke-free mental health inpatient ward._ What should be offered promptly to manage their nicotine withdrawal?
- **A. Nicotine replacement therapy (NRT) and behavioural support [correct]**
- B. A cigarette break in the garden
- C. Nothing; cravings should be ignored
- D. A benzodiazepine as first-line
_Why:_ On smoke-free wards, NICE recommends offering stop-smoking pharmacotherapy such as NRT promptly plus behavioural support, to relieve nicotine withdrawal which can otherwise cause agitation and aggression.
_Source:_ NICE - NG209 Tobacco: treating dependence / QS207 (2021, updated 2023) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng209

**Q725. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient stable on clozapine stops smoking on admission to a smoke-free ward._ What is the key clinical consequence of stopping smoking for their clozapine level?
- **A. Clozapine plasma levels rise (risk of toxicity), so monitoring and dose reduction may be needed [correct]**
- B. Clozapine levels fall, so the dose must be increased
- C. Smoking status has no effect on clozapine
- D. Clozapine must be stopped immediately and permanently
_Why:_ Tobacco smoke induces the liver enzyme CYP1A2, which metabolises clozapine and olanzapine. Stopping smoking removes this induction, so plasma levels can rise by 50% or more within days, risking toxicity. Dose review and monitoring are needed; NRT does not affect CYP1A2.
_Source:_ Oxford Health Formulary - Smoking and drug interactions (2023) - https://www.oxfordhealthformulary.nhs.uk/

**Q726. [Easy]** 'Dual diagnosis' in mental health services usually refers to the co-occurrence of which two things?
- **A. Severe mental illness and substance misuse [correct]**
- B. Two different physical illnesses
- C. Depression and anxiety only
- D. Learning disability and epilepsy
_Why:_ Dual diagnosis (co-occurring conditions) refers to severe mental illness (such as schizophrenia or bipolar disorder) occurring alongside substance misuse. NICE NG58 covers coordinated care for this group.
_Source:_ NICE - NG58 Coexisting severe mental illness and substance misuse (2016) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng58

## Older Adult & Delirium

**Q727. [Easy]** Which statement best describes delirium (an acute confusional state)?
- A. A gradual, irreversible decline in memory over years
- **B. An acute, usually reversible disturbance of attention and awareness that develops over hours to days [correct]**
- C. A permanent loss of language ability
- D. A mood disorder with no cognitive features
_Why:_ Delirium is an acute, fluctuating disturbance of attention and awareness developing over hours to days and is often reversible when the underlying cause is treated, unlike the chronic progressive course of dementia.
_Source:_ NICE - CG103 Delirium: prevention, diagnosis and management (2010, updated 2023) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg103

**Q728. [Easy]** Which subtype of delirium is characterised by drowsiness, withdrawal, reduced movement and quietness, making it easy to miss?
- A. Hyperactive delirium
- **B. Hypoactive delirium [correct]**
- C. Mixed delirium
- D. Persistent delirium
_Why:_ Hypoactive delirium presents with lethargy, reduced motor activity and withdrawal. It is common in older adults and is frequently missed because patients appear quiet rather than agitated.
_Source:_ NICE CG103 / NCBI review of delirium in hospitalised older adults (2023) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg103

**Q729. [Easy]** In the mnemonic PINCH ME for common causes of delirium, what does the 'I' stand for?
- A. Inflammation
- **B. Infection [correct]**
- C. Insomnia
- D. Isolation
_Why:_ PINCH ME stands for Pain, Infection, Nutrition, Constipation, Hydration, Medication and Environment - a practical checklist for reversible precipitants of delirium. The 'I' is Infection, a very common trigger in older adults.
_Source:_ British Geriatrics Society - PINCH ME delirium causes mnemonic - https://www.bgs.org.uk/

**Q730. [Easy]** According to the Medicines and Healthcare products Regulatory Agency (MHRA), antipsychotics used in people with dementia are associated with an increased risk of which two serious outcomes?
- A. Weight loss and constipation
- **B. Stroke and death [correct]**
- C. Hearing loss and rash
- D. Diabetes and glaucoma
_Why:_ Regulatory reviews established that antipsychotics increase the risk of cerebrovascular events (stroke) and mortality in older people with dementia, so use must be restricted.
_Source:_ MHRA / NICE NG97 - Antipsychotics in dementia safety guidance (2018, updated 2023) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng97/chapter/Recommendations

**Q731. [Easy]** When managing behavioural and psychological symptoms of dementia (BPSD) such as agitation, what does NICE National Guideline 97 recommend as the first-line approach?
- A. Antipsychotic medication for all patients
- **B. Psychosocial and environmental interventions [correct]**
- C. Physical restraint
- D. Benzodiazepines as routine sedation
_Why:_ NICE NG97 recommends first assessing for causes (such as pain or delirium) then offering psychosocial and environmental interventions. Antipsychotics are reserved for people at risk of harming themselves/others or with severe distress.
_Source:_ NICE - NG97 Dementia: assessment, management and support (2018, updated 2023) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng97/chapter/Recommendations

**Q732. [Easy]** The 4AT is a bedside tool used at the hospital 'front door'. What is it primarily used to detect?
- A. Depression
- **B. Delirium [correct]**
- C. Pain severity
- D. Nutritional status
_Why:_ The 4AT (Alertness, AMT4, Attention, Acute change) is a rapid screening tool for delirium and cognitive impairment, requiring no special training and usable in drowsy or agitated patients.
_Source:_ the4AT.com - 4AT User Guide - https://www.the4at.com/userguide

**Q733. [Easy]** Compared with delirium, the typical onset of dementia is best described as:
- A. Sudden, over hours to days
- **B. Gradual, over months to years [correct]**
- C. Immediate, within minutes
- D. Only ever occurring after surgery
_Why:_ Dementia has an insidious, gradual onset over months to years with progressive decline, whereas delirium develops acutely over hours to days with a fluctuating course.
_Source:_ StatPearls - Differentiating Delirium Versus Dementia in Older Adults (2023) - https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK570594/

**Q734. [Easy]** Which of the following is the most common type of dementia overall?
- A. Vascular dementia
- **B. Alzheimer's disease [correct]**
- C. Dementia with Lewy bodies
- D. Frontotemporal dementia
_Why:_ Alzheimer's disease is the most common cause of dementia, typically presenting with early, prominent short-term memory loss, followed by vascular dementia as the second most common.
_Source:_ NICE - NG97 Dementia (Alzheimer's Society epidemiology) (2018, updated 2023) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng97

**Q735. [Medium]** _Scenario: An 82-year-old admitted with a urinary tract infection is drowsy and inattentive. You complete a 4AT and the total score is 6._ What does a 4AT total score of 4 or above indicate?
- A. Delirium and cognitive impairment are unlikely
- B. Possible cognitive impairment only, no further action
- **C. Possible delirium (with or without cognitive impairment), needing clinical assessment [correct]**
- D. A confirmed, definite diagnosis of dementia
_Why:_ On the 4AT (scored 0-12), a score of 4 or more suggests possible delirium with or without cognitive impairment and prompts full clinical assessment. It is a screen, not a diagnosis.
_Source:_ the4AT.com - 4AT User Guide - https://www.the4at.com/userguide

**Q736. [Medium]** _Scenario: A 79-year-old on the ward becomes confused in the evening. He is lucid at 2pm but disoriented and picking at the bedsheets by 8pm, then settled again by morning._ Which feature most strongly points to delirium rather than dementia?
- A. The confusion is present but stable all day
- **B. The symptoms fluctuate markedly over the course of the day [correct]**
- C. There is a slow decline in memory over two years
- D. There is complete absence of any attention problem
_Why:_ A fluctuating course - symptoms varying markedly over hours, often worse in the evening - together with inattention and acute onset is characteristic of delirium.
_Source:_ StatPearls - Differentiating Delirium Versus Dementia in Older Adults (2023) - https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK570594/

**Q737. [Medium]** _Scenario: A 76-year-old with early dementia has fluctuating alertness, recurrent detailed visual hallucinations of small animals, and stiffness with slowness of movement._ Which type of dementia does this picture most suggest?
- A. Alzheimer's disease
- **B. Dementia with Lewy bodies [correct]**
- C. Frontotemporal dementia
- D. Purely vascular dementia
_Why:_ Fluctuating cognition, recurrent well-formed visual hallucinations and spontaneous parkinsonism are core clinical features of dementia with Lewy bodies. REM sleep behaviour disorder is also characteristic.
_Source:_ McKeith et al - DLB Consortium consensus criteria / Merck Manual (2017) - https://www.merckmanuals.com/professional/neurologic-disorders/delirium-and-dementia/dementia-with-lewy-bodies-and-parkinson-disease-dementia

**Q738. [Medium]** _Scenario: A 60-year-old is being assessed for dementia. Family report early personality change, socially inappropriate behaviour and loss of empathy, but relatively preserved memory in the early stages._ Which dementia type does this presentation most suggest?
- A. Alzheimer's disease
- **B. Behavioural variant frontotemporal dementia [correct]**
- C. Dementia with Lewy bodies
- D. Vascular dementia
_Why:_ Behavioural variant frontotemporal dementia typically has a younger onset with early changes in personality, disinhibition, apathy and loss of empathy, while memory is relatively preserved early on.
_Source:_ Behavioural-variant frontotemporal dementia review (PMC) (2017) - https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC5619539/

**Q739. [Medium]** _Scenario: A 78-year-old with dementia is prescribed oxybutynin for urinary urgency, amitriptyline for sleep and an antihistamine for itch._ What is the main concern about this combination in an older person with dementia?
- **A. High cumulative anticholinergic burden increasing confusion, falls and delirium risk [correct]**
- B. Risk of severe hypoglycaemia
- C. They will cause excessive salivation
- D. They have no interaction and are entirely safe
_Why:_ Oxybutynin, amitriptyline and sedating antihistamines all have anticholinergic effects. Cumulative anticholinergic burden in older adults increases the risk of cognitive impairment, delirium, falls and mortality.
_Source:_ Anticholinergic burden systematic review (PMC) (2015) - https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC4541969/

**Q740. [Medium]** _Scenario: An older inpatient with delirium is agitated but not putting anyone at immediate risk of harm._ According to NICE guidance, what should be tried first before considering any medication?
- A. Immediate intramuscular antipsychotic
- **B. Verbal and non-verbal de-escalation and addressing the underlying cause [correct]**
- C. Physical restraint until calm
- D. A benzodiazepine for all agitated patients
_Why:_ NICE CG103 advises identifying and treating the underlying cause and using verbal and non-verbal de-escalation first. Short-term medication is only considered if a person is distressed or a risk and non-drug measures are ineffective.
_Source:_ NICE - CG103 Delirium: prevention, diagnosis and management (2010, updated 2023) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg103

**Q741. [Medium]** _Scenario: A ward is trying to reduce new cases of delirium among older admissions._ Which of the following is an evidence-based non-pharmacological measure to prevent delirium?
- A. Routine sedation of all older patients at night
- **B. Ensuring hydration, orientation, hearing/glasses aids and good sleep hygiene [correct]**
- C. Keeping patients in a dark, quiet side room at all times regardless of orientation
- D. Restricting all family visiting
_Why:_ NICE CG103 recommends addressing modifiable risk factors: promoting orientation, ensuring hydration and nutrition, providing sensory aids (glasses, hearing aids), promoting good sleep and early mobilisation.
_Source:_ NICE - CG103 Delirium: prevention, diagnosis and management (2010, updated 2023) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg103

**Q742. [Medium]** _Scenario: A 77-year-old presents after a fall and reports two further falls in the past year, with an unsteady gait._ What does NICE recommend should be offered to this patient?
- A. Reassurance only, as falls are a normal part of ageing
- **B. A multifactorial falls risk assessment [correct]**
- C. Immediate antipsychotic medication
- D. Complete bed rest to prevent further falls
_Why:_ NICE CG161 recommends that older people who present after a fall, report recurrent falls, or have gait/balance problems be offered a multifactorial falls risk assessment, followed by tailored interventions.
_Source:_ NICE - CG161 Falls in older people (2013) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg161/chapter/1-recommendations

**Q743. [Medium]** A patient with dementia may have capacity that fluctuates. What is the correct approach to a specific decision when their capacity varies?
- A. Assume they permanently lack capacity once diagnosed with dementia
- **B. Assess capacity for the specific decision at the time it needs to be made, and where practicable wait for a lucid period [correct]**
- C. Ask a relative to decide all matters for them
- D. Capacity only needs assessing once, on admission
_Why:_ Under the Mental Capacity Act 2005, capacity is decision- and time-specific. Where capacity fluctuates, assessment must relate to the specific decision at the relevant time, and where the decision can wait, choose a time when the person is most able to decide.
_Source:_ NICE NG108 / Mental Capacity Act 2005 Code of Practice (2018) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng108

**Q744. [Medium]** _Scenario: A 74-year-old developed sudden confusion 6 hours ago; her daughter says her memory and thinking were completely normal last week._ Which single feature is most useful in distinguishing this from an underlying dementia?
- A. Presence of any memory problem
- **B. The very acute onset over hours with a clear recent baseline of normal cognition [correct]**
- C. Her age
- D. Living alone
_Why:_ Acute onset over hours to days against a background of previously normal cognition points strongly to delirium. A reliable collateral history of the person's baseline is one of the most valuable pieces of information.
_Source:_ StatPearls - Differentiating Delirium Versus Dementia in Older Adults (2023) - https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK570594/

**Q745. [Hard]** _Scenario: You are performing the 4AT on a confused older patient. On the Months Backward test she manages only three months before losing track._ How is the Attention item (Months Backward) scored on the 4AT?
- **A. 7 or more months correct = 0; starts but scores fewer than 7 = 1; untestable/refuses = 2 [correct]**
- B. Any error = 4 points automatically
- C. It is not scored, only observed
- D. Correct months = 2; errors = 0
_Why:_ On the 4AT Attention item, reciting 7 or more months backwards correctly scores 0, starting but achieving fewer than 7 (or refusing) scores 1, and being untestable scores 2. Alertness and Acute change items score 0 or 4.
_Source:_ the4AT.com - 4AT User Guide - https://www.the4at.com/userguide

**Q746. [Hard]** On the 4AT, a clearly abnormal level of alertness (markedly drowsy or agitated) and clear evidence of acute change or fluctuating course each score how many points?
- A. 1 point each
- B. 2 points each
- **C. 4 points each [correct]**
- D. 0 points each
_Why:_ On the 4AT, the Alertness item and the Acute change/fluctuating course item each score 0 or 4. This weighting means an abnormal alertness alone reaches the score of 4 that flags possible delirium.
_Source:_ the4AT.com - 4AT User Guide - https://www.the4at.com/userguide

**Q747. [Hard]** _Scenario: A 71-year-old with suspected dementia with Lewy bodies becomes distressed with hallucinations. A colleague suggests a standard antipsychotic._ Why is particular caution needed with antipsychotics in dementia with Lewy bodies or Parkinson's disease dementia?
- A. They have no effect at all in these conditions
- **B. They can cause severe antipsychotic sensitivity reactions and worsen motor features [correct]**
- C. They always cause immediate cardiac arrest
- D. They are completely safe and preferred first-line
_Why:_ NICE NG97 warns that in dementia with Lewy bodies and Parkinson's disease dementia, antipsychotics can worsen the motor features and, in some cases, cause severe antipsychotic sensitivity reactions.
_Source:_ NICE - NG97 Dementia (recommendation 1.7.4) (2018, updated 2023) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng97/chapter/Recommendations

**Q748. [Hard]** Regarding delirium motor subtypes in hospitalised older adults, which statement is correct?
- A. Hyperactive delirium is the most common and carries the best prognosis
- **B. Hypoactive delirium is the most common subtype and is associated with a worse prognosis, partly because it is often missed [correct]**
- C. All delirium is hyperactive and easily spotted
- D. Motor subtype has no bearing on detection or outcome
_Why:_ Hypoactive delirium is the predominant motor subtype in older inpatients and is frequently under-detected. Studies show it is independently associated with higher hospital mortality than the more obvious hyperactive form.
_Source:_ Detection and management of hyperactive and hypoactive delirium (PMC) (2018) - https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC6221174/

**Q749. [Hard]** _Scenario: A 75-year-old with several small strokes has a history of cognitive decline that worsened suddenly on two occasions rather than smoothly declining._ This 'stepwise' pattern of decline is most characteristic of which dementia type?
- A. Alzheimer's disease
- **B. Vascular dementia [correct]**
- C. Frontotemporal dementia
- D. Dementia with Lewy bodies
_Why:_ Vascular dementia classically shows a stepwise deterioration, with abrupt worsening related to cerebrovascular events, alongside focal neurological signs - contrasting with the smoother decline typical of Alzheimer's disease.
_Source:_ NICE - NG97 Dementia / StatPearls (2018, updated 2023) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng97

**Q750. [Hard]** _Scenario: An older person with dementia is started on a short course of an antipsychotic for severe distress after non-drug measures failed._ What does NICE advise about how such antipsychotic treatment should be used?
- A. Highest tolerated dose, continued indefinitely
- **B. Lowest effective dose for the shortest possible time, with regular review (for example every 6 weeks) [correct]**
- C. Once started, it should never be reviewed
- D. Given only intravenously
_Why:_ NICE NG97 states antipsychotics for BPSD should be used at the lowest effective dose for the shortest possible time, with the situation reassessed regularly (at least every 6 weeks).
_Source:_ NICE - NG97 Dementia (recommendation 1.7.3) (2018, updated 2023) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng97/chapter/Recommendations

**Q751. [Hard]** _Scenario: An older inpatient with known dementia has become acutely more confused than her documented baseline over the last day._ What is the most accurate way to describe this situation and the priority action?
- A. It cannot be delirium because she already has dementia; no action needed
- **B. Delirium is superimposed on dementia; screen for and treat reversible precipitants such as infection or constipation [correct]**
- C. She should be immediately sedated with a benzodiazepine
- D. Assume it is simply progression of her dementia and discharge her
_Why:_ People with dementia are at high risk of delirium, and delirium can be superimposed on dementia. A sudden change from baseline should be treated as possible delirium: screen (e.g. 4AT), and identify/treat precipitants such as infection, pain, constipation, dehydration or medication.
_Source:_ NICE - CG103 Delirium: prevention, diagnosis and management (2010, updated 2023) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg103

## Children, Young People & CAMHS

**Q752. [Easy]** What does 'Gillick competence' assess?
- A. Whether a person aged 16 or 17 can refuse treatment
- **B. Whether a child under 16 has sufficient understanding and maturity to consent to their own medical treatment without parental knowledge or consent [correct]**
- C. Whether a parent can consent on behalf of a child
- D. Whether a child can be detained under the Mental Health Act
_Why:_ Gillick competence assesses whether a child under 16 fully understands what is proposed and has sufficient maturity to consent to their own treatment, following the Gillick ruling and Lord Scarman's test.
_Source:_ NSPCC Learning - Gillick competence and Fraser guidelines (2022) - https://learning.nspcc.org.uk/child-protection-system/gillick-competence-fraser-guidelines

**Q753. [Easy]** The Fraser guidelines apply specifically to which area of advice and treatment for under-16s?
- A. Admission to hospital under the Mental Health Act
- **B. Contraception and sexual health (and, since 2006, sexually transmitted infections and termination of pregnancy) [correct]**
- C. Detention in police custody
- D. Consent to surgery of any kind
_Why:_ Fraser guidelines apply specifically to contraception and sexual health advice/treatment; a 2006 ruling extended them to sexually transmitted infections and termination of pregnancy. Gillick competence is the broader concept.
_Source:_ NSPCC Learning - Gillick competence and Fraser guidelines (2022) - https://learning.nspcc.org.uk/child-protection-system/gillick-competence-fraser-guidelines

**Q754. [Medium]** _Scenario: A 14-year-old asks for contraceptive advice and refuses to tell her parents._ Under the Fraser guidelines, which of the following is NOT one of the conditions a practitioner must be satisfied about before providing advice or treatment?
- A. The young person cannot be persuaded to inform their parents or allow the practitioner to do so
- B. The young person understands the advice given
- **C. The young person has obtained written consent from a person with parental responsibility [correct]**
- D. The young person's physical or mental health is likely to suffer without the advice or treatment
_Why:_ The Fraser conditions do not require parental consent - the whole point is that treatment can proceed without it. The conditions concern the young person's understanding, best interests, likely continuation of sexual activity, and risk to health.
_Source:_ NSPCC Learning - Gillick competence and Fraser guidelines (2022) - https://learning.nspcc.org.uk/child-protection-system/gillick-competence-fraser-guidelines

**Q755. [Medium]** Under the Mental Capacity Act 2005, from what age is a young person presumed to have capacity to make their own decisions in the same way as an adult (unless assessed as lacking it)?
- A. 12
- B. 14
- **C. 16 [correct]**
- D. 18
_Why:_ The Mental Capacity Act 2005 applies to people aged 16 and over, so 16- and 17-year-olds are presumed to have capacity to make decisions unless assessed otherwise.
_Source:_ SCIE - Mental Capacity Act 2005 at a glance - https://www.scie.org.uk/mca/introduction/mental-capacity-act-2005-at-a-glance/

**Q756. [Hard]** _Scenario: A 17-year-old with capacity refuses informal admission to hospital for treatment of a mental disorder._ According to the Mental Health Act 1983 Code of Practice (Chapter 19), can a person with parental responsibility consent to the admission on the young person's behalf?
- A. Yes, parental responsibility always overrides a young person's refusal
- **B. No - if a 16 or 17 year old with capacity refuses, a person with parental responsibility cannot consent on their behalf to override it [correct]**
- C. Only if two parents both agree
- D. Only if the young person is under a Care Order
_Why:_ The Code of Practice states that where a 16-17 year old with capacity refuses informal admission, a person with parental responsibility cannot consent on their behalf; it is inadvisable to rely on parental consent to override a capacitous young person's refusal.
_Source:_ Mental Health Act 1983 Code of Practice - Chapter 19 (2015) - https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/code-of-practice-mental-health-act-1983

**Q757. [Medium]** Under the Children Act 1989, which parent automatically has parental responsibility from birth in England and Wales?
- **A. The birth mother (unless removed by an adoption or parental order) [correct]**
- B. The father only
- C. Whichever parent registers the birth first
- D. Both parents only if they are cohabiting
_Why:_ The birth mother automatically has parental responsibility (unless removed by adoption or a parental order). A married father also has it; an unmarried father acquires it only by birth registration, agreement, or court order.
_Source:_ GOV.UK - Parental responsibility (Children Act 1989) - https://www.gov.uk/parental-rights-responsibilities

**Q758. [Hard]** _Scenario: An unmarried couple have a baby. The father is not yet registered on the birth certificate._ Under the Children Act 1989, how can an unmarried father acquire parental responsibility?
- A. It happens automatically once the child is born
- B. Only through adoption
- **C. By being registered as the child's father on the birth certificate, by a parental responsibility agreement with the mother, or by court order [correct]**
- D. Only if he later marries the mother
_Why:_ An unmarried father acquires parental responsibility by jointly registering the birth (being named on the certificate), a parental responsibility agreement with the mother, or a court order.
_Source:_ GOV.UK - Parental responsibility (Children Act 1989) - https://www.gov.uk/parental-rights-responsibilities

**Q759. [Easy]** Which section of the Mental Health Act 1983 places a duty on hospital managers to ensure patients under 18 are accommodated in an environment suitable for their age?
- A. Section 2
- B. Section 117
- **C. Section 131A [correct]**
- D. Section 136
_Why:_ Section 131A requires hospital managers to ensure that under-18s admitted for mental disorder are accommodated in an environment suitable for their age, subject to their needs.
_Source:_ Mental Health Act 1983, Section 131A / Mental Health Law Online - https://www.mentalhealthlaw.co.uk/MHA_1983_s131A

**Q760. [Medium]** _Scenario: An under-18 is being considered for admission to an adult psychiatric ward because no CAMHS bed is immediately available._ Under section 131A of the Mental Health Act 1983, when determining whether the environment is suitable, whom must hospital managers consult?
- A. A police officer
- **B. A person with experience in child and adolescent mental health services (CAMHS) cases [correct]**
- C. The Care Quality Commission chair
- D. The child's headteacher
_Why:_ Section 131A requires managers to consult a person they consider suitable because of their experience in CAMHS cases when determining whether the environment is age-suitable.
_Source:_ Mental Health Act 1983, Section 131A / Mental Health Law Online - https://www.mentalhealthlaw.co.uk/MHA_1983_s131A

**Q761. [Hard]** _Scenario: A 16-year-old is placed on an adult psychiatric ward in exceptional circumstances._ A provider must notify the Care Quality Commission (CQC) when a patient under 18 is placed on an adult psychiatric ward for a continuous period longer than how many hours?
- A. 24 hours
- **B. 48 hours [correct]**
- C. 72 hours
- D. 1 week
_Why:_ CQC must be notified when an under-18 is placed on an adult psychiatric ward for a continuous period longer than 48 hours.
_Source:_ Care Quality Commission - Children and young people in adult psychiatric units notification - https://www.cqc.org.uk/guidance-providers/notifications/children-young-people-adult-units

**Q762. [Medium]** Under section 131A, admission of an under-18 to an adult psychiatric ward should occur in what circumstances?
- A. Routinely, as adult wards are equally suitable
- **B. Only in exceptional circumstances, where it is the most suitable place for that under-18 [correct]**
- C. Only for those aged 17
- D. Never, under any circumstances
_Why:_ Admissions of under-18s to adult wards are permissible only in exceptional circumstances, where this is judged the most suitable place for the young person.
_Source:_ Mental Health Law Online - Age-appropriate accommodation for children (s131A) - https://www.mentalhealthlaw.co.uk/New_requirements_for_age-appropriate_accommodation_for_children

**Q763. [Easy]** Under the Children Act 1989, a 'child in need' falls under which section?
- **A. Section 17 [correct]**
- B. Section 47
- C. Section 20
- D. Section 31
_Why:_ Section 17 of the Children Act 1989 defines a 'child in need' and places a duty on local authorities to provide services to safeguard and promote their welfare.
_Source:_ Children Act 1989, Section 17 / legislation.gov.uk (1989) - https://www.legislation.gov.uk/ukpga/1989/41/section/17

**Q764. [Medium]** A section 47 enquiry under the Children Act 1989 is triggered when there is reasonable cause to suspect that a child is:
- A. Not achieving a reasonable standard of health without services
- **B. Suffering, or likely to suffer, significant harm [correct]**
- C. Absent from school
- D. Living in temporary accommodation
_Why:_ Section 47 places a duty to make enquiries where there is reasonable cause to suspect a child is suffering, or is likely to suffer, significant harm - the child protection threshold, distinct from the 'child in need' (s17) threshold.
_Source:_ Children Act 1989, Section 47 / legislation.gov.uk (1989) - https://www.legislation.gov.uk/ukpga/1989/41/section/47

**Q765. [Hard]** _Scenario: A referral raises concerns that a child may be at risk of harm from a caregiver._ According to Working Together to Safeguard Children 2023, what is the purpose of a strategy discussion?
- A. To arrange respite care for the family
- **B. To determine whether the significant harm threshold is met and whether a section 47 enquiry is needed [correct]**
- C. To decide school placement
- D. To close the case
_Why:_ A strategy discussion takes place where there is reasonable cause to suspect a child is suffering or likely to suffer significant harm, to determine whether the threshold is met and a section 47 enquiry is required.
_Source:_ HM Government - Working Together to Safeguard Children 2023 (2023) - https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/working-together-to-safeguard-children--2

**Q766. [Medium]** Which distinction did the Children Act 1989 introduce between two categories of concern?
- A. Physical harm versus emotional harm
- **B. 'Child in need' (section 17) versus 'child protection'/significant harm (section 47) [correct]**
- C. Voluntary versus court-ordered care
- D. Under-5s versus over-5s
_Why:_ The Children Act 1989 introduced the distinction between 'child in need' (s17, provision of support services) and 'child protection' (s47, investigation of suspected significant harm).
_Source:_ Children Act 1989 / legislation.gov.uk (1989) - https://www.legislation.gov.uk/ukpga/1989/41/section/17

**Q767. [Easy]** Working Together to Safeguard Children 2023 is best described as:
- A. Optional best-practice advice
- **B. Statutory guidance for multi-agency working to safeguard children [correct]**
- C. A charity fundraising campaign
- D. Guidance that applies only to schools
_Why:_ Working Together to Safeguard Children 2023 is the statutory guidance for multi-agency working to safeguard and promote the welfare of children.
_Source:_ HM Government - Working Together to Safeguard Children 2023 (2023) - https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/working-together-to-safeguard-children--2

**Q768. [Medium]** The 'Think Family' approach in safeguarding practice emphasises:
- A. Working only with the individual child in isolation
- **B. Recognising and responding to the needs of the whole family, including how parental issues affect children and vice versa [correct]**
- C. Removing children from families as a first response
- D. Focusing solely on financial support
_Why:_ Think Family promotes coordinated support that considers the whole family - including how adult problems (e.g. parental mental illness) affect children - through joint working and information sharing across services.
_Source:_ HM Government - Working Together to Safeguard Children 2023 (2023) - https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/working-together-to-safeguard-children--2

**Q769. [Easy]** According to NICE guideline NG225 (2022), a child or young person who has self-harmed should receive which key assessment?
- A. A numerical risk-prediction score only
- **B. A psychosocial assessment by a mental health professional experienced in assessing this group [correct]**
- C. No assessment if physically well
- D. A blood test only
_Why:_ NG225 recommends a psychosocial assessment by a mental health professional experienced in assessing children and young people who self-harm.
_Source:_ NICE - NG225 Self-harm: assessment, management and preventing recurrence (2022) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng225

**Q770. [Hard]** _Scenario: A 15-year-old presents to the ward following an episode of self-harm._ According to NICE NG225, what is the guidance on using risk assessment tools and scales to predict future suicide or self-harm repetition?
- A. They should be the main basis for decisions
- **B. They should not be used to predict future suicide or self-harm repetition or to determine who should receive treatment [correct]**
- C. They are mandatory for all under-18s
- D. They replace the need for a clinical conversation
_Why:_ NG225 advises against using risk assessment tools and scales to predict future self-harm or suicide, or to decide who should be offered treatment, favouring a collaborative psychosocial assessment instead.
_Source:_ NICE - NG225 Self-harm: assessment, management and preventing recurrence (2022) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng225

**Q771. [Medium]** NICE NG225 states that a psychosocial assessment of a child or young person who has self-harmed should additionally explore which areas?
- A. Only the method of self-harm
- **B. Social, peer group, education and home situations, caring responsibilities, social media/internet use, and any safeguarding issues [correct]**
- C. Only family psychiatric history
- D. Only physical injuries
_Why:_ NG225 specifies that assessment of children and young people should additionally cover social, peer, education and home situations, caring responsibilities, social media/internet use, and child protection/safeguarding issues.
_Source:_ NICE - NG225 Self-harm: assessment, management and preventing recurrence (2022) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng225

**Q772. [Easy]** In the four-tier Child and Adolescent Mental Health Services (CAMHS) model, Tier 4 refers to:
- A. Universal services such as GPs and schools
- **B. Highly specialist services, including day and inpatient care [correct]**
- C. School counselling
- D. Primary mental health workers
_Why:_ Tier 4 CAMHS covers highly specialist services such as inpatient and day-patient units for young people at greatest risk, needing intensive input.
_Source:_ NHS England - CAMHS Tier 4 service specification - https://www.england.nhs.uk/publication/child-and-adolescent-mental-health-services-camhs-tier-4-general-adolescent-services-including-specialist-eating-disorder-services/

**Q773. [Medium]** In the four-tier CAMHS model, which tier describes universal services such as general practice, health visiting, school nursing and schools?
- **A. Tier 1 [correct]**
- B. Tier 2
- C. Tier 3
- D. Tier 4
_Why:_ Tier 1 is universal services (GPs, school nursing, health visiting, schools) that promote mental wellbeing and identify early problems.
_Source:_ Open University OpenLearn - Introduction to adolescent mental health (CAMHS tiers) - https://www.open.edu/openlearn/

**Q774. [Hard]** _Scenario: A young person with a learning disability and autism is at risk of admission to a CAMHS Tier 4 inpatient unit._ What is the primary purpose of a Care, Education and Treatment Review (CETR)?
- A. To decide the young person's school timetable
- **B. To ensure the young person is only admitted if absolutely necessary and for the minimum time, receiving the right care in the right place [correct]**
- C. To assess the parents' capacity
- D. To calculate the cost of care
_Why:_ CETRs, part of NHS England's Transforming Care programme, aim to ensure admission only occurs when necessary and for the shortest time, with the young person receiving good care in the right place.
_Source:_ NHS England - Care, Education and Treatment Reviews - https://www.england.nhs.uk/learning-disabilities/care/

**Q775. [Medium]** Care, Education and Treatment Reviews (CETRs) are aimed at which group of young people?
- A. All young people admitted to any hospital
- **B. Young people with a learning disability and/or autism who are in, or at risk of admission to, a Tier 4 inpatient unit [correct]**
- C. Only young people detained under section 2
- D. Young people with physical health conditions only
_Why:_ CETRs are for young people with learning disabilities and/or autism (and mental health needs) who are admitted to, or at risk of admission to, CAMHS Tier 4 inpatient units.
_Source:_ NHS England - Care, Education and Treatment Reviews - https://www.england.nhs.uk/learning-disabilities/care/

**Q776. [Hard]** _Scenario: A 15-year-old assessed as Gillick competent consents to admission for mental health treatment, but her parents object._ Where a Gillick-competent under-16 gives valid consent to treatment or informal admission, what is the effect of parental objection?
- A. Parental objection automatically blocks the treatment
- **B. The competent child's valid consent provides a lawful basis to proceed, so parental objection does not by itself prevent it [correct]**
- C. Treatment cannot proceed without both parents agreeing
- D. The child must be detained under the Mental Health Act instead
_Why:_ A Gillick-competent child's valid consent provides lawful authority; a young person with capacity who consents to informal admission can be admitted irrespective of the views of a person with parental responsibility. (Refusal by a competent child raises separate, more complex issues.)
_Source:_ Mental Health Act 1983 Code of Practice - Chapter 19 (2015) - https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/code-of-practice-mental-health-act-1983

## Perinatal Mental Health

**Q777. [Easy]** Postpartum (puerperal) psychosis is best described as which type of clinical situation?
- A. A routine self-limiting condition needing no treatment
- **B. A psychiatric emergency requiring urgent assessment [correct]**
- C. A normal part of the 'baby blues'
- D. A condition only affecting women with no prior history
_Why:_ The NHS states postpartum psychosis is a serious mental illness that should be treated as a medical emergency, given the risk of harm to mother and baby.
_Source:_ NHS - Postpartum psychosis - https://www.nhs.uk/mental-health/conditions/post-partum-psychosis/

**Q778. [Easy]** Postpartum (puerperal) psychosis symptoms usually start within which timeframe after giving birth?
- **A. Within the first 2 weeks [correct]**
- B. Only after 3 months
- C. Only after 6 months
- D. Exactly at 1 year
_Why:_ The NHS states symptoms usually start suddenly within the first 2 weeks after giving birth, often within hours or days.
_Source:_ NHS - Postpartum psychosis - https://www.nhs.uk/mental-health/conditions/post-partum-psychosis/

**Q779. [Hard]** _Scenario: A woman 5 days postpartum develops sudden confusion, agitation and delusional beliefs. Staff suspect postpartum psychosis._ According to the National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) guideline CG192, within what maximum time should she be referred for assessment?
- **A. Within 4 hours of referral [correct]**
- B. Within 24 hours
- C. Within 72 hours
- D. Within 1 week
_Why:_ NICE CG192 recommendation 1.5.12 states a woman with sudden onset of symptoms suggesting postpartum psychosis should be referred to a secondary mental health service for immediate assessment within 4 hours of referral.
_Source:_ NICE - CG192 Antenatal and postnatal mental health (2014, updated 2020) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg192/chapter/Recommendations

**Q780. [Medium]** According to the National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) guideline CG192, a woman needing inpatient mental health care within 12 months of childbirth should normally be admitted to which setting?
- A. A general adult acute psychiatric ward
- **B. A specialist Mother and Baby Unit (MBU) [correct]**
- C. A general medical ward
- D. A community crisis house without her baby
_Why:_ NICE CG192 recommendation 1.10.1 states women needing inpatient care within 12 months of childbirth should normally be admitted to a specialist Mother and Baby Unit unless there are specific reasons not to.
_Source:_ NICE - CG192 Antenatal and postnatal mental health (2014, updated 2020) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg192/chapter/Recommendations

**Q781. [Easy]** What is the main purpose of a Mother and Baby Unit (MBU) compared with a general psychiatric ward?
- A. It provides cheaper care with fewer staff
- **B. It allows the mother to be admitted together with her baby while receiving specialist perinatal mental health care [correct]**
- C. It only admits women who are physically unwell
- D. It is used only for detained patients under the Mental Health Act
_Why:_ MBUs are specialist inpatient units that admit the mother together with her infant so treatment of severe perinatal mental illness can continue without separating the mother-baby dyad.
_Source:_ Royal College of Psychiatrists - Mother and baby units (MBUs) - https://www.rcpsych.ac.uk/mental-health/treatments-and-wellbeing/mother-and-baby-units-(mbus)

**Q782. [Easy]** Under the Medicines and Healthcare products Regulatory Agency (MHRA) Pregnancy Prevention Programme, valproate is:
- A. Recommended first-line in pregnancy
- **B. Contraindicated in pregnancy [correct]**
- C. Safe at low doses in pregnancy
- D. Only avoided in the third trimester
_Why:_ The MHRA states valproate is contraindicated in pregnancy because of the high risk of birth defects and neurodevelopmental disorders; it must not be used in women or girls of childbearing potential unless Pregnancy Prevention Programme conditions are met.
_Source:_ MHRA - Drug Safety Update: Valproate Pregnancy Prevention Programme (2018) - https://www.gov.uk/drug-safety-update/valproate-medicines-epilim-depakote-contraindicated-in-women-and-girls-of-childbearing-potential-unless-conditions-of-pregnancy-prevention-programme-are-met

**Q783. [Hard]** According to Medicines and Healthcare products Regulatory Agency (MHRA) figures, approximately what proportion of children exposed to valproate in the womb develop physical birth defects (congenital malformations)?
- A. About 1 in 100 (1%)
- **B. About 10 in 100 (10%) [correct]**
- C. About 50 in 100 (50%)
- D. About 90 in 100 (90%)
_Why:_ MHRA guidance states around 10 in every 100 babies exposed to valproate in the womb develop physical birth defects, compared with a background rate of 2-3 in 100.
_Source:_ MHRA - Drug Safety Update: Valproate (2018) - https://www.gov.uk/drug-safety-update/valproate-medicines-epilim-depakote-contraindicated-in-women-and-girls-of-childbearing-potential-unless-conditions-of-pregnancy-prevention-programme-are-met

**Q784. [Hard]** According to Medicines and Healthcare products Regulatory Agency (MHRA) figures, approximately what proportion of children exposed to valproate in the womb develop neurodevelopmental disorders?
- A. About 5 in 100 (5%)
- **B. About 30 to 40 in 100 (30-40%) [correct]**
- C. About 70 in 100 (70%)
- D. About 95 in 100 (95%)
_Why:_ MHRA guidance states approximately 30 to 40 in every 100 children exposed to valproate in the womb experience neurodevelopmental disorders such as delayed learning, speech and language problems.
_Source:_ MHRA - Drug Safety Update: Valproate (2018) - https://www.gov.uk/drug-safety-update/valproate-medicines-epilim-depakote-contraindicated-in-women-and-girls-of-childbearing-potential-unless-conditions-of-pregnancy-prevention-programme-are-met

**Q785. [Medium]** _Scenario: A woman of childbearing potential on your ward is prescribed valproate for a mental health problem._ Under the Pregnancy Prevention Programme, which requirement must be in place?
- A. No contraception is needed if she is single
- **B. Highly effective contraception plus an annual specialist review and a signed Risk Acknowledgement Form [correct]**
- C. A one-off leaflet handed out at discharge
- D. Contraception only during the third trimester
_Why:_ The Pregnancy Prevention Programme requires women of childbearing potential on valproate to use highly effective contraception, attend at least an annual specialist review, and sign a Risk Acknowledgement Form confirming they understand the risks.
_Source:_ MHRA - Drug Safety Update: Valproate (2018) - https://www.gov.uk/drug-safety-update/valproate-medicines-epilim-depakote-contraindicated-in-women-and-girls-of-childbearing-potential-unless-conditions-of-pregnancy-prevention-programme-are-met

**Q786. [Easy]** The main fetal risk associated with lithium taken during the first trimester of pregnancy is:
- A. Neural tube defects
- **B. Fetal cardiac (heart) malformations [correct]**
- C. Limb shortening
- D. Deafness
_Why:_ NICE CG192 warns of the risk of fetal heart malformations (including Ebstein's anomaly) when lithium is taken in the first trimester.
_Source:_ NICE - CG192 Antenatal and postnatal mental health (2014, updated 2020) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg192/chapter/Recommendations

**Q787. [Hard]** The specific heart defect classically associated with first-trimester lithium exposure is:
- **A. Ebstein's anomaly [correct]**
- B. Tetralogy of Fallot
- C. Coarctation of the aorta
- D. Patent ductus arteriosus
_Why:_ Ebstein's anomaly, a malformation of the tricuspid valve, is the cardiac defect classically linked with first-trimester lithium exposure, although the absolute risk is now known to be low.
_Source:_ UK Teratology Information Service (UKTIS) - Use of lithium in pregnancy - https://uktis.org/monographs/use-of-lithium-in-pregnancy/

**Q788. [Hard]** _Scenario: A woman continues lithium during pregnancy after specialist review._ How should serum lithium levels typically be monitored in later pregnancy?
- A. Only once at booking
- **B. Every 4 weeks, then weekly from the 36th week, and during labour [correct]**
- C. Only after delivery
- D. Every 6 months
_Why:_ Where lithium is continued in pregnancy, serum lithium levels should be checked every 4 weeks, then weekly from the 36th week, with levels and fluid balance monitored during labour, because pharmacokinetics change and toxicity risk rises around delivery.
_Source:_ Royal College of Psychiatrists - Lithium in pregnancy and breastfeeding - https://www.rcpsych.ac.uk/mental-health/treatments-and-wellbeing/lithium-in-pregnancy-and-breastfeeding

**Q789. [Medium]** Regarding lithium and breastfeeding, the National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) advises that:
- A. Lithium is the safest option for breastfeeding mothers
- **B. Lithium is not routinely recommended in breastfeeding because it reaches high levels in breast milk with a risk of infant toxicity [correct]**
- C. Breast milk contains no lithium
- D. Breastfeeding increases the mother's lithium requirement
_Why:_ NICE CG192 notes lithium levels may be high in breast milk with a risk of toxicity for the baby, so lithium is not routinely recommended during breastfeeding.
_Source:_ NICE - CG192 Antenatal and postnatal mental health (2014, updated 2020) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg192/chapter/Recommendations

**Q790. [Medium]** When selecting a selective serotonin reuptake inhibitor (SSRI) antidepressant in pregnancy, the National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) advises considering the risk to the newborn of:
- **A. Persistent pulmonary hypertension and neonatal adaptation syndrome [correct]**
- B. Congenital deafness
- C. Neural tube defects
- D. Cleft palate only
_Why:_ NICE CG192 advises weighing the risk of fetal cardiac abnormalities and persistent pulmonary hypertension of the newborn, plus discontinuation/neonatal adaptation symptoms, when choosing antidepressants in pregnancy.
_Source:_ NICE - CG192 Antenatal and postnatal mental health (2014, updated 2020) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg192/chapter/Recommendations

**Q791. [Hard]** Which antidepressant is particularly noted for a possible small increased risk of fetal cardiac malformation, so its choice in early pregnancy is weighed carefully?
- A. Sertraline
- **B. Paroxetine [correct]**
- C. Mirtazapine
- D. Duloxetine
_Why:_ Paroxetine has been associated with a small increased risk of fetal cardiac malformations when taken in the first trimester, which is why its use in early pregnancy is considered carefully against alternatives.
_Source:_ NICE - CG192 Antenatal and postnatal mental health (2014, updated 2020) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg192/chapter/Recommendations

**Q792. [Easy]** The Edinburgh Postnatal Depression Scale (EPDS) is best described as:
- A. A blood test for postnatal depression
- **B. A 10-item self-report questionnaire screening for perinatal depression [correct]**
- C. A diagnostic brain scan
- D. A physical observations chart
_Why:_ The EPDS is a validated 10-item self-report questionnaire used to help identify women at risk of perinatal depression; it is a screening aid, not a diagnostic test.
_Source:_ Cox, Holden & Sagovsky - Edinburgh Postnatal Depression Scale - British Journal of Psychiatry (1987) - https://www.rcpsych.ac.uk/

**Q793. [Medium]** _Scenario: A new mother completes the Edinburgh Postnatal Depression Scale (EPDS) and scores above the cut-off._ What does a raised EPDS score indicate?
- A. A confirmed diagnosis of depression requiring immediate medication
- **B. That further clinical assessment is needed, as she may have depression [correct]**
- C. That she definitely has postpartum psychosis
- D. That no follow-up is required
_Why:_ A raised EPDS score (commonly a cut-off of 10 or above, or 13 or above for probable depression) flags the need for further clinical assessment; it does not by itself diagnose depression.
_Source:_ Cox, Holden & Sagovsky - Edinburgh Postnatal Depression Scale (1987) - https://www.rcpsych.ac.uk/

**Q794. [Hard]** _Scenario: A woman scores 2 on item 10 of the Edinburgh Postnatal Depression Scale (EPDS)._ Item 10 of the EPDS specifically asks about which of the following, meaning any positive score requires further enquiry?
- A. Appetite
- **B. Thoughts of self-harm [correct]**
- C. Sleep quality
- D. Physical pain
_Why:_ Item 10 asks about thoughts of harming oneself. Any score of 1, 2 or 3 on item 10 requires further enquiry and risk assessment regardless of the total score.
_Source:_ Cox, Holden & Sagovsky - Edinburgh Postnatal Depression Scale (1987) - https://www.rcpsych.ac.uk/

**Q795. [Medium]** According to the National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE), which brief tool is recommended to help identify possible depression when first asking about a woman's mental health in the perinatal period?
- **A. The two Whooley questions [correct]**
- B. A full personality assessment
- C. The Mini-Mental State Examination
- D. A dexamethasone suppression test
_Why:_ NICE CG192 recommends considering the two Whooley depression identification questions as part of a general discussion about mental wellbeing, with tools such as the EPDS or PHQ-9 used for further assessment or monitoring.
_Source:_ NICE - CG192 Antenatal and postnatal mental health (2014, updated 2020) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg192/chapter/Recommendations

**Q796. [Easy]** According to MBRRACE-UK reports, which is a leading cause of direct maternal death in the year after the end of pregnancy?
- **A. Suicide [correct]**
- B. Road traffic accidents
- C. Appendicitis
- D. Asthma
_Why:_ MBRRACE-UK reports consistently find that maternal suicide is a leading cause of direct maternal death across the first year after pregnancy, and the leading cause of death occurring between six weeks and one year postnatally.
_Source:_ MBRRACE-UK - Saving Lives, Improving Mothers' Care (2025) - https://www.npeu.ox.ac.uk/mbrrace-uk

**Q797. [Hard]** According to MBRRACE-UK data, maternal suicide is specifically the leading cause of maternal death during which period?
- A. The first 24 hours after birth only
- **B. Between 6 weeks and 1 year after the end of pregnancy [correct]**
- C. Only during labour
- D. Only in the antenatal period
_Why:_ MBRRACE-UK reports that maternal suicide is the leading cause of death occurring between six weeks and one year after the end of pregnancy (the late maternal period), where psychiatric causes account for about a third of deaths.
_Source:_ MBRRACE-UK - Saving Lives, Improving Mothers' Care (2025) - https://www.npeu.ox.ac.uk/mbrrace-uk

**Q798. [Medium]** _Scenario: A woman with a history of bipolar disorder has just given birth._ Why should staff be especially alert for postpartum psychosis in the first 2 weeks?
- A. Bipolar disorder always resolves after birth
- **B. A history of bipolar disorder or severe mental illness markedly increases the risk of postpartum psychosis [correct]**
- C. Postpartum psychosis only affects first-time mothers
- D. She cannot develop psychosis while breastfeeding
_Why:_ NICE CG192 advises being alert for symptoms of postpartum psychosis in the first 2 weeks in women with past or present severe mental illness (such as bipolar disorder) or a family history of severe perinatal illness, as their risk is greatly increased.
_Source:_ NICE - CG192 Antenatal and postnatal mental health (2014, updated 2020) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg192/chapter/Recommendations

**Q799. [Medium]** _Scenario: On a postnatal ward, a woman 3 days after delivery becomes increasingly elated, is not sleeping, and describes unusual beliefs that her baby is 'special and chosen'._ What is the most appropriate immediate nursing action?
- A. Reassure her these are normal 'baby blues' and review in a week
- **B. Treat as a possible psychiatric emergency and arrange urgent perinatal mental health assessment [correct]**
- C. Discharge her home to rest
- D. Advise her to stop breastfeeding and wait
_Why:_ Sudden elation, insomnia and delusional ideas days after birth suggest postpartum psychosis, a psychiatric emergency; NICE requires urgent (within 4 hours) referral for assessment rather than watchful waiting.
_Source:_ NICE - CG192 Antenatal and postnatal mental health (2014, updated 2020) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg192/chapter/Recommendations

**Q800. [Hard]** Approximately how common is postpartum (puerperal) psychosis?
- **A. About 1 to 2 in every 1,000 births [correct]**
- B. About 1 in every 10 births
- C. About 1 in every 3 births
- D. About 1 in every 100,000 births
_Why:_ Postpartum psychosis is rare, occurring in approximately 1 to 2 per 1,000 women after childbirth, but it is a psychiatric emergency because of the elevated risk of suicide and harm to the infant.
_Source:_ NCBI StatPearls - Postpartum Psychosis (2023) - https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK544304/

**Q801. [Easy]** Which of the following is a warning sign that a woman in the perinatal period may be deteriorating and needs urgent mental health review?
- **A. New thoughts of harming herself or the baby, or rapidly changing mental state [correct]**
- B. Feeling tired in the first week after birth
- C. Occasional tearfulness that quickly settles
- D. Mild anxiety about feeding that responds to reassurance
_Why:_ Red-flag signs of serious deterioration include new or persistent thoughts of self-harm or harm to the infant, and rapid or marked change in mental state, which warrant urgent assessment. Transient tiredness or brief tearfulness are common and less concerning.
_Source:_ NICE - CG192 Antenatal and postnatal mental health (2014, updated 2020) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg192/chapter/Recommendations

## Learning Disability & Autism

**Q802. [Easy]** The Oliver McGowan Mandatory Training on Learning Disability and Autism was made a statutory requirement for Care Quality Commission (CQC) registered health and social care providers under which piece of legislation?
- **A. Health and Care Act 2022 [correct]**
- B. Mental Health Act 1983
- C. Care Act 2014
- D. Autism Act 2009
_Why:_ Section 181 of the Health and Care Act 2022 introduced the requirement that CQC-registered providers ensure staff receive learning disability and autism training appropriate to their role; the Oliver McGowan Mandatory Training is the government's preferred programme to meet this.
_Source:_ NHS England - Oliver McGowan Mandatory Training - https://www.hee.nhs.uk/our-work/learning-disability/oliver-mcgowan-mandatory-training-learning-disability-autism

**Q803. [Easy]** The Oliver McGowan Mandatory Training on Learning Disability and Autism is delivered across how many tiers?
- A. One
- **B. Two [correct]**
- C. Three
- D. Four
_Why:_ The training has two tiers: Tier 1 for staff needing general awareness, and Tier 2 for staff who may provide care and support for people with a learning disability and autistic people. Both share the same eLearning; Tier 2 adds a full day of face-to-face training.
_Source:_ Skills for Care - Oliver McGowan Mandatory Training - https://www.skillsforcare.org.uk/Developing-your-workforce/Care-topics/Learning-disability-and-autism/The-Oliver-McGowan-Mandatory-Training.aspx

**Q804. [Medium]** _Scenario: A ward manager is deciding which tier of Oliver McGowan training a clinical nurse who directly cares for autistic inpatients should complete._ Which tier of the Oliver McGowan Mandatory Training is intended for staff who provide direct care and support to people with a learning disability and autistic people?
- A. Tier 1
- **B. Tier 2 [correct]**
- C. Tier 3
- D. Tier 0
_Why:_ Tier 2 is for staff who may need to provide care and support; it includes the eLearning plus a one-day face-to-face session co-delivered with experts with lived experience. Tier 1 is for general awareness only.
_Source:_ National Autistic Society - Tier 2 Oliver McGowan Training - https://www.autism.org.uk/what-we-do/professional-development/training-and-conferences/oliver-mcgowan

**Q805. [Medium]** A distinctive feature of the Oliver McGowan Mandatory Training is that the interactive and face-to-face sessions are co-delivered by which group of people?
- A. Only senior medical consultants
- **B. Experts by experience - a person with a learning disability and an autistic person - alongside a facilitator [correct]**
- C. External management consultants
- D. Care Quality Commission inspectors
_Why:_ The training is co-delivered by people with lived experience: a person with a learning disability and an autistic person, working with a facilitator. This co-production is central to the programme's design.
_Source:_ NHS England - Oliver McGowan Mandatory Training - https://www.hee.nhs.uk/our-work/learning-disability/oliver-mcgowan-mandatory-training-learning-disability-autism

**Q806. [Easy]** Under the Equality Act 2010, health and care providers have a legal duty to make what for disabled people, including people with a learning disability?
- **A. Reasonable adjustments [correct]**
- B. Financial compensation
- C. Separate hospitals
- D. Mandatory detention
_Why:_ The Equality Act 2010 places an anticipatory duty on organisations, including hospitals and GP surgeries, to make reasonable adjustments to remove barriers disabled people face in accessing services.
_Source:_ GOV.UK - Reasonable adjustments: a legal duty - https://www.gov.uk/rights-disabled-person/education-rights

**Q807. [Medium]** _Scenario: An autistic patient is admitted to a mental health ward. Staff want a patient-held document summarising his needs, likes, dislikes and how he communicates._ What is the name of the patient-held, personalised document that captures key information about a person with a learning disability or autism and their needs to support reasonable adjustments in hospital?
- **A. Hospital passport [correct]**
- B. Discharge summary
- C. Section 17 leave form
- D. Consent to treatment form
_Why:_ A hospital passport is a patient-held, personalised record of what a person with a learning disability or autism needs, helping staff make reasonable adjustments and understand the individual quickly.
_Source:_ Mencap - Reasonable adjustments in hospital - https://www.mencap.org.uk/advice-and-support/health/health-guides

**Q808. [Hard]** By which date must all publicly funded NHS and adult social care services be able to share, write to and read from the national Reasonable Adjustment Digital Flag?
- **A. 30 September 2026 [correct]**
- B. 1 April 2010
- C. 1 August 2016
- D. 6 September 2025
_Why:_ NHS England states that all NHS and other publicly-funded health and social care services must be able to share, write to and read from the Reasonable Adjustment Digital Flag by 30 September 2026.
_Source:_ NHS England - Reasonable adjustment digital flag - https://www.england.nhs.uk/learning-disabilities/improving-health/reasonable-adjustments/

**Q809. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient with a learning disability repeatedly bangs their head. Staff assume this is simply part of their learning disability and do not investigate further; the patient actually has a painful dental abscess._ This failure to investigate a physical cause because symptoms were attributed to the person's learning disability is an example of what?
- **A. Diagnostic overshadowing [correct]**
- B. Best interests decision
- C. A reasonable adjustment
- D. Positive risk taking
_Why:_ Diagnostic overshadowing is attributing physical or mental health symptoms to a person's learning disability or autism rather than considering a separate new illness, which delays diagnosis and treatment.
_Source:_ Local Government Association - Diagnostic overshadowing - https://www.local.gov.uk/our-support/partners-care-and-health/care-and-health-improvement/systems-resilience/diagnostic

**Q810. [Easy]** What does the abbreviation STOMP stand for in the context of people with a learning disability and autistic people?
- **A. Stopping Over-Medication of People with a learning disability, autism or both [correct]**
- B. Standard Treatment Of Mental Patients
- C. Supporting Therapy Over Medical Prescribing
- D. Safe Transfer Of Medical Prescriptions
_Why:_ STOMP stands for Stopping Over-Medication of People with a learning disability, autism or both. It is a national NHS England programme to reduce inappropriate prescribing of psychotropic medicines.
_Source:_ NHS England - STOMP and STAMP - https://www.england.nhs.uk/learning-disabilities/improving-health/stomp-stamp/

**Q811. [Medium]** Which type of medication is STOMP (Stopping Over-Medication of People with a learning disability, autism or both) particularly concerned with reducing when prescribed inappropriately?
- **A. Psychotropic medicines such as antipsychotics [correct]**
- B. Antibiotics
- C. Vaccines
- D. Insulin
_Why:_ STOMP focuses on reducing the inappropriate prescribing of psychotropic medicines, including antipsychotics and antidepressants, often used to manage behaviour without a clear clinical justification.
_Source:_ NHS England - STOMP and STAMP - https://www.england.nhs.uk/learning-disabilities/improving-health/stomp-stamp/

**Q812. [Hard]** STOMP is not an anti-medication campaign. Which statement best describes its actual aim?
- **A. To ensure medicines are used in the right way, reviewed regularly and optimised, so people stay well and have a good quality of life [correct]**
- B. To stop all people with a learning disability from ever taking any medication
- C. To replace all medicines with talking therapies only
- D. To transfer prescribing responsibility solely to families
_Why:_ NHS England is explicit that STOMP and STAMP are not anti-medication; they aim to ensure the right use, review and optimisation of medication so people stay well and have a good quality of life.
_Source:_ NHS England - STOMP and STAMP - https://www.england.nhs.uk/learning-disabilities/improving-health/stomp-stamp/

**Q813. [Hard]** The paediatric programme that runs alongside STOMP to ensure children and young people get the right medication for the right reason is known by which abbreviation?
- **A. STAMP [correct]**
- B. STEP
- C. SNAP
- D. SWAP
_Why:_ STAMP stands for Supporting Treatment and Appropriate Medication in Paediatrics, the sister programme to STOMP for autistic children and young people and those with a learning disability.
_Source:_ NHS England - STOMP and STAMP - https://www.england.nhs.uk/learning-disabilities/improving-health/stomp-stamp/

**Q814. [Easy]** What does LeDeR stand for in NHS England's programme to improve care for people with a learning disability and autistic people?
- **A. Learning from Lives and Deaths - people with a learning disability and autistic people [correct]**
- B. Learning Disability Emergency Response
- C. Legal Duty for Enhanced Reviews
- D. Local Death and Emergency Register
_Why:_ LeDeR stands for Learning from Lives and Deaths - people with a learning disability and autistic people. It reviews the care people received to identify learning and reduce health inequalities and premature deaths.
_Source:_ NHS England - Learning from lives and deaths (LeDeR) - https://www.england.nhs.uk/learning-disabilities/improving-health/learning-from-lives-and-deaths/

**Q815. [Medium]** What is the main purpose of the LeDeR (Learning from Lives and Deaths) programme?
- **A. To learn from the care people received in order to improve services and reduce health inequalities and premature deaths [correct]**
- B. To prosecute individual clinicians for errors
- C. To decide compensation payments for families
- D. To register every birth of a child with a learning disability
_Why:_ LeDeR is a service-improvement programme that reviews the health and social care of people with a learning disability and autistic people who have died, to identify good practice and where improvements are needed. It is not a disciplinary or compensation process.
_Source:_ NHS England - Learning from lives and deaths (LeDeR) - https://www.england.nhs.uk/learning-disabilities/improving-health/learning-from-lives-and-deaths/

**Q816. [Hard]** _Scenario: A person with a learning disability aged 25 dies on your ward. A colleague asks who is allowed to report the death to LeDeR._ Who can notify a death to the LeDeR (Learning from Lives and Deaths) programme?
- **A. Anyone can notify a death via the online LeDeR system [correct]**
- B. Only the coroner
- C. Only a hospital consultant
- D. Only the person's next of kin
_Why:_ NHS England states that anyone can notify a death to the online LeDeR system. Reviews cover people with a learning disability and autistic people who have died aged 18 and above.
_Source:_ NHS England - Learning from lives and deaths (LeDeR) - https://www.england.nhs.uk/learning-disabilities/improving-health/learning-from-lives-and-deaths/

**Q817. [Easy]** From what age is a person on their GP's learning disability register normally invited for an annual health check?
- **A. 14 years and over [correct]**
- B. 5 years and over
- C. 18 years and over
- D. 65 years and over
_Why:_ Anyone aged 14 or over with a learning disability who is on their GP practice's learning disability register should be invited for a free annual health check.
_Source:_ NHS England - Annual health checks - https://www.england.nhs.uk/learning-disabilities/improving-health/annual-health-checks/

**Q818. [Medium]** To be invited for an annual health check, a person with a learning disability normally needs to be recorded where?
- **A. On their GP practice's learning disability register [correct]**
- B. On the electoral roll
- C. On a hospital waiting list
- D. On the safeguarding register
_Why:_ Invitations for annual health checks are sent to people aged 14 and over who are on their GP practice's learning disability register. A formal clinical diagnosis is not always required for the GP to add someone to the register.
_Source:_ NHS England - Learning Disability Register - https://www.england.nhs.uk/learning-disabilities/improving-health/annual-health-checks/

**Q819. [Hard]** Annual health checks for people with a learning disability are important largely because this group experiences what compared with the general population?
- **A. Poorer health and higher rates of avoidable, premature death [correct]**
- B. Identical health outcomes
- C. Better than average life expectancy
- D. No increased health risks
_Why:_ People with a learning disability experience significant health inequalities and die earlier than the general population, often from avoidable causes. Annual health checks help find and treat problems early.
_Source:_ NHS England - Annual health checks - https://www.england.nhs.uk/learning-disabilities/improving-health/annual-health-checks/

**Q820. [Medium]** _Scenario: An autistic patient becomes distressed on a busy ward with bright lights, alarms and constant noise._ Which reasonable adjustment is most appropriate to address this patient's sensory needs?
- **A. Reduce sensory triggers, for example by lowering noise and lighting or offering a quieter space [correct]**
- B. Increase the number of staff talking to them at once
- C. Turn all the lights up brighter
- D. Move them to the busiest part of the ward
_Why:_ Many autistic people experience sensory sensitivities. Reducing triggers such as noise and bright lighting, or providing a low-stimulation space, is a recognised reasonable adjustment that reduces distress.
_Source:_ NHS England - Reasonable adjustments - https://www.england.nhs.uk/learning-disabilities/improving-health/reasonable-adjustments/

**Q821. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient with a learning disability struggles to understand written appointment letters full of medical jargon._ Under the Accessible Information Standard, which communication adjustment would be most appropriate?
- **A. Provide information in an accessible format such as easy read [correct]**
- B. Send longer letters with more technical detail
- C. Only communicate by email
- D. Refuse to change the format
_Why:_ The Accessible Information Standard requires services to identify and meet people's information and communication needs, for example by providing easy read materials for people with a learning disability.
_Source:_ NHS England - Accessible Information Standard - https://www.england.nhs.uk/about/equality/equality-hub/patient-equalities-programme/equality-frameworks-and-information-standards/accessibleinfo/

**Q822. [Hard]** The Accessible Information Standard, which requires NHS services to meet the communication needs of people with a disability, impairment or sensory loss, has been mandatory for NHS and publicly funded adult social care organisations since which date?
- **A. 1 August 2016 [correct]**
- B. 1 April 2010
- C. 11 November 2016
- D. 6 September 2025
_Why:_ From 1 August 2016 all organisations providing NHS or publicly funded adult social care have been legally required to follow the Accessible Information Standard, under section 250 of the Health and Social Care Act 2012.
_Source:_ NHS England - Accessible Information Standard - https://www.england.nhs.uk/about/equality/equality-hub/patient-equalities-programme/equality-frameworks-and-information-standards/accessibleinfo/

**Q823. [Easy]** Under the Mental Capacity Act 2005, what must you assume about an adult unless it is established otherwise?
- **A. That they have capacity to make the decision [correct]**
