- B. That they lack capacity
- C. That a relative should decide
- D. That the doctor should always decide
_Why:_ The first principle of the Mental Capacity Act 2005 is the presumption of capacity: every adult must be assumed to have capacity to make a decision unless it is proved otherwise.
_Source:_ SCIE - Mental Capacity Act 2005 at a glance - https://www.scie.org.uk/mca/introduction/mental-capacity-act-2005-at-a-glance/

**Q824. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient with a learning disability makes a decision that staff think is unwise._ Under the Mental Capacity Act 2005, how should staff regard a person who makes what appears to be an unwise decision?
- **A. An unwise decision alone does not mean the person lacks capacity [correct]**
- B. They automatically lack capacity
- C. A best interests decision must immediately be made for them
- D. Their learning disability proves they cannot decide
_Why:_ A core principle of the Mental Capacity Act 2005 is that a person is not to be treated as unable to make a decision merely because it is unwise. Capacity cannot be assumed to be absent because of a diagnosis such as a learning disability.
_Source:_ SCIE - Mental Capacity Act 2005 at a glance - https://www.scie.org.uk/mca/introduction/mental-capacity-act-2005-at-a-glance/

**Q825. [Hard]** The Autism Act 2009 is historically significant in England because it was the first law of its kind. What made it unique?
- **A. It was the first disability-specific piece of legislation in England [correct]**
- B. It abolished the Mental Health Act
- C. It applied only to children
- D. It removed the need for reasonable adjustments
_Why:_ The Autism Act 2009 was the first ever disability-specific law in England. It placed a duty on the Secretary of State to publish and keep under review an autism strategy and to issue statutory guidance.
_Source:_ House of Commons Library - Autism policy and services - https://commonslibrary.parliament.uk/

**Q826. [Medium]** Which combination best reflects the recognised NHS definition of a learning disability?
- **A. A significantly reduced ability to understand new or complex information and learn new skills, with reduced ability to cope independently, which started before adulthood [correct]**
- B. A physical disability acquired in old age
- C. A short-term difficulty caused by tiredness or stress
- D. A specific difficulty with reading only, such as dyslexia
_Why:_ The NHS definition of a learning disability includes impaired intelligence (reduced ability to understand new or complex information and learn new skills), impaired social functioning (reduced ability to cope independently), with onset before adulthood. A specific learning difficulty such as dyslexia is not the same as a learning disability.
_Source:_ NHS Data Dictionary - Learning Disability (May 2024) - https://www.datadictionary.nhs.uk/

## Medicines - Lithium & Mood Stabilisers

**Q827. [Easy]** What is the recommended therapeutic serum lithium concentration range for treatment in adults, measured 12 hours after the dose?
- A. 0.1-0.4 mmol/L
- **B. 0.4-1.0 mmol/L [correct]**
- C. 1.2-2.0 mmol/L
- D. 2.0-3.0 mmol/L
_Why:_ The BNF gives a therapeutic range of 0.4-1.0 mmol/L (sample taken 12 hours post-dose), with the lower end favoured for maintenance and in older adults. Lithium has a narrow therapeutic index.
_Source:_ BNF - Lithium carbonate, therapeutic drug monitoring - https://bnf.nice.org.uk/drugs/lithium-carbonate/

**Q828. [Easy]** _Scenario: A patient on lithium is due for a routine lithium level._ How long after the last dose should a blood sample for a routine serum lithium level be taken?
- A. 2 hours
- B. 6 hours
- **C. 12 hours [correct]**
- D. 24 hours
_Why:_ Routine lithium samples are taken 12 hours after the dose (a 'trough' level). Sampling at the wrong time gives a misleading result.
_Source:_ BNF - Lithium carbonate, therapeutic drug monitoring - https://bnf.nice.org.uk/drugs/lithium-carbonate/

**Q829. [Medium]** In a stable patient on lithium, how often should renal function (estimated glomerular filtration rate, eGFR), thyroid function (thyroid-stimulating hormone, TSH) and calcium normally be checked?
- A. Every month
- B. Every 3 months
- **C. Every 6 months [correct]**
- D. Once a year only
_Why:_ Renal function, thyroid function and calcium are checked every 6 months (more often if clinically indicated) in stable patients. Lithium serum levels are also checked at least every 6 months once stable.
_Source:_ BNF / NICE - Lithium monitoring - https://bnf.nice.org.uk/drugs/lithium-carbonate/

**Q830. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient on long-term lithium reports new blurred vision, vomiting, diarrhoea, coarse tremor and unsteadiness._ What should these symptoms make you suspect?
- A. A normal, expected side effect needing no action
- **B. Lithium toxicity [correct]**
- C. Thyroid overactivity
- D. A simple viral illness only
_Why:_ Blurred vision, vomiting, diarrhoea, muscle weakness/coarse tremor, drowsiness and loss of coordination are recognised signs of lithium toxicity. Lithium should be withheld and an urgent level and review arranged.
_Source:_ BNF - Lithium carbonate, toxicity - https://bnf.nice.org.uk/drugs/lithium-carbonate/

**Q831. [Easy]** As part of the national lithium patient safety pack, what should every patient on lithium be given and encouraged to carry?
- **A. A lithium alert card and record book [correct]**
- B. A steroid emergency card
- C. An anticoagulant alert card
- D. No card is needed for lithium
_Why:_ Patients starting lithium receive a pack containing an information booklet, a lithium alert card (to show any healthcare professional) and a record book for blood results, which they should bring to appointments and admissions.
_Source:_ MHRA / NPSA - lithium patient safety pack - https://www.gov.uk/drug-safety-update

**Q832. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient stable on lithium is newly prescribed regular ibuprofen for back pain and started on a thiazide diuretic._ What is the main risk of these combinations, and why?
- A. They lower lithium levels, risking relapse
- **B. They raise lithium levels and risk toxicity, by reducing renal lithium clearance [correct]**
- C. They have no effect on lithium
- D. They speed up lithium excretion causing dehydration
_Why:_ Non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs (NSAIDs) and thiazide diuretics reduce renal clearance of lithium, raising serum levels and risking toxicity. ACE inhibitors do the same. Extra monitoring is needed when these are added.
_Source:_ BNF - Lithium interactions - https://bnf.nice.org.uk/interactions/lithium/

## Medicines - Antipsychotics & Clozapine

**Q833. [Easy]** Why do patients taking clozapine require mandatory regular blood tests?
- A. To check kidney function only
- **B. To detect a fall in white cells / neutrophils (neutropenia and agranulocytosis) [correct]**
- C. To monitor cholesterol only
- D. To measure vitamin D levels
_Why:_ Clozapine can cause neutropenia and potentially fatal agranulocytosis, so full blood count monitoring of the white cell count and neutrophils is mandatory throughout treatment.
_Source:_ BNF - Clozapine, monitoring requirements - https://bnf.nice.org.uk/drugs/clozapine/

**Q834. [Medium]** For a patient with stable blood counts, what is the standard clozapine full blood count monitoring schedule in the United Kingdom?
- A. Weekly for life
- **B. Weekly for 18 weeks, then every 2 weeks to 1 year, then every 4 weeks [correct]**
- C. Monthly from the start
- D. Only at the start of treatment
_Why:_ UK monitoring is weekly for the first 18 weeks, then fortnightly until the end of the first year, then every 4 weeks thereafter provided counts remain stable.
_Source:_ BNF - Clozapine, monitoring requirements - https://bnf.nice.org.uk/drugs/clozapine/

**Q835. [Hard]** _Scenario: A clozapine blood result comes back as a 'red' result on the monitoring service._ Which neutrophil threshold defines a 'red' result requiring clozapine to be stopped (in a patient without benign ethnic neutropenia)?
- A. Absolute neutrophil count below 3.0 x10^9/L
- B. Absolute neutrophil count below 2.0 x10^9/L
- **C. Absolute neutrophil count below 1.5 x10^9/L [correct]**
- D. Absolute neutrophil count below 0.5 x10^9/L
_Why:_ A 'red' result is a white cell count below 3.0 x10^9/L and/or an absolute neutrophil count below 1.5 x10^9/L; clozapine must be stopped. An 'amber' result is a neutrophil count 1.5 to below 2.0 x10^9/L.
_Source:_ NHS SPS - clozapine haematological monitoring - https://www.sps.nhs.uk/articles/clinical-considerations-for-patients-prescribed-clozapine/

**Q836. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient has missed all of their clozapine for more than 48 hours after a period of disengagement._ What must happen before clozapine is restarted?
- A. Restart at the previous full maintenance dose
- **B. Re-titrate from a low dose (usually 12.5 mg), as if starting for the first time [correct]**
- C. Give a double dose to catch up
- D. Restart at half the previous dose with no titration
_Why:_ If clozapine is missed for more than 48 hours it must be re-titrated from a low starting dose (usually 12.5 mg once or twice daily) because tolerance is lost, and restarting at the old dose risks serious adverse effects such as profound hypotension.
_Source:_ BNF - Clozapine, missed doses - https://bnf.nice.org.uk/drugs/clozapine/

**Q837. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient started clozapine 3 weeks ago and now has a persistent resting tachycardia, chest discomfort, breathlessness and flu-like symptoms._ Which serious clozapine adverse effect must be urgently excluded, and when is the risk highest?
- **A. Myocarditis, with highest risk in the first 2 months of treatment [correct]**
- B. Agranulocytosis, with highest risk after 1 year
- C. Diabetes, with highest risk in the first week
- D. Tardive dyskinesia, with highest risk in the first month
_Why:_ Clozapine can cause myocarditis (and cardiomyopathy), with the risk greatest in the first two months of treatment. Persistent tachycardia, chest pain, dyspnoea, fever or flu-like symptoms in this window warrant urgent cardiac assessment.
_Source:_ MHRA / BNF - Clozapine cardiac safety - https://bnf.nice.org.uk/drugs/clozapine/

**Q838. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient on clozapine reports not having opened their bowels for several days._ Why is constipation on clozapine treated as a potentially serious issue rather than a minor complaint?
- A. It only affects appetite
- **B. It can progress to intestinal obstruction, faecal impaction or paralytic ileus, which can be fatal [correct]**
- C. It always resolves without treatment
- D. It indicates the dose is too low
_Why:_ The MHRA has warned that clozapine-induced gastrointestinal hypomotility can cause potentially fatal intestinal obstruction, faecal impaction and paralytic ileus. Bowel habit should be monitored and constipation treated promptly.
_Source:_ MHRA Drug Safety Update - clozapine gastrointestinal risk (2017) (2017) - https://www.gov.uk/drug-safety-update/clozapine-reminder-of-potentially-fatal-risk-of-intestinal-obstruction-faecal-impaction-and-paralytic-ileus

**Q839. [Easy]** Which of the following is an extrapyramidal side effect of antipsychotic medication?
- A. Constipation
- **B. Acute dystonia (sustained muscle spasm) [correct]**
- C. Dry mouth
- D. Weight gain
_Why:_ Extrapyramidal side effects include acute dystonia, parkinsonism, akathisia and tardive dyskinesia. Dry mouth, constipation and weight gain are other antipsychotic side effects but are not extrapyramidal.
_Source:_ BNF - Antipsychotic drugs, extrapyramidal symptoms - https://bnf.nice.org.uk/treatment-summaries/antipsychotic-drugs/

**Q840. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient on an antipsychotic develops a high fever, marked muscle rigidity, fluctuating blood pressure and confusion._ Which life-threatening reaction should you suspect?
- A. Serotonin syndrome
- **B. Neuroleptic malignant syndrome (NMS) [correct]**
- C. Anaphylaxis
- D. Diabetic ketoacidosis
_Why:_ Neuroleptic malignant syndrome presents with hyperthermia, severe muscle rigidity, autonomic instability and altered consciousness, usually with a raised creatine kinase. It is a medical emergency; the antipsychotic must be stopped.
_Source:_ BNF - Antipsychotics / neuroleptic malignant syndrome - https://bnf.nice.org.uk/treatment-summaries/antipsychotic-drugs/

**Q841. [Hard]** Which investigation is characteristically elevated in neuroleptic malignant syndrome?
- A. Serum sodium
- **B. Creatine kinase (CK) [correct]**
- C. Thyroid-stimulating hormone (TSH)
- D. Serum lithium level
_Why:_ Serum creatine kinase is elevated in the large majority of neuroleptic malignant syndrome cases, reflecting muscle breakdown from severe rigidity, and can lead to rhabdomyolysis and acute kidney injury.
_Source:_ BNF - Antipsychotics / neuroleptic malignant syndrome - https://bnf.nice.org.uk/treatment-summaries/antipsychotic-drugs/

**Q842. [Medium]** Which set of checks is part of routine metabolic monitoring for a patient on a regular antipsychotic?
- **A. Weight/waist circumference, blood glucose or HbA1c, and blood lipids [correct]**
- B. Only blood pressure
- C. Only full blood count
- D. Only an eye examination
_Why:_ Antipsychotics carry metabolic risk, so monitoring includes weight and waist circumference, blood glucose or glycated haemoglobin (HbA1c) and blood lipids, alongside blood pressure and pulse, at baseline and at regular intervals.
_Source:_ NICE CG178 / BNF - antipsychotic monitoring - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg178

**Q843. [Medium]** Some antipsychotics can prolong the QT interval on the electrocardiogram (ECG). Why does this matter?
- A. It causes weight gain
- **B. It increases the risk of serious ventricular arrhythmia [correct]**
- C. It lowers the seizure threshold only
- D. It has no clinical significance
_Why:_ QT prolongation increases the risk of the ventricular arrhythmia torsades de pointes, which can be fatal. This is why an ECG and electrolyte checks are recommended, especially with higher doses or QT-prolonging drugs.
_Source:_ BNF - Antipsychotics, QT prolongation - https://bnf.nice.org.uk/treatment-summaries/antipsychotic-drugs/

**Q844. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient is prescribed a total antipsychotic dose above the maximum recommended in the British National Formulary (high-dose antipsychotic treatment)._ Which additional monitoring is specifically recommended because of the increased cardiac risk?
- **A. Regular electrocardiogram (ECG) and electrolyte monitoring [correct]**
- B. Weekly eye tests
- C. Daily urinalysis only
- D. No additional monitoring is required
_Why:_ High-dose antipsychotic treatment increases the risk of QT prolongation, so regular ECGs plus electrolytes, pulse, blood pressure and temperature are recommended. A QTc above about 500 ms, or a marked increase, should prompt review.
_Source:_ Royal College of Psychiatrists - high-dose antipsychotic monitoring - https://www.rcpsych.ac.uk/

## Medicines - General & Physical Health

**Q845. [Easy]** Because of the risk of dependence, for how long are benzodiazepines generally recommended for anxiety or insomnia?
- **A. Short-term only, about 2 to 4 weeks [correct]**
- B. At least 6 months
- C. Indefinitely
- D. Only for a single dose ever
_Why:_ The BNF advises benzodiazepines for short-term relief (2 to 4 weeks only) of severe, disabling anxiety or insomnia, because tolerance and dependence can develop with longer use.
_Source:_ BNF - Hypnotics and anxiolytics - https://bnf.nice.org.uk/treatment-summaries/hypnotics-and-anxiolytics/

**Q846. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient on long-term high-dose lorazepam needs to withdraw safely._ What withdrawal approach does the BNF suggest?
- A. Stop the benzodiazepine abruptly
- **B. Switch to an equivalent dose of a long-acting benzodiazepine such as diazepam, then reduce gradually in small steps [correct]**
- C. Double the dose then stop
- D. Replace it immediately with an antipsychotic
_Why:_ The BNF suggests transferring to an equivalent daily dose of diazepam (long-acting), then reducing gradually in small steps. Abrupt withdrawal after prolonged use can cause serious withdrawal effects including seizures.
_Source:_ BNF - Benzodiazepines, withdrawal - https://bnf.nice.org.uk/treatment-summaries/hypnotics-and-anxiolytics/

**Q847. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient taking a selective serotonin reuptake inhibitor (SSRI) is started on a second serotonergic drug and develops agitation, tremor, clonus, hyperreflexia and a raised temperature._ Which reaction should you suspect?
- **A. Serotonin syndrome [correct]**
- B. Neuroleptic malignant syndrome
- C. Lithium toxicity
- D. Alcohol withdrawal
_Why:_ Serotonin syndrome features neuromuscular hyperactivity (clonus, hyperreflexia, tremor), autonomic instability and altered mental state, typically after adding or increasing serotonergic drugs. The serotonergic agent should be stopped and supportive care given.
_Source:_ BNF - Antidepressants / serotonin syndrome - https://bnf.nice.org.uk/treatment-summaries/antidepressant-drugs/

**Q848. [Hard]** _Scenario: An older adult on a selective serotonin reuptake inhibitor (SSRI) becomes drowsy and confused, and blood tests show a low sodium level._ Which SSRI-associated problem does this suggest?
- **A. Hyponatraemia (often due to the syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion, SIADH) [correct]**
- B. Hyperkalaemia
- C. Hypercalcaemia
- D. Hyperglycaemia
_Why:_ SSRIs can cause hyponatraemia, usually via the syndrome of inappropriate antidiuretic hormone secretion (SIADH); older adults are at higher risk. The BNF advises considering hyponatraemia in anyone who develops drowsiness, confusion or convulsions on an antidepressant.
_Source:_ BNF - Antidepressants, hyponatraemia - https://bnf.nice.org.uk/treatment-summaries/antidepressant-drugs/

**Q849. [Medium]** _Scenario: A newly admitted acute psychiatric inpatient is on an antipsychotic, has reduced mobility and recently required physical restraint._ What preventive assessment should be carried out, given these are recognised venous thromboembolism (VTE) risk factors?
- **A. A venous thromboembolism (VTE) risk assessment on admission / at first consultant review [correct]**
- B. No assessment, as psychiatric patients are not at VTE risk
- C. Only a falls assessment
- D. Only a blood glucose check
_Why:_ NICE recommends VTE risk assessment for acute psychiatric admissions as soon as possible after admission or by first consultant review. Antipsychotics, physical restraint, reduced mobility and poor hydration all raise VTE risk.
_Source:_ NICE NG89 - Venous thromboembolism in over 16s - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng89

## Physical Health & Deterioration

**Q850. [Easy]** The National Early Warning Score 2 (NEWS2) is based on measuring how many physiological parameters?
- A. Three
- B. Five
- C. Six
- **D. Seven [correct]**
_Why:_ NEWS2 uses seven parameters: respiration rate, oxygen saturation, use of supplemental oxygen, temperature, systolic blood pressure, heart rate, and level of consciousness (ACVPU, which includes new confusion).
_Source:_ Royal College of Physicians - NEWS2 (2017) - https://www.rcp.ac.uk/improving-care/resources/national-early-warning-score-news-2/

**Q851. [Medium]** In the National Early Warning Score 2 (NEWS2), what does a single individual parameter scoring 3 (a 'red score') indicate?
- A. No action is needed if the total is low
- **B. It triggers urgent review by a clinician competent in acute care, even if the aggregate score is low [correct]**
- C. It can be ignored until the next routine observation round
- D. It only matters if two parameters score 3
_Why:_ A single (red) parameter scoring 3 warrants urgent clinical review regardless of the aggregate score, because extreme derangement in one system can signal serious deterioration.
_Source:_ Royal College of Physicians - NEWS2 thresholds and triggers (2017) - https://www.rcp.ac.uk/improving-care/resources/national-early-warning-score-news-2/

**Q852. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient's National Early Warning Score 2 (NEWS2) aggregate total is 7._ What level of clinical response does a NEWS2 aggregate score of 7 or more require?
- A. Routine 12-hourly observations only
- B. Minimum hourly observations and review by a ward-based nurse
- **C. Emergency assessment by a clinical team with critical care competencies and continuous monitoring [correct]**
- D. No change to the current plan
_Why:_ An aggregate NEWS2 of 7 or more is the high-risk band, requiring emergency assessment by a team with critical care competencies, usually continuous monitoring, and consideration of transfer to a higher level of care. (Score 5-6 is medium risk / hourly; 0-4 is low risk.)
_Source:_ Royal College of Physicians - NEWS2 thresholds and triggers (2017) - https://www.rcp.ac.uk/improving-care/resources/national-early-warning-score-news-2/

**Q853. [Medium]** The 'Sepsis Six' care bundle from the UK Sepsis Trust should ideally be delivered within what timeframe of recognising sepsis?
- **A. One hour [correct]**
- B. Six hours
- C. Twelve hours
- D. Twenty-four hours
_Why:_ The Sepsis Six is a bundle of six interventions intended to be delivered within one hour of recognising sepsis by frontline staff.
_Source:_ UK Sepsis Trust - Sepsis Six - https://sepsistrust.org/

**Q854. [Hard]** Which of the following correctly lists components of the 'Sepsis Six' bundle?
- **A. Give oxygen, take blood cultures, give intravenous antibiotics, give intravenous fluids, check lactate, monitor urine output [correct]**
- B. Give aspirin, take an ECG, give oxygen, elevate legs, check glucose, monitor temperature
- C. Give antibiotics only and reassess in six hours
- D. Take observations, document, escalate, wait, review, discharge
_Why:_ The Sepsis Six comprises: give high-flow oxygen, take blood cultures, give intravenous antibiotics, give intravenous fluids, check serial lactate, and monitor hourly urine output (three 'take' and three 'give' actions).
_Source:_ UK Sepsis Trust - Sepsis Six - https://sepsistrust.org/

**Q855. [Easy]** _Scenario: You find an adult who is unresponsive and not breathing normally._ According to Resuscitation Council UK adult basic life support, what is the ratio of chest compressions to rescue breaths?
- A. 15 compressions to 2 breaths
- **B. 30 compressions to 2 breaths [correct]**
- C. 5 compressions to 1 breath
- D. 10 compressions to 2 breaths
_Why:_ Resuscitation Council UK adult basic life support recommends alternating 30 chest compressions with 2 rescue breaths.
_Source:_ Resuscitation Council UK - Adult basic life support (2021) - https://www.resus.org.uk/library/2021-resuscitation-guidelines/adult-basic-life-support-guidelines

**Q856. [Hard]** According to Resuscitation Council UK adult basic life support, what is the recommended depth and rate of chest compressions?
- **A. At least 5 cm but not more than 6 cm, at a rate of 100-120 per minute [correct]**
- B. At least 3 cm, at a rate of 60-80 per minute
- C. At least 8 cm, at a rate of 140 per minute
- D. Depth does not matter provided compressions are fast
_Why:_ Compressions should be to a depth of at least 5 cm but not more than 6 cm, on the lower half of the sternum, at a rate of 100-120 per minute with minimal interruptions.
_Source:_ Resuscitation Council UK - Adult basic life support (2021) - https://www.resus.org.uk/library/2021-resuscitation-guidelines/adult-basic-life-support-guidelines

**Q857. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient is choking, appears distressed, cannot speak, and has an ineffective cough (severe airway obstruction) but is still conscious._ According to Resuscitation Council UK, what is the correct sequence of treatment?
- A. Give a blind finger sweep, then lie the patient down
- **B. Give up to 5 back blows, then up to 5 abdominal thrusts, alternating until relieved [correct]**
- C. Immediately start chest compressions
- D. Give water to help dislodge the object
_Why:_ For a conscious adult with severe choking, give up to 5 back blows then up to 5 abdominal thrusts, alternating until the obstruction clears or the person becomes unresponsive (then start CPR). Blind finger sweeps must not be used.
_Source:_ Resuscitation Council UK - Adult choking algorithm (2021) - https://www.resus.org.uk/library/2021-resuscitation-guidelines/adult-basic-life-support-guidelines

**Q858. [Medium]** According to National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) guidance, when should a patient be risk assessed for venous thromboembolism (VTE)?
- A. Only if they report leg pain
- **B. On admission to hospital, and reassessed within 24 hours and when the clinical situation changes [correct]**
- C. Only before discharge
- D. Only for surgical patients
_Why:_ NICE recommends all patients are assessed for VTE risk on admission, and reassessed within 24 hours of admission and whenever the clinical situation changes, so that thromboprophylaxis can be given where appropriate.
_Source:_ NICE NG89 - VTE risk assessment - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng89

**Q859. [Easy]** How should sepsis be regarded when a deteriorating patient is suspected of having an infection?
- A. A routine issue to review at the next ward round
- **B. A time-critical medical emergency [correct]**
- C. Only relevant in general hospitals
- D. Something for the GP to follow up
_Why:_ Sepsis is a life-threatening, time-critical medical emergency. Early recognition and escalation save lives.
_Source:_ Trust Sepsis Policy; NICE NG51 / QS161

**Q860. [Medium]** Which tool is used to track physical observations and trigger escalation for a deteriorating adult?
- A. Malnutrition Universal Screening Tool (MUST)
- **B. National Early Warning Score 2 (NEWS2) [correct]**
- C. Glasgow Coma Scale only
- D. Waterlow Score
_Why:_ The National Early Warning Score 2 (NEWS2) aggregates vital signs into a score that triggers a graded escalation response for physical deterioration.
_Source:_ Royal College of Physicians - NEWS2 (2017) - https://www.rcp.ac.uk/improving-care/resources/national-early-warning-score-news-2/

## Physical Health & Clinical Emergencies

**Q861. [Easy]** What is the recommended first intramuscular (IM) dose of adrenaline (epinephrine) for an adult with anaphylaxis, using 1 in 1000 (1 mg/mL) adrenaline?
- A. 50 micrograms
- B. 150 micrograms
- **C. 500 micrograms [correct]**
- D. 5 milligrams
_Why:_ Resuscitation Council UK recommends 500 micrograms IM (0.5 mL of 1:1000) for adults and teenagers, given into the anterolateral thigh.
_Source:_ Resuscitation Council UK - Emergency treatment of anaphylaxis (May 2021) - https://www.resus.org.uk/library/additional-guidance/guidance-anaphylaxis

**Q862. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient develops facial swelling, wheeze and a widespread rash minutes after a new medication. You give IM adrenaline but symptoms have not resolved after 5 minutes._ According to Resuscitation Council UK, what should you do next?
- A. Wait a further 15 minutes before any further adrenaline
- **B. Repeat the IM adrenaline dose [correct]**
- C. Switch to an oral antihistamine only
- D. Do not give any further adrenaline; it is unsafe to repeat
_Why:_ If features of anaphylaxis do not resolve, the IM adrenaline dose should be repeated after 5 minutes.
_Source:_ Resuscitation Council UK - Emergency treatment of anaphylaxis (May 2021) - https://www.resus.org.uk/library/additional-guidance/guidance-anaphylaxis

**Q863. [Hard]** Why does Resuscitation Council UK recommend an adrenaline ampoule with needle and syringe over an adrenaline auto-injector in healthcare settings for adult anaphylaxis?
- **A. Auto-injectors deliver a maximum of 300 micrograms, below the 500 microgram adult dose [correct]**
- B. Auto-injectors contain a different, weaker drug
- C. Ampoules can be given by any route including oral
- D. Auto-injectors are only licensed for children
_Why:_ Auto-injectors deliver a maximum of 300 micrograms, whereas the appropriate adult/older-child dose is 500 micrograms, so ampoule/needle/syringe is preferred where staff are trained.
_Source:_ Resuscitation Council UK - Emergency treatment of anaphylaxis (May 2021) - https://www.resus.org.uk/library/additional-guidance/guidance-anaphylaxis

**Q864. [Easy]** At or below which capillary blood glucose level is a person generally considered to have hypoglycaemia (a 'hypo')?
- **A. Below 4.0 mmol/L (millimoles per litre) [correct]**
- B. Below 7.0 mmol/L
- C. Below 10.0 mmol/L
- D. Below 2.0 mmol/L
_Why:_ Any blood glucose below 4.0 mmol/L is classed as hypoglycaemia. The common prompt is 'four is the floor'.
_Source:_ Diabetes UK / TREND-UK - Hypoglycaemia guidance - https://www.diabetes.org.uk/about-diabetes/complications/hypos

**Q865. [Medium]** _Scenario: A conscious patient who can swallow safely has a blood glucose of 3.2 mmol/L and feels shaky and sweaty._ What is the correct first treatment?
- A. A slice of wholemeal toast with butter
- **B. 15 to 20 grams of fast-acting carbohydrate, such as glucose tablets or a sugary (non-diet) drink [correct]**
- C. Intramuscular glucagon
- D. Nothing; recheck in one hour
_Why:_ A conscious patient who can swallow is treated with 15-20 g of fast-acting carbohydrate, then blood glucose is rechecked after 15 minutes. Fatty/slow carbs act too slowly for the initial correction.
_Source:_ Diabetes UK - Treating hypoglycaemia - https://www.diabetes.org.uk/about-diabetes/complications/hypos

**Q866. [Medium]** _Scenario: After giving a conscious patient 15 g of fast-acting carbohydrate for a hypo, you recheck their blood glucose._ After how long should you recheck the blood glucose?
- A. After 1 to 2 minutes
- **B. After 15 to 20 minutes [correct]**
- C. After 60 minutes
- D. After 3 hours
_Why:_ Recheck blood glucose after 15-20 minutes; if still below 4.0 mmol/L, repeat the fast-acting carbohydrate. Once recovered, follow with longer-acting carbohydrate if the next meal is not due.
_Source:_ Diabetes UK - Treating hypoglycaemia - https://www.diabetes.org.uk/about-diabetes/complications/hypos

**Q867. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient with diabetes is found unconscious and cannot swallow. Their blood glucose is 2.1 mmol/L. There is no intravenous access._ What is the appropriate emergency treatment?
- A. Place glucose gel or sugary drink into the mouth
- **B. Give 1 mg of glucagon by intramuscular injection [correct]**
- C. Wait for the patient to wake before treating
- D. Give oral glucose tablets between the cheek and gum
_Why:_ An unconscious patient must never be given oral food or fluid (aspiration risk). Give 1 mg glucagon IM (or IV glucose if trained/access available) and call for emergency help.
_Source:_ Diabetes UK / JBDS - Severe hypoglycaemia management - https://www.diabetes.org.uk/about-diabetes/complications/hypos

**Q868. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient on antipsychotic medication reports excessive thirst, passing large amounts of urine, and has a fruity smell on their breath. Blood glucose is high._ These are warning signs of which emergency?
- A. Hypoglycaemia
- **B. Diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA) [correct]**
- C. Acute dystonia
- D. Anaphylaxis
_Why:_ Thirst (polydipsia), passing lots of urine (polyuria), and a fruity (ketone) smell on the breath are classic warning signs of diabetic ketoacidosis, a hyperglycaemic emergency.
_Source:_ NHS - Diabetic ketoacidosis - https://www.nhs.uk/conditions/diabetic-ketoacidosis/

**Q869. [Hard]** Deep, rapid, sighing breathing seen in advanced diabetic ketoacidosis (DKA) is known as what?
- A. Cheyne-Stokes breathing
- **B. Kussmaul breathing [correct]**
- C. Agonal breathing
- D. Paradoxical breathing
_Why:_ Kussmaul breathing is the deep, laboured, rapid breathing pattern by which the body tries to blow off excess acid (compensating for the metabolic acidosis) in DKA.
_Source:_ StatPearls - Adult Diabetic Ketoacidosis - https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/books/NBK560723/

**Q870. [Easy]** _Scenario: You witness a patient having a generalised tonic-clonic (convulsive) seizure on the ward._ What should you do during the seizure?
- A. Restrain their limbs to stop the shaking
- B. Put a padded object between their teeth
- **C. Protect their head, clear nearby hazards, and time the seizure [correct]**
- D. Sit them upright and give a sugary drink
_Why:_ Cushion the head, move dangerous objects away, and note the time the seizure starts. Never restrain the person or put anything in their mouth.
_Source:_ Epilepsy Society - First aid for epileptic seizures - https://epilepsysociety.org.uk/about-epilepsy/first-aid-epileptic-seizures

**Q871. [Easy]** Which of these is explicitly advised AGAINST during a convulsive seizure?
- A. Timing how long the seizure lasts
- **B. Putting something between the person's teeth or in their mouth [correct]**
- C. Cushioning the head
- D. Staying with the person
_Why:_ Nothing should be put in the mouth; a person cannot swallow their tongue and this causes injury. Do not restrain the person either.
_Source:_ Epilepsy Society - Seizure first aid - https://epilepsysociety.org.uk/about-epilepsy/first-aid-epileptic-seizures

**Q872. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient has a convulsive (tonic-clonic) seizure that continues without stopping._ After how many minutes of continuous convulsive seizure should you call 999 for an ambulance?
- A. After 1 minute
- **B. After 5 minutes [correct]**
- C. After 15 minutes
- D. After 30 minutes
_Why:_ Call 999 if a convulsive seizure lasts more than 5 minutes, or if seizures repeat without recovery in between. This also defines convulsive status epilepticus.
_Source:_ Epilepsy Action - Emergency treatment of prolonged seizures - https://www.epilepsy.org.uk/info/firstaid

**Q873. [Hard]** Convulsive status epilepticus is defined as a single convulsive seizure lasting 5 minutes or more, OR:
- **A. Two or more seizures within 5 minutes without full recovery of consciousness between them [correct]**
- B. Any seizure occurring at night
- C. A seizure preceded by an aura
- D. A first-ever seizure of any length
_Why:_ Status epilepticus is a single convulsive seizure of 5 minutes or longer, or repeated seizures (2 or more) without recovery of consciousness in between. It is a medical emergency.
_Source:_ Epilepsy Action - Emergency treatment of seizures - https://www.epilepsy.org.uk/info/firstaid

**Q874. [Medium]** _Scenario: After a tonic-clonic seizure ends, the person is unresponsive but breathing normally._ What is the appropriate immediate action?
- **A. Place them in the recovery position (on their side) and stay with them [correct]**
- B. Sit them upright and give food
- C. Perform chest compressions
- D. Leave them alone to recover in private
_Why:_ Once jerking stops, place the person on their side (recovery position) to protect the airway, stay with them, and reassure as they recover. Only start CPR if they are not breathing normally.
_Source:_ Epilepsy Society - First aid for epileptic seizures - https://epilepsysociety.org.uk/about-epilepsy/first-aid-epileptic-seizures

**Q875. [Medium]** _Scenario: A young man given a high-potency antipsychotic develops a sustained, painful upward deviation of both eyes and neck stiffness a few hours after his dose._ What is this acute reaction called?
- A. Tardive dyskinesia
- **B. Acute dystonia (with oculogyric crisis) [correct]**
- C. Akathisia
- D. Neuroleptic malignant syndrome
_Why:_ Acute dystonia is a sustained, often painful muscle spasm appearing within hours to days of antipsychotic exposure; oculogyric crisis (forced upward eye deviation) is one presentation. Young men on high-potency antipsychotics are at highest risk.
_Source:_ RCEMLearning - Acute dystonia - https://www.rcemlearning.co.uk/reference/dystonia/

**Q876. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient has an acute drug-induced dystonic reaction after antipsychotic medication._ Which medication is used to treat acute dystonia?
- **A. Procyclidine [correct]**
- B. Adrenaline
- C. Salbutamol
- D. Glucagon
_Why:_ An anticholinergic such as procyclidine (given IM or IV in an acute reaction) reverses acute drug-induced dystonia.
_Source:_ RCEMLearning - Treatment of acute dystonia - https://www.rcemlearning.co.uk/reference/dystonia/

**Q877. [Hard]** _Scenario: An acute dystonic reaction is treated with intravenous (IV) procyclidine._ Within roughly what time should symptoms begin to improve after an IV dose?
- **A. Within about 5 minutes [correct]**
- B. Only after 24 hours
- C. Within about 6 hours
- D. Symptoms never respond to procyclidine
_Why:_ IV procyclidine typically improves symptoms within about 5 minutes (IM within about 20 minutes). A second dose may be needed, and prophylaxis is usually continued for 48-72 hours to prevent recurrence.
_Source:_ RCEMLearning - Management of acute dystonia - https://www.rcemlearning.co.uk/reference/dystonia/

**Q878. [Easy]** The Lester tool (Positive Cardiometabolic Health Resource) is designed to support the physical health of which group?
- **A. People with severe mental illness taking antipsychotic medication [correct]**
- B. Children under 5 years
- C. People having elective surgery
- D. Pregnant women only
_Why:_ The Lester resource helps clinicians assess and manage cardiovascular and metabolic risk in people with severe mental illness on antipsychotics, using the mantra 'Don't just screen, intervene'.
_Source:_ RCPsych / NHS England - Lester Positive Cardiometabolic Health Resource (2014) - https://www.rcpsych.ac.uk/

**Q879. [Easy]** What is the memorable mantra of the Lester cardiometabolic health resource?
- A. Screen and reassure
- **B. Don't just screen, intervene [correct]**
- C. Watch and wait
- D. Refer and forget
_Why:_ 'Don't just screen, intervene' captures the resource's aim: not only measuring cardiometabolic parameters but acting on abnormal results.
_Source:_ RCPsych - Lester cardiometabolic resource - https://www.rcpsych.ac.uk/

**Q880. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient is started on a second-generation (atypical) antipsychotic._ According to NICE guidance on psychosis and schizophrenia, which set of measurements should be recorded at baseline and monitored during treatment?
- A. Only pulse and temperature
- **B. Weight, waist, pulse, blood pressure, fasting glucose (or HbA1c) and blood lipids [correct]**
- C. Only visual acuity
- D. Only urine output
_Why:_ NICE CG178 requires baseline and ongoing monitoring of weight, waist circumference, pulse, blood pressure, fasting blood glucose (or HbA1c) and blood lipids because of cardiometabolic risk.
_Source:_ NICE - CG178 Psychosis and schizophrenia in adults (2014) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/cg178

**Q881. [Hard]** Under the metabolic syndrome criteria (for example NCEP ATP III), how many of the component features must be present to diagnose it?
- A. Any 1 of 5
- **B. Any 3 of 5 [correct]**
- C. All 5 of 5
- D. Any 2 of 3
_Why:_ Metabolic syndrome (NCEP ATP III) is diagnosed when 3 or more of five features are present: raised waist circumference, raised blood pressure, raised triglycerides, low HDL cholesterol, and raised fasting glucose.
_Source:_ AHA/NHLBI - NCEP ATP III metabolic syndrome criteria - https://www.ahajournals.org/doi/10.1161/circulationaha.105.169405

**Q882. [Medium]** Which combination of features is characteristic of metabolic syndrome?
- A. Low blood pressure, high HDL cholesterol and low waist circumference
- **B. Increased waist circumference, raised blood pressure, raised triglycerides, low HDL cholesterol and raised glucose [correct]**
- C. Slow pulse, low temperature and high oxygen saturation
- D. Raised platelets, low sodium and high potassium
_Why:_ Metabolic syndrome combines central (visceral) obesity, raised blood pressure, raised triglycerides, reduced HDL cholesterol and impaired glucose regulation, driven by insulin resistance.
_Source:_ AHA/NHLBI - Diagnosis and Management of the Metabolic Syndrome - https://www.ahajournals.org/doi/10.1161/circulationaha.105.169405

**Q883. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient taking clozapine reports not having opened their bowels for several days._ Why is constipation on clozapine treated as a serious clinical concern rather than a minor issue?
- A. It always means the dose is too low
- **B. It can progress to life-threatening gastrointestinal hypomotility, such as ileus or bowel obstruction [correct]**
- C. It only affects taste
- D. It is a sign the medication is working
_Why:_ Clozapine markedly slows gut motility; untreated constipation can progress to paralytic ileus, obstruction, ischaemia or necrosis, which can be fatal. The MHRA strengthened warnings and constipation should be actively monitored and treated.
_Source:_ MHRA / PMC - Clozapine-induced gastrointestinal hypomotility (2017) - https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC5533872/

**Q884. [Easy]** Why are people with severe mental illness on antipsychotics at higher risk of tooth decay and gum disease?
- A. Antipsychotics turn teeth blue
- **B. Medication-induced dry mouth (reduced saliva) plus factors like diet and self-care [correct]**
- C. Antipsychotics dissolve enamel directly on contact
- D. They are immune to dental problems
_Why:_ Many psychotropic medications reduce saliva (xerostomia/dry mouth), which raises the risk of caries and gum disease; poorer self-care and diet add further risk. Oral health screening and advice should be part of routine care.
_Source:_ PMC - Oral health in severe mental illness - https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12437780/

**Q885. [Medium]** _Scenario: During a hypo, a colleague suggests giving a chocolate bar as the first treatment for a conscious patient._ Why is chocolate NOT the best first choice to correct a hypo?
- A. Chocolate is toxic in diabetes
- **B. Its fat content slows sugar absorption, so it acts too slowly to correct the low glucose quickly [correct]**
- C. Chocolate lowers blood glucose further
- D. There is no reason; chocolate is the preferred first-line treatment
_Why:_ Fatty foods such as chocolate delay glucose absorption, so they act too slowly for the initial correction. Use fast-acting carbohydrate (glucose tablets, sugary drink, or juice) first, then a longer-acting carbohydrate afterwards.
_Source:_ Diabetes UK - Treating a hypo - https://www.diabetes.org.uk/about-diabetes/complications/hypos

## Infection Prevention & Control

**Q886. [Easy]** _Scenario: You are about to start a physical examination on a patient._ Which of the World Health Organization (WHO) Five Moments for Hand Hygiene applies at this point?
- A. After touching patient surroundings
- **B. Before touching a patient [correct]**
- C. After body fluid exposure risk
- D. Before leaving the ward
_Why:_ 'Before touching a patient' is Moment 1 of the WHO Five Moments. It protects the patient from germs carried on your hands.
_Source:_ WHO Five Moments for Hand Hygiene - https://www.who.int/teams/integrated-health-services/infection-prevention-control/hand-hygiene

**Q887. [Medium]** _Scenario: You are caring for a patient with suspected Clostridioides difficile (C. difficile) infection._ What is the correct approach to hand hygiene?
- A. Alcohol-based hand rub alone is sufficient
- B. No hand hygiene needed if gloves were worn
- **C. Wash with liquid soap and water [correct]**
- D. Use hand cream from the communal ward tub first
_Why:_ Alcohol hand rub does not kill C. difficile spores. Hands must be washed with soap and water for these patients (and for norovirus / diarrhoea and vomiting).
_Source:_ Trust Hand Hygiene Policy; UK Health Security Agency guidance

**Q888. [Easy]** A colleague says wearing disposable gloves removes the need for hand hygiene. Are they right?
- A. Yes, gloves replace hand washing
- **B. No, hand hygiene is still needed after removing gloves [correct]**
- C. Only if the gloves were sterile
- D. Only for high-risk patients
_Why:_ Gloves are not a substitute for hand hygiene. Hands can be contaminated during glove removal, so you must decontaminate hands after taking gloves off.
_Source:_ Trust Standard Infection Control Principles Policy

**Q889. [Easy]** What does the 'bare below the elbows' principle support during direct patient care?
- A. A smarter uniform appearance
- **B. Effective hand and wrist decontamination [correct]**
- C. Staying cooler on the ward
- D. Easier blood pressure recording
_Why:_ Short or rolled-up sleeves and no wrist jewellery or watches allow hands and wrists to be cleaned properly, reducing the spread of infection.
_Source:_ Trust Standard Infection Control Principles Policy

## Sharps & Inoculation Incidents

**Q890. [Easy]** _Scenario: You sustain a needlestick injury from a used sharp._ What is the correct immediate first aid?
- A. Suck the wound to draw out contamination
- **B. Encourage bleeding, wash with soap and water, cover with a waterproof dressing [correct]**
- C. Scrub the wound hard with a nail brush
- D. Apply alcohol gel and carry on
_Why:_ Encourage the wound to bleed, wash liberally with soap and running water (do not scrub), dry and cover with a waterproof dressing. Do NOT suck the wound.
_Source:_ Trust Inoculation Incidents Policy

**Q891. [Hard]** _Scenario: A high-risk exposure to blood-borne virus has occurred and Human Immunodeficiency Virus (HIV) exposure is possible._ What is the timeframe for post-exposure prophylaxis (PEP)?
- **A. Ideally within 1 hour; not started beyond 72 hours after exposure [correct]**
- B. Any time within the first month
- C. Within 24 hours only
- D. Only after the source patient's status is confirmed
_Why:_ HIV post-exposure prophylaxis is most effective started as soon as possible (ideally within 1 hour) and is not recommended more than 72 hours after exposure.
_Source:_ Trust Inoculation Incidents Policy; UK PEP guidance

**Q892. [Medium]** When should a sharps container be closed and disposed of?
- A. Only when completely full
- **B. When three-quarters full (or at the fill line), or after 3 months [correct]**
- C. Once a year
- D. Whenever the ward is quiet
_Why:_ Sharps bins are closed and replaced when filled to the marked line (about three-quarters full) or after a maximum of three months in use, whichever comes first.
_Source:_ Trust Inoculation Incidents Policy; Health and Safety (Sharp Instruments) Regulations 2013

## Nutrition & Hydration

**Q893. [Easy]** Which screening tool is used to identify adults at risk of malnutrition?
- **A. Malnutrition Universal Screening Tool (MUST) [correct]**
- B. National Early Warning Score 2 (NEWS2)
- C. Glasgow Coma Scale
- D. Abbreviated Mental Test Score
_Why:_ The Malnutrition Universal Screening Tool (MUST) is the recognised tool for screening adults for malnutrition risk.
_Source:_ BAPEN - MUST; NICE CG32

## Eating Disorders & Nutrition

**Q894. [Medium]** _Scenario: A ward is deciding whether to admit a person with anorexia nervosa and wants to set a strict BMI cut-off for admission._ What does National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) NG69 advise about using an absolute weight or body mass index (BMI) threshold to decide on admission for an eating disorder?
- A. Always admit below a BMI of 15
- **B. Do not use an absolute weight or BMI threshold when deciding whether to admit [correct]**
- C. Admission should be based on BMI alone
- D. Only admit if BMI is below 13
_Why:_ NG69 states: do not use an absolute weight or BMI threshold when deciding whether to admit. Decisions should consider factors such as rate of weight loss, physical decline, and carer support.
_Source:_ NICE - Eating disorders (NG69) (2017, updated 2020) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng69/chapter/Recommendations

**Q895. [Easy]** According to National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) NG69, should medication be offered as the sole treatment for anorexia nervosa?
- A. Yes, an antidepressant alone is first-line
- **B. No, do not offer medication as the sole treatment for anorexia nervosa [correct]**
- C. Yes, an antipsychotic alone is first-line
- D. Only medication is effective for anorexia nervosa
_Why:_ NG69 states: do not offer medication as the sole treatment for anorexia nervosa. First-line for adults is a psychological therapy such as CBT-ED, MANTRA or SSCM.
_Source:_ NICE - Eating disorders (NG69) (2017, updated 2020) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng69/chapter/Recommendations

**Q896. [Hard]** _Scenario: A malnourished patient with anorexia nervosa is starting refeeding on the ward._ What does National Institute for Health and Care Excellence (NICE) NG69 require of inpatient services regarding refeeding syndrome?
- A. That refeeding is delayed until BMI is normal
- **B. That staff are trained to recognise and manage refeeding syndrome, using a standard operating procedure that references national guidance such as Medical Emergencies in Eating Disorders (MEED) [correct]**
- C. That all patients receive intravenous nutrition first
- D. That electrolytes are only checked weekly
_Why:_ NG69 requires staff to be trained to recognise and manage refeeding syndrome and to use a standard operating procedure that avoids both under-nutrition and refeeding syndrome, referencing national guidance such as the Royal College of Psychiatrists' MEED.
_Source:_ NICE - Eating disorders (NG69) (2017, updated 2020) - https://www.nice.org.uk/guidance/ng69/chapter/Recommendations

## Safeguarding

**Q897. [Easy]** How many safeguarding principles are set out in the Care Act 2014?
- A. Three
- B. Four
- **C. Six [correct]**
- D. Ten
_Why:_ The Care Act 2014 sets out six safeguarding principles: Empowerment, Prevention, Proportionality, Protection, Partnership and Accountability.
_Source:_ SCIE - six principles of adult safeguarding - https://www.scie.org.uk/safeguarding/adults/introduction/six-principles/

**Q898. [Medium]** Which set correctly lists the six safeguarding principles of the Care Act 2014?
- **A. Empowerment, Prevention, Proportionality, Protection, Partnership, Accountability [correct]**
- B. Consent, Dignity, Respect, Choice, Safety, Trust
- C. Assess, Plan, Implement, Evaluate, Review, Record
- D. Prevent, Pursue, Protect, Prepare, Partner, Promote
_Why:_ The six Care Act 2014 principles are Empowerment, Prevention, Proportionality, Protection, Partnership and Accountability.
_Source:_ SCIE - six principles of adult safeguarding - https://www.scie.org.uk/safeguarding/adults/introduction/six-principles/

**Q899. [Medium]** _Scenario: A safeguarding concern is raised for an adult with capacity, and staff are deciding how to proceed._ What does 'Making Safeguarding Personal' require staff to do?
- A. Make decisions on the adult's behalf to keep them safe
- B. Follow a fixed process regardless of the adult's wishes
- **C. Engage the adult in conversations to find person-centred, outcome-focused solutions that reflect their wishes and choices [correct]**
- D. Automatically remove the adult from the situation
_Why:_ Making Safeguarding Personal is a person-centred, outcomes-focused approach: adults are seen as experts in their own lives, and staff work with them to enhance involvement, choice and control while improving wellbeing and safety.
_Source:_ SCIE - Making Safeguarding Personal - https://www.scie.org.uk/safeguarding/adults/safeguarding-adults-boards-checklist-and-resources/making-safeguarding-personal/

**Q900. [Easy]** Which of the following is recognised as a type of abuse under the Care Act 2014 statutory guidance?
- **A. Financial or material abuse [correct]**
- B. Constructive criticism
- C. Health promotion
- D. Care planning
_Why:_ The Care Act 2014 statutory guidance lists ten categories of abuse including physical, sexual, psychological/emotional, financial and material, organisational, discriminatory, domestic abuse, modern slavery, neglect and self-neglect.
_Source:_ GOV.UK - Care and support statutory guidance, Chapter 14 - https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/care-act-statutory-guidance/care-and-support-statutory-guidance

**Q901. [Hard]** _Scenario: During a ward conversation, a patient expresses views suggesting they may be being drawn into supporting extremist violence._ Under the Prevent duty, what is the appropriate action for NHS staff?
- A. Ignore it as it is not a clinical matter
- **B. Treat it as a safeguarding concern and refer through the trust's Prevent pathway (which may lead to a Channel referral) [correct]**
- C. Discharge the patient immediately
- D. Report the patient directly to counter-terrorism police without any internal process
_Why:_ The Prevent duty (Counter-Terrorism and Security Act 2015) requires NHS staff to have due regard to preventing people being drawn into terrorism, recognise signs of radicalisation, and refer concerns as a safeguarding issue via the trust Prevent lead, potentially to the multi-agency Channel programme.
_Source:_ GOV.UK - Prevent duty guidance (2015) - https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/prevent-duty-guidance

**Q902. [Hard]** Under Multi-Agency Public Protection Arrangements (MAPPA), which offenders fall into Category 1?
- A. Violent offenders sentenced to 12 months or more
- **B. Registered sexual offenders subject to notification requirements [correct]**
- C. Any offender assessed as dangerous
- D. Offenders on community sentences only
_Why:_ MAPPA Category 1 covers registered sexual offenders subject to notification requirements. Category 2 covers violent offenders sentenced to 12 months or more; Category 3 covers other dangerous offenders assessed as posing a risk of serious harm.
_Source:_ GOV.UK - Multi-agency public protection arrangements (MAPPA) - https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/multi-agency-public-protection-arrangements-mappa--3

**Q903. [Medium]** 'Making Safeguarding Personal' in adult safeguarding is best described as an approach that is:
- A. Process-led and paperwork-focused
- **B. Person-led and outcome-focused [correct]**
- C. Led entirely by the police
- D. Decided by the ward manager alone
_Why:_ Making Safeguarding Personal means working WITH the adult about the outcomes they want - it is person-led and outcome-focused, not a tick-box process.
_Source:_ Care Act 2014; Making Safeguarding Personal (LGA/ADASS)

**Q904. [Easy]** Who is responsible for safeguarding children and vulnerable adults?
- A. Only the safeguarding lead
- B. Only senior clinicians
- **C. Everyone who comes into contact with them [correct]**
- D. Only social services
_Why:_ Safeguarding is everyone's responsibility. All staff have a role in recognising and responding to concerns.
_Source:_ Care Act 2014; Working Together to Safeguard Children

## Information Governance & Confidentiality

**Q905. [Easy]** How many Caldicott Principles are there following the National Data Guardian's 2020 update?
- A. 6
- B. 7
- **C. 8 [correct]**
- D. 10
_Why:_ The National Data Guardian published a revised, expanded set of 8 Caldicott Principles in December 2020, adding an eighth principle about informing patients how their confidential information is used.
_Source:_ National Data Guardian (GOV.UK) - The Caldicott Principles (December 2020) - https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/the-caldicott-principles

**Q906. [Easy]** Which Caldicott Principle states that 'the duty to share information for individual care is as important as the duty to protect patient confidentiality'?
- A. Principle 1
- B. Principle 4
- **C. Principle 7 [correct]**
- D. Principle 8
_Why:_ Principle 7 confirms that appropriately sharing information for a patient's care is as important as protecting confidentiality, so professionals should have the confidence to share in patients' best interests.
_Source:_ National Data Guardian (GOV.UK) - The Caldicott Principles (December 2020) - https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/the-caldicott-principles

**Q907. [Easy]** What is the primary role of a Caldicott Guardian in an NHS organisation?
- A. To lead cyber-security and IT infrastructure
- **B. To be a senior person who safeguards the confidentiality of patient information and advises on ethical and lawful information sharing [correct]**
- C. To respond to all subject access requests
- D. To act as the organisation's Data Protection Officer under UK GDPR
_Why:_ A Caldicott Guardian is a senior person responsible for protecting the confidentiality of people's health and care information and enabling appropriate, lawful, ethical information sharing. It is a distinct role from the Data Protection Officer.
_Source:_ National Data Guardian (GOV.UK) - Caldicott Guardian guidance (August 2021) - https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/national-data-guardian-guidance-on-the-appointment-of-caldicott-guardians-their-role-and-responsibilities

**Q908. [Medium]** _Scenario: A colleague argues that because patient confidentiality is paramount, staff should never share information about a patient with other members of the multidisciplinary team._ According to the Caldicott Principles, which statement is most accurate?
- A. Confidential information can never be shared without written consent
- **B. The duty to share information for a patient's individual care is as important as the duty to protect confidentiality [correct]**
- C. Only the Caldicott Guardian may share any patient information
- D. Sharing is only permitted once the patient is discharged
_Why:_ Caldicott Principle 7 makes clear that sharing for individual care is as important as protecting confidentiality; staff should have the confidence to share appropriately in the patient's best interests within the Caldicott framework.
_Source:_ National Data Guardian (GOV.UK) - The Caldicott Principles (December 2020) - https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/the-caldicott-principles

**Q909. [Medium]** _Scenario: You need to send a patient's information to another team for a specific purpose._ Which Caldicott Principle requires that you use only the minimum amount of confidential information necessary?
- **A. Principle 3 - Use the minimum necessary confidential information [correct]**
- B. Principle 5 - Everyone should be aware of their responsibilities
- C. Principle 6 - Comply with the law
- D. Principle 8 - Inform patients how their information is used
_Why:_ Principle 3 requires that where use of confidential information is necessary, each item must be justified so that only the minimum amount necessary for the function is included.
_Source:_ National Data Guardian (GOV.UK) - The Caldicott Principles (December 2020) - https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/the-caldicott-principles

**Q910. [Medium]** The eighth Caldicott Principle, added in 2020, is best summarised as which of the following?
- A. Access to confidential information should be on a strict need-to-know basis
- **B. Inform patients and service users about how their confidential information is used [correct]**
- C. Justify the purpose for using confidential information
- D. Every use of confidential information must be lawful
_Why:_ Principle 8, added in the 2020 update, requires that steps are taken so there are 'no surprises' for patients about how and why their confidential information is used and what choices they have.
_Source:_ National Data Guardian (GOV.UK) - The Caldicott Principles (December 2020) - https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/the-caldicott-principles

**Q911. [Easy]** Under the United Kingdom General Data Protection Regulation (UK GDPR), information about a person's physical or mental health is classified as what type of data?
- A. Ordinary personal data
- **B. Special category data [correct]**
- C. Anonymised data
- D. Publicly available data
_Why:_ Health data (including mental health) is 'special category data' under Article 9 of the UK GDPR, which attracts additional protections and conditions for processing.
_Source:_ Information Commissioner's Office (ICO) - Special category data - https://ico.org.uk/for-organisations/uk-gdpr-guidance-and-resources/lawful-basis/special-category-data/what-is-special-category-data/

**Q912. [Hard]** To lawfully process a patient's health data, which combination must an NHS organisation identify under the United Kingdom General Data Protection Regulation (UK GDPR)?
- A. Only an Article 6 lawful basis
- B. Only an Article 9 condition
- **C. Both an Article 6 lawful basis and a separate Article 9 condition [correct]**
- D. Written consent in every case regardless of Article 6 or 9
_Why:_ Health data is special category data, so a controller must identify both an Article 6 lawful basis and a separate Article 9 condition (several of which also require a basis in UK law under Schedule 1 of the Data Protection Act 2018).
_Source:_ ICO - Conditions for processing special category data - https://ico.org.uk/for-organisations/uk-gdpr-guidance-and-resources/lawful-basis/special-category-data/what-are-the-conditions-for-processing/

**Q913. [Hard]** Under the Data Protection Act 2018, several Article 9 conditions for processing health data require an additional condition to be met. Where in UK law are these additional conditions and safeguards set out?
- **A. Schedule 1 of the Data Protection Act 2018 [correct]**
- B. The NMC Code
- C. Article 6 of the UK GDPR
- D. The Mental Health Act 1983
_Why:_ Five of the Article 9 conditions require you to meet additional conditions and safeguards set out in UK law, specifically Schedule 1 of the Data Protection Act 2018 (for example, the health or social care condition).
_Source:_ ICO - Rules on special category data - https://ico.org.uk/for-organisations/uk-gdpr-guidance-and-resources/lawful-basis/special-category-data/what-are-the-rules-on-special-category-data/

**Q914. [Medium]** Under the common law duty of confidentiality, which of the following is a recognised basis for disclosing confidential patient information?
- A. Because a colleague is simply curious about the case
- **B. Where disclosure is required by law, the patient consents, or it is justified in the public interest [correct]**
- C. Whenever information would be quicker to share verbally
- D. Only after the patient has been discharged
_Why:_ The common law permits disclosure of confidential information where the person consents, where there is a legal requirement, or where disclosure is justified in the public interest (for example to protect others from serious harm).
_Source:_ NHS England - Information governance and data protection - https://www.england.nhs.uk/long-read/information-governance-and-data-protection/

**Q915. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient discloses a credible, specific intention to seriously harm a named third party and refuses to consent to you telling anyone._ What does professional guidance say about breaching confidentiality in this situation?
- A. Confidentiality is absolute; you may never disclose without consent
- **B. Disclosure may be justified in the public interest where failure to disclose would leave others at risk of death or serious harm [correct]**
- C. You must wait for a court order before any disclosure
- D. You may only tell the patient's family
_Why:_ Disclosure without consent can be justified in the public interest where the failure to disclose would leave others at risk of death or serious harm; the professional must weigh their duty to the patient against protecting others.
_Source:_ General Medical Council (GMC) - Confidentiality - https://www.gmc-uk.org/professional-standards/professional-standards-for-doctors/confidentiality

**Q916. [Medium]** _Scenario: A safeguarding concern arises about a child living with one of your patients._ How does the common law duty of confidentiality apply to safeguarding concerns about a child?
- A. Confidentiality always prevents sharing without the patient's consent
- **B. Safeguarding concerns about a child are a recognised exception where information may be shared appropriately [correct]**
- C. You may only share after the child reaches 18
- D. Only the police may be told, never social care
_Why:_ NHS England guidance notes there are exceptions to the common law duty of confidentiality, for example where there are safeguarding concerns about a child, allowing appropriate information sharing.
_Source:_ NHS England - Information governance and data protection - https://www.england.nhs.uk/long-read/information-governance-and-data-protection/

**Q917. [Medium]** What is the difference between sharing information based on consent and sharing based on the public interest?
- A. There is no difference; the terms are interchangeable
- **B. Consent means the patient has agreed to the sharing, whereas a public interest disclosure can be made without consent to prevent serious harm [correct]**
- C. Public interest sharing always requires the patient's written signature
- D. Consent-based sharing is unlawful in the NHS
_Why:_ Consent-based sharing relies on the patient agreeing; a public interest disclosure can occur without consent where necessary to prevent death or serious harm to others, balancing competing duties.
_Source:_ General Medical Council (GMC) - Confidentiality - https://www.gmc-uk.org/professional-standards/professional-standards-for-doctors/confidentiality

**Q918. [Easy]** _Scenario: A patient asks for a copy of all the information your organisation holds about them._ What is this request called under the United Kingdom General Data Protection Regulation (UK GDPR)?
- A. A freedom of information request
- **B. A subject access request [correct]**
- C. A Caldicott request
- D. A public interest disclosure
_Why:_ An individual asking for a copy of their own personal data is exercising their right of access, made via a subject access request under the UK GDPR.
_Source:_ ICO - A guide to subject access - https://ico.org.uk/for-organisations/uk-gdpr-guidance-and-resources/subject-access-requests/a-guide-to-subject-access/

**Q919. [Medium]** Within what standard timescale must an organisation normally respond to a subject access request under the United Kingdom General Data Protection Regulation (UK GDPR)?
- A. Within 72 hours
- **B. Within one calendar month of receipt [correct]**
- C. Within 40 working days
- D. Within six months
_Why:_ Organisations must respond without undue delay and within one calendar month of receiving the request. This can be extended by up to two further months if the request is complex or numerous.
_Source:_ ICO - Time limits for responding to rights requests - https://ico.org.uk/for-the-public/time-limits-for-responding-to-data-protection-rights-requests/

**Q920. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient's subject access request is genuinely complex and involves a large volume of records._ By how long may the one-month response deadline be extended, and what must you do?
- **A. By up to two further months, informing the requester within one month and explaining why [correct]**
- B. By up to one further month, with no need to notify the requester
- C. By up to six further months automatically
- D. No extension is ever permitted
_Why:_ The deadline can be extended by up to two further months where the request is complex or numerous, but the requester must be told within one month of receipt and given the reason.
_Source:_ ICO - A guide to subject access - https://ico.org.uk/for-organisations/uk-gdpr-guidance-and-resources/subject-access-requests/a-guide-to-subject-access/

**Q921. [Medium]** In most circumstances, what fee can an organisation charge an individual for making a subject access request?
- A. A standard fee of 10 pounds
- **B. No fee; requests are generally free [correct]**
- C. A fee based on the number of pages
- D. A fee set by the Caldicott Guardian
_Why:_ In most circumstances organisations cannot charge a fee to deal with a subject access request; it is free, though a reasonable fee may be charged only for manifestly unfounded, excessive or repeat requests.
_Source:_ ICO - A guide to subject access - https://ico.org.uk/for-organisations/uk-gdpr-guidance-and-resources/subject-access-requests/a-guide-to-subject-access/

**Q922. [Easy]** According to the Nursing and Midwifery Council (NMC) Code, when should records be completed?
- A. Only at the end of the shift
- **B. At the time of the event, or as soon as possible afterwards [correct]**
- C. Within seven days of the event
- D. Only when requested by a manager
_Why:_ The NMC Code requires nurses to complete records at the time or as soon as possible after an event, recording clearly whether notes are made some time after the event.
_Source:_ Nursing and Midwifery Council - The Code (2018) - https://www.nmc.org.uk/standards/code/read-the-code-online/

**Q923. [Medium]** _Scenario: You are writing an entry in a patient's notes about their presentation this morning._ Which set of standards best reflects the Nursing and Midwifery Council (NMC) Code's requirements for record-keeping?
- A. Records may include personal opinion, guesswork and jargon
- **B. Records should be clear, accurate, factual, completed without delay, dated, timed and attributable, with no falsification [correct]**
- C. Records only need to be legible if they will be audited
- D. It is acceptable to leave blank gaps to fill in later
_Why:_ The NMC Code requires records to be accurate, clear, legible, completed without unnecessary delay, dated, timed, attributable to the author, and free from falsification, unnecessary abbreviations, jargon or speculation.
_Source:_ Nursing and Midwifery Council - The Code (2018) - https://www.nmc.org.uk/standards/code/read-the-code-online/

**Q924. [Hard]** _Scenario: A nurse asks how long an adult patient's mental health records should be retained after they are last seen._ According to the Records Management Code of Practice for Health and Social Care, what is the recommended minimum retention period for mental health records?
- A. 8 years after last contact
- **B. 20 years after the patient was last seen, or 3 years after death [correct]**
- C. 6 years after last contact
- D. Indefinitely
_Why:_ The Records Management Code of Practice recommends mental health records be retained for 20 years after the patient was last seen, or 3 years after the patient's death.
_Source:_ NHS England - Records Management Code of Practice (2021) - https://transform.england.nhs.uk/information-governance/guidance/records-management-code/

**Q925. [Medium]** _Scenario: A laptop containing unencrypted patient records is lost, and there is a real risk to patients' rights and freedoms._ Within what timescale must a notifiable personal data breach be reported to the Information Commissioner's Office (ICO)?
- A. Within 24 hours
- **B. Without undue delay and no later than 72 hours after becoming aware of it [correct]**
- C. Within 7 days
- D. Within one month
_Why:_ A notifiable personal data breach must be reported to the ICO without undue delay and not later than 72 hours after becoming aware of it; if reporting is delayed, reasons must be given.
_Source:_ ICO - Personal data breaches: a guide - https://ico.org.uk/for-organisations/report-a-breach/personal-data-breach/personal-data-breaches-a-guide/

**Q926. [Hard]** _Scenario: A minor breach occurs but, on assessment, it is unlikely to result in any risk to individuals' rights and freedoms._ What is the correct approach to reporting this breach to the Information Commissioner's Office (ICO)?
- A. Every breach must always be reported to the ICO regardless of risk
- **B. It need not be reported to the ICO, but it must still be documented internally [correct]**
- C. It should be reported to the police instead
- D. It should be ignored entirely with no record kept
_Why:_ A breach only needs to be reported to the ICO if it is likely to result in a risk to individuals' rights and freedoms. Even where reporting is not required, the organisation must still record and document the breach.
_Source:_ ICO - Personal data breaches: a guide - https://ico.org.uk/for-organisations/report-a-breach/personal-data-breach/personal-data-breaches-a-guide/

**Q927. [Medium]** _Scenario: A patient's daughter phones the ward asking for a detailed update on her mother's mental state and treatment._ What is the most appropriate response consistent with the Nursing and Midwifery Council (NMC) Code and confidentiality principles?
- A. Provide full clinical details because she is a close relative
- B. Refuse to share anything under any circumstances
- **C. Consider the patient's wishes and consent (and capacity); share with families and carers as far as the law allows and the patient agrees [correct]**
- D. Only share information if the daughter puts the request in writing
_Why:_ The NMC Code expects information to be shared with families and carers as far as the law allows and sensitively, but this is governed by the patient's consent and wishes (and capacity), not simply by family relationship.
_Source:_ Nursing and Midwifery Council - The Code (2018) - https://www.nmc.org.uk/standards/code/read-the-code-online/

**Q928. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient who lacks the mental capacity to decide about sharing information has a family carer requesting an update._ What should primarily guide a decision about sharing information in this situation?
- A. Always share, because the carer has a legal right to all information
- B. Never share, because the patient cannot consent
- **C. An assessment under the Mental Capacity Act 2005, acting in the patient's best interests while keeping their rights at the centre of decision-making [correct]**
- D. Wait until the patient regains capacity, whatever the circumstances
_Why:_ Where a patient lacks capacity to consent to disclosure, decisions should follow the Mental Capacity Act 2005, made in the patient's best interests, keeping the rights and best interests of those lacking capacity central.
_Source:_ Nursing and Midwifery Council - The Code (2018) - https://www.nmc.org.uk/standards/code/read-the-code-online/

**Q929. [Hard]** The Health and Social Care (National Data Guardian) Act 2018 gave the National Data Guardian statutory power to do what?
- A. Prosecute individuals for data breaches directly
- **B. Publish official guidance about the processing of health and adult social care data in England, which relevant bodies must have regard to [correct]**
- C. Replace the Information Commissioner's Office (ICO)
- D. Approve every subject access request nationally
_Why:_ The 2018 Act placed the National Data Guardian on a statutory footing, giving the power to publish guidance about processing health and adult social care data in England, to which relevant public bodies must have regard.
_Source:_ Health and Social Care (National Data Guardian) Act 2018 (legislation.gov.uk) (2018) - https://www.legislation.gov.uk/ukpga/2018/31/enacted

## Professional Practice & NMC Code

**Q930. [Easy]** The Nursing and Midwifery Council (NMC) Code is built around four themes. Which of the following is one of those four themes?
- **A. Prioritise people [correct]**
- B. Maximise efficiency
- C. Follow orders
- D. Reduce costs
_Why:_ The NMC Code (2018) is structured around four themes: Prioritise people, Practise effectively, Preserve safety, and Promote professionalism and trust.
_Source:_ NMC - The Code (2018) - https://www.nmc.org.uk/standards/code/

**Q931. [Medium]** _Scenario: A medication error reaches a patient and causes moderate harm. The nurse involved is unsure whether to tell the patient._ Under the professional duty of candour, what must the nurse do?
- A. Wait to see if the patient notices before saying anything
- **B. Be open and honest with the patient, tell them when something has gone wrong and apologise [correct]**
- C. Only report it internally and not tell the patient
- D. Refer the decision entirely to the ward manager and take no personal action
_Why:_ The professional duty of candour requires healthcare professionals to be open and honest with patients when something goes wrong, to tell them, apologise, and offer support - this is separate from and additional to internal incident reporting.
_Source:_ NMC / GMC - professional duty of candour (2024) - https://www.nmc.org.uk/standards/guidance/the-professional-duty-of-candour/

**Q932. [Medium]** How often must a nurse complete Nursing and Midwifery Council (NMC) revalidation to remain on the register?
- A. Every year
- B. Every two years
- **C. Every three years [correct]**
- D. Every five years
_Why:_ NMC revalidation is completed every three years, at the point of renewing registration.
_Source:_ NMC - revalidation - https://www.nmc.org.uk/revalidation/requirements/practice-hours/

**Q933. [Hard]** For standard Nursing and Midwifery Council (NMC) revalidation, how many practice hours and continuing professional development (CPD) hours are required over the three-year period?
- A. 300 practice hours and 20 CPD hours
- **B. 450 practice hours and 35 CPD hours (at least 20 participatory) [correct]**
- C. 600 practice hours and 40 CPD hours
- D. 900 practice hours and 50 CPD hours
_Why:_ Revalidation requires at least 450 hours of registered practice and 35 hours of CPD over three years, of which at least 20 hours must be participatory learning. (900 practice hours applies only to dual registrants.)
_Source:_ NMC - revalidation requirements - https://www.nmc.org.uk/revalidation/requirements/practice-hours/

**Q934. [Hard]** The statutory duty of candour (Care Quality Commission Regulation 20) is triggered by a notifiable safety incident. A notifiable safety incident must, in the reasonable opinion of a healthcare professional, have resulted in (or risk) which minimum level of harm?
- A. Any harm, however minor
- B. Low harm
- **C. Moderate harm [correct]**
- D. Only death
_Why:_ A notifiable safety incident is one that was unintended/unexpected, occurred during a regulated activity, and in a healthcare professional's reasonable opinion has resulted in (or could result in) death, or severe or moderate harm. Moderate harm is the minimum threshold.
_Source:_ CQC - Regulation 20 Duty of candour (2022) - https://www.cqc.org.uk/guidance-regulation/providers/regulations/regulation-20-duty-candour

**Q935. [Medium]** _Scenario: A senior nurse delegates a clinical task to a healthcare support worker._ Under the Nursing and Midwifery Council (NMC) Code, what is the registered nurse's responsibility when delegating?
- A. Accountability transfers entirely to the support worker once delegated
- **B. The nurse remains accountable for the decision to delegate and must ensure the task is within the other person's competence and appropriately supervised [correct]**
- C. Delegation removes all responsibility from the nurse
- D. The nurse only needs to document that the task was delegated
_Why:_ The Code requires registrants to only delegate tasks within the other person's competence, ensure supervision and support, and confirm the outcome meets the required standard. The registered nurse remains accountable for the decision to delegate.
_Source:_ NMC - The Code (2018) - https://www.nmc.org.uk/standards/code/

**Q936. [Medium]** Under the Mental Capacity Act 2005, what must you assume about a person's capacity before any assessment?
- A. That they lack capacity until they prove otherwise
- **B. That they have capacity unless it is established that they lack it [correct]**
- C. That capacity depends on their diagnosis alone
- D. That relatives decide whether they have capacity
_Why:_ The first statutory principle of the Mental Capacity Act 2005 is the presumption of capacity: a person must be assumed to have capacity unless it is established that they lack it.
_Source:_ SCIE - Mental Capacity Act 2005 at a glance - https://www.scie.org.uk/mca/introduction/mental-capacity-act-2005-at-a-glance/

**Q937. [Hard]** _Scenario: A patient with capacity refuses recommended treatment, making a decision the team considers unwise._ Under the Mental Capacity Act 2005, does making an unwise decision mean the patient lacks capacity?
- A. Yes, an unwise decision proves a lack of capacity
- **B. No, a person is not to be treated as unable to make a decision merely because it is unwise [correct]**
- C. Only if two clinicians agree the decision is unwise
- D. Only if the decision could cause harm
_Why:_ A core principle of the Mental Capacity Act 2005 is that people have the right to make what others might regard as an unwise or eccentric decision; an unwise decision does not of itself indicate a lack of capacity.
_Source:_ SCIE - Mental Capacity Act 2005 at a glance - https://www.scie.org.uk/mca/introduction/mental-capacity-act-2005-at-a-glance/

**Q938. [Hard]** The Royal College of Nursing (RCN) describes restrictive interventions and least restrictive practice. Which of the following counts as a restrictive intervention?
- A. Offering a patient a choice of activities
- **B. Manual restraint, seclusion, mechanical restraint and rapid tranquillisation [correct]**
- C. Writing a care plan with the patient
- D. Providing information leaflets
_Why:_ Restrictive interventions are those that may infringe a person's human rights and freedom of movement, and include observation, seclusion, manual (physical) restraint, mechanical restraint and rapid tranquillisation. The least restrictive option should always be used, as a last resort after de-escalation.
_Source:_ Royal College of Nursing - restrictive interventions - https://www.rcn.org.uk/

---

## How to grow this bank

- Each topic lives in a `research-*.json` batch under `src/lib/data/quiz/`. Add a new file (same shape), regenerate, and the new questions fold in automatically with fresh numbering.
- Duplicate questions (same wording) are removed automatically.
- To sign a question off as checked, tell Claude the question number(s).
- The live `/quiz` page reads the same JSON, so signed-off content flows straight through.
